\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\xi\cdot x}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\nu(h)\,\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\xi\cdot x}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\,\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{u}(\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(e^{i\xi\cdot h}+e^{-i\xi\cdot h}-2)\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\widehat{u}(\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\cos{(\xi\cdot h)})\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

∎

Remark 2.6
.

Assume that
\nu
is radial. In this case we abuse the notation by writing
\nu(h)=\nu(|h|)
for all
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
O\in\mathcal{O}(d)
be a rotation on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
O(|\xi|e_{1})=\xi
and enforce the change of variables
h^{\prime}=O^{T}h
. Then
dh=dh^{\prime}
by the rotation invariance of the Lebesgue measure. Note that the rotation preserves the inner product and
O^{T}=O^{-1}
hence
\xi\cdot h=O(|\xi|e_{1})\cdot OO^{T}h=|\xi|e_{1}\cdot O^{T}h=|\xi|e_{1}\cdot h^{\prime}
. In addition since
\nu
is radial, we have
\nu(|h|)=\nu(|h^{\prime}|)
. As a result, the Fourier symbol
\psi(\xi)
becomes,

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\cos{(\xi\cdot h)})\nu(|h|)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\cos{(|\xi|e_{1}\cdot h^{\prime})})\nu(|h^{\prime}|)\,\,\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}

(2.5)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\cos{(h_{1}})\nu(h/|\xi|)\frac{\,\,\mathrm{d}h}{|\xi|^{-d}}\quad(\xi\neq 0)

\displaystyle=\psi(|\xi|e_{1}).

D.6: Generator of a symmetric Lévy process and of a semi-group
Assume that
\nu
is radial, almost decreasing and
\int_{\R^{d}}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\infty
. According to
[
KR16
, Lemma 2.5]
for each
t\geq 0
, there exists a continuous function
p_{t}\geq 0
in
\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that in the Fourier space we have

\displaystyle\widehat{p_{t}}(\xi)=\int_{\R^{d}}e^{-i\xi\cdot x}p_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=e^{-t\psi(\xi)},\qquad\qquad(\xi\in\R^{d}).

Obviously,
p_{t+s}=p_{t}p_{s}=p_{s}p_{t}
for all
t,s\geq 0
. Therefore, the family
(P_{t})_{t}
is defined by

\displaystyle P_{t}u(x)=u*p_{t}=\int_{\R^{d}}u(y)p_{t}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\qquad (x\in\R^{d}).

(P_{t})_{t}
is clearly a strong continuous semigroup on
L^{2}(\R^{d})
, whose generator is
-L
. We solely show that the operator
-L
is the generator of
(P_{t})_{t}
. Let
u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d})
we know that
\widehat{Lu}(\xi)=\widehat{u}(\xi)\psi(\xi)
. The Plancherel theorem implies,

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{P_{t}u-u}{t}-(-Lu)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\R^{d})}=\Big\|\frac{\widehat{p_{t}}\widehat{u}-\widehat{u}}{t}+\widehat{u}\psi\Big\|_{L^{2}(\R^{d})}=\Big\|\widehat{u}\psi\frac{e^{-t\psi}-1+t\psi}{t\psi}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{t\to 0}0.

The rightmost term goes to
0
as
t\to 0
since the function
\zeta:s\mapsto\frac{e^{-s}-1+s}{s}
with
\zeta(0)=0
is continuous and bounded on
[0,\infty)
. Thus, applying the dominated convergence theorem suffices.

The Kolmogorov extension theorem (see
[
Sat13
]
) actually infers the existence of a stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t}
with the transition density is
p_{t}(x,y)=p_{t}(x-y)
, namely
\mathbb{P}^{x}(X_{t}\in A)=\mathbb{E}^{x}[\mathds{1}_{A}(X_{t})]
. More generally

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{x}[u(X_{t})]=\int_{\R^{d}}u(y)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Here
\mathbb{P}^{x}
(resp.
\mathbb{E}^{x}
) is the probability (resp. the expectation) corresponding to a process
(X_{t})_{t}
starting from the position
x
, i.e.
\mathbb{P}^{x}(X_{0}=x)=1
.
The generator of such a stochastic process turns out to be
-L
. Indeed for a smooth function
u
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}^{x}[u(X_{t})]-u(x)}{t}=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{P_{t}u(x)-u(x)}{t}=-Lu(x).

In fact,
(X_{t})_{t}
is a pure-jump isotropic unimodal Lévy process in
\R^{d}
, i.e., a stochastic process with stationary and independent increments and càdlàg paths whose transition function
p_{t}(x)
is isotropic and unimodal. We refer to
[
Sat13
]
for a more extensive study on Lévy processes.

D.7: Energy form
We now show that the integrodifferential operator
L
is intimately related with a Hilbert space of greatest interest in its own right. Let
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the space of functions
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,u)<\infty
where we consider the bilinear form,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad(u,v\in H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

The space
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
becomes a Hilbert space when furnished with the inner product

\displaystyle(u,v)_{H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=(u,v)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,v),\qquad u,v\in H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Regarding the increments between the variables
x
and
y
involved in the integrand of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,v)
one legitimately suspects some close relation with integrodifferential operator
L
. This intuition is in fact correct, and we show below that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\cdot,\cdot)
can be viewed as the energy form associated with
L
. To do this, let us first observe that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed, for
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we clearly
have that
u,|\nabla u|\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and arguing analogously as the proof of Proposition
2.5
ones readily arrives at

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}

\displaystyle\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{B_{1}}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{2}\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}x+2\int_{B_{1}^{c}}\hskip-8.61108pt\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)|^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-2.15277pt<\infty.

Noting that
|1-e^{-it}|^{2}=4\big|\tfrac{|e^{it/2}-e^{-it/2}}{2i}\big|^{2}=4\sin^{2}\frac{t}{2}=2(1-\cos{t})
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, it follows that for
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Plancherel theorem yields,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(x+h))^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{u}(\xi)|^{2}|1-e^{-i\xi\cdot h}|^{2}\,\,\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{u}(\xi)|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\cos{(\xi\cdot h)})\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\,\,\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{u}(\xi)|^{2}\psi(\xi)\,\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{u}(\xi)\psi(\xi)\overline{\widehat{u}(\xi)}\,\,\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{Lu}(\xi)\overline{\widehat{u}(\xi)}\,\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Here, the notation
\overline{z}
denotes the conjugate of a complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Employing the Plancherel theorem again to the last expression produces the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)Lu(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Replacing
u
by
u+v
leads to the relation,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(x)Lu(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)Lv(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all}~~u,v\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Therefore, due to the density of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
Lu
can be seen as a continuous linear form on
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, through the dual pairing

\displaystyle\langle Lu,v\rangle=\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u,v)\qquad\text{for all}~~v\in H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The integrodifferential operator
L
can be extended to functions
u
in
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thereupon,
L
can legitimately be regarded as a linear bounded operator from
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into its dual, i.e.
L:H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\big(H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)^{\prime}
. In this case, we observe that
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a fairly large domain for
L
. It is worthy nothing that through the correspondence
L:H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\big(H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)^{\prime}
,
L
may not always be evaluated in the classical sense.

D.8: Distributions
It is natural to want to know the largest possible functional space for which
Lu
is defined.
In an attempt to answer this question, assume in addition that
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
is unimodal, i.e.
\nu
is radial and almost decreasing and there is a constant
c
such that
\nu(y)\leq c\nu(x)
whenever
|y|\geq|x|
. Let us define the function

\widehat{\nu}(x)=\nu(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+|x|)).

It is not difficult to show that
\widehat{\nu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(c.f. the proof of Lemma
3.24
). Note that the space
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
is fairly large and contains the spaces
C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Most importantly, we show in Chapter
3
that
H_{\nu}(\R^{d})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
.
Furthermore, for
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
, we have that
Lu(x)
exists in the classical sense for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let us prove this formally.

Proposition 2.7
.

Assume
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
is unimodal. Let
u\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
, then
Lu(x)
is well defined for all
x\in\R^{d}
.

Proof.
.

For
x\in\R^{d}
, set
R=2|x|+1
, then for
y\in B^{c}_{R}(0)
we have
|x-y|\geq\frac{|y|}{2}+\frac{R}{2}-|x|\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+|y|).

As
\nu
is almost deceasing, if
y\in B^{c}_{R}(0)
, we have
\nu(x-y)\leq c\widehat{\nu}(y)
. Consequently, for
u\in L^{1}(\R^{d},\widehat{\nu})
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{B^{c}_{R}(0)}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|\widehat{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq c|u(x)|\|\widehat{\nu}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}+c\int_{B^{c}_{R}(0)}|u(y)\widehat{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

By exploiting (
2.4
) we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{B_{R}(0)}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{R}(0)}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 4\|u\|_{C^{2}(B_{R}(0))}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

In conclusion,
Lu(x)
exists since

\displaystyle Lu(x)=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{B_{R}(0)}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{B^{c}_{R}(0)}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

∎

A refinement of Proposition
2.7
for a possibly larger space, with an analog proof is given as follows:

Proposition 2.8
.

Assume
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
is radial and almost decreasing. Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small and
\beta_{\nu}
be the Blumenthal-Getoor index of
\nu
. Then, for
u\in C^{\beta_{\nu}+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
,
Lu(x)
is well defined for all
x\in\R^{d}
.

Remark 2.9
.

For the simple instance
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-\alpha}
(
\alpha\in(0,2)
) we have
\widehat{\nu}\asymp 1\land\nu\asymp(1+|h|)^{-d-\alpha}
.

We now deal with the situation where
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
and show that
Lu
is a distribution. We recall that
\mathcal{D}(\R^{d})
is the space
C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
endowed its natural topology and a sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n}\subset\mathcal{D}(\R^{d})
,

\varphi_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
in
\mathcal{D}(\R^{d})
if and only if there exists a Compact set
K\subset\R^{d}
such that
\supp\varphi_{n}\subset K
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\|\partial^{\alpha}\varphi_{n}\|_{C(K)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}
. The space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\R^{d})

whose elements are usually called
distributions
, is the collection of continuous linear forms on
\mathcal{D}(\R^{d})
. Furthermore, a sequence of linear forms
(T_{n})_{n}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\R^{d})
converges to another one
T
if and only if
\langle T_{n}-T,\varphi\rangle\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\R^{d})
. Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the dual pairing between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\R^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}(\R^{d})
.

Proposition 2.10
.

For
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
,
Lu
defines a distribution
via the mapping

\varphi\mapsto\langle Lu,\varphi\rangle:=(u,L\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\qquad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d}).

Moreover, by applying this procedure, the linear map
L:\,L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\R^{d})
with
u\mapsto Lu
is continuous.

Proof.
.

Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be supported in
B_{R}(0)
where
R\geq 1
is sufficiently large.
First, we claim that

(2.6)

\displaystyle|L\varphi(x)|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\widehat{\nu}(x)\qquad\text{for all}~x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Here the constant
C=C(R,d,\nu)
depends only on
R,d
and
\nu
. To be sure, suppose
|x|\geq 4R
, so that
\varphi(x)=0
. Since
|x-y|\geq\frac{|x|}{2}+\frac{|x|}{2}-|y|\geq\frac{|x|}{2}+R\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+|x|)
for
y\in B_{R}(0)
, the monotonicity of
\nu
implies
\nu(x-y)\leq c\widehat{\nu}(x)
. Accordingly,

\displaystyle|L\varphi(x)|\leq\int_{B_{R}(0)}|\varphi(y)|\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\leq c|B_{R}(0)|\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\widehat{\nu}(x).

Whereas, if
|x|\leq 4R
the proof of (
2.6
) is completed by applying (
2.4
) as follows: we have
\widehat{\nu}(x)\geq c_{1}
for an appropriate constant
c_{1}>0
, depending on
R
and
\nu
since
\frac{1}{2}(1+|x|)\leq 4R
.

\displaystyle|L\varphi(x)|\leq 4\Theta\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq c_{1}^{-1}4\Theta\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\widehat{\nu}(x)

with
\Theta=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h
. Finally, (
2.6
) yields

\displaystyle|(u,L\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u(x)|\widehat{\nu}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

This spontaneously shows that
Lu
is a distribution when
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
and that
L:L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\R^{d})
is continuous, i.e.
Lu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}Lu
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\R^{d})
if
u_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}u
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\widehat{\nu})
. Indeed the above estimate implies

\displaystyle|\langle Lu_{n}-Lu,\varphi\rangle|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|\widehat{\nu}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\quad\text{for all}\,\,\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\R^{d}).

∎

In light of Proposition
2.10
, we are forced to formulate the following definition.

Definition 2.11
.

A function
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
will be called to be weakly integrodifferentiable (or integrodifferentiable in the sense of distributions) with respect to
\nu
on an open set
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
(eventually
\Omega=\R^{d}
) if there exists a function
g\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
such that for every compact set
K\subset\Omega
and for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\supp\varphi\subset K
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}g(x)\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{K}u(x)L\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

If
\nu
is well understood, we briefly say that
Lu
is the weak integrodifferential of
u
or that
Lu
is the integrodifferential of
u
in the sense of distributions. In fact, as it can be easily shown that
g
is unique up to a set of measure zero, we shall merely write
g=Lu
a.e on
\Omega
.

Remark 2.12
.

Of course, if for some locally integrable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, the expression

Lu
exists almost everywhere and belongs to
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, then
u
is weakly integrodifferentiable and its weak integrodifferential coincides with
Lu
.

Let us collect some simple facts involving the nonlocal operator
L
under consideration.

Proposition 2.13
.

The following assertions are true:

1.

The operator
L
commutes with translations. More generally,
L
commutes with rigid motions
4
4

4

A rigid motion is any transformation that can be obtained as a finite composition of translations and rotations.
if in addition
\nu
is radial. To be more precise, if
Lu(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\tau:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a rigid motion then
[Lu]\circ\tau(x)=L[u\circ\tau](x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2.

For any multiindex
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
we have
\partial^{\alpha}(Lu)=L\partial^{\alpha}u
provided that
u
and
Lu
are sufficiently smooth.

3.

The convolution rule
L(u*\varphi)=Lu*\varphi
holds for all
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

4.

Assume
Lu
is well defined in a distributional sense, then for all
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the equality
L(u*\varphi)=u*L\varphi
holds as well in the distributional sense.

5.

Given two functions
u,v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, the relation
L[uv]=u[Lv]+[Lu]v-\Gamma(u,v)
holds provided that
L[uv],

Lu
and
Lv
exist. Here
\Gamma(u,v)
is the so called carré du champs operator associated with
L
and is defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma(u,v)(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\,\mathrm{d}y.

6.

Let
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, such that for each
j\geq 1
,
\varphi_{j}=1
on
B_{j}(0)
. Assume
u\in H_{\nu}(\R^{d})
, such that
Lu\in L^{1}2(\Omega)
, then we have
\|L[\varphi_{j}u]-Lu\|_{L^{2}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0
.

Proof.
.

(i)
is a routine verification.
(ii)
follows by iterating the procedure from Proposition
2.2
.
(iv)
follows from
(iii)
.
To prove
(iii)
, observe that from the estimate (
2.4
), for all
x,h,z\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\big|u(z)\big(\varphi(x-z+h)+\varphi(x-z-h)-2\varphi(x-z)\big)\big|\nu(h)\leq C|u(z)|(1\land|h|^{2})\nu(h)\in L^{1}(\R^{d}\times\R^{d}).

Thus, by applying Fubini’s theorem, one easily arrives at
L(u*\varphi)=Lu*\varphi
.

(vi)
is a consequence of
(v)
whereas
(v)
follows by integrating the identity

u(x)v(x)-u(y)v(y)=u(x)(v(x)-v(y))-(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))+v(x)(u(x)-u(y)).

∎

2
Case of the fractional Laplacian

In this section we focus on the most studied integrodifferential operator which is of course interesting in its own right. Before we define this object formally, let us observe that for
s\in\R
the function
h\mapsto|h|^{-d-2s}
is Lévy integrable if and only if
0<s<1
. Concretely,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})|h|^{-d-2s}\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\quad\text{if and only if}\quad 0<s<1.

A prototypical example of a symmetric Lévy operator is obtained by putting,
\nu(h)=C_{d,s}|h|^{-d-2s}
with
s\in(0,1)
. The resulting Lévy operator is the so called fractional Laplace operator and is denoted by
(-\Delta)^{s}
or
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
with
\alpha=2s
. The "
s
" notation "with a fractional tendency" usually suites to PDEs community whereas the probability community uses the "
\alpha
" notation as its remind the
\alpha
-stable processes.
Here, and in what follows depending on the context, we may simultaneously use the notation
\alpha=2s
. The constant
C_{d,s}\equiv C_{d,\alpha}
is chosen so that the Fourier relation
\widehat{(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u}(\xi)=|\xi|^{\alpha}\widehat{u}(\xi)
holds for all
u
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. It follows from (
2.5
) that the Fourier symbol associated with
\nu(h)=C_{d,s}|h|^{-d-2s}
is given by

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)=C_{d,s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\cos{(x_{1}})|x/|\xi||^{-d-2s}\frac{\,\,\mathrm{d}x}{|\xi|^{-d}}=C_{d,s}|\xi|^{-2s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1-\cos(x_{1})}{|x|^{d+2s}}\,dx.

By identification it follows that

\displaystyle C_{d,\alpha}\equiv C_{d,s}:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1-\cos(x_{1})}{|x|^{d+2s}}\,dx\right)^{-1}.

Note that within the Fourier symbol
\psi(\xi)
we have already shown (see characterization D.5) that
C_{d,s}
is well defined. Later, we use a simple approach to compute this constant and study its asymptotic(c.f Section
3
). For the moment, it is important to keep in mind that

(2.7)

\displaystyle C_{d,\alpha}=\frac{2^{\alpha}\Gamma\big(\frac{d+\alpha}{2}\big)}{\pi^{d/2}\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}=\frac{2^{2s}\Gamma\big(s+\frac{d}{2}\big)}{\pi^{d/2}\big|\Gamma\big(-s\big)\big|}.

Patently from this Fourier characterization of
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
, we can already glimpse that for
u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d})
,
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}-\Delta u(x)
and
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 0}u(x)
. Here, we are reminded that
\xi\mapsto|\xi|^{2}
corresponds to the Fourier multiplier of
-\Delta
.
This type of convergence extends to a more general context for continuous bounded functions that are
C^{2}
in the vicinity of
x
(see Section
5
). It is worth emphasizing that the conclusions from Section
1
apply to the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
. In particular with
\alpha=2s\in(0,2)
, from D.3 we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)&:=C_{d,\alpha}\,\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{(u(x)-u(y))}{|x-y|^{d+\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&=-\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}((u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}h}{|h|^{d+\alpha}}.\end{split}

The fractional operator
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
naturally appears as the generator of the rotationally symmetric
\alpha
-stable Lévy processes see
[
Sat13
]
. More extensive works on the fractional Laplace operator can be found in
[
Aba15
,
Buc16
,
Sil05
,
Sti19
]
. Let us proceed with some further representations of the fractional Laplace operator. Most of these representations are inspired by
[
Kwa17
]
.

D.9: Inverse of Riesz’s potential
The fractional Laplacian can be realized as the inverse of the Riesz potential. The Riesz potential of order
\alpha\in(0,d)
which we denote as
I_{\alpha}
is a pseudo-differential operator whose Fourier multiplier is
\xi\mapsto|\xi|^{-\alpha}
, i.e. for all
u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d})
we have
\widehat{I_{\alpha}u}(\xi)=|\xi|^{-\alpha}\widehat{u}(\xi)
. It is easy to show that (see Remark
2.22
) for all
u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle I_{\alpha}u(x)=\frac{1}{\gamma_{d,\alpha}}\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{u(y)}{|x-y|^{d-\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\text{($x\in\R^{d}$)},

where the constant
\gamma_{d,\alpha}
(see Remark
2.22
for the computation) is given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{d,a}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{e^{-ix_{1}}}{|x|^{d-a}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-a}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-a}{2}\right)}.

Using the Fourier characterization of
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
and
I_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(0,2)
we get
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}\circ I_{\alpha}u=u
and
I_{\alpha}\circ(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=u
. That is,
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}=I_{\alpha}^{-1}
(the inverse of the operator
I_{\alpha}
).

D.10: Dynkin’s definition
For all
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
and for
x\in\R^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)

\displaystyle=C_{d,\alpha}\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}(0)}\frac{(u(x)-u(x+h))}{|h|^{2}(|h|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})^{\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}h.

The Dynkin definition of the fractional Laplacian is useful for studying (see
[
Aba15
]
)
\alpha
-harmonic functions, i.e. functions solving the equation
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=0
on
\R^{d}
. Let now us establish it. Fix,
0<\varepsilon<1
. From the relation (
2.4
), the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))}{|h|^{2}(|h|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})^{\alpha/2}}\mathds{1}_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}(0)}(h)\Big|\leq 4\|u\|_{C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})}\frac{(1\land|h|^{2})}{|h|^{2}\big||h|^{2}-1\big|^{\alpha/2}}.

It is possible to show that the dominating function on the right side is integrable on
\R^{d}
. Thus the claim follows from the dominated convergence theorem, as we find from (
2.8
) that

\displaystyle C_{d,\alpha}\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}(0)}\frac{(u(x)-u(x+h))}{|h|^{2}(|h|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})^{\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\varepsilon}(0)}\frac{(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))}{|h|^{2}(|h|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2})^{\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}\,\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))}{|h|^{d+\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}h=(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x).

D.11: Bochner’s formula
The claim here is that for
\alpha\in(0,2)
we have the identity

B_{\alpha}u=(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u\qquad\text{for all $u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d}).$}

Here
B_{\alpha}
is accomplished through the following Bochner’s integral formula

\displaystyle B_{\alpha}u(x):=\frac{1}{\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(e^{-t\Delta}u(x)-u(x))}{t^{1+\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

For a rigorous proof of this identity (see for example
[
ST10
]
) one would need some advance knowledge on Bochner integrals and functional calculus for unbounded operators. Here we provide some intuitive approaches for guessing the Bochner identity for
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
. For
\lambda>0
, let us apply the integration by part to following the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{-t\lambda}-1)t^{-\alpha/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Big[-\frac{2}{\alpha}(e^{-t\lambda}-1)t^{-\alpha/2}\Big]_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}-\frac{2\lambda}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t\lambda}t^{-\alpha/2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{-\alpha/2}\frac{2}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-s}s^{-\alpha/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}s=-\lambda^{-\alpha/2}\frac{2}{\alpha}\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\big).

Since
\alpha\in(0,2)
, by using the duplication formula for the Gamma function (see the formula (
2.41
) ), we can write,

\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\big)=-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\big)=\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\big|\Gamma\big(-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|.
Finally we have

(2.9)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\alpha/2}u(x)=\frac{1}{\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(e^{-t\lambda}-1)}{t^{1+\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

By roughly substituting
\lambda
with
\Delta
and
1
with the identity operator, this gives

(2.10)

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=\frac{1}{\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(e^{-t\Delta}u-u)}{t^{1+\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

A more serious approach would be to consider the Fourier multiplier. By knowing that,
\xi\mapsto|\xi|^{2}
is the Fourier multiplier of
-\Delta
it is not difficult to show that
\xi\mapsto e^{-t|\xi|^{2}}
is the Fourier multiplier of
e^{-t\Delta}
. Therefore, using the formula (
2.9
) we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{B_{\alpha}u}(\xi)=\frac{\widehat{u}(\xi)}{\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(e^{-t|\xi|^{2}}-1)}{t^{1+\alpha/2}}\,\mathrm{d}t=|\xi|^{\alpha}\widehat{u}(\xi)=\widehat{(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u}(\xi),\quad\text{for $u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d})$ and $\xi\in\R^{d}$}.

The uniqueness of the Fourier transform, implies
B_{\alpha}u=(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u
, i.e. the identity (
2.10
) holds true.

D.12: Balakrishnan’s formula
The idea here is the same as previously: fix
\lambda>0
, then using the Schwartz reflexion formula (
2.40
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda t^{\alpha/2-1}}{t+\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}t=\lambda^{\alpha/2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{-\alpha/2}t^{\alpha/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}t=\lambda^{\alpha/2}B\big(\tfrac{\alpha}{2},1-\tfrac{\alpha}{2}\big)=\frac{\lambda^{\alpha/2}\pi}{\sin\big(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\big)}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{\alpha/2}=\frac{\sin\big(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\big)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda t^{\alpha/2-1}}{t+\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Substituting
\lambda
by
-\Delta
yields the following Balakrishnan formula with
u\in\mathcal{S}(\R^{d})

(2.11)

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=\frac{\sin\big(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\big)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}-\Delta(tI-\Delta)^{-1}u\,\,t^{\alpha/2-1}\,\mathrm{d}t

It is worth noting that one is able to establish this formula using the Fourier multiplier as in D.11

D.13: From long jump random walk
The aim here (see
[
Val09
]
) is to show that the fractional Laplacian can be approximated by the generator of random walks. Define
K:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
with
K(h)=\theta_{d,\alpha}|h|^{-d-\alpha}
for
h\neq 0
and
K(0)=0
, where the constant
\theta_{d,\alpha}
is chosen so that

\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}K(k)=1.

For
\delta>0
, assume there is a random particle on the lattice
\delta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with the following properties.

•

At any unit of time
\tau
the particle jumps from any point of
\delta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to any other point.

•

The probability that the particle jumps from
x\in\delta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to
x+\delta k\in\delta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is given by
\theta_{d,\alpha}|k|^{-d-\alpha}
. This includes the fact that the particle can also experience a long jump with a small probability.

•

Let
u(x,t)
be the probability that the particle sites at the point
x\in\delta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
at the time
t\in\tau\mathbb{N}
.

Patently,
u(x,t+\tau)
(the probability that the particle sites at point
x
, at time
t+\tau
) corresponds to the probability that the particle sites at any other point
y=x+\delta k\in\delta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
at time
t
weighted with the jump rate
\theta_{d,\alpha}|k|^{-d-\alpha}
. Together with the fact that
\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\theta_{d,\alpha}|h|^{-d-\alpha}=1
, this yields

(2.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}u(x,t+\tau)-u(x,t)&=\theta_{d,\alpha}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|k|^{-d-\alpha}u(x+\delta k,t)-u(x,t)\\
&=\theta_{d,\alpha}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|k|^{-d-\alpha}\big(u(x+\delta k,t)-u(x,t)\big).\end{split}

Assume the unit of time is
\tau=\delta^{\alpha}
then
\frac{|k|^{-d-\alpha}}{\tau}=\delta^{d}|\delta k|^{-d-\alpha}
. Combining this with(
2.12
) and the Riemann sum of
h\mapsto(u(x+h,t)-u(x,t))|h|^{-d-\alpha}
over
\R^{d}
yields the following

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{u(x,t+\tau)-u(x,t)}{\tau}

(2.13)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{d}\theta_{d,\alpha}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\delta k|^{-d-\alpha}\big(u(x+\delta k,t)-u(x,t)\big)

(2.14)

\displaystyle=\theta_{d,\alpha}\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{(u(x+h,t)-u(x,t))}{|h|^{d+\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=-\theta_{d,\alpha}C^{-1}_{d,\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x,t).

One should observe that the expression in (
2.13
) is the Riemann sum of (
2.14
). We have shown that
\partial_{t}u(x,t)=-\theta_{d,\alpha}C^{-1}_{d,\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x,t)
. In other words, the generators of our random particle is
-(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
up to a positive constant.

D.14: Caffarelli-Silvestre extension

The Caffarelli-Silvestre extension is probably the most skillful way to derive the fractional Laplacian. Indeed, Caffarelli and Silvestre showed in
[
CS07
]
that the fractional Laplacian can be determined as an operator that maps a Dirichlet boundary condition to a Neumann-type condition (Dirichlet-to-Neumann correspondence) via a PDE-extension problem. Concretely, for
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
and
\alpha\in(0,2)
, assume that a function
U:\R^{d}\times[0,\infty)\to\R
satisfies the extension problem

(2.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla U)=0\quad\text{ in $\R^{d}\times[0,\infty)$}\quad\text{and}\quad U(\cdot,0)=u\quad\text{on $\,\R^{d}$}.\end{split}

Then, for a constant
c_{\alpha}
to be specified later, we have

(2.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\lim_{t\to 0}t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}U(x,t)=-\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U(x,t)-U(x,0)}{t^{\alpha}}.\end{split}

First, a routine check shows that we have
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)=t^{1-\alpha}\big(\Delta_{x}+\frac{1-\alpha}{t}\partial_{t}+\partial^{2}_{tt}\big)
, where
\Delta_{x}
is the
d
-dimensional Laplacian with respect to the
x
variable. We shall now introduce the Green function and the
Poisson kernel associated with the operator
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)
which will be indispensable for establishing the relations in (
2.16
).

Definition 2.14

(
[
CS07
]
)
.

The fundamental solution associated with the operator
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)
is the radial function
\Phi_{d,\alpha}:(\R^{d}\times\R)\setminus\{0\}\to(0,\infty)
defined by

(2.17)

\displaystyle\Phi_{d,\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\text{ if $d+2-\alpha=2$\,, i.e. $(d=1,\alpha=1)$},

\displaystyle\Phi_{d,\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle:=-\omega^{-1}_{d,\alpha}\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{-\frac{d-\alpha}{2}}\,\,

\displaystyle\text{ if $d+2-\alpha>2$}.

Assume that
d+2+\alpha>2
. The Poisson kernel associated with the operator
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)
is the function
P_{d,\alpha}:\R^{d}\times(0,\infty)\setminus\to(0,\infty)
defined by

(2.18)

\displaystyle P_{d,\alpha}(x,t):=-t^{\alpha-1}\partial_{t}\Phi_{d,2-\alpha}(x,t)=\frac{t^{\alpha}(d-2+\alpha)}{\omega_{d,2-\alpha}\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}}.

Note that the constant
\omega_{d,\alpha}
is determined so that for every
t>0
the function
P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t):\R^{d}\to(0,\infty)
with
x\mapsto P_{d,\alpha}(x,t)
has a unit mass, i.e. it satisfies the following integral condition

(2.19)

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}P_{d,\alpha}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x=1.

Proposition 2.15
.

Assume that
d+2-\alpha>2
then we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{d,\alpha}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{d,\alpha}}\frac{1}{\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{d-\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{with}\quad\omega_{d,\alpha}=\frac{1}{(d-\alpha)}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+2-\alpha}{2}\big)}{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\big)},

\displaystyle P_{d,\alpha}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{d,\alpha}t^{\alpha}}{\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{with}\quad V_{d,\alpha}=\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+\alpha}{2}\big)}{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)}.

Proof.
.

It is sufficient to compute
\omega_{d,2-\alpha}
. The relation (
2.19
) implies that

\displaystyle 1=\omega^{-1}_{d,2-\alpha}(d+2-\alpha)\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{t^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x}{\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle\overset{x=tz}{=}\omega^{-1}_{d,2-\alpha}(d-2+\alpha)\int_{\R^{d}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}z}{\big(1+|z|^{2}\big)^{\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\omega^{-1}_{d,2-\alpha}(d-2+\alpha)A(d+1,\frac{\alpha-1}{2}).

Here,
A(d+1,\frac{\alpha-1}{2})
is given by the expressions (
2.23
) and (
2.25
) below from which the claim follows since,

\displaystyle\omega_{d,2-\alpha}=\frac{1}{(d-2+\alpha)A(d+1,\frac{\alpha-1}{2})}=\frac{1}{(d-2+\alpha)}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+\alpha}{2}\big)}{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)}.

∎

Remark 2.16
.

For the particular case
\alpha=1
,
\Phi_{d,1}
and
P_{d,1}
are the Green kernel and the Poisson kernel of the Laplacian in the
d+1
dimension space, respectively. Moreover, if
\omega_{d}
denotes the area of the
d+1
-dimensional unit sphere of
\R^{d+1}
then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega_{d,1}}=\frac{1}{(d-1)\omega_{d}}\quad\text{with}\quad\omega_{d}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d+1}{2}\big)}.

We omit the computational details of the next proposition. Recall,
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)=t^{1-\alpha}\big(\Delta_{x}+\frac{1-\alpha}{t}\partial_{t}+\partial^{2}_{tt}\big)
.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
such that
d+2-\alpha>2
. The following assertions are true.

1.

For each
t>0
,
P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)
is radial positive, satisfies
P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)=t^{-d}P_{d,\alpha}(\frac{x}{t},1)
and
\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\hskip-4.30554ptP_{d,\alpha}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
.

2.

The family
(P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t))_{t}
weakly converges to the Dirac mass
\delta_{0}
as
t\to 0
. To be more precise,

\displaystyle\varphi(0)=\lim_{t\to 0}\int_{\R^{d}}P_{d,\alpha}(x,t)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{ for all $\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})$}.

3.

P_{d,\alpha}
satisfies the equation
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)P_{d,\alpha}=0
in
\R^{d}\times(0,\infty)
.

4.

\Phi_{d,\alpha}
satisfies the equation
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)\Phi_{d,\alpha}=\delta_{0}
in
\R^{d+1}
.

We can now derive the fractional Laplace operator from the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map associated with the extension problem (
2.15
).

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})
and
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Define the convolution
U(x,t)=[P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)*u](x)
then the following assertions are true.

1.

U
solves the equation (
2.16
) in the sense that we have
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)U(x,t)=0
for every
x\in\R^{d}
and every
t>0
and
for every
x\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle U(x,0)=\lim_{t\to 0}[P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)*u](x)=u(x).

2.

Consider the constant
c_{\alpha}=\frac{V_{d,\alpha}}{C_{d,\alpha}}=\frac{\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}{2^{\alpha}\Gamma\big(\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)}
then for every
x\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle-\lim_{t\to 0}t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}U(x,t)=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U(x,t)-U(x,0)}{t^{\alpha}}=c_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x).

3.

We define
U^{*}(x,t)=U(x,\alpha t^{1/\alpha})
, for
x\in\R^{d}
and
t>0
. Let
\beta=-\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}
. Then
U^{*}
verifies

(2.20)

\displaystyle\big(\Delta_{x}+t^{\beta}\partial^{2}_{tt}\big)U^{*}=0\quad\text{ in \, $\R^{d}\times[0,\infty)$}\quad\text{and}\quad U^{*}(\cdot,0)=u\quad\text{on $\,\R^{d}$}.

Moreover, for all
x\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}U^{*}(x,0)=-\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U^{*}(x,t)-U^{*}(x,0)}{t}=\alpha^{\alpha}c_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x).

Proof.
.

The differentiation rule under the integral sign and Proposition
2.17

(iv)
imply that

\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)U(x,t)=[\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)]*u(x)=0\quad\text{for all $x\in\R^{d}$ and all $t>0$}.

Next, for
x\in\R^{d}
and
0<t<1
, using the condition (
2.19
), we have

\displaystyle U(x,t)-u(x)=

\displaystyle[P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)*u](x)-u(x)=\int_{\R^{d}}(v(x-h)-v(x))P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{d,\alpha}}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\frac{t^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}h}{\big(|h|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}}.

In view of the estimate (
2.4
), we find that for every
h\in\R^{d}
,

\displaystyle\Big|(u(x+h)+v(x-h)-2u(x))\frac{t^{\alpha}}{\big(|h|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}}\Big|\leq 4\|u\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|^{2})|h|^{-d-\alpha}.

The dominant
h\mapsto(1\land|h|^{2})|h|^{-d-\alpha}
is integrable over
\R^{d}
. For each
h\in\R^{d}
we have
P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\xrightarrow{t\to 0}0
and
t^{-\alpha}P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\xrightarrow{t\to 0}|h|^{-d-\alpha}
. The convergence dominated theorem yields

U(x,0)=\lim\limits_{t\to 0}[P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)*u](x)=u(x)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U(x,t)-U(x,0)}{t^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{d,\alpha}}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}h}{|h|^{d+\alpha}}=-c_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x).

Here
c_{\alpha}=\frac{V_{d,\alpha}}{C_{d,\alpha}}
is deduced from the expressions of
V_{d,\alpha}
and
C_{d,\alpha}
(see(
2.7
)). Furthermore, since

t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)=\alpha V_{d,\alpha}\big(|h|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{-\frac{d+\alpha}{2}}-t^{2}V_{d,\alpha}(d+\alpha)\big(|h|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{-\frac{d+\alpha+2}{2}},

for
0<t<1
, we also have the estimate

\big|(u(x+h)+v(x-h)-2u(x))t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\big|\leq 8\|u\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\R^{d})}(d+\alpha)V_{d,\alpha}(1\land|h|^{2})|h|^{-d-\alpha}.

and
t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\xrightarrow{t\to 0}\alpha|h|^{-d-\alpha}
, for each
h\in\R^{d}
. Therefore, by using the differentiation rule under the integral sign and the convergence dominated theorem one finds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha}\lim_{t\to 0}t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}U(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\lim_{t\to 0}t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}\big(U(x,t)-u(x)\big)=\frac{1}{\alpha}\lim_{t\to 0}[t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}P_{d,\alpha}(\cdot,t)*(u-u(x))](x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{V_{d,\alpha}}{2\alpha}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\,t^{1-\alpha}\partial_{t}P_{d,\alpha}(h,t)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=-c_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x).

We have shown
(i)
and
(ii)
, while
(iii)
is not yet proven. Clearly, for
x\in\R^{d}
we have
U^{*}(x,0)=U(x,0)=u(x)
. Furthermore, for
t>0
, we have
\Delta_{x}U^{*}(x,t)=\Delta_{x}U(x,\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}})
and

\displaystyle t^{\beta}\partial^{2}_{tt}U^{*}(x,t)=t^{\beta}\big(\partial_{t}(t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}\partial_{t}U(x,\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}))\big)=\partial^{2}_{tt}U(x,\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}})+\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\partial_{t}U(x,\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}).

Recall that
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-\alpha}\nabla)=t^{1-\alpha}\big(\Delta_{x}+\frac{1-\alpha}{t}\partial_{t}+\partial^{2}_{tt}\big)
. Therefore, since
U
solves (
2.15
), letting
z=\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}
, we get

\displaystyle\big(\Delta_{x}+t^{\beta}\partial^{2}_{tt}\big)U^{*}(x,t)=\big(\Delta_{x}U(x,z)+\frac{1-\alpha}{z}\partial_{t}U(x,z)+\partial^{2}_{tt}U(x,z\big)=0.

Moreover, it follows from
(ii)
that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U^{*}(x,t)-U^{*}(x,0)}{t}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U(x,\alpha t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}})-U(x,0)}{t}=\alpha^{\alpha}\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{U(x,z)-U(x,0)}{z^{\alpha}}=-\alpha^{\alpha}c_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x).

∎

3
Renormalization constant of the fractional Laplacian

Here, we provide a mere alternative method to compute the exact value of the constant
C_{d,s}
with
0<s<1
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
given by

\displaystyle C_{d,s}:=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1-\cos(x_{1})}{|x|^{d+2s}}\,dx\Big)^{-1}.

Afterwards we provide its asymptotic behavior when
s\to 1
and
s\to 0
.
Although the constant
C_{d,s}
has already been computed, we believe that our approach is simpler and uses
basic elementary calculus tools. To the best of our knowledge the computation of a similar constant first appeared in
[
Lan72
, formula
(1.1.2)
]
while studying the Riesz potential.

This constant is nicely deduced in
[
BV16
, Chapter 1]
through Fourier analysis techniques while establishing a relationship between: (i) the function
U(x,t)
solution of the heat equation:
\partial_{t}U(x,t)=\Delta U(x,t)
for all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,+\infty)
with initial data
U(x,0)=u(x)
for some
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, (ii) its natural semigroup and (iii) the fractional power of the associated infinitesimal generator which is nothing but the fractional Laplace operator (see D.11 the Bochner definition for
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
above ). Although this ingenuous idea requires some further understanding
of the related subjects, the identification process therein does not provide a direct computation of the integral quantity defining the constant
C_{d,s}
.

The art work in
[
Buc16
]
consists of first showing that the function
u(x)=[\max(1-|x|^{2},0)]^{s}
is the solution to the Poisson equation
(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=\theta_{d,s}
in
B_{1}(0)
and
u(x)=0
on
\R^{d}\setminus B_{1}(0)
. Here,
\theta_{d,s}
is a specific constant from
[
Buc16
]
. Afterwards the Green function
G_{s}(x,y)
for the fractional Laplacian in the unit ball is exploited to deduce the value
C_{d,s}
since one has
1=u(0)=\theta_{d,s}\int_{B_{1}}G_{s}(0,y)\,\mathrm{d}y
.

In contrast, Mathieu Felsinger
[
Fel13
]
opts for a direct computational method using polar coordinates. This method brings into play the notion of Bessel functions of the first order
k\in\mathbb{R}
the understanding of which requires some advanced knowledge of integral calculus and the concept of analytic functions. The computational details therein refer to several advanced and pre-established formulae for Bessel functions.

Instead of these two appointed techniques, we rather propose a straightforward computation using basic elementary methods of integral calculus. Although the explicit value of
C_{d,s}
is not of particular interest to
us and only plays a minor role in our work, having it at hand significantly simplified the study of its asymptotic behavior. Curious readers may look up
[
NPV12
, Section 4]
for a lengthy elementary study of the asymptotic behavior of
C_{d,s}
.

To this scope, we start writing
x=(x_{1},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
assuming
d\geq 2
. Thus, performing the change of variables
x^{\prime}=z|x_{1}|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
that is
dx^{\prime}=|x_{1}|^{d-1}dz
yields

(2.21)

\displaystyle C_{d,s}^{-1}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1-\cos(t)}{|t|^{1+2s}}\,dt\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{d+2s}{2}}}\,dx\right):=\frac{G(s)A(d,s)}{s(1-s)}.

With

(2.22)

\displaystyle G(s)

\displaystyle:=s(1-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1-\cos(t)}{|t|^{1+2s}}\,dt

and if
\omega_{d-2}
denotes the surface measure of the
d-2
-dimensional unit sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}

(2.23)

\displaystyle A(d,s)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{d+2s}{2}}}\,dx=\omega_{d-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{d-2}}{(1+r^{2})^{\frac{d+2s}{2}}}\,dr.

Note that from the expression of
C(1,s)
one can postulate the convention that
A(1,s)\equiv 1
for all
s\in(0,1)
. Let us recall the well known formula
5
5

5

This can be obtained by computing the Gaussian integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-|x|^{2}}dx
in two different
fashions: first using polar coordinates and second using Fubini ’s
theorem observing that
|x|^{2}=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{d}^{2}
.

\omega_{d-1}:=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}
where
\Gamma
is the Euler’s Gamma function mapping
\alpha>0
to

\displaystyle\Gamma(\alpha):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{\alpha-1}\,dt.

To compute the constant
A(d,s)
we consider the following general integral

\displaystyle I(a,b):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{a-1}}{(1+r^{2})^{\frac{b}{2}}}\,dr~~~0\leq a<b.

The change of variables
u=\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}
that is
r=\left(\frac{1}{u}-1\right)^{1/2}
and
dr=-\frac{1}{2u^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{u}-1\right)^{-1/2}du
yields,

\displaystyle I(a,b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-u\right)^{\frac{a}{2}-1}u^{\frac{b}{2}-\frac{a}{2}-1}\,du=\frac{1}{2}B\left(\frac{a}{2},\frac{b}{2}-\frac{a}{2}\right)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{a}{2})\Gamma(\frac{b}{2}-\frac{a}{2})}{2\Gamma(\frac{b}{2})}.

Here
B(\cdot,\cdot)
is the beta function defined for
x,y>0
and given by the Legendre’s duplication formula by

(2.24)

\displaystyle B(x,y):=\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-u\right)^{x-1}u^{y-1}\,du=\frac{\Gamma(x)\Gamma(y)}{\Gamma(x+y)}.

Then, taking into account that
\omega_{d-2}=\frac{2\pi^{(d-1)/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d-1}{2})}
then from the expression of
I(a,b)
and (
2.23
), we get

(2.25)

\displaystyle A(d,s)

\displaystyle=\omega_{d-2}I(d-1,d+2s)=\pi^{(d-1)/2}\frac{\Gamma(s+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(s+\frac{d}{2})}

On the other hand, by applying integration by parts, to the expression of
G(s)
we get,

(2.26)

\displaystyle G(s)

\displaystyle=2s(1-s)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\cos(t)}{t^{1+2s}}\,dt=(1-s)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(t)}{t^{2s}}\,dt.

Our main interest is to compute the last integral term appearing in the expression of
G(s)
. To do this, we need to bring into play the integrals defined for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
by

(2.27)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt\qquad\hbox{and }\qquad\varphi_{2}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt.

A routine check reveals that
\varphi_{1}(\alpha)
exists if and only if
0<\alpha<2
whereas,
\varphi_{2}(\alpha)
exists if and only if
0<\alpha<1
. Moreover a more precise investigation results in the below table

summing over
(0,\infty)

Riemann integrable

Lebesgue integrable

\frac{\sin t}{t^{\alpha}}

0<\alpha<2

1<\alpha<2

\frac{\cos t}{t^{\alpha}}

0<\alpha<1

no value

Details of this are left to the interested reader. Integrals in (
2.27
) are somehow linked to the so called general Fresnel’s
6
6

6

For
\alpha=2
integrals in (
2.28
) are commonly known as Fresnel’s integrals.
integrals which are defined in their general forms for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
as follows:

(2.28)

\displaystyle f_{1}(\alpha):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t^{\alpha})\,dt\qquad\hbox{and }\qquad f_{2}(\alpha):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t^{\alpha})\,dt.

In fact, if we assume
\alpha\neq 1
(observe that
f_{i}(1)=\varphi_{i}(0)
does not exists), then enforcing the change of variables
x=t^{\alpha}
, i.e
dt=\frac{1}{\alpha}x^{\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}}dx
we are led to the following relationships

(2.29)

\displaystyle f_{i}(\alpha)=\frac{\text{sign}(\alpha)}{\alpha}\varphi_{i}\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\right)\qquad\text{equivalently,}\qquad\varphi_{i}(\alpha)=\alpha\text{sign}(\alpha)f_{i}\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right).

In contrast to the functions under the integrals in (
2.27
) the functions
t\mapsto\cos(t^{\alpha})
and
t\mapsto\sin(t^{\alpha})
have the advantage of being analytical on the half complex plan
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Re}(z)\geq 0\}
for
\alpha>1
. It can be deduced from the existence conditions on
\varphi_{i}
and the above relationships that
f_{1}(\alpha)
exists only for
|\alpha|>1
and
f_{2}(\alpha)
exists only for
\alpha>1
.
Over all, the computation of
\varphi_{i}(\alpha)
will be carried out by computing
f_{i}(\alpha)
while the
computation of this latter springs from Cauchy’s theorem.

Theorem 2.19

(Cauchy Theorem)
.

Every analytic function
on an open connected set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
has a null integral over any closed oriented piecewise smooth simple curve supported in
\Omega
.

Proposition 2.20
.

The values of
\varphi_{1}(\alpha)
and
\varphi_{2}(\alpha)
are respectively given by

(2.30)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\cos\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right)~~\alpha\in(0,2)

and

(2.31)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\sin\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right)~~\alpha\in(0,1).

Immediately, one gets

(2.32)

\displaystyle G(s)=(1-s)\frac{\Gamma(2-2s)}{1-2s}\cos\left(s\pi\right).

Proof.
.

First we assume that
0<\alpha<1
, i.e.
\beta=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}>1
. Next, we introduce the function
z\mapsto e^{\mathrm{i}z^{\beta}}
which is analytic on
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Re}(z)\geq 0\}
.
Let us also introduce the close counter-clockwise directed curve denoted by
\Gamma_{R}
and given by

\Gamma_{R}=[O,A]\cup\gamma_{R}\cup[B,O]
where
R>1
is an arbitrarily positive large enough,
\gamma_{R}=\{Re^{\mathrm{i}t}:t\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2\beta}]\}
is the arc of radius
R
angle
\frac{\pi}{2\beta}
,
[B,O]=\{xe^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2\beta}},x\in[0,R]\}
is the inclined segment of angle
\frac{\pi}{2\beta}
with length
R
and
[O,A]=[0,R]
is the segment on the real line of length
R
(see the figure
1
).

O
A
\gamma_{R}
B
R
R
\frac{\pi}{2\beta}

Figure 1:
Curve
\Gamma_{R}=[O,A]\cup\gamma_{R}\cup[B,O]

In virtue of Cauchy’s theorem, one has

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\Gamma_{R}}f(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{[O,A]}f(z)\,dz+\int_{\gamma_{R}}f(z)\,dz+\int_{[B,O]}f(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{R}f(x)\,dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2\beta}}f(Re^{\mathrm{i}t})\,d(Re^{\mathrm{i}t})+\int_{R}^{0}f(xe^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2\beta}})\,d(xe^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2\beta}})

(2.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{R}e^{\mathrm{i}x^{\beta}}\,dx+\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2\beta}}e^{-R^{\beta}\sin\beta t}e^{\mathrm{i}R^{\beta}\cos\beta t}Re^{\mathrm{i}t}\,dt-e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2\beta}}\int_{0}^{R}e^{-x^{\beta}}\,dx.

Applying a simple change of variables along with the inequality
\sin t\geq\frac{2}{\pi}t
for every
t\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
, we have the following

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2\beta}}e^{-R^{\beta}\sin\beta t}e^{\mathrm{i}R^{\beta}\cos\beta t}Re^{\mathrm{i}t}\,dt\right|\leq\frac{R}{\beta}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e^{-R^{\beta}\sin t}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R}{\beta}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e^{-R^{\beta}\frac{2t}{\pi}}\,dt=\frac{\pi}{2\beta R^{\beta-1}}\left(1-e^{-R^{\beta}}\right)\xrightarrow{R\to\infty}0.

Hence for
\beta>1
the integral of
f(z)
over
\gamma_{R}
vanishes as
R
goes to infinity. So that by letting
R\to\infty
in (
2.33
) after identification, we simultaneously get the formulae

\displaystyle f_{1}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(t^{\beta})\,dt=\sin\frac{\pi}{2\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x^{\beta}}\,dx\overset{t=x^{\beta}}{=}\frac{1}{\beta}\sin\frac{\pi}{2\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{\frac{1}{\beta}-1}\,dt=\frac{1}{\beta}\sin\frac{\pi}{2\beta}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(t^{\beta})\,dt=\cos\frac{\pi}{2\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x^{\beta}}\,dx\overset{t=x^{\beta}}{=}\frac{1}{\beta}\cos\frac{\pi}{2\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{\frac{1}{\beta}-1}=\frac{1}{\beta}\cos\frac{\pi}{2\beta}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right).

This, together with the relation (
2.29
) gives the following for
0<\alpha<1,

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha f_{1}\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)=\Gamma(1-\alpha)\sin(1-\alpha)\frac{\pi}{2}

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha f_{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)=\Gamma(1-\alpha)\cos(1-\alpha)\frac{\pi}{2}.

For
\varphi_{1}(\alpha),
the case
1<\alpha<2
(i.e.
0<\alpha-1<1
) obviously springs from the previous case and the integration by part as follows:

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\underbrace{\left[\frac{\sin t}{t^{\alpha-1}}\right]_{0}^{\infty}}_{0}+\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos t}{t^{\alpha-1}}=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\varphi_{2}(\alpha-1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\cos(2-\alpha)\frac{\pi}{2}=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\sin(1-\alpha)\frac{\pi}{2}.

Finally, from the relation
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)~~x>0
, it can be deduced from the above steps that

(2.34)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\sin(1-\alpha)\frac{\pi}{2}=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\cos\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right)~~\alpha\in(0,2)

(2.35)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos t}{t^{\alpha}}\,dt=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\cos(1-\alpha)\frac{\pi}{2}=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{1-\alpha}\sin\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right)~~\alpha\in(0,1).

We deduce from (
2.26
) and (
2.34
) that the expression of
G(s)
is given by

\displaystyle G(s)

\displaystyle=(1-s)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(t)}{t^{2s}}\,dt=(1-s)\varphi_{1}(2s)=(1-s)\frac{\Gamma(2-2s)}{1-2s}\cos\left(s\pi\right).

∎

We are now in a position to give the explicit value of
C_{d,s}
and to study its asymptotic behavior as obtained in
[
NPV12
]
. In addition, the Proposition below shows there is a surprising coincidence between constants
C_{d,s}
and the Brezis-Bourgain-Mironescu constant
K_{d,p}
(see the Section
23
), for
p=2
, which will be useful for studying the convergence of nonlocal structures to local ones.

Proposition 2.21
.

The following assertions are true:

1.

Let
0<s<1
, we admit that
\Gamma(1-s)=-s\Gamma(-s)
extending the definition of
\Gamma
to
-s
. Then,

(2.36)

\displaystyle C_{d,s}^{-1}=\pi^{(d-1)/2}\frac{\Gamma(s+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(2-2s)}{s(1-2s)\Gamma(s+\frac{d}{2})}\cos\left(s\pi\right)=\frac{\pi^{d/2}|\Gamma(-s)|}{2^{2s}\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}=\frac{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(1-s)}{s2^{2s}\Gamma(\frac{d+2s}{2})}.

Another simple expression is to set
\alpha=2s
so that we get

\displaystyle C_{d,s}\equiv C_{d,\alpha}=\frac{2^{\alpha}\Gamma\big(\frac{d+\alpha}{2}\big)}{\pi^{d/2}\big|\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\big|}.

2.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
e
be any element of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Then,

(2.37)

\displaystyle K_{d,p}:=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|^{p}d\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{p+1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{p+d}{2}\right)}.

3.

Let
|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|:=\omega_{d-1}=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}
be the surface measure of unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We have,

(2.38)

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{C_{d,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{2}{\omega_{d-1}}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\frac{4}{\omega_{d-1}K_{d,2}}=\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}\frac{C_{d,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{4d}{\omega_{d-1}}.

Proof.
.

(i) The first equality clearly holds by combining (
2.21
), (
2.25
) and (
2.32
). For the second equality we need some helping hand formulae related to Gamma function. Another version of Legendre’s duplication formula stipulates that for all
z>0
,

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Gamma(2z)=\frac{2^{2z-1}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(z)\Gamma(z+\frac{1}{2}).

A justification of this equality comes from the original Legendre duplication formula in (
2.24
) as follows

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(z)\Gamma(z)}{\Gamma(2z)}

\displaystyle=B(z,z)=\int_{0}^{1}t^{z-1}(1-t)^{z-1}\,dt

\displaystyle\overset{t=\frac{x+1}{2}}{=}2^{1-2z}\left[2\int_{0}^{1}(1-x^{2})^{z-1}\,dx\right]

\displaystyle\overset{u=x^{2}}{=}2^{1-2z}\int_{0}^{1}u^{\frac{1}{2}-1}(1-u)^{z-1}\,du

\displaystyle=2^{1-2z}B(\frac{1}{2},z)

\displaystyle=2^{1-2z}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(z)}{\Gamma(z+\frac{1}{2})}=2^{1-2z}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(z)}{\Gamma(z+\frac{1}{2})}.

Let us also recall that the Schwarz reflexion formula
7
7

7

The Legendre duplication formula implies
\Gamma(\varepsilon)\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)=B(\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\varepsilon}dt}{1+t}
thus the Schwarz reflexion formula can be obtained by applying the residues theorem using an appropriate domain.
infers that for every
0<\varepsilon<1
,

(2.40)

\displaystyle B(\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon)=\Gamma(\varepsilon)\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\varepsilon)}\qquad\qquad(\textrm{Schwarz's reflexion formula}).

Wherefrom, by using the parity of the
t\mapsto\sin t
one obviously gets the relation

(2.41)

\displaystyle\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)=-\varepsilon\Gamma(-\varepsilon)=\varepsilon|\Gamma(-\varepsilon)|.

More generally, the analytic extension formula of Gamma function given for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
0<|\varepsilon|<1
can be obtained by induction:

(2.42)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\varepsilon-m)=(-1)^{m}\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)\Gamma(\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(m+1-\varepsilon)}.

Indeed, considering the extended Gamma function for negative value, Schwarz’s reflexion formula above is also valid for
-1<z<0
. By taking
m=1~
and replacing
~\varepsilon
in (
2.42
) by
1-\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon<1
, however one observes that

\Gamma(-\varepsilon)=-\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ that is}\qquad|\Gamma(-\varepsilon)|=\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}.

Within the relation (
2.41
), a routine check shows that for all
0<s<1
with
s\neq\frac{1}{2}
one has

(2.43)

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+1-s\right)=\frac{1-2s}{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-2s}{2}\right).

Thus, for
0<s<1
from relations (
2.39
), (
2.41
), (
2.43
) and (
2.40
) we get

\displaystyle\Gamma(2(1-s))

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-2s+1}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+1-s\right)

\displaystyle=s(1-2s)\frac{2^{-2s}}{\sqrt{\pi}}|\Gamma(-s)|\Gamma\left(\frac{1-2s}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=s(1-2s)\frac{2^{-2s}}{\sqrt{\pi}}|\Gamma(-s)|\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi(\frac{1-2s}{2}))\Gamma(s+\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=s(1-2s)2^{-2s}\sqrt{\pi}\frac{|\Gamma(-s)|}{\cos(s\pi)\Gamma(s+\frac{1}{2})}.

This shows the second equality in (
2.36
) for
s\neq\frac{1}{2}
for which the result is also valid for
s=\frac{1}{2}
simply by taking the limit as
s\to\frac{1}{2}
directly in (
2.36
).

(ii) Taking into account the rotation invariance of the Lebesgue measure one glimpses that
K_{d,p}
is independent of the chosen unit vector
e
and whence it is sufficient to consider
e:=(0,\cdots,0,1)\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Now we let
w=(w^{\prime},t)\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with
t\in(-1,1)
so that
w^{\prime}\in\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
. In virtue of the Jacobian formula for spherical coordinates one has
\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-2}(w^{\prime})dt}{\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}
(see
[
Gra08
, Appendix D.2]
). Therefore,

\displaystyle K_{d,p}

\displaystyle=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w_{1}|^{p}d\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{d-1}}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{w^{\prime}\in\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}|t|^{p}d\frac{\sigma_{d-2}(w^{\prime})dt}{\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\omega_{d-1}}\int_{0}^{1}t^{p}\left|\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\mathbb{S}^{d-2}\right|\tfrac{dt}{\sqrt{1-t^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\omega_{d-2}}{\omega_{d-1}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}t^{p}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{d-2}}{\omega_{d-1}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{\frac{d-1}{2}-1}t^{\frac{p+1}{2}-1}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{d-2}}{\omega_{d-1}}B\left(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{p+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{d-2}}{\omega_{d-1}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{p+1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d+p}{2}\right)}.

Here
B(\cdot,\cdot)
is the beta function and we have used the relation (
2.24
). The result follows by using the formula
|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|=\omega_{d-1}=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma\big(d/2\big)}
along with the relation
\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})=\pi^{1/2}
.

(iii) This result plainly follows from expressions of
G(s)
and
A(d,s)
. Indeed, one can check that

\displaystyle G(s)=(1-s)\Gamma(1-2s)\cos\left(s\pi\right)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Gamma(3-2s)}{1-2s}\cos\left(s\pi\right)

from where,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}G(s)=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{s\to 1}G(s)=\frac{1}{2}.

Since
\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})=\pi^{1/2}
, from (
2.23
) we get the following after cancellation

\displaystyle A(d,0)=\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}=\frac{\omega_{d-1}}{2}\quad\text{and }\quad A(d,1)=\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{d\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}=\frac{\omega_{d-1}}{2d}.

Therefore, as
s(1-s)C_{d,s}^{-1}=G(s)A(d,s)
and
K_{d,2}=\frac{1}{d}
, we obtain the following results:

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{C_{d,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{2}{\omega_{d-1}}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\lim_{s\to 1}\frac{C_{d,s}}{s(1-s)}=\frac{4d}{\omega_{d-1}}=\frac{4}{\omega_{d-1}K_{d,2}}.

Alternatively, the above limits follows after one easily verifies that

\displaystyle\frac{s(1-s)}{C_{d,s}}=\frac{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(2-s)}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s+\frac{d}{2})}.

∎

Remark 2.22
.

For
0<a<1
, from the foregoing computations, it follows that the constant

\displaystyle\gamma_{d,a}=2\phi_{2}(1-a)A(d,-a/2)=\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-a}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-a}{2}\right)}

is the constant for which the Riesz Potential (also known as the inverse of the fractional Laplacian)

\displaystyle I_{a}f(x):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{d,a}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{f(y)dy}{|x-y|^{d-a}}

satisfies the relation
\widehat{I_{a}f}(\xi)=|\xi|^{-a}\widehat{f}(\xi)
for all
f\in S(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with equality understood in the sense of distributions. Indeed, the invariance of the Lebesgue measure under rotation implies

\displaystyle\widehat{I_{a}f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\gamma_{d,a}}\widehat{f}(\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{e^{-ix\cdot\xi}}{|x|^{d-a}}dx=\frac{|\xi|^{-a}}{\gamma_{d,a}}\widehat{f}(\xi)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{e^{-ix_{1}}}{|x|^{d-a}}dx.

Letting
x^{\prime}=x_{1}z
with
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
that is
dx^{\prime}=x_{1}^{d-1}dz

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{e^{-ix_{1}}}{|x|^{d-a}}dx=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\cos(t)}{t^{1-a}}dt\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\frac{dz}{(1+|z|^{2})^{\frac{d-a}{2}}}\right)=2\varphi_{2}(1-a)A(d,-a/2).

4
Order of the fractional Laplace operator

Now, we show that the fractional Laplacian acts on Hölder spaces like an operator of order
2s
.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\sigma<1
. The Hölder space
C_{b}^{m+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
also denoted,
by
C_{b}^{m,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, is the collection of functions in
C_{b}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whose classical derivative of order
|\alpha|=m
belongs to
C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

In the spirit of the estimate (
2.4
), one can easily establish the following estimates.

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
u\in C_{b}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\gamma=1+\tau
,
m=1
and
0\leq\tau\leq 1
. Define the second order discrete difference of
u
by
D^{2}_{h}u(x)=u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x).
Then for all
x,h,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{h}u(x+z)-D^{2}_{h}u(x)|\leq C\|u\|_{C_{b}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(|z||h|^{\tau}\land|h|^{1+\tau}).

Furthermore, if
u\in C_{b}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
then

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{h}u(x+z)-D^{2}_{h}u(x)|\leq C\|u\|_{C_{b}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(|z|^{\gamma}\land|h|^{\gamma}).

The next result shows that the fractional Laplace operator
(-\Delta)^{s}
is an integrodifferential of order
2s
.

Theorem 2.25

(Order of fractional Laplacian)
.

Let
\gamma>0
and
0<s<1
such that
0<2s<\gamma
. The fractional Laplacian defined
(-\Delta)^{s}:C_{b}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to C_{b}^{\gamma-2s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a bounded operator.

Proof.
.

First, assume
\gamma-2s=m+\sigma
with
m=0,1
and
0<\sigma\leq 1
. In the case
m=0
, i.e.
\gamma=2s+\sigma
there are subcases:
0<2s<\gamma\leq 1
or
1<2s<\gamma\leq 2
. Suppose that
0<2s<\gamma\leq 1
then in view of Proposition
2.24
we have

\displaystyle|(-\Delta)^{s}u(x+z)-(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)|

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{D^{2}_{h}u(x+z)-D^{2}_{h}u(x)}{|h|^{d+2s}}\mathrm{d}h\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|z|^{\gamma}\land|h|^{\gamma}}{|h|^{d+2s}}\mathrm{d}h=\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}|z|^{\gamma-2s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1\land|h|^{\gamma}}{|h|^{d+2s}}\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}|z|^{\gamma-2s}c_{d}\Big(\frac{1}{\gamma-2s}+\frac{1}{2s}\Big)=C_{s}|z|^{\gamma-2s}.

For the second subcase where
1<2s<\gamma\leq 2
we necessarily have
0<\sigma<1
. Now we put
\gamma=1+\tau
with
\tau\in[0,1]
and recall that
\gamma-2s=\sigma
so that
\tau=2s+\sigma-1
.
From Proposition
2.24
we get

\displaystyle|(-\Delta)^{s}u(x+z)-(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)|

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{D^{2}_{h}u(x+z)-D^{2}_{h}u(x)}{|h|^{d+2s}}\mathrm{d}h\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|z||h|^{\tau}\land|h|^{1+\tau}}{|h|^{d+2s}}\mathrm{d}h=\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}|z|^{\gamma-2s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|h|^{-d+\sigma-1}(1\land|h|)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,s}}{2}|z|^{\gamma-2s}c_{d}\Big(\frac{1}{\sigma}+\frac{1}{1-\sigma}\Big)=C_{s}|z|^{\gamma-2s}.

In either case, we have
(-\Delta)^{s}u\in C_{b}^{\gamma-2s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Now, if
m=1
that is
\gamma=2s+\sigma+1
then,
0<2s<1\leq\gamma\leq 2
,
which implies that
0<2s+\sigma<1
. Furthermore, we have
\nabla u\in C^{2s+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since
\nabla u
is bounded, we have
|D^{2}_{h}u(x+te_{i})-D^{2}_{h}u(x)|\leq C|t|
, and from Proposition
2.24
one also gets
|D^{2}_{h}u(x+te_{i})-D^{2}_{h}u(x)|\leq C|t||h|^{2s+\sigma}
so that

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{h}u(x+te_{i})-D^{2}_{h}u(x)|\leq C|t|(1\land|h|^{2s+\sigma}).

On the other hand,
h\mapsto(1\land|h|^{2s+\sigma})|h|^{-d-2s}
is integrable. Using the dominated convergence theorem one arrives at
\partial_{x_{i}}(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=(-\Delta)^{s}\partial_{x_{i}}u(x).

Furthermore, as
\partial_{x_{i}}u\in C_{b}^{2s+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

by applying dominated convergence again one gets that the map
x\mapsto(-\Delta)^{s}\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)
is continuous. In sum,
x\mapsto(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)
is differentiable and
\nabla(-\Delta)^{s}u=(-\Delta)^{s}\nabla u
. We know from the previous case
\nabla(-\Delta)^{s}u\in C_{b}^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is
(-\Delta)^{s}u\in C_{b}^{\sigma+1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=C_{b}^{\gamma-2s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, since
\gamma-2s=\sigma+1
. Proceeding by induction with a similar argument the result remains true when
\gamma=m+\sigma>2
and
\partial^{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{s}u=(-\Delta)^{s}\partial^{\alpha}u
for each multiindex
|\alpha|\leq m
.
The boundedness of
(-\Delta)^{s}
blatantly follows from the previous estimates.
∎

5
Nonlocal elliptic operators

In this section, we define some concepts analogous to those from elliptic partial differential operators of second order. We then provide some concrete connections between the nonlocal and local notions. Let us recall that the modern theory of elliptic partial differential operators of second order is governed by two influential classes. The class of operators in the divergence form
\mathscr{A}
and the class of operators in the non-divergence form
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
. To be more precise these are operators of the following forms

(
divergence form
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}u

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla)u+b\cdot\nabla u+cu=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\big(a_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\big)u+\sum_{i=1}^{d}b_{i}\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_{i}}+cu

(
non-divergence form
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}u

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{tr}(A(\cdot)D^{2})u+b\cdot\nabla u+cu=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}b_{i}\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_{i}}+cu.

Here,
c,b_{i}:\R^{d}\to\R
are measurable functions and
A=(a_{ij})_{ij}:\R^{d}\to\mathrm{M}(d\times d)
is a matrix valued measurable function satisfying the ellipticity condition, i.e. there is a constant
\Lambda_{0}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\langle A(x)\xi,\xi\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\Lambda_{0}|\xi|^{2}\quad\text{for all $x,\xi\in\R^{d}$}.

The operators
\mathscr{A}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
are symmetric if the matrix
A
is symmetric, i.e.
a_{ij}=a_{ji}
,
1\leq i,j\leq d
and translation invariant if and only if the coefficients
c,b_{i},a_{ij}

1\leq i,j\leq d
are constants. If the coefficients are constants then
\mathscr{A}=\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
. If
A
is regular enough, we then have the relation

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}u=\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}u+\widetilde{b}\cdot\nabla u\quad\text{with}\quad\widetilde{b}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}\frac{\partial a_{ij}}{\partial x_{j}}.

The most studied elliptic partial differential operator of second order is the Laplacian

\displaystyle\Delta u=\operatorname{div}(\nabla u)=\operatorname{tr}(D^{2}u)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial^{2}x_{i}}\quad\text{for $u\in C^{2}(\R^{d})$}.

Our purpose here is to introduce the analog notion of an elliptic operator in divergence and non-divergence form for nonlocal operators especially for integrodifferential operators.

Definition 2.26

(
Integrodifferential operator in divergence form
)
.

An integrodifferential operator in divergence form is any nonlocal operator
\mathscr{L}
that can be written in the form

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}u(x)=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))\,\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y),\quad\quad\text{$(x\in\R^{d})$}

for a sufficiently smooth function
u:\R^{d}\to\R
. Here
\big(\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
is a family of Borel measures satisfying
\mu(x,\{x\})=0
for every
x\in\R^{d}
. In practice, for consistency reasons, it is often assumed that the family
\big(\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
satisfies the uniform Lévy integrability condition

(2.45)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|x-y|^{2})\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)<\infty.

We say that
\mathscr{L}
is symmetric if for all measurable sets
A,B\subset\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{AB}\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}x=\iint\limits_{BA}\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Definition 2.27

(
Integrodifferential operator in non-divergence form
)
.

An integrodifferential operator in non-divergence form is any nonlocal operator
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
that can be written in the form

(2.46)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(2u(x)-u(x+h)-u(x-h)\,\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h),\quad\quad\text{$(x\in\R^{d})$}

for a sufficiently smooth function
u:\R^{d}\to\R
. Here
\big(\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
is a family of Borel measures satisfying
\widetilde{\mu}(x,\{0\})=0
for every
x\in\R^{d}
. In practice, for consistency reasons, it is often assumed that the family
\big(\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
satisfies the uniform Lévy integrability condition

(2.47)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)<\infty.

We say that
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
is symmetric if
\widetilde{\mu}(x,A)=\widetilde{\mu}(x,-A)
for all measurable set
A\subset\R^{d}
and all
x\in\R^{d}
.

Remark 2.28
.

If
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
then the pointwise evaluation
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}u(x)
obviously makes sense and under the condition (
2.47
), the pointwise evaluation
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u(x)
is also well defined (it is sufficient to adapt the Proposition
2.1
). In general, however, the pointwise evaluation
\mathscr{L}u(x)
might not be defined under the condition (
2.45
) even for
bona fide
test functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This operator is as good or as bad as the local operator
\mathscr{A}
can be. In fact, if the coefficients
(a_{ij})_{ij}
are
sufficiently rough then the pointwise expression
\mathscr{A}u(x)
might not make sense. If we assume that
\mathscr{L}
is symmetric and the condition (
2.45
) holds and that
\mathscr{A}
is
symmetric and elliptic, however, then
\mathscr{A}u
and
\mathscr{L}u
can be evaluated in the generalized sense. It other words, for all
u,\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
, the expressions
\langle\mathscr{A}u,\varphi\rangle
and
\langle\mathscr{L}u,\varphi\rangle
are well defined:

(2.48)

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{A}u,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle:=\int_{\R^{d}}(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

(2.49)

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{L}u,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle:=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Additional discussions on the operators
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
are included in
[
Kas07
]
.

Remark 2.29
.

Assume
\big(\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
is symmetric i.e
\mu(x,-A)=\mu(x,A)
for every
A\subset\R^{d}
and is translation invariant, i.e.
\mu(x,A+h)=\nu(x,A)
for every
A\subset\R^{d}
and
x,h\in\R^{d}
then
\mathscr{L}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
. In the local setting, this corresponds to the situation where the matrix
A
is constant, hence
\mathscr{A}=\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
. In a sense, an integrodifferential operator that is symmetric and translation invariant corresponds in the local setting to the situation where the coefficients
c,b_{i},a_{ij}
are constants. A simple instance is provided if we let
\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)=\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y
where the function
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is measurable and satisfies
\nu(-h)=\nu(h)
for all
h\in\R^{d}
. In this particular case, one recognizes the operator
L=\mathscr{L}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
discussed in the first section.

Remark 2.30
.

Assume
\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)=\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y
and
\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)=\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h
where
\nu
is radial. Assume
A=I_{d}
(identity matrix), then we have
L=\mathscr{L}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
and
-\Delta=\mathscr{A}=\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
. Moreover, it is not difficult to show that the operators
L
and
-\Delta
are isotropic, i.e. invariant by under rotations. Therefore, when
\nu
is radial, the operator
L
appears to be a prototypical example of an elliptic integrodifferential operator, just as the Laplace operator
-\Delta
is a prototypical example of an elliptic differential operator of second order.

Next we introduce the ellipticity condition associated with the operators
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
. We restrict ourselves to the case where for each
x\in\R^{d}
, the measures
\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)
and
\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

Definition 2.31
.

A measurable function
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
will be called unimodal if
\nu
is radial and almost decreasing,
i.e. there is
c>0
such that
\nu(x)\geq c\nu(y)
whenever
|x|\leq|y|
.

Definition 2.32
.

We say that the family
\big(\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
is weakly elliptic if there exist a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
and a unimodal function
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
that is Lévy integrable, i.e.
\nu\in L^{1}((1\land|h|^{2}),\,\mathrm{d}h)
such that for every Borel set
A,B\subset\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\iint\limits_{AB}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\iint\limits_{AB}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\Lambda\iint\limits_{AB}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

The operator
\mathscr{L}
is weakly elliptic if
\big(\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\big)_{x\in\R^{d}}
is weakly elliptic.

Definition 2.33

(
Elliptic kernel
)
.

We say that a kernel
J:\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\setminus\operatorname{diag}\to[0,\infty]
is elliptic if

(2.50)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\R^{d}}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|x-y|^{2})J(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

and there exist a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
and a unimodal function
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
that is Lévy integrable, i.e.
\nu\in L^{1}((1\land|h|^{2}),\,\mathrm{d}h)
such that

(2.51)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu(x-y)\leq J(x,y)\leq\Lambda\nu(x-y)\quad\text{for all $0<|x-y|\leq 1$}.

We say that
J
is globally elliptic if instead we have

(2.52)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu(x-y)\leq J(x,y)\leq\Lambda\nu(x-y)\quad\text{for all $x\neq y$}.

Obviously the global ellipticity condition implies the ellipticity condition and the ellipticity condition implies the weak ellipticity condition.

Remark 2.34
.

It is possible to omit the condition that
\nu
is almost decreasing in Definition

2.33
as it does not really influence the concept of ellipticity defined here. One should keep in mind, however, that this condition only plays a technical role when dealing with function spaces and Poincaré types inequalities.

Definition 2.35

(
Elliptic operator
)
.

Assume
\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)=J(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y
and
\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)=J(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h
for a kernel
J:\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\setminus\operatorname{diag}\to[0,\infty]
. We say that the operators
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
are elliptic if the kernel
J
is elliptic. Recall that here,

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}u(x)=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))J(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(2u(x)-u(x+h)-u(x-h)\,J(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

Next, we bridge a transition from elliptic integrodifferential operators of the forms
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
to elliptic partial differential operators of the forms
\mathscr{A}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}
. We intend to convince that the aforementioned notions (symmetry, translation invariance, ellipticity, divergence form and non-divergence form) are correlated. Let us introduce
(\nu_{\alpha})_{0<\alpha<2}
, a family of Lévy radial functions approximating the Dirac measure at the origin, i.e. for every
\alpha,\delta>0

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{\alpha}\geq 0,\,\,\text{ is radial},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=1,\quad\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0\,.\end{split}

Note that several examples of
(\nu_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
are provided in Section
22
. For a family
(J^{\alpha})_{0<\alpha<2}
of positive symmetric kernels
J^{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\operatorname{diag}\to[0,\infty]
we set-up the following:

(E)

There exists a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
such that for every
\alpha\in(0,2)
and all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
0<|x-y|\leq 1

(
E
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\leq J^{\alpha}(x,y)\leq\Lambda\nu_{\alpha}(x-y).

(L)

For every
\delta>0

(
L
)

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{|h|>\delta}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh=0.

(I)

For each
\alpha\in(0,2)
the kernel
J^{\alpha}
is translation invariant, i.e., for every
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(I)

\displaystyle J^{\alpha}(x+h,y+h)=J^{\alpha}(x,y).

(G-E)

There exists a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
such that for every
\alpha\in(0,2)
and all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
x\neq y
,

(
G
-
E
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\leq J^{\alpha}(x,y)\leq\Lambda\nu_{\alpha}(x-y).

It is clear that (

G

-

E

) implies (

E

) and (

L
