). Let us define the elliptic matrix uniquely determined by the family
(J^{\alpha})_{\alpha}
. Given
x\in\R^{d}
and
\delta>0
, we define the symmetric matrix as
A(x)=(a_{ij}(x))_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
by

(2.53)

\displaystyle a_{ij}(x)=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh.

Remark 2.36
.

(i)
Under conditions (

E

) and (

L

) the expression
\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha_{n}}(x,x+h)dx
converges for a suitable subsequence of
(\alpha_{n})
. The existence of the limit in (
2.53
) poses an implicit condition on the family
(J^{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.

(ii)
(

E

) and (

L

) ensure that the quantity
a_{ij}(x)
does not depend on the choice of
\delta
and is bounded as a function in
x
. Indeed for all
\delta,r>0
,

\displaystyle a_{ij}(x)=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{r}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh.

(iii)
Under condition (
I
) the functions
a_{ij}(x)
are constant in
x
.

Example 2.37
.

The conditions (

E

), (

L

) and (
I
) are fulfilled for each of the examples below.

\displaystyle J_{1}^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\nu_{\alpha}(x-y),

\displaystyle J_{2}^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}}(x-y)+(2-\alpha)J(x,y)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{1}}(x-y)\,,

where
J
is a symmetric and translation invariant kernel such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{\delta}}J(x,x+h)dh<\infty~\text{ for every }~~\delta>0.

We can also consider the standard kernels

\displaystyle J_{3}^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle J_{4}^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}}(x-y)+(2-\alpha)|x-y|^{-d-\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{1}}(x-y)\,,

Here,
\beta>0
and
C_{d,\alpha}
is the normalization constant of the fractional Laplacian. Another example is given as follows. For
e\in\R^{d}
we set

\displaystyle J_{5}^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\big(2+\cos(e\cdot(x-y)))\nu_{\alpha}(x-y).

The matrix corresponding to
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
above, is
A(x)=\frac{1}{d}I_{d}=(\frac{1}{d}\delta_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
and for
J_{3}
and
J_{4}
the corresponding matrix is
A(x)=I_{d}=(\delta_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
, where
I_{d}
is the identity matrix; see Proposition
2.38
).

Proposition 2.38
.

Assume (

E

) and (

L

). Consider the symmetric matrix
A=(a_{ij})_{ij}
from (
2.53
), i.e.

\displaystyle a_{ij}(x)=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{1}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh.

1.

The matrix
A
is elliptic and has bounded coefficients. To be more precise, we have

\displaystyle d^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\langle A(x)\xi,\xi\rangle\leq d^{-1}\Lambda|\xi|^{2},\quad\text{ for every }x,\xi\in\R^{d}.

2.

Under condition (
I
) each
x\mapsto a_{ij}(x)
is constant function. In particular, for
J_{1}^{\alpha}(x,y)=\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)
we have
A(x)=\frac{1}{d}(\delta_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
, i.e., the matrix
A
equals the identity matrix and for
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
we have
A(x)=(\delta_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
.

3.

For
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
we have

\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\alpha}u(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(A(x)\nabla u)(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}u(x),\quad\text{for all $x\in\R^{d}$}

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\alpha}u(x):=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

4.

For all
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
we have
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}-\Delta u(x)
and
L_{\alpha}u(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}-\frac{1}{2d}\Delta u(x)
. Recall that

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}h}{|h|^{d+\alpha}}

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}u(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

5.

For
u\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
we have

\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\big\langle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u,\varphi\big\rangle=\big\langle-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla u),\varphi\big\rangle=\big\langle\mathscr{A}u,\varphi\big\rangle.

Here,

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u(x)

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathscr{A}u,\varphi\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}\big(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle:=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

(i)
Let
x,\xi\in\R^{d}
and
|h|\leq 1
. The condition (

E

) implies that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\int_{B_{1}}[\xi\cdot h]^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\int_{B_{1}}[\xi\cdot h]^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\Lambda\int_{B_{1}}[\xi\cdot h]^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

From the definition of the matrix
A
we have

\displaystyle\langle A(x)\xi,\xi\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}h_{i}h_{j}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}[\xi\cdot h]^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,.

Since the Lebesgue measure is invariant under rotations, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}[\xi\cdot h]^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq d}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}h_{i}h_{j}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h+\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\xi_{i}^{2}h_{i}^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq d}\xi_{i}^{2}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}h_{1}^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}|\xi|^{2}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}h_{1}^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}|\xi|^{2}d^{-1}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}\sum_{1\leq i\leq d}h_{i}^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}|\xi|^{2}d^{-1}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=|\xi|^{2}d^{-1}\,.

Indeed, due to the symmetric, the sum over
i\neq j
vanishes. Altogether this gives

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}d^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\langle A(x)\xi,\xi\rangle\leq\Lambda d^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\quad\text{for all }\quad x,\xi\in\R^{d}

(ii)
Obviously
x\mapsto a_{ij}(x)
is constant if the condition (
I
) holds. Assume
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)
. We show that
A=\frac{1}{d}I_{d}
. By symmetry, for
i\neq j
is easy to show that
a_{ij}=0
. From the fact that the Lebesgue measure is rotationally invariant and Remark
5.4
we find that

\displaystyle a_{ii}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}h^{2}_{i}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}h_{1}^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\frac{1}{d}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\frac{1}{d}\int\limits_{|h|\leq 1}|h|^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\frac{1}{d}\,.

If
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
then
a_{ii}=1
and thus we get
A=I_{d}
. Indeed, it suffices to proceed as above and accounting Proposition
2.21
which asserts that
\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2d\omega_{d-1}(2-\alpha)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}1
.

(iii)
We know from (
2.3
) that

\displaystyle\left|u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x)\right|\leq 4\|u\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(1\land|h|^{2}).

This combined with the assumption (

L

) yields that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\left|u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x)\right|J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0.

The fundamental theorem of calculus suggests that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\alpha}u(x)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}\left(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2(x)\right)J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=-\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}[D^{2}(x)\cdot h]\cdot h~J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}2t\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}[D^{2}u(x-th+2sth)\cdot h-D^{2}u(x)\cdot h]\cdot h~J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\,\,\,\mathrm{d}s\,\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
D^{2}u
(the Hessian of
u
) is continuous at
x
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there is sufficiently small
\delta>0
such that
|D^{2}(x+z)-D^{2}u(x)|<\varepsilon
for all
|z|<4\delta
. This implies,

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}2t\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}|((D^{2}u(x-th+2sth)-D^{2}u(x))\cdot h)\cdot h|~\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\,\,\,\mathrm{d}s\,\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}(1\land|h|^{2})~\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

Hence we get

\displaystyle-2\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\alpha}u(x)=\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}[D^{2}(x)\cdot h]\cdot h~J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}u(x)}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}=\operatorname{tr}\big(A(x)D^{2}u(x)\big)=\widetilde{\mathscr{A}}u(x).

Note that
(iv)
is a consequence of
(ii)
and
(iii)
whereas
(v)
is a particular case of Theorem
5.69
.
∎

6
Mixed Lévy operators

Loosely speaking, we define
a mixed Lévy operator or a Lévy operator with mixed jumps
or
a generalized anisotropic operator
as Lévy operators that can be viewed as the sum of lower-dimensional Lévy operators. In recent years, the study of the sum of the one dimensional fractional Laplacian has attracted much attention. The Fourier multiplier of such operators are of the form
\psi(\xi)=|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha_{1}}+|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha_{2}}+\cdots+|\xi_{d}|^{\alpha_{d}}
with
\alpha_{i}\in(0,2)
for all
\xi\in\R^{d}
. Such operators are known as anisotropic operators, see for instance
[
Cha17
]
and several other references therein. We wish to generalize this to lower dimensions greater than one and due to this geometrical consideration, we believe there is more to learn from such operators.

Let us view
\R^{d}
as
\R^{d_{1}}\times\R^{d_{2}}\times\cdots\times\R^{d_{n}}
where
d=d_{1}+d_{2}+\cdots+d_{n}
with
1\leq n\leq d
and
d_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
. For
x\in\R^{d}
we write
x=(x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*},\cdots,x_{n}^{*})
where
x_{j}^{*}\in\R^{d_{j}}
. In addition we define
\widetilde{x}_{j}=(0,0,\cdots,x_{j}^{*},0,\cdots,0)\in\R^{d_{1}}\times\R^{d_{2}}\times\cdots\times\R^{d_{n}}
so that
x=\widetilde{x}_{1}+\widetilde{x}_{2}+\cdots+\widetilde{x}_{n}
. We identify
\R^{d_{j}}
as a linear sub-variety of
\R^{d}
by the means of the correspondence
\R^{d_{j}}\ni x_{j}^{*}\mapsto\widetilde{x}_{j}=(0,\cdots,0,x_{j}^{*},0,\cdots,0)\in\R^{d}
. Let
\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})
be a symmetric Lévy measure on
\R^{d_{j}}
, i.e.

\nu_{j}(A_{j})=\nu_{j}(-A_{j})
for all
A_{j}\subset\R^{d_{j}}
,
\nu_{j}(\{0\})=0
and

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(1\land|h_{j}^{*}|^{2})\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})<\infty.

Definition 2.39
.

For each
x\in\R^{d}
we define the (mixed) measures

(2.54)

\displaystyle\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}(x_{j}^{*}-\,\mathrm{d}y_{j}^{*})\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{x_{i}^{*}}(\,\mathrm{d}y_{i}^{*})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{0_{i}^{*}}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{i}^{*}).

Here
\delta_{x_{i}^{*}}(\,\mathrm{d}y_{i}^{*})
represents the Dirac measure at
x_{i}^{*}\in\R^{d_{i}}
.

It is noteworthy to mention that for each
x\in\R^{d}
the measures
\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)
is supported on the sub-varieties
\R^{d_{1}},\R^{d_{2}},\cdots,\R^{d_{n}}
whereas the measure
\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)
is supported on the sub-varieties
\widetilde{x}_{1}+\R^{d_{1}},\widetilde{x}_{2}+\R^{d_{2}},\cdots,\widetilde{x}_{n}+\R^{d_{n}}
. To be more precise we have the following.

Proposition 2.40
.

(
Integration rule
)

1.

For each
x\in\R^{d}
we have
\operatorname{supp}\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)\subset\R^{d_{1}}\cup\R^{d_{2}}\cup\cdots\cup\R^{d_{n}}
and

\operatorname{supp}\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)\subset\widetilde{x}_{1}+\R^{d_{1}}\cup\,\widetilde{x}_{2}+\R^{d_{2}}\cup\cdots\cup\,\widetilde{x}_{n}+\R^{d_{n}}
. Moreover, for a Borel set
A\subset\R^{d}
, if we identify
A_{j}=A\cap\R^{d_{j}}
as a subset of
\R^{d_{j}}
then, we have

\displaystyle\mu(x,A)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}(x_{j}^{*}+A_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\mu}(x,A)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}(A_{j}).

2.

Let
f:\R^{d}\to\R
be measurable then for each
x\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}f(y)\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}f(x+\widetilde{h}_{j})\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\R^{d}}f(h)\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}f(\widetilde{h}_{j})\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*}).

As a consequence, we have the following Lévy integrability condition:

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|x-y|^{2})\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(1\land|h_{j}^{*}|^{2})\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})=\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)<\infty.

Definition 2.41
.

We shall call a mixed (or anisotropic) Lévy operator any Lévy type integrodifferential operator whose Lévy measure can be represented in one of the forms in (
2.54
). For instance the following operator
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
are mixed Lévy operators.

(2.55)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathscr{L}u(x)&=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)\\
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u(x)&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x+h)-u(x-h)-2u(x))\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h).\end{split}

According to Proposition
2.40
we get the following proposition.

Proposition 2.42
.

Let
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and let
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
be as in (
2.55
) then for
x\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}u(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(u(x)-u(x+\widetilde{h}_{j})\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(u(x+\widetilde{h}_{j})+u(x-\widetilde{h}_{j})-2u(x))\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*}).

In addition, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{L}u,u\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d_{j}}}(u(x)-u(x+\widetilde{h}_{j})^{2}\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})\,\mathrm{d}x.

We see that
\mathscr{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
are in some way the sum of lower dimensional Lévy operators. This is verified within the Fourier symbol.

Proposition 2.43
.

Let
\psi
be the Fourier symbol of
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
, i.e.
\widehat{\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u}(\xi)=\psi(\xi)\widehat{u}(\xi)
for
\xi\in\R^{d}
and
u\in C_{c}^{d}(\R^{d})
. Then for each
\xi\in\R^{d}
we have
\psi(\xi)=\psi_{1}(\xi_{1}^{*})+\psi_{1}(\xi_{2}^{*})+\cdots+\psi_{1}(\xi_{n}^{*})
, where
\psi_{j}(\xi_{j}^{*})
,
j=1,2,\cdots,n
is the Fourier symbol of the operator
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}
defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}u(x_{j}^{*})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(u(x_{j}^{*}+h_{j}^{*})+u(x_{j}^{*}+h_{j}^{*})-2u(x_{j}^{*}))\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*}).

Proof.
.

According to Proposition
2.40
we get

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}(1-\cos(\xi\cdot h))\widetilde{\mu}(x,\,\mathrm{d}h)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(1-\cos(\xi\cdot\widetilde{h}_{j}))\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(1-\cos(\xi_{j}^{*}\cdot h_{j}^{*}))\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\psi_{j}(\xi_{j}^{*}).

∎

Notation:
To alleviate the notations we write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}u=\big[\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{1}+\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{2}+\cdots+\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{n}\big]u

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}u=\big[\mathscr{L}_{1}+\mathscr{L}_{2}+\cdots+\mathscr{L}_{n}\big]u.

Meaning in term of process:
Assume that
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
is the generator of a Lévy process
(X_{t})_{t}
, then the process
(X_{t})_{t}
jumps according to the following rules:

•

The process can start at any point
x\in\R^{d}
.

•

If the process sites at a point
x\in\R^{d}
then the process is only allowed to jump to the points of the form
y=x+\widetilde{h}_{j}
,
j=1,2,\cdots,n
, where we recall
\widetilde{h}_{j}=(0,\cdots,0,h_{j}^{*},0,\cdots,0)\in\R^{d}
and
h_{j}^{*}\in\R^{d_{j}}
.

•

The rate jump from
x
to
y=x+\widetilde{h}_{j}
is according to the Lévy measure
\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})
on
\R^{d_{j}}
. Roughly speaking, the process behaves like a
d_{j}
-dimensional Lévy process associated with the generator
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}
.

Let us see some concrete examples. For simplicity one may assume that
\nu_{j}(\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*})=\nu_{j}(h_{j}^{*})\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*}
where

\nu_{j}:\R^{d_{j}}\to[0,\infty]
is a symmetric Lévy measure on
\R^{d_{j}}
, i.e.
\nu_{j}(h_{j}^{*})=\nu_{j}(-h_{j}^{*})
for all
h_{j}^{*}\in\R^{d_{j}}
and

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(1\land|h_{j}^{*}|^{2})\nu_{j}(h_{j}^{*})\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*}<\infty.

•

If
n=1
, (i.e.
d=d_{1}
), then
\mu(x,\,\mathrm{d}y)=\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y
(see the first section).

•

Let
\nu_{j}(h_{j}^{*})=C_{d_{j},\alpha_{j}}|h_{j}^{*}|^{-d_{j}-\alpha_{j}}
with
\alpha_{j}\in(0,2)
then the Fourier symbol of
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
which we denote by
(-\Delta)^{\alpha_{1}/2,\cdots,\alpha_{n}/2}
(
mixed fractional Laplacian)
is given by
\psi(\xi)=|\xi_{1}^{*}|^{\alpha_{1}}+\cdots+|\xi_{n}^{*}|^{\alpha_{n}}
and we have

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{\alpha_{1}/2,\cdots,\alpha_{n}/2}u(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{C_{d_{j},\alpha_{j}}}{2}\int_{\R^{d_{j}}}(u(x+\widetilde{h}_{j})+u(x-\widetilde{h}_{j})-2u(x))\frac{\,\mathrm{d}h_{j}^{*}}{|h_{j}^{*}|^{d_{j}+\alpha_{j}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\big[(-\Delta_{1})^{\alpha_{1}/2}+(-\Delta_{2})^{\alpha_{2}/2}+\cdots+(-\Delta_{n})^{\alpha_{n}/2}\big]u(x).

Here,
(-\Delta_{j})^{\alpha_{j}/2}
is the fractional Laplacian of order
\alpha_{j}\in(0,2)
on
\R^{d_{j}}
. It is worth noting that for every
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
, we have
(-\Delta)^{\alpha_{1}/2,\cdots,\alpha_{n}/2}u(x)\to-\Delta u(x)
as
\min\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\}\to 2
. Note that the special case
n=d
, i.e.
d_{1}=d_{2}=\cdots=d_{n}=1
, is considered in
[
Cha17
]
where the authors established the Harnack inequality and Hölder regularity for such operators. Similar work is carried out in
[
BS07
]
for the case
\alpha_{1}=\cdots=\alpha_{n}=\alpha
and
d_{1}=d_{2}=\cdots=d_{n}=1
.

Chapter 3
Nonlocal Sobolev-like Spaces

In this chapter we introduce some nonlocal Sobolev-like spaces that are generalizations of Sobolev-Slobodeckij spaces which we will very often encounter. Roughly speaking these are just some refinements of classical Lebesgue
L^{p}
-spaces (just like the classical Sobolev spaces
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
) whose additional structures are of importance. In short, one can perceive them as nonlocal versions of classical Sobolev spaces of first order generalizing the usual fractional Sobolev spaces. When needed we will recall some basics on classical Sobolev spaces. Nonetheless to better understand the correlation between the nonlocal spaces and the local spaces, we recommend curious readers to hitch-hike some classical text books like
[
AF03
,
Alt16
,
Bre10
,
EE87
,
HT08
]
. For a thorough investigation on the theory of Sobolev spaces we recommend,
[
Maz13
]
. We shall begin this chapter by reviewing some elementary properties of standard Lebesgue spaces and the usual Sobolev spaces on an open set. Non-advanced readers should be aware that some complementary basic notions on Lebesgue spaces are added in Appendix
6
. In the next section we first visit the nonlocal Hilbert spaces which are crucial for the study of complement values problems. After, we introduce nonlocal Sobolev-like spaces in their general form. Since such spaces are less common, we will examine some of their rudimentary properties useful later in our analysis. The usual fractional Sobolev spaces will appear as a particular case of our set-up. After, we show that functions in such spaces can be realized as approximation of smooth functions. Finally under some additional assumptions we derive some compact embedding wherefrom we prove some Poincaré type inequalities. The theoretical effort spent in this chapter will be rewarded in the following ones. Throughout this chapter, unless otherwise stated,
\Omega
is an open subset of
\R^{d}
and
1\leq p<\infty
.
If
1\leq p<\infty
then it is very often assumed that the function
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
satisfies the
p
-Lévy integrability condition

(
J_{1}
)

\displaystyle\nu(-h)=\nu(h)~~\text{ for all $h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$ and}~~\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\,.

7
Preliminaries

Our main focus in this section is to present the convolution product in
L^{p}(\R^{d})
along with some applications to the approximation by smooth functions and the compactness result like the Riesz Frécht-Kolomogorov theorem. Those are in some ways the cornerstone in the sequel.

7.1
Convolution product

Let us recall that the convolution product of two measurable functions
u
and
v
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is given by

\displaystyle u*v(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(y)u(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

provided that for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the integral on the right hand side exists and makes sense. Of course,
u*v
will not exist unless suitable restrictions are imposed upon
u
and
v
. If it does exist, it is a painless exercise to verify that convolution as product is commutative, associative and distributive over the addition and the multiplication by scalars (at least for integrable functions).

We commence this section with a preparation result. Recall that for
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\tau_{h}
denotes the shift function defined by
\tau_{h}u(x)=u(x+h)
.

The following result often known as the continuity of the shift operator on
L^{p}
-spaces, profoundly serves our purposes in many perspectives.

Theorem 3.1

(Continuity of shift)
.

Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
1\leq p<\infty
then

\displaystyle\lim_{|h|\to 0}\|\tau_{h}u-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.

Proof.
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a simple function such that
\|u-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\varepsilon
. We have

\displaystyle\|\tau_{h}u-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tau_{h}u-\tau_{h}g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\tau_{h}g-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|u-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon+\|\tau_{h}g-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Hence, it suffices to prove the result for the simple function
g
in particular if
g=\mathds{1}_{A}
for a measurable set
A
. Note that in this case
A
necessarily has finite measure. Thereupon, the result for this case comes from the regularity of Lebesgue measure as follows

\displaystyle\|\tau_{h}\mathds{1}_{A}-\mathds{1}_{A}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathds{1}_{A}(x-h)-\mathds{1}_{A}(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x=|(A-h)\setminus A)|+|A\setminus(A-h)|\xrightarrow{|h|\to 0}0.

∎

Note that the above result holds true for
p=\infty
if and only if
u
is uniformly continuous.

The most fundamental inequality involving convolutions is Young’s inequality which determines some special situations where the convolution of two functions exist. Recall that for
1\leq p\leq\infty
we shall define the number
p^{\prime}
by
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
with the understanding that
p^{\prime}=\infty
if
p=1
, and
p^{\prime}=1
if
p=\infty
.

Theorem 3.2

(Young’s inequality)
.

Let
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1+\frac{1}{r}
. Suppose that
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then
u*v\in L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\|u*v\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

Moreover if
r=\infty
, the map
x\mapsto u*v(x)
is uniformly continuous.

Proof.
.

First of all observe that
\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{q}{r}=1
,
\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{p}{r}=1
and
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
. Thus, applying the generalized Hölder inequality with exponents
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
and
r
we get

\displaystyle|u*v(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)||u(x-y|)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)|^{q/p^{\prime}}|u(x-y)^{p/q^{\prime}}[|v(y)|^{q/r}|u(x-y)|^{p/r}]\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|^{q/p^{\prime}}_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{p/q^{\prime}}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)|^{q}|u(x-y)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/r}.

Consequently for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle|u*v(x)|^{r}

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|^{qr/p^{\prime}}_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{pr/q^{\prime}}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)|^{q}|u(x-y)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

Employing Fubini’s theorem and using once more the relations
\frac{q}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{q}{r}=1
and
\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{p}{r}=1
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u*v(x)|^{r}\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|^{qr/p^{\prime}}_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{pr/q^{\prime}}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)|^{q}|u(x-y)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\|v\|^{qr/p^{\prime}+q}_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{pr/q^{\prime}+p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|v\|^{r}_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{r}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

which proves the desired inequality. Now assume that
r=\infty
and let
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By the previous inequality and the continuity of the shift we get that

\displaystyle\|\tau_{h}u*v-u*v\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\|u*(|\tau_{h}v-v)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\||\tau_{h}v-v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{h\to 0}0.

Thereby providing the uniform continuity and the proof is now complete.

∎

It is worthwhile mentioning that in general Young’s inequality can be established on every locally compact group furnished with the left invariant Haar measure.
Some special cases of Young’s inequality are much simpler to establish. For instance, assume
r=\infty
which means
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
or
q=p^{\prime}
. Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it follows from Hölder inequality that for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(y)u(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq\|v\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\|u*v\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In the case where
q=1
we have
p=r
and hence if we let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then applying the Hölder inequality again yields the following for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(y)u(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)|^{1/p}|u(x-y)||v(y)|^{1/p^{\prime}}\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|^{1/p^{\prime}}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)||u(x-y)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}.

Fubini’s theorem implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u*v(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\|v\|^{p/p^{\prime}}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(y)||u(x-y)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\|v\|^{p/p^{\prime}}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|v\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|v\|^{p}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

which is

\displaystyle\|u*v\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This inequality is often referred to as the Minkowski’s inequality for convolution. The particular case
q=p=r=1
gives

\displaystyle\|u*v\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In this way
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a commutative Banach algebra with the convolution as product.

7.2
Approximation by smooth functions via convolution

Next we want to approximate a given function
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by smooth functions. This will be derived as an application of the convolution product and the continuity of the shift. We begin with some basic facts. Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then as
\operatorname{supp}\varphi
has compact support, it is routine
to check that
u*\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\partial^{\alpha}(u*\varphi)=u*\partial^{\alpha}\varphi
for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Assume
u,v\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are continuous functions. If
u
and
v
have compact supports so has
u*v
. Indeed, in general the convolution
u*v
, if it exists, satisfies the inclusion

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}u*v\subset\overline{\operatorname{supp}u+\operatorname{supp}v}.

Let
(\eta_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be the standard mollifier family that is
\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-d}\eta\big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\big)
with

\displaystyle\eta(x)=c\exp{-\frac{1}{1-|x|^{2}}}\quad\text{if}~|x|<1\quad\text{and}\quad\eta(x)=0\quad\text{if}~|x|\geq 1,

where the constant
c>0
is chosen such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=1.

It is not difficult to establish that for each
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\eta_{\varepsilon}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\operatorname{supp}\eta_{\varepsilon}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(0)\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=1.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
, for all
g\in L^{p}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\|g-g*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.

Furthermore,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a dense subspace of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.
.

Let
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=1
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle|g*\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)-g(x)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(g(x-y)-g(x))\varepsilon^{-d}\eta(\varepsilon^{-1}y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|g(x-\varepsilon y)-g(x)\Big|\eta(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

Applying Jensen’s inequality with respect to the measure
\eta(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y
in combination with Fubini’s theorem leads to

\displaystyle\|g*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g*\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)-g(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|g(x-\varepsilon y)-g(x)\Big|\eta(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|g(x-\varepsilon y)-g(x)\Big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\eta(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big\|g(\cdot-\varepsilon y)-g\Big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\eta(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

For each
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\eta(y)\|g(\cdot-\varepsilon y)-g(x)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2\eta(y)\|g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and by continuity of the shift,
\|g(\cdot-\varepsilon y)-g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
.
In virtue of the foregoing, the dominated convergence theorem implies that we also have
\|g*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
.

Let us now prove the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and fix
\delta>0
. From the dominated convergence theorem we are able to find
j_{0}\geq 1
large enough such that
\|u-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta/2
with
g=u\mathds{1}_{B_{j_{0}}(0)}
.
Since
g
has compact support it turns out that
g*\eta_{\varepsilon}
is of compact support too. Furthermore, we have
g*\eta_{\varepsilon}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and as previously shown, there is
\varepsilon>0
small enough for which
\|g*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta/2
so that

\displaystyle\|u-g*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|u-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|g*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open subset and let
1\leq p<\infty
. Then
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
L^{p}(\Omega)
.

Proof.
.

Let
(K_{j})_{j}
be an exhaustion of compact sets of
\Omega
with
\operatorname{dist(K_{j},\partial\Omega)>\frac{1}{j}}
. For
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and
\delta>0
small enough there exists
j\geq 1
sufficiently large such that
\|u-u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}<\delta/2
. Assume
u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}}
is extended by zero to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
then by Theorem
3.3
for
\varepsilon<1/2j
sufficiently small, we have
\|u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}}-(u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}})*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta/2
so that

\displaystyle\|u-(u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}})*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\|u-u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}}-(u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}})*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta.

Moreover
u*\eta_{\varepsilon}
belongs to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
since
\operatorname{supp}(u\mathds{1}_{K_{j}})*\eta_{\varepsilon}\subset\overline{K_{j}+B_{1/2j}(0)}\subset K_{2j}
and
K_{2j}
is a compact subset of
\Omega
. This achieves the proof.
∎

7.3
The Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov theorem

With the help of approximation by means of convolutions, we shall provide a compactness criterion of subsets in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is very effective in applications. The concerned result will be obtained through the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem which gives a criterion for compactness in spaces of functions. We state the result explicitly for the spaces of interest here and we shall omit the proof which can be found in
[
Yos80
, chapter 3]
.

Theorem 3.5

(Arzelà-Ascoli)
.

Assume that
K
is a nonempty compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. A subset
\mathcal{F}
of
C(K)
normed by
\|u\|_{C(K)}=\max\limits_{x\in K}|u(x)|
, is precompact if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is bounded in
C(K)
and
\mathcal{F}
is equicontinuous on
C(K)
, i.e.

\lim\limits_{|h|\to 0^{+}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|\tau_{h}u-u\|_{C(K)}=0.

Explicitly, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
|h|<\delta
, if
x,x+h\in K
then

\displaystyle|u(x+h)-u(x)|<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all }\quad u\in\mathcal{F}.

In connection to the equicontinuity we also have the following analogous concept in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
1\leq p<\infty
. A subset
\mathcal{F}
of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is said to be
p
-equicontinuous if

\displaystyle\lim_{|h|\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|\tau_{h}u-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.

The following result known as the Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov
8
8

8

This was originally proved by M. Riesz. A further
characterization, given by Fréchet and Kolmogorov, is the approximation of
precompact sets by finite-dimensional ones
Theorem gives conditions analogous to
the ones in the Arzelà- Ascoli theorem for a set to be precompact in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The proof of the following version follows
[
Bre10
, Theorem 26]
.

Theorem 3.7

(Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov)
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. Assume
\mathcal{F}
is a bounded
p
-equicontinuous subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\mathcal{F}\mid_{\Omega}
is precompact in
L^{p}(\Omega
) for any measurable subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite measure .

Proof.
.

Given that
L^{p}(\Omega)
is complete it suffices to show that
\mathcal{F}\mid_{\Omega}
is totally bounded therein. To this end, we fix
\delta>0
since
p
-equicontinuous we choose
\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small such that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|u-u*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\delta/2

In truth, with the
p
-equicontinuity at hand, by arguing as for the proof of Theorem
3.3
and applying the dominated convergence theorem, one comes to conclusion that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|u-u*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\Big\|u(\cdot-\varepsilon y)-u\Big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\eta(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y=0.

Meanwhile, for each
\varepsilon>0
and each
u\in\mathcal{F}
, Young’s inequality yields that we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\|u*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\|\nabla(u*\eta_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\nabla\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where we have used
\nabla(u*\eta_{\varepsilon})=u*\nabla\eta_{\varepsilon}
and set
C=\sup\limits_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.
Let
K
be a compact subset of
\Omega
then using the left estimate from the previous display and (
3.1
) we have that for all
u\in\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\setminus K)}\leq\|u-u*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|u*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\setminus K)}\leq\delta/2+C|\Omega\setminus K|^{1/p}\|\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

Wherefore, choosing the compact set
K
large so that
|\Omega\setminus K|
is small enough, we obtain that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\setminus K)}<\delta.

For such a compact set
K
and fixed
\varepsilon>0
as above we claim that the family
\mathcal{F}*\eta_{\varepsilon}\hskip-4.30554pt\mid_{K}
is equicontinuous in
C(K)
. From the second estimate in (
3.2
) it follows that

\displaystyle\hskip-4.30554pt\sup\limits_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|\tau_{h}u*\eta_{\varepsilon}-u*\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{C(K)}\leq|h|\sup\limits_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|\nabla(u*\eta_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C|h|\|\nabla\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{|h|\to 0}0.

In view of the Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem
3.5
the set
\mathcal{F}*\eta_{\varepsilon}\hskip-4.30554pt\mid_{K}
is precompact in
C(K)
and hence is totally bounded. Whence there exist
g_{1},\cdots,g_{N}\in\mathcal{F}*\eta_{\varepsilon}\hskip-4.30554pt\mid_{K}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}*\eta_{\varepsilon}\hskip-4.30554pt\mid_{K}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{\infty}_{\delta_{K}}(g_{i})\quad\textrm{with}\quad\delta_{K}=\delta|K|^{-1/p}.

To conclude that
\mathcal{F}\mid_{\Omega}
is totally bounded, we show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\mid_{\Omega}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B_{3\delta}(\overline{g}_{i}),

where
\overline{g}_{i}
is the zero extension to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
g_{i}
. Let
u\in\mathcal{F}
then by the previous
inclusion, for some
i
we have
\|u*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g_{i}\|_{C(K)}\leq\delta_{K}
. This implies that

(3.4)

\displaystyle\|u*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(K)}\leq\delta.

Combining (
3.1
), (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) one arrives at

\displaystyle\|u-\overline{g}_{i}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{\Omega\setminus K}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K}|u(x)-g_{i}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\setminus K)}+\|u*\eta_{\varepsilon}-u\|_{L^{p}(K)}+\|u*\eta_{\varepsilon}-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(K)}<3\delta.

That is
u\in B_{3\delta}(\overline{g}_{i})
, and the proof ends here.
∎

Theorem 3.8

(Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov)
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. A subset
\mathcal{F}
of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is precompact if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is bounded,
p
-tight and
p
-equicontinuous in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.
.

Assume
\mathcal{F}
is bounded,
p
-tight and
p
-equicontinuous. In light of the
p
-tightness, for
\delta>0
let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be of finite measure and such that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\delta/2.

Theorem
3.7
reveals that
\mathcal{F}\hskip-2.15277pt|_{\Omega}
is precompact in
L^{p}(\Omega)
thus it is possible to cover
\mathcal{F}\hskip-2.15277pt|_{\Omega}
by finitely many balls of radii
\delta/2
centred at
g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. Let
\overline{g}_{i}
be the zero extension to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
g_{i}
. For
u\in\mathcal{F}
such that
u\in B_{\delta/2}(g_{i})
we have

\displaystyle\|u-\overline{g}_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|u-\overline{g}_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega)}+\|u-\overline{g}_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega)}+\|u-\overline{g}_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}<\delta.

It follows that

\mathcal{F}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B_{\delta}(\overline{g}_{i}).

Thus
\mathcal{F}
is totally bounded in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and hence precompact.

Conversely assume
\mathcal{F}
is precompact. Then it is evidently bounded. Let
\varepsilon>0
and let there exists
g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{F}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B_{\delta}(g_{i})
. The
p
-tightness and
p
-equicontinuity (by continuity of the shift) of the set
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}\}
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
implies that of
\mathcal{F}
within the estimates

\displaystyle\|\tau_{h}u-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\tau_{h}g_{i}-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+2\|u-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(0))}\leq\|u-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(0))}.

for
u\in\mathcal{F}
,
R>0
,
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i=1,2,\cdots,n
.
∎

The following theorem generalizes
[
Bre10
, Corollary 4.28]
or
[
ACS+14
, Theorem 6.23]
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1+\frac{1}{r}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a bounded subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then
\mathcal{F}*g
is relatively compact in
L^{r}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.
.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for each
u\in\mathcal{F}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
Young’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\|u*(\tau_{h}g-g)\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\tau_{h}g-g\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\tau_{h}g-g\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Together with the continuity of the shift, we get the
r
-equicontinuity of
\mathcal{F}*g
as follows:

\displaystyle\lim_{|h|\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|\tau_{h}(u*g)-u*g\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\lim_{|h|\to 0}\|\tau_{h}g-g\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0.

In case
r=\infty
we know from Young’s inequality (cf Theorem
3.2
) that
\mathcal{F}*g\subset C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that by Arzelá-Ascoli Theorem
3.5
we get that
\mathcal{F}*g
is relatively compact in
C(K)
for every compact subset
K
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that is to say
\mathcal{F}*g
is relatively compact in
L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
r<\infty
then the result readily follows from the Kolmogorov–Riesz–Fréchet Theorem
3.5
.
∎

8
Classical Sobolev spaces

In this section we go through a rudimentary review of Sobolev spaces. We refer the reader to
[
AF03
,
Maz13
]
for more discussions on the theory of Sobolev spaces. Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an open set. Let
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{1})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}
be a multiindex
and
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
(space of locally integrable function on
\Omega
). A function
g\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
usually denote by
g=\partial^{\alpha}u
is called weak derivative or distributional derivative of

u
of order
\alpha
, if

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\int_{\Omega}g(x)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all}\quad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega).

The uniqueness of the weak derivative
g=\partial^{\alpha}u
follows from the fundamental lemma of calculus of variation which asserts that in
L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
, only the null function
u=0
a.e. on
\Omega
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0\quad\text{for all}\quad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega).

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
. The space
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
is the equivalence classes of functions
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
whose distributional derivatives
D^{\alpha}u
, up to the other
m
, belong to
L^{p}(\Omega)
. In other words we have
W^{m,p}(\Omega):=\Big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega)~:D^{\alpha}u\in L^{p}(\Omega),\,|\alpha|\leq m\Big\}
.
The space
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
is furnished with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{m,p}(\Omega)}
defined by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{m,p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle:=\Big(\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}\|D^{\alpha}u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\qquad\text{for}\quad 1\leq p<\infty,

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{m,\infty}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle:=\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}\|D^{\alpha}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

The closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
is denoted by
W_{0}^{m,p}(\Omega)
.

Notation:
For
p=2
and
m=1
we shall write
H^{1}(\Omega)
(resp.
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
) in place of
W^{1,2}(\Omega)
(resp.
W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)
).

The space
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
is a separable Banach space (Hilbert space for
p=2
) for
1\leq p<\infty
and reflexive for
1<p<\infty
(c.f.
[
AF03
]
). The absence of reflexivity for the case
p=1
gives rise to another type of function space. When
m=1
this is known to be the space of bounded variation functions. Roughly speaking it is the space of elements in
L^{1}(\Omega)
whose derivatives in the sense of distributions are bounded Radon measures. This is formally defined as follows.

Definition 3.10
.

The space of functions with bounded variation on
\Omega
denoted by
BV(\Omega)
is defined as the space of functions
u\in L^{1}(\Omega)
such that
|u|_{BV(\Omega)}<\infty
(in which case
u
is said to has bounded variation on
\Omega
) where

(3.5)

\displaystyle|u|_{BV(\Omega)}:=\sup\left\{\int_{\Omega}u(x)\operatorname{div}\phi(x)\mathrm{d}x:~\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}),~\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1\right\}.

We will still denote the distributional derivative of a function
u\in BV(\Omega)
by
\nabla u
. Roughly speaking,
\nabla u=(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\cdots,\Lambda_{d})
can be seen as a vector valued Radon measure
9
9

9

That is any Borel measure which is inner and outer regular and finite on each Compact subset of
\Omega.
on
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{i}(x),\quad\text{for all }\quad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega),~~i=1,\cdots,d.

In particular, if
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
then
|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\leq d^{2}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}
that is
u\in BV(\Omega)
and we have
\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\,\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{i}(x)
. Indeed, note that for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
then
e\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\R^{d})
and
\operatorname{div}(e\varphi)=\nabla\varphi\cdot e.
By duality we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\nabla u\cdot e_{i}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sup_{\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1}\Big|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\,\operatorname{div}(e_{i}\varphi)(x)~\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq d^{2}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

Conversely, since
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
, the integration by part implies the following

\displaystyle|u|_{BV(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1}\Big|\int_{\Omega}\nabla u(x)\,\cdot\phi(x)~\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\sup_{\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)||\phi(x)|~\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}.

The quantity
|\nabla u|
may be regarded as a positive Radon measure whose value on an open set
U\subset\Omega
is
|\nabla u|(U)=|u|_{BV(U)}
. Notationally when no confusion may arise, it is often the case to write
\int_{\Omega}\,\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|
or
|\nabla u|(\Omega)
to synonymously denote the semi-norm
|u|_{BV(\Omega)}
also called the total variation of
|\nabla u|
. The space
BV(\Omega)
becomes a Banach space under the norm

\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}=\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+|u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

We recommend the books
[
EG15
,
Maz13
,
Zie12
]
for further details on the space of functions with bounded variation.

Remark 3.11
.

Given
1<p<\infty
by the reflexivity of
L^{p}(\Omega)
it is possible to show that for

\displaystyle|u|_{BV^{p}(\Omega)}:=\sup\left\{\int_{\Omega}u(x)\operatorname{div}\phi(x)\mathrm{d}x:~\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d}),~\|\phi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}y(\Omega)}\leq 1\right\},

the space
BV^{p}(\Omega)
of
L^{p}(\Omega)
such that
|u|_{BV^{p}(\Omega)}<\infty
coincides with the Sobolev space
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. We show this fact implicitly in the proof of Theorem
5.22
. Moreover, as for the
p=1
, one shows that
|u|_{BV^{p}(\Omega)}=\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
.
However, the inclusion
W^{1,1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow BV(\Omega)
is strict and continuous. For instance the weak derivative of the function
u(x)=\mathds{1}_{(0,1)}(x)-\mathds{1}_{(-1,0)}(x)
is
2\delta_{0}
(Dirac mass at the origin) whence it belongs to
BV(-1,1)
but is not in
W^{1,1}(-1,1)
. Actually, in this specific case the radon measure

|\nabla u|
equals
2\delta_{0}
and
|\nabla u|(-1,1)=|u|_{BV}(-1,1)=2
.

Later we shall need the following less common approximation result.

Theorem 3.12

(
[
EG15
, p.172]
,
[
AFP00
, Theorem 3.9]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open. Assume
u\in BV(\Omega)
. There exist functions
(u_{n})_{n}
in
BV(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that

\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}=|\nabla u|(\Omega)
.

Remark 3.13
.

Note that the above approximation theorem does not claim that
|u_{n}-u|_{BV(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
but rather implies that
\|u_{n}\|_{W^{1,1}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}
. Strictly speaking,
BV(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}(\Omega)
is not necessarily dense in
BV(\Omega)
.
On the other hand, if a function
u\in L^{1}(\Omega)
is regular enough say
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
then
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\asymp|u|_{BV(\Omega)}
. From this we find that
BV(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}(\Omega)=W^{1,1}(\Omega)\cap C^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

A separate aim of this chapter is to provide some strong connections between the aforementioned Sobolev spaces and the upcoming nonlocal spaces of higher interest. Most of these properties will be realized under some additional assumption on the geometry of
\Omega
. Especially if
\Omega
is an extension domain understood in the following sense.

Definition 3.14

(cf.
[
AF03
]
)
.

An open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called an
W^{m,p}
-extension (resp.
BV
-extension) domain if there exists a linear operator
E:W^{m,p}(\Omega)\to W^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(resp.
E:BV(\Omega)\to BV(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) and a constant
C:=C(\Omega,d)
depending only on the domain
\Omega
and the dimension
d
such that

\displaystyle Eu\mid_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=u\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\|Eu\|_{W^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{m,p}(\Omega)}\qquad\text{for all}\quad u\in W^{m,p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle(\text{resp.}\quad Eu\mid_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=u\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\|Eu\|_{BV(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}\qquad\qquad\text{for all}\quad u\in BV(\Omega)).

Extension domains are significant in applications and are necessary in order to extend certain embeddings theorems on function spaces defined on domains. Note that, bounded Lipschitz domains are both
W^{1,p}
-extension and
BV
-extension domains. The geometry characterization of extension domains has been extensively studied in the recent years. The
W^{1,p}
-extension property of an open set
\Omega
implies certain regularity of the boundary
\partial\Omega
. For instance, according to
[
HKT08
, Theorem 2]
, a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is necessarily is a
d
-set, i.e. satisfies the volume density condition: there exists
a constant
c>0
such that for all
x\in\partial\Omega
and
0<r<1
we have
|\Omega\cap B(x,r)|\geq cr^{d}
. Using the Lebesgue differentiation theorem, it is easy to show that the boundary of a
d
-set
\Omega
, has a zero Lebesgue measure, i.e.
|\partial\Omega|=0
. Therefore, given a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain
\Omega
, we have the following

(3.6)

\int_{\partial\Omega}|\nabla Eu(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x=0
for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
.

To the best of our knowledge, we do not know whether the geometric characterization (
3.6
) remains true for a
BV
-extension domain. However, we emphasize that
[
HKT08
, Lemma 2.4]
every
W^{1,1}
-extension is a
BV-
extension domain. Hence, we will require a
BV
-extension domain
\Omega
to satisfy the additional condition

(3.7)

|\nabla Eu|(\partial\Omega)=\int_{\R^{d}}\mathds{1}_{\partial\Omega}(x)\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla Eu|=0
for all
u\in BV(\Omega)
.

Some authors prefer
[
AFP00
]
to define a
BV
-extension domain together with the condition (
3.7
). Discussions on
BV-
extension domains can be found in
[
KMS10
,
Lah15
]
. Several references on extension domains for Sobolev spaces can be found in
[
Zho15
]
.

Trace spaces of Sobolev spaces

Next we recall the notion of traces spaces that are in a certain sense Sobolev spaces on the boundary of a smooth domain. Loosely speaking trace spaces are models of Sobolev spaces on lower dimensional smooth manifolds. Some cares are needed in order to properly build such spaces. Indeed for functions of Sobolev spaces the classical restriction to a lower dimensional manifold are meaningless. Because a smooth manifold of lower manifolds has Lebesgue a vanishing measure and Sobolev functions are solely defined in the almost everywhere sense. Notwithstanding, by means of functional methods one can generalize the concept of
restriction by introducing the notion of trace
. Let us commence by recalling the trace theorem for a smooth domain
\Omega
in the Hilbert setting.

Theorem 3.15

(Trace theorem,
[
HT08
,
Pon16
]
)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is bounded domain with
C^{2}-
boundary. Then there exists a bounded linear map (or trace operator)
\gamma_{0}:H^{1}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
such that
\operatorname{Ker}\gamma_{0}=H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, for every
v\in C(\overline{\Omega})
we have
\gamma_{0}v=v|_{\partial\Omega}
, and there is some constant
C>0
for which

\|\gamma_{0}v\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|v\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)},\qquad\forall~v\in H^{1}(\Omega).

Similarly the linear map
\gamma_{1}:C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap H^{1}(\Omega)\to C(\partial\Omega)\cap L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
defined by

\gamma_{1}v:=\gamma_{0}\circ\frac{\partial v}{\partial\nu}=\nabla v\cdot\mathrm{n}|_{\partial\Omega}

continuously extends to
H^{2}(\Omega)
and
\operatorname{Ker}\gamma_{0}\cap\operatorname{Ker}\gamma_{1}=H^{2}_{0}(\Omega).

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded smooth domain. The trace spaces
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and
H^{3/2}(\partial\Omega)
are respectively the range of the trace operators
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
that is

H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega):=\gamma_{0}(H^{1}(\Omega))\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad H^{3/2}(\partial\Omega):=\gamma_{1}(H^{2}(\Omega))

so that the mappings
\gamma_{0}:H^{1}(\Omega)\to H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and
\gamma_{1}:H^{2}(\Omega))\to H^{3/2}(\partial\Omega)
are onto and remain bounded respectively under the natural norms

\|v\|_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}:=\inf\{\|w\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}:w\in H^{1}(\Omega)~\hbox{and}~v=\gamma_{0}w\}

and

\|v\|_{H^{3/2}(\partial\Omega)}:=\inf\{\|w\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}:w\in H^{1}(\Omega)~\hbox{and}~v=\gamma_{1}w\}.

Usually
H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
denote the dual space of
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
. Clearly we have the following continuous embeddings:
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
. Note that, if
\Omega
is not smooth, then an intelligent way to define
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
is to set
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\equiv H^{1}(\Omega)/H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
that is, the quotient space of
H^{1}(\Omega)
by
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
via the equivalent relation
u\sim v
if and only if
u-v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
.

Let us also emphasize that for a domain with sufficiently smooth boundary, it is possible to define an intrinsic norm
[
Din96
,
Mik11
]
on the spaces
H^{s}(\partial\Omega)
with
s=1/2,3/2
as follows:

\|v\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\partial\Omega)}:=\int_{\partial\Omega}|u(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)+\iint\limits_{\partial\Omega\times\partial\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{d-1+2s}}\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(y).

Here
\mathrm{d}\sigma
represents the Lebesgue surface measure on
\partial\Omega
. This is an avant-gout
towards the introduction of the so called fractional Sobolev spaces. For the general case, where
s\in(0,1)
see
[
Gri11
, Section 1.3.3]
.

Remark 3.17
.

Roughly speaking the operator
\gamma_{0}
models the restriction of
H^{1}(\Omega)
functions on the
boundary
\partial\Omega
(indeed the classical restriction on
\partial\Omega
of such functions apriori does not make sense in general since
\partial\Omega
has zero Lebesgue measure as
d-1
-dimensional manifold). The operator
\gamma_{1}
can be seen as extension of the notion of the normal derivative on
H^{2}(\Omega)
. Meanwhile, one can prove that operators
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are not defined on
L^{2}(\Omega)
and
H^{1}(\Omega)
respectively. A modern treatise on trace spaces in general is included in
[
Pon16
]
.

9
Nonlocal Hilbert function spaces

In this section we introduce generalized Sobolev-Slobodeckij-like function spaces with respect to a symmetric Lévy measure
\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h
tailor made for
L^{2}
-theory of nonlocal elliptic complement value problems. We will extend this to
L^{p}
-spaces later. Our standing object is a function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
which satisfies the Lévy integrability condition, i.e.,
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},(1\wedge|h|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}h)
and is symmetric, i.e.,
\nu(h)=\nu(-h)
for all
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The function
\nu
then is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure. In case
\nu
is radial we adopt the convention by identifying
\nu
with its radial profile, i.e.,
\nu(h)=\nu(|h|),h\in\R^{d}.
Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open.

\bullet
We define the space
H_{\nu}(\Omega)
by,

\displaystyle H_{\nu}(\Omega)=\Big\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega):\,\,|u|^{2}_{H_{\nu}(\Omega)}:=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}\,.

equipped with the norm defined as follows

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{H_{\nu}(\Omega)}=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Note that we also have the following nice representation

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big]\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

\bullet
We also introduce the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is of highest interest in this work.

\displaystyle V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Big\{u:\R^{d}\to\R\text{ meas. }:\,\mathcal{E}(u,u):=\frac{1}{2}\!\!\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}\,.

Note that
(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}=(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})=(\Omega\times\Omega)\cup(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega)\cup(\Omega\times\Omega^{c})
. Thus, an equal simple representation of the form
\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u):=\frac{1}{2}\!\!\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\lor\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big]\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

\bullet
Assume
L
is the Lévy operator associated with the measure
\nu
. Define the new space
V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
by

\displaystyle V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})=\Big\{u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,\text{ $Lu$ exists weakly and }Lu\in L^{2}(\Omega)\,\Big\}\,.

Here the weak integrodifferentiability of
Lu
is understood in the sense of Definition
2.11
.

\bullet
Denote by
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of functions that vanish on the complement of
\Omega
i.e

\displaystyle V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~:~u=0~~\text{a.e. on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega\}\,.

Proposition 3.18
.

The space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be equally defined as follows

\displaystyle V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Big\{u:\R^{d}\to\R\text{ meas. }|\,|u|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}\,.

Moreover, for all
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
|u|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq 2|u|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

Proof.
.

A routine check, shows that we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\!\!\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\lor\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big]\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle|u|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)+\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big]\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

Clearly we have
\frac{1}{2}\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)+\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big]\leq\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\lor\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big]\leq 2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\big[\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)+\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big].

Thus the following comparison holds true

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\ \leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)

\displaystyle\leq 2\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

∎

Therefore, for some proofs the usage of the quadratic form
\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x
which is simpler to handle in place of
\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\!\!\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x
should not alarm the reader. In reality, as we can observe, the notation
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is to emphasize that the integral of the measurable map
(x,y)\mapsto\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)
performed over
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is finite.
Another possible suitable notation of the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
could be
H_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. But we shall use only the notation
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We shall visit some fundamental results in connection to the aforementioned spaces in wider context.

9.1
Natural embeddings of nonlocal energy spaces

Here we intend to answer the following questions:
(i)
What is the most suitable way to define a norm on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
?
(ii)
What are the possible ways to embed
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
into some
L^{2}
-space of functions defined on
\Omega^{c}
or on the whole
\R^{d}
? The answer to the latter question is of great interest for our framework as it is a cornerstone for the study of the Inhomogeneous Neumann and Robin complement value problems.

Before we start with our investigation, let us observe that under certain conditions on
\nu
and
\Omega
, there exists at least a natural norm on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let us recall the following.

Definition 3.19
.

A function
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is said to be the density of an unimodal Lévy measure if it is radial,
\nu\in L^{1}(1\land|h|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}h)
and almost decreasing, i.e., there is
c>0
such that
|y|\geq|x|
implies
\nu(y)\leq c\nu(x)
. We merely say that the function
\nu
is unimodal.

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}
be unimodal and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set. Assume
\Omega\subset B_{|\xi|/2}(0)
for some
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\nu(\xi)\neq 0
. In particular, if
\nu
is fully supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{2}(\Omega)
. Equivalently
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\Omega)=V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.
.

First, if
\Omega\subset B_{|\xi|/2}(0)
, then for all
x,y\in\Omega
we have
\nu(x-y)\geq c^{\prime}
with
c^{\prime}=c\nu(\xi)>0
. By Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}(|u(x)|-|u(y)|)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}|\Omega|\int\limits_{\Omega}\Big(|u(x)|-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}|u|$}\Big)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

This shows that the mean value
\fint_{\Omega}|u|
is finite. We conclude
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
because of

\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2\int_{\Omega}\Big(|u(x)|-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}|u|\Big)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+2|\Omega|\Big(\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}|u|$}\Big)^{2}\,.

∎

Therefore, when
\Omega
is bounded, under the assumptions of Proposition
3.20
it is natural to endow the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle:=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u,u)\asymp\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+|u|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Accordingly on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
it is convenient to define the corresponding inner product

\displaystyle\big(u,v\big)_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\big(u,v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u,v)~~\text{or}~~\big(u,v\big)_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\big(u,v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+[u,v]^{2}\,.

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle[u,v]_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

When the function
\nu
is bounded, e.g. in the case
\nu(h)=\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}}(h)
, the space
H_{\nu}(\Omega)

equals
L^{2}(\Omega)
. The same holds true if
\nu\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed in such situation if
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
then we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 4\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}u^{2}(x)\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 4\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{2}(x)\,\nu(h)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}h=4\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,.

Definition 3.21
.

From now on, for a general measure
\nu
with full support, even if
\Omega
is not bounded, we refer to the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\Omega)
equipped with the norm defined by

\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u,u).

The space
V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
shall be equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}=\|Lu\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u,u)=\|Lu\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Some authors find it convenient to work with space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
equipped with the norm

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle:=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\mathcal{E}(u,u).

Remark 3.22
.

The space
V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
can be understood as a nonlocal version of
H^{2}(\Omega)
. Indeed it is well-known from Caldéròn-Zygmund inequality that for a smooth domain
\Omega,

H^{2}(\Omega)=\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\,\,\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}
where
\Delta u
is understood in the distributional sense. Moreover
\|\cdot\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}\asymp\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\Delta\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
.

Clearly,
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for all
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that the embedding
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is continuous. Note that as in (
9.1
), for an integrable function
\nu
, the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
equals
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whereas,
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
reduces to the subspace of functions
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
with

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

This shows that in many situations the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
might be a little larger than
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Nevertheless, when
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
the normed spaces
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
,
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
and
\big(H_{\nu}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{H_{\nu}(\Omega)}\big)
are all equal and coincide in norms and will be denoted by
(H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
.

Theorem 3.23
.

Assume that
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\R
is a symmetric Lévy measure. The function spaces
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\R^{d}),{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
and
\big(H_{\nu}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{H_{\nu}(\Omega)}\big)
are separable Hilbert spaces.

In addition if
\nu
has full support in
\R^{d}
then the same is true for the space
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
and the space
\big(V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})}\big)

Proof.
.

For the spaces
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\R^{d}),{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
and
\big(H_{\nu}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}\big)
and
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
the statements are contained in Theorem
3.46
. Now assume
\nu
is fully supported and let
(u_{n})_{n}\subset V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Then
(u_{n})_{n}
and
(Lu_{n})_{n}
are Cauchy sequences in
\big(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
and
L^{2}(\Omega)
respectively. Let
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
\|Lu_{n}-g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. It remains to show that
Lu=g
distributionally which is straightforward. According to Definition
2.11
, since
\|Lu_{n}-g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, we have
Lu=g
. Indeed since each
Lu_{n}
is define in the weak sense, for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
K=\supp\varphi
we get

\displaystyle\int_{K}g(x)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{K}Lu_{n}(x)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{K}u_{n}(x)L\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{K}u(x)L\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

∎

The following lemma shows that it is possible to define certain norms on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
(with nice properties) which are equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
when the function
\nu
is unimodal.

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open
(
not necessarily bounded
)
such that
\Omega^{c}
has a
non-empty interior. Assume
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}
is unimodal and has full support. Then there exists an almost decreasing radial Radon measure
\widetilde{\nu}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
with full support and a constant
C>0
both depending only on
\nu,d
and
\Omega
such that the following assertions hold true.

1.

\widetilde{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})<\infty
.

2.

0<\widetilde{\nu}\leq C(1\land\nu)
a.e. Moreover, we have
\widetilde{\nu}\asymp 1\land\nu
if in addition
\nu
satisfies the doubling grow condition: there is a constant
\kappa>0
such that
\nu(h)\leq\kappa\nu(2h)
for all
|h|\geq 1
.

3.

We have the continuous embeddings

V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu}).

4.

On
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the norms
\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
are equivalent. Where,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{*2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{\#2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

Furthermore, if
\Omega
is bounded then the norms
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
are also equivalent.

Proof.
.

Assume
\nu
has full support. We take
R\geq 1
sufficiently large such that we have
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega|>0
and
|B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega^{c}|>0
. Set
\Omega(R)=B_{R}(0)\cap\Omega
(if
\Omega
is bounded take
R>1
large so that
\Omega\subset B_{R}(0)
, i.e.
\Omega(R)=\Omega
). In any case, for all
x\in\Omega(R)
and all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
|x-y|\leq R(1+|y|)
. The monotonicity condition on
\nu
implies
\nu(R(1+|y|))\leq c\nu(x-y)
. Set
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=\nu(R(1+|h|))
for
h\in\R^{d}
, where we abuse the notation and write
\nu(|y|)
instead of
\nu(y)
for
y\in\R^{d}
. Let us show that
\widetilde{\nu}
satisfies the desired conditions. Firstly, using the scaling and the translation invariance properties of the Lebesgue measure it is easy to show that
\widetilde{\nu}
also has full support. Note that
|h|\leq R(1+|h|)
and
R\leq R(1+|h|)
for all
h\in\R^{d}.
Whence,
0<\widetilde{\nu}\leq C(1\land\nu)
a.e. In addition let us assume that
\nu(h)\leq\kappa\nu(2h)
for all
|h|\geq 1
. Let
n\geq 1
be the unique integer such that
2^{n}\leq R<2^{n+1}
and consider
\delta=\tfrac{R}{2^{n+1}-R}\geq 1
. For this choice of
\delta
we have
R(1+|h|)\leq\dfrac{R|h|(\delta+1)}{\delta}=2^{n}|h|
whenever
|h|\geq\delta
or
|h|/\delta\geq 1
. This entails that
\widetilde{\nu}(h)\geq c\nu(2^{n}|h|)
and by assumption we get
\widetilde{\nu}(h)\geq c\kappa^{-n}\nu(|h|)
. Now, if
|h|\leq\delta
then
R(1+|h|)\leq R(\delta+1)
so that
\widetilde{\nu}(h)\geq c\nu(R(\delta+1))=C
. We have proved that
\widetilde{\nu}(h)\geq C(1\land\nu(h))
and hence
(ii)
is verified. Passing through polar coordinates, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(\R^{d})

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\nu(R(1+|h|))\,\mathrm{d}h=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(R(1+r))r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|R^{-1}\int_{R}^{\infty}\nu(r)\Big(\frac{r}{R}-1\Big)^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|R^{-d}\int_{R}^{\infty}\nu(r)r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=R^{-d}\int_{|h|\geq R}(1\land|h|^{2})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq R^{-d}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\,.

This proves
(i)
and hence
L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h)\subset L^{1}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h)
. We recall that
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a subspace of
L^{2}(\Omega)
endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Let
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
since we know that
\widetilde{\nu}(x)\leq C
and
\widetilde{\nu}(x)\leq c\nu(x-y)
for all
y\in\Omega(R)
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the following estimates hold

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}

\displaystyle(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1}\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1}\int_{\Omega(R)}u^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega(R)\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=C^{-1}\widetilde{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{-1}\iint\limits_{\Omega(R)\Omega^{c}}u^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega(R)\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq(C^{-1}\widetilde{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{-1}\land c^{-1})\iint\limits_{\Omega(R)\Omega^{c}}\Big[u^{2}(x)+(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\Big]\widetilde{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq(C^{-1}\widetilde{\nu}(\Omega^{c})^{-1}\land c^{-1})\frac{|\Omega(R)|}{2}\int\limits_{\Omega^{c}}u^{2}(y)\widetilde{\nu}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

The second and the last line imply that
u\in L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu})
thereby proving that
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h)
. Obviously the proof of
(iii)
is also complete. The first, second and the last line of the above estimates show that there exist two constants
C_{1}>0
and
C_{2}>0
depending only on
\Omega,\nu,R
and
d
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\geq C_{1}\|u\|^{\#}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\geq C_{2}\|u\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

Together with the trivial inequality
\|u\|^{\#}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|u\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, the norms
\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
turn out to be equivalent.

Moreover, if
\Omega
is bounded, then
R\leq R(1+|h|)\leq R(1+R)
for all
h\in\Omega
. The monotonicity of
\nu
yields
C^{-1}\leq\widetilde{\nu}(h)\leq C
for all
h\in\Omega
. Hence,
C^{-1}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega,\,\widetilde{\nu}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h)}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
which implies the equivalent of
\|\cdot\|^{\#}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
thereby proving the equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Part
(iv)
is proved.
∎

Example 3.25
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
consider
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-\alpha}
and one obtains
\widetilde{\nu}\asymp 1\land\nu
with
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=(1+|h|)^{-d-\alpha}
or
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=(1+|h|^{d+\alpha})^{-1}
. In the aforementioned case, the space
H_{\nu}(\Omega)
equals the classical Sobolev-Slobodeckij space
H^{\alpha/2}(\Omega)
. For the same choice of
\nu
we define
V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. So that we have
V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},(1+|h|)^{-d-\alpha})
.

In the special case where
\nu
is unimodal, the Lemma
3.24
provides several possible ways to define other norms on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. In particular we have the continuous embedding
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu})
.
Next we obtain similar results for a more general symmetric Lévy kernel
\nu
. We start with the following definition

Definition 3.26
.

Let
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be the density of a symmetric Lévy measure and
K\subset\R^{d}
be a measurable set such that
|K|>0
. We write
\nu_{K}
to denote the measurable function defined by
\nu_{K}:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
such that for
x\in\R^{d}
,

\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}\limits_{y\in K}\nu(x-y).

Remark 3.27
.

Since
\nu
is integrable away from the origin it has some decay at infinity. Typically, for
x\in\R^{d}
the infimum of
\nu(x-y)
is realized when
y
is far from
x.
