In some sense the function
\nu_{K}
destroys the singularity of
\nu
. A simple way to illustrate this is to consider the example where
\nu
is unimodal.

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
\nu
be the density of a unimodal Lévy measure with full support on
\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Then (i)
\nu_{B_{1}}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
, (ii)
\nu_{B_{1}}\leq c(1\land\nu)
and (iii) if in addition
\nu
satisfies the doubling growth condition:
\exists\,\,\kappa>0
such that
\nu(h)\leq\kappa\nu(2h)
for all
|h|\geq 1
then

\displaystyle\nu_{B_{1}}\asymp 1\land\nu\qquad\text{with}\qquad\nu_{B_{1}}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}\limits_{y\in B_{1}}\nu(x-y).

The same conclusions hold with
B_{1}
replaced by any other ball
B
. Furthermore for a ball
B\subset\Omega
we have

\displaystyle V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\nu_{B})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\nu_{B}).

Proof.
.

For
x\in B_{1}
there exists
x_{*}\in B_{1}
(diametrically opposite to
x
) such that
2\geq|x-x_{*}|=2-|x|\geq 1
. Since
\nu
is almost decreasing we have
c\nu(2)\leq\nu_{B_{1}}(x)\leq\nu(x-x_{*})\leq c^{-1}\nu(1)
. If
x\in\R^{d}\setminus B_{1}
there exists
x_{*}\in B_{1}
(diametrically opposite to
x
) such that
|x|\leq|x|+1=|x-x_{*}|
that is
c\nu_{B_{1}}(x)\leq c\nu(|x|)
for all
y\in B_{1}

|x-y|\leq|x|+1\leq 2|x|
which implies
c\nu(2|x|)\leq\nu_{B_{1}}(x)
.
We have
c\nu(|x|)\leq\nu_{B_{1}}(x)\leq c^{-1}\nu(|x|)
.

Observe that
\nu_{B_{1}}
is bounded on
B_{1}
and
\nu_{B_{1}}\nu
on
\R^{d}\setminus B_{1}
and
\nu
is integrable on
\R^{d}\setminus B_{1}
. Therefore,
\nu_{B_{1}}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
and thus
L^{2}(\R^{d},\nu_{B_{1}})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\nu_{B_{1}})
.
Further we have shown that
c\nu_{B_{1}}\leq(1\land\nu)
and
\nu_{B_{1}}(x)\geq c(1\land\nu(2|x|))
. If
\nu
satisfies
\nu(h)\leq\kappa\nu(2|h|)
for all
|h|\geq 1
then
\kappa^{-1}c(1\land\nu)\leq\nu_{B_{1}}\leq c^{-1}(1\land\nu)
. One reaches the similar conclusions for any other ball
B
. If
B\subset\Omega
we let
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
y\in B
then

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{2}\nu_{B}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2|u(y)|^{2}\|\nu_{B}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Integrating both sides with respect to the variable
y
over
B
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{2}\nu_{B}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{B}|u(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y+C\iint\limits_{K^{\prime}\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y+C\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

with
C=2|B|^{-1}(\|\nu_{B}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}+1)
. Hence
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\nu_{B})
since

\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\leq C\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

∎

Remark 3.29
.

(i)
The conclusions of Lemma
3.24
remain true with
\widetilde{\nu}
replaced by
\nu_{B}
for a ball
B\subset\Omega
.

(ii)
Under the doubling scaling condition on a unimodal kernel
\nu
, it is possible to analogously show that for any ball
B
we have
\nu_{B}\asymp 1\land\nu
. Hence for any compact set
K
with a non-empty interior we have
\nu_{K}\asymp 1\land\nu
. This means that almost all
\nu_{K}
are comparable. But this is not always the case in general. For instance consider
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d}e^{-|h|^{2}}
, then we claim that
\nu_{B_{1}}
and
\nu_{B_{2}}
are not comparable. Indeed assume there is a constant
C>0
such that
\nu_{B_{1}}(x)\leq c\nu_{B_{1}}(x)
for all
x\in\R^{d}
. Let
|x|\geq 4
then there are two points
x_{*}\in\partial B_{1}
and
x^{\prime}_{*}\in\partial B_{2}
(diametrically opposite to
x
) such that
|x-x_{*}|=|x|+1
,
|x-x^{\prime}_{*}|=|x|+2
and

\displaystyle\nu_{B_{1}}(x)=\nu(x-x_{*})=(|x|+1)^{-d}e^{-(1+|x|)^{2}}\quad\text{and }\quad\nu_{B_{2}}(x)=\nu(x-x^{\prime}_{*})=(|x|+2)^{-d}e^{-(2+|x|)^{2}}.

That
\nu_{B_{1}}(x)\leq C\nu_{B_{2}}(x)
implies
1\leq C\big(\frac{|x|+2}{|x|+1}\big)^{d}e^{-2|x|}\xrightarrow{|x|\to\infty}0
, which is impossible.

(iii)
Note that, although the class of the almost decreasing unimodal Lévy kernel is fairly large, there exists some radial Lévy kernels which are not almost decreasing . For example, for
\beta\in[-1,2)
define

\displaystyle\nu_{\beta}(h)=|h|^{-d-\beta}\,\,\rho(h)\text{ with }\,\,\rho(h):=\Big(\frac{2+\cos|h|}{3}\Big)^{|h|^{4}}.

Note that
\nu_{\beta}
is not almost decreasing since
\rho(2\pi n)=1
and
\rho(\pi(2n-1))=3^{-\pi^{4}(2n-1)^{4}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now if
\beta\in(0,2)
, it is clear that
\nu_{\beta}
is Lévy integrable since
\rho
is bounded. If
-1\leq\beta\leq 0
then
\nu_{\beta}
is also Lévy integrable since the map
r\mapsto\rho(r)
is in
L^{1}(\R)
.

The following result is more general and provides an alternative to the Lemma
3.24
when
\nu
is not a unimodal function.

Lemma 3.30
.

Assume
\nu
is a symmetric Lévy kernel and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set. For any measurable subset
K\subset\Omega
with positive measure
|K|>0
, consider the map
\nu_{K}:\Omega^{c}\to[0,\infty]
with

\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}\limits_{y\in K}\nu(x-y).

Then
\nu_{K}\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c})
and we have the continuous embeddings

V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}).

Proof.
.

Given that
|K|>0
, for a suitable
z\in K\subset\Omega
we have
\delta_{z}=\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)>0
and
\nu_{K}(x)\leq\nu(x-z)
for almost every
x\in\Omega^{c}
. As
\Omega^{c}\subset B^{c}_{\delta_{z}}(z)
by Lévy integrability of
\nu
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}\nu_{K}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{B^{c}_{\delta_{z}}(z)}\nu(x-z)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{B^{c}_{\delta_{z}}(0)}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

In particular,
\nu_{K}\in L^{1}(\Omega^{c})
. This implies that
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
. Let
y\in K^{\prime}\subset K
be measurable such that
0<|K^{\prime}|<\infty
. Proceeding as in the proof of Proposition
3.28
, one obtains the estimate
\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\leq C\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
with
C=2|K^{\prime}|^{-1}(\|\nu_{K}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega^{c})}+1)
.
∎

As we have demonstrated above, the main feature of the weight
\nu_{K}
consists of removing the singularity of
\nu
. Another way of defining such a weight is as follows.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be the density of a symmetric Lévy measure and
K\subset\R^{d}
be a measurable set such that
|K|>0
. We write
\mathring{\nu}_{K}
to denote the measurable function defined by
\mathring{\nu}_{K}:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
such that for
x\in\R^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathring{\nu}_{K}(x)=\int_{K}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Proposition 3.32
.

Assume that
\nu
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set. For any measurable set
S\subset\R^{d}
such that
0<|S|<\infty
we have
\mathring{\nu}_{S}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
. Let
K\subset\Omega
be measurable with
|K|>0
then the embedding
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{K})
is continuous. Moreover If
0<|K|<\infty
then the embeddings
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{K})
are continuous.

Proof.
.

The integrability of
\mathring{\nu}_{S}
is readily obtained as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}\mathring{\nu}_{S}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{S}\int_{\R^{d}}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=|S|\int_{\R^{d}}1\land\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq|S|\int_{B_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}h+|S|\int_{B_{1}^{c}}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

Let
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
K\subset\Omega
, we get the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{2}\mathring{\nu}_{K}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{K}\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)+u(y)|^{2}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2\|1\land\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\int_{\Omega}|u(y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y+2\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

∎

Let us look at the case where
\nu
is radial.

Proposition 3.33
.

Let
\nu
be the density of a unimodal Lévy measure with full support on
\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. In addition
\nu
satisfies the doubling growth condition, i.e.
\exists\,\,\kappa>0
such that
\nu(h)\leq\kappa\nu(2h)
for all
|h|\geq 1
then for any ball
B\subset\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\mathring{\nu}_{B}\asymp 1\land\nu\quad\text{with}\quad\mathring{\nu}_{B}(x)=\int_{B}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Furthermore, if
B\subset\Omega
then the embeddings
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{B})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{B})
are continuous.

Proof.
.

Since
\nu
is unimodal, let
c>0
be a constant such that
c\nu(y)\leq\nu(x)
if
|y|\geq|x|
.
Let
R\geq 1
be large enough such that
B\subset B_{R}
. We know from Lemma
3.24

(ii)
that there exists
\lambda>1

such that

\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\nu}(h)\leq 1\land\nu(h)\leq\lambda\widetilde{\nu}(h)\quad\text{ for all $h\in\R^{d}$},

where
\widetilde{\nu}(h)=\nu(R(1+|h|))
.

Assume
|x|\geq 4R
. For
y\in B
we have
|y|\leq R\leq 4R\leq|x|
and
thus
R(1+|x-y|)\leq 3R|x|
. We also have
|x-y|\geq|x|-|y|\geq\frac{|x|}{2}
. Hence, for each
y\in B
, we have
\frac{|x|}{2}\leq|x-y|\leq R(1+|x-y|)\leq 3R|x|
, which implies
c\nu(3R|x|)\leq\widetilde{\nu}(x-y)\leq c^{-1}\nu(\frac{|x|}{2}).
Since
\frac{|x|}{2}\geq 2R\geq 1
, the scaling condition implies that
\nu(\frac{|x|}{2})\leq\kappa\nu(|x)
and there exists a constant
\eta>0
such that
\eta\,\nu(|x|)\leq\nu(3R|x|)
.
It follows that

c\eta\,\nu(|x|)\leq\widetilde{\nu}(x-y)\leq c^{-1}\kappa\nu(|x|).

Assume
|x|\leq 4R
then for each
y\in B\subset B_{R}
we have
R\leq R(1+|x-y|)\leq 6R
. This implies that
c\nu(6R)\leq\widetilde{\nu}(x-y)\leq c^{-1}\nu(R)
for all
y\in B
.

Altogether, it follows that for some constant
C>1
and for all
y\in B
and all
x\in\R^{d}
we have

C^{-1}\,(1\land\nu(x))\leq\widetilde{\nu}(x-y)\leq C(1\land\nu(x)).

Finally, for all
y\in B
and all
x\in\R^{d}
we get

\lambda^{-1}C^{-1}\,(1\land\nu(x))\leq 1\land\nu(x-y)\leq\lambda C(1\land\nu(x)).

Integrating over
B
yields,
\mathring{\nu}_{B}\asymp 1\land\nu
since

\lambda^{-1}C^{-1}|B\,(1\land\nu(x))\leq\mathring{\nu}_{B}(x)\leq\lambda C(|B|1\land\nu(x)).

If
B\subset\Omega
then according to Proposition (
3.32
) the embeddings
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{B})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{B})
are continuous.
∎

Example 3.34
.

Assume
K
is a compact subset of
\Omega
then
\delta_{K}=\operatorname{dist}(K,\partial\Omega)>0
. For the particular choice
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-\alpha},~\alpha\in(0,2)
one has that
\nu_{K}(x)\asymp(1+|x|)^{-d-\alpha}\asymp 1\land\nu(x)
. Indeed, for fixed
x\in\Omega^{c}
and choose
y\in K
such that
\nu_{K}(x)=\nu(x-y)
. Let
R\geq 1
be large enough such that
K\subset B_{R}(0)
then
|x-y|\leq R(1+|x|)
that is
R^{-d-\alpha}(1+|x|)^{-d-\alpha}\leq\nu_{K}(x)
. We now show that upper bound in two cases. If
|x|\geq 4R
then
|x-y|\geq|x|-|y|\geq\frac{|x|}{2}+R\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+|x|)
so that
\nu_{K}(x)\leq 2^{d+\alpha}(1+|x|)^{-d-\alpha}
. If
|x|\leq 4R
since
\delta_{K}\leq|x-y|
we get
\nu_{K}(x)\leq(5R\delta_{K}^{-1})^{-d-\alpha}(1+|x|)^{-d-\alpha}
.

Analogously, we also have
\mathring{\nu}_{K}(x)\asymp(1+|x|)^{-d-\alpha}\asymp 1\land\nu(x)
.

From this example, the inclusion
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
turns out to be a variant of the inclusion
V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{d+\alpha}})
from
[
DK18
, Proposition 13]
.

Now we resume the conclusions we have obtained following Definition
3.26
and Definition
3.31
.

Theorem 3.35
.

Let
\nu
be the density of a symmetric Lévy measure. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open.
The following assertions are true.

1.

For a ball
B\subset\Omega
we have
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\nu_{B})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\nu_{B}).
If
\nu
is unimodal and satisfies the doubling growth condition then we have
\nu_{B}\asymp 1\land\nu
. The same holds for
\mathring{\nu}_{B}.

2.

For a measurable set
K\subset\Omega
with
|K|>0
we have
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}).
If
K
is compact then
\nu_{K}\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
and
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\nu_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\nu_{K}).

3.

For a measurable set
K\subset\Omega
with
|K|>0
we have
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{K})
. If
0<|K|<\infty
then
\mathring{\nu}_{K})\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
and
L^{2}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{K}).

9.2
Nonlocal trace spaces

The main goal of this part is to design an abstract notion of a trace space of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
similarly as one does for the space
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Due the nonlocal feature of the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the trace space assumes functions defined on the complement of
\Omega
. Indeed, one reason is that elements of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
are defined on the whole
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This contrasts with the local situation, where the trace space of
H^{1}(\Omega)
(with
\Omega
sufficiently smooth) consists of elements defined on the boundary
\partial\Omega
. In this part, we assume that
\nu
is fully supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is solely endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. We define
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, as the space of restrictions to
\Omega^{c}
of functions of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. That is,

\displaystyle T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})=\{v:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}~\text{meas.}~~\hbox{ such that }~~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}~~\hbox{with }~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\}.

We endow
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
with its natural norm,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}=\inf\{\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}:~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{ with }~~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\}.

As an immediate consequence of the definition of the space
\big(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}),\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\big)
, the trace map

\operatorname{Tr}:V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
with
u\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(u)=u\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
is continuous, onto, i.e.
\operatorname{Tr}(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))=T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
,
\ker(\operatorname{Tr})=V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and satisfies
\|\operatorname{Tr}(u)\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for all
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Theorem 3.36
.

The space is a separable Hilbert space with the scalar product

\displaystyle(u,v)_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\|u+v\|^{2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}-\|u\|^{2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}-\|v\|^{2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\right).

Proof.
.

Clearly the norm
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
verifies the parallelogram law since the norm
\|\cdot\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
does. It follows that
\big(\cdot,\cdot\big)_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
is a scalar product on
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
with associated norm
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
. We want to prove that
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
is complete under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
. Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
then up to extraction of a subsequence we may assume that

\displaystyle\|u_{n}-u_{n+1}\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}<\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\qquad\hbox{for all }~~~n\geq 1\,.

Let us fix
\overline{u}_{1}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
u_{1}=\overline{u}_{1}|_{\Omega^{c}}
. By definition of
\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
there exists
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
u_{1}-u_{2}=v|_{\Omega^{c}}
and

\|v\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+4^{-1}\,.
Letting
\overline{u}_{2}=v+\overline{u}_{1}
, i.e.
v=\overline{u}_{1}-\overline{u}_{2}
yields
\overline{u}_{2}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
u_{2}=\overline{u}_{2}|_{\Omega^{c}}
and hence

\|\overline{u}_{1}-\overline{u}_{2}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+4^{-1}<2^{-1}\,.
Repeating this process, one constructs a sequence of functions
\overline{u}_{n}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle\|\overline{u}_{n}-\overline{u}_{n+1}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\frac{1}{2^{n}}\qquad\hbox{for all}~~~n\geq 1,

which turns out to be a Cauchy sequence in the complete space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Let
\overline{u}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the limit of
(\overline{u}_{n})_{n}
.
Clearly, setting
u=\overline{u}\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
we have
\|u_{n}-u\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|\overline{u}_{n}-\overline{u}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Finally, the original Cauchy sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
converges up to extraction of subsequence to
u
and hence converges itself to
u
.
∎

It is natural to ask the following question: Can the space
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
be defined with an intrinsic scalar product preserving its initial Hilbert structure such that its trivial connection to
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is less evident? In the local situation, it is possible to define a scalar product on the space
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
when
\Omega
is a special Lipschitz domain (see
[
Din96
]
) . We give an answer to this question provided that some regularity and growth bound conditions on
\nu
are assumed. We follow
[
BGPR20
]
where the authors enforce the following assumptions.

A1
:
\nu
is unimodal, twice continuously differentiable and there is a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle|\nu^{\prime}(r)|,|\nu^{\prime\prime}(r)|\leq C_{1}\nu(r).

A2
: There exist constants
\beta\in(0,2)
and
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\nu(\lambda r)\leq C\lambda^{d-\beta}\nu(r),\,\,0<r,\lambda\leq 1,\quad\text{and}\quad\nu(r)\leq C\nu(r+1),\,\,\,r\geq 1.

Assume
\Omega^{c}
satisfies the volume density condition, i.e.
\exists\,c>0
such that
|\Omega^{c}\cap B_{r}(x)|\geq cr^{d}
for all
x\in\partial\Omega
and all
r>0
. Then under assumptions,
A1
and
A2

[
BGPR20
, Theorem 2.3]
reveals that for any
g\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, there exists a unique
u_{g}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
u_{g}|_{\Omega^{c}}=g
and

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}(g,g):=\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\!\!\big(g(x)-g(y)\big)^{2}\,\gamma_{\Omega}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\!\!\big(u_{g}(x)-u_{g}(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Furthermore,
u_{g}
satisfies the weak formulation

(3.10)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\!\!\big(u_{g}(x)-u_{g}(y)\big)(\phi(x)-\phi(y))\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=0,\qquad\text{for all}~~~\phi\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The interaction kernel
\gamma_{\Omega}(x,y)
is given via the Poisson kernel
P_{\Omega}(\cdot,\cdot)
of
\Omega
by the formula

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Omega}(x,y)=\int_{\Omega}P_{\Omega}(x,z)\nu(z-y)\,\mathrm{d}z\,\qquad x,y\in\Omega^{c}.

Furthermore, a precise formula for
u_{g}
in
\Omega
is given by the Poisson integral

\displaystyle u_{g}(x)=P_{\Omega}[g](x)=\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)P_{\Omega}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\qquad x\in\Omega\,.

Now let
v\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
by definition of
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
we have

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}:~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{with}~~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\Big\{\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x:~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{with}~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\Big\}+\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}(v,v)\,.

It is rather challenging to find or to estimate the quantity

\displaystyle\inf\Big\{\int_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x:~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{with}~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\Big\}.

Let us remind that our goal here is to explicitly define a norm which is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
and has less visible connection to
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. To this end, we bring into play the norm
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
defined as in Lemma
3.24
.

Proposition 3.37
.

Assume
\Omega
is open and bounded, such that
\Omega^{c}
satisfies the volume density condition. Assume
\nu
satisfies conditions
A1
and
A2
(in particular
\nu
is unimodal and has full support on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
). Let
\widetilde{\nu}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
be the measure and the norm respectively given in Lemma
3.24
. Then,

\displaystyle T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})=\Big\{v:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}~\text{meas.}~~\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}(v,v)=\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\!\!\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}\,\gamma_{\Omega}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}\,

and the norms
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
,
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{\dagger}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
are all equivalent. The norms are defined by

\displaystyle\|v\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|u\|^{*}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}:~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{with}~~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\}

\displaystyle\|v\|^{\dagger 2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{c}}v^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\!\!\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}\,\gamma_{\Omega}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.
.

The equivalence between
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
is an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.24

(iv)
. By (
3.9
) it follows that

\displaystyle\|v\|^{*2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|u\|^{*2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{with}~~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\Big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x~~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~~\hbox{with}~v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}\Big\}+\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}(v,v)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega^{c}}v^{2}(x)\widetilde{\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}(v,v).

We have
\|v\|^{\dagger}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|v\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
. Hence the identity
Id:(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}),\|\cdot\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})})\to(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}),\|\cdot\|^{\dagger}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})})
is continuous. The space
(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}),\|\cdot\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})})
is a Hilbert space since
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
are equivalent. Also, using Fatou’s lemma one can easily show that
(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}),\|\cdot\|^{\dagger}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})})
is a Hilbert space. As a consequence of the open mapping theorem the norms
\|\cdot\|^{\dagger}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
and
\|\cdot\|^{*}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
are equivalent.

∎

In the special case
\nu(h)=(2-\alpha)|h|^{-d-\alpha}
, noting
\delta_{z}=\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)
, the authors in
[
DK18
, Theorem 3]
claim that if
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
then

(3.11)

\displaystyle\iint\limits\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\frac{\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}}{(|x-y|+\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})^{-d-\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\,.

Conversely if (
3.11
) holds true for
v=g
on
\Omega^{c}
then there exists
u_{g}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
u_{g}|_{\Omega^{c}=g}
and

(3.12)

\displaystyle(2-\alpha)\iint\limits\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\frac{\big(u_{g}(x)-u_{g}(y)\big)^{2}}{(|x-y|+\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})^{-d-\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\asymp\iint\limits\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\frac{\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}}{(|x-y|+\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})^{-d-\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

with the constants independent of
g
and
u_{g}
. Therefore, it readily follows that

\displaystyle T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})=\Big\{v:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}~\text{meas.}~~\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\!\!\frac{\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}}{(|x-y|+\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})^{-d-\alpha}}\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}\,.

Actually it is not straightforward to conclude on the equivalence of norms in this case. In view of (
3.12
) we only have
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq C\|\cdot\|^{{}^{\prime}}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
with the norm

\displaystyle\|v\|^{{}^{\prime}2}_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}=\int_{\Omega^{c}}\frac{v^{2}(x)}{(1+|x|)^{d+\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}\frac{\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}}{(|x-y|+\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})^{-d-\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

Remark 3.38
.

Let us emphasize the nonlocal trace
\operatorname{Tr}
does not need any special construction via the
functional analysis and density argument. Since
\Omega^{c}
is still a d-dimensional manifold, it makes sense to consider the restriction of a measurable function on
\Omega^{c}.

Moreover no regularity on
\Omega
is required. Whereas in the local situation, the trace of a Sobolev function

u
on the boundary
\partial\Omega
requires the smoothness of both
u
and
\partial\Omega
.

Let us give an important result concerning the notion of nonlocal trace, thereby providing some analogies with the classical notion traces.

Proposition 3.39
.

Let
K\subset\Omega
be measurable with
|K|>0
. The following properties are true.

1.

The trace map
\operatorname{Tr}:V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
with
u\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(u)=u\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
is linear and continuous.

2.

The trace map
\operatorname{Tr}:V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mathring{\nu}_{K})
with
u\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(u)=u\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
is linear and continuous.

3.

In both cases we have
\ker(\operatorname{Tr})=V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\operatorname{Tr}(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))=T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
.

Proof.
.

This is a straightforward consequence of Theorem
3.35
.

∎

Remark 3.40
.

Let us observe that the interplay between ,
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
may be view as the nonlocal counterpart of that between
H^{1}(\Omega)
,
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),

H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
. Indeed, it is well known that if the classical trace operator
\gamma_{0}:H^{1}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
exists then we have

•

\gamma_{0}:H^{1}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
is linear and continuous.

•

\gamma_{0}(H^{1}(\Omega))=H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and
\ker(\gamma_{0})=H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
.

In this regard it is fair to view
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
respectively as the nonlocal replacement of
H^{1}(\Omega)
,
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),

H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
.

Proposition 3.41
.

Assume
\Omega
is a Lipschitz bounded domain. Then
T:H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\to T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
with
Tu=\operatorname{Tr}\circ E\circ\operatorname{Ext}u
is a linear operator bounded.

Here
E:H^{1}(\Omega)\to H^{1}(\R^{d})
is the Sobolev extension, i.e.
Eu|_{\Omega}=u
and
\|Eu\|_{H^{1}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}
for all
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. And
\operatorname{Ext}:H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\to H^{1}(\Omega)
is the trace-extension operator, i.e.
\gamma_{0}\circ\operatorname{Ext}u=u
and
\|\operatorname{Ext}u\|_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}
for all
u\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
.

Proof.
.

The proof is immediate.
∎

Proposition 3.42
.

Let
C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega^{c}})=C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})|_{\Omega^{c}}
be set of restrictions on
\Omega^{c}
of
C^{\infty}
functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with compact support. Then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega^{c}})
is dense in
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
.

Proof.
.

For
v\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
we write
v=u|_{\Omega^{c}}
with
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. According to Theorem
3.70
there exists
u_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0.
Put,
v_{n}=u_{n}|_{\Omega^{c}}
we get

\displaystyle\|v_{n}-v\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|u_{n}-u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

∎

Note the recent result on the nonlocal trace from
[
Von19
]
is rather restrictive compared to our existing results. Indeed therein the space
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mu)
appears to be the trace of

V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},m)
where
m(x)=\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)+\mu(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(x)
and
\mu(x)=\int_{\Omega}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y
for
x\in\Omega^{c}\,.
The corresponding extension operator defined from
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mu)
to
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},m)
is merely the extension operator by zero outside
\Omega
. The main defect of this approach is that the measure
m
is too singular across the boundary of
\Omega
. Another point is that our trace space
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
turns out to be larger than
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mu)
. In the sense that
L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mu)
is continuously embedded in
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. Indeed, for
v\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mu)
its zero extension
v_{0}=v\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}
belongs to
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
since we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\!\!\big(v_{0}(x)-v_{0}(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=2\int_{\Omega^{c}}v^{2}(x)\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

So that
v\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and the continuity is obtained as follows
\|v\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\leq\|v_{0}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\sqrt{2}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mu)}.

10
Nonlocal Sobolev-like spaces

An important role in our study is played by function spaces. We assume that
\Omega
an open subset of
\R^{d}
. Let us introduce generalized Sobolev-Slobodeckij-like spaces with respect to a symmetric
p
-Lévy measure
\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h
(
1\leq p<\infty
) We will show some strong connections with the classical Sobolev spaces. Our standing assumption is that
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
satisfies

(
J_{1}
)

\displaystyle\nu(-h)=\nu(h)~~\text{ for all $h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$ and}~~\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\,.

In case
\nu
is radial we adopt the convention by identifying
\nu
with its radial profile, i.e.
\nu(h)=\nu(|h|),h\in\R^{d}.
For several results
\nu
will be assumed to have full support. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open.

\bullet
We define the space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
as

\displaystyle W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)=\Big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,\,|u(x)-u(y)|\nu^{1/p}(x-y)\in L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega)\Big\}\,.

equipped with the norm defined as follows

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}=\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}\quad\text{with}\quad|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}:=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Note that norm
\|\cdot\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}
is equivalent to norm defined by

\displaystyle u\mapsto\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\delta\}}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all}~~~\delta>0.

Indeed it suffice to observe that letting
C_{\delta}=\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h>0

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\geq\delta\}}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{|x-y|\geq\delta}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y=C_{\delta}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

\bullet
We also introduce the space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
defined as follows

\displaystyle W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Big\{u:\R^{d}\to\R\text{ meas. }:\,\,\,|u(x)-u(y)|\nu^{1/p}(x-y)\in L^{p}(\Omega\times\R^{d})\Big\}\,.

The space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
shall be equipped with the following norm

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{with}\quad|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Likewise as above, the norm
\|\cdot\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is equivalent to the norm defined by

\displaystyle u\mapsto\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}\cap\{|x-y|\leq\delta\}}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all}~~~\delta>0.

Another possibility is to consider the norm

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|u\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

\bullet
The space
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of functions that vanish on the complement of
\Omega
i.e

\displaystyle W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})~:~u=0~~\text{a.e. on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega\}\,.

We set

\|u\|_{W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Remark 3.43
.

1.

For
p=2
the spaces
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega),W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
become the spaces
H_{\nu}(\Omega),V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
of Section
9
respectively.

2.

It is a little exercise to show that the investigations from Section
9
extend to the case
p\neq 2
.

3.

For
\Omega=\R^{d}
the spaces
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
all coincide. We shall denote by
W^{p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
or
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

if
p=2
.

4.

Obviously the norms
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
\|\cdot\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
agree on
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore,
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a closed subspace of
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

5.

Note that
h\mapsto|h|^{-d-sp}
belongs to
L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
if and only if
0<s<1
. In this case taking

\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
the space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
turns out to be the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
. The space
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
shall be denoted by
W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.

6.

Along the lines of Section
9
one can analogously establish that
u\in L^{p}(\R^{d},(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}\,\mathrm{d}h)
for any
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
provided that
|\Omega|>0
and
|\R^{d}\setminus\Omega|>0
.

7.

The integrability of the increments
|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)
over
\Omega\times\R^{d}
encodes certain regularity of the function
u
in
\Omega
and across the boundary of
\Omega
. We concretely deal with the regularity inside
\Omega
in Section
23
under certain conditions. To picture the regularity across the boundary, for the instance case
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
with
sp\geq 1
, let us assume
u
is continuous and
u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
. If
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
then

\displaystyle|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\geq 2\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|x-y|^{-d-sp}\,\mathrm{d}y=C\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)^{-sp}\,\mathrm{d}x.

For the case
sp\geq 1
, this implies that
u(x)\xrightarrow{\dist(x,\partial\Omega)\to 0}0
.

Here we highlight the reason why it is crucial to assume
\nu
to be of full support.

Proposition 3.44
.

Assume
\nu
has full support then
|u|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0
if and only if
u=c
a.e on
\R^{d}

(c\in\R)
. In particular,
(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})})
is a normed space. This conclusion may be false if
\nu
is not of full support.

Proof.
.

In the case
|u|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}=0,
there exists
N\subset\Omega
with
|N|=0
and for all
\Omega\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

Since
\nu(h)>0
a.e, for
a\in\Omega\setminus N
we have
u(a+h)=u(a)
for almost all
h\in\R^{d}
. That
u
is almost everywhere constant on
\R^{d}
.
If
\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}=0,
then
|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0
,
u
is constant almost everywhere and since
u=0
a.e on
\Omega
it follows that
u=0
a.e on
\R^{d}
. This enables
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
to be a norm on
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Next assume that
\Omega
is bounded and
\nu
has a compact support. Let
S=\R^{d}\setminus\big(\Omega\cup\operatorname{supp}\nu+\Omega\big)
and consider the function
u(x)=\mathds{1}_{S}(x)
. A routine verification shows that
\|u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0
but
u\neq 0
. This means that
(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})})
cannot be a normed space.
∎

It is noteworthy to mention that the assumption (

J
1

) is optimal in the sense of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.45
.

Assume that
\nu:\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is symmetric. The following assertions hold true.

1.

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
, then
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)=L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{p}(\R^{d})=L^{p}(\R^{d})
.

2.

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
and
\Omega
is bounded, then
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
and
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
contain all bounded Lipschitz functions.

3.

Assume
\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\infty
for all
\delta>0

(in particular
\nu\not\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
),
\nu
is radial and
\Omega
is connected. Then the only smooth functions contained in
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
are constants.

4.

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
and
\nu
is radial then there exists two constant
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
we have
C_{1}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\leq\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})}\leq C_{2}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}
.

Proof.
.

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d})
then
(i)
is obtained through the following estimate

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\big|u(x)-u(y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\nu(h)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}h=2^{p}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,.

For a bounded Lipschitz function
u
, there is a constant
C>0
such that
|u(x)-u(y)|\leq C(1\land|x-y|)
for all
x,y\in\R^{d}
. Hence it clearly follows that
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
and
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.
(ii)
is true.

Now assume
\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\infty
for all
\delta>0
and let
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Assume
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
, let
K\subset\Omega
be a compact and let
\delta>0
such that
K(\delta)=K+B_{\delta}(0)\subset\Omega
. Using the fundamental theorem of calculus and passing through polar coordinates yields

(3.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|u|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}&=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\geq\int_{K}\int_{|x-y|\leq\delta}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+t(y-x))\cdot\frac{(y-x)}{|y-x|}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|^{p}|x-y|^{p}\nu(x-y),\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{K(\delta)}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\nabla u(x)\cdot w\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)\int_{0}^{\delta}r^{p+d-1}\nu(r)\mathrm{d}r\\
&=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}\Big(\int_{K(\delta)}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\nabla u(x)\cdot w\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big).\end{split}

However, this is possible only if
\nabla u=0
since
\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=\infty
. Hence
u
must be a constant function. To prove
(iv)
, observe that, taking
K=\R^{d}
in (
3.13
) and noting that the Lebesgue measure is invariant by rotation we have

\displaystyle|u|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\geq|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\nabla u(x)\cdot w\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)

\displaystyle=K_{p,d}\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x:=C_{\delta}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}.

With
K_{p,d}=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)
(see Lemma
5.13
). From this, the estimate
C_{1}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\leq\|u\|_{W^{p}_{\nu}(\R^{d})}
follows. The reverse estimate is a direct consequence of the estimate (
3.14
) below.
∎

Let us now see some fundamental properties of the spaces under consideration.

Theorem 3.46
.

Assume that
\nu
satisfies the condition (

J
1

). The function spaces
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
and
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}\big)
are separable Banach (Hilbert for
p=2
) spaces and reflexive for
1<p<\infty
.

In addition, if
\nu
has full support in
\R^{d}
, then the same is true for the space
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
.

Proof.
.

It is not difficult to check that
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
are norms on
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
respectively. We know that
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is a norm on
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Now, let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
. It converges to some
u
in the topology of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and pointwise almost everywhere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
up to a subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
. Fix
k
large enough, the Fatou’s lemma implies

\displaystyle|u_{n_{k}}-u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\big|[u_{n_{k}}-u_{n_{\ell}}](x)-([u_{n_{k}}-u_{n_{\ell}}](y)\big|^{p}\,\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Since
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
is a Cauchy sequence, the right hand side is finite for any
k
and tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
. This implies
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
|u_{n_{k}}-u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0
. Finally,
u_{n}\to u
in
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore, the map
\mathcal{I}:W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{p}(\R^{d})\times L^{p}(\Omega\times\R^{d})
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(u)=\Big(u(x),(u(x)-u(y))\nu^{1/p}(x-y)\Big)

is an isometry. From its Banach structure, the space
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
, which can be identified with
\mathcal{I}\Big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\Big)
, is separable (and reflexive for
1<p<\infty
) as a closed subspace of the separable (and reflexive for
1<p<\infty
) space
L^{p}(\R^{d})\times L^{p}(\Omega\times\R^{d})
. Analogously, one can show that
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}\big)
is a separable Banach space.

It remains to prove that
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is a separable Banach space. Here we assume that
\nu
has full support on
\R^{d}
. Without loss of generality we assume
\nu(h)>0
for every
h\in\R^{d}
.
Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\big(W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
. Then there exist a subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
of
(u_{n})_{n}
, a function
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, a function
U\in L^{p}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and null sets
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{R}\subset\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

-

(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
,

-

(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
converges to
u
pointwise on
\Omega\setminus N
,

-

(U_{n_{k}})_{k}
converges to
U
in
L^{p}(\Omega\times\R^{d})
,

-

(U_{n_{k}})_{k}
converges to
U
pointwise on
(\Omega\times\R^{d})\setminus\mathcal{R}
,

where
U_{n}(x,y)=(u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y))\nu^{1/p}(x-y)
. For
(x,y)\in(\Omega\times\R^{d})\setminus\mathcal{R^{\prime}}
with
x\neq y
where
\mathcal{R^{\prime}}=\mathcal{R}\cup(N\times\emptyset)
,

\displaystyle u_{n_{k}}(y)=u_{n_{k}}(x)-U_{n_{k}}(x,y)/\nu^{1/p}(x-y)\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}u(x)-U(x,y)/\nu^{1/p}(x-y)

Finally,
U(x,y)=(u(x)-u(y))\nu^{1/p}(x-y)\in L^{p}(\Omega\times\R^{d})
so that
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We easily conclude
\|u_{n}-u\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
which proves completeness.

∎

Actually the spaces
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
(just like the Sobolev space
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
) are refinement of
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Next we highlight some strong connections with the classical Sobolev spaces
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
) (
1\leq p<\infty
). To start, let us point out some useful estimates. First of all observe that for
h\in\R^{d}
we have
\|\tau_{h}u-u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. On the other hand since smooth functions of compact support are dense in
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
, using the fundamental theorem of calculus along with Jensen’s inequality we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x+h)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\R^{d}}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\Big|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq|h|^{p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}.

Therefore, the following estimate holds true for all for
1\leq p<\infty

(3.14)

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x+h)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}(1\land|h|^{p})\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})},\quad\text{for every}\quad u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d}),~\text{and}~h\in\R^{d}.

Next, since by Theorem
3.12
the
BV
-norm of an element in
BV(\R^{d})
can be approximated by the
W^{1,1}
-norms of elements in
W^{1,1}(\R^{d})
, we easily find the following analogous estimate

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x+h)-u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2(1\land|h|)\|u\|_{BV(\R^{d})},\quad\text{for every}\quad u\in BV(\R^{d})~\text{and}~h\in\R^{d}.

Lemma 3.47
.

Let
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
be a nonnegative function with
1\leq p<\infty
. Let
\Omega
be a
W^{1,p}
-extension (in particular a Lipschitz domain) open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there is a constant
C=C(\Omega,p,d)
depending only
\Omega

p
and
d
such that for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\leq C\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})}.

Proof.
.

Since
\Omega
is an extension domain, let
\overline{u}
be an
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
-extension of
u
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
First of all recall (
3.14
) that
\|\tau_{h}\overline{u}-\overline{u}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2^{p}(1\land|h|^{p})\|\overline{u}\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for all
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Hence using Fubini’s theorem yields

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\overline{u}(x+h)-\overline{u}(x)|^{p}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\overline{u}(x+h)-\overline{u}(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\|\overline{u}\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2^{p}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|^{p})}.

∎

For the case
p=1
the above result still holds true with
W^{1,1}(\Omega)
replaced by the larger space
BV(\Omega)
.

Lemma 3.48
.

If
\nu\in L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|)
and
\Omega
is a
BV
-extension open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
there exists a constant
C=C(\Omega,d)>0
such that for all
u\in BV(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\leq C\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|)}.

Proof.
.

Consider
\overline{u}\in BV(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be the extension on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of a function
u\in BV(\Omega)
, i.e.
\|\overline{u}\|_{BV(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}
with constant
C>0
independent of
u
. We know from Theorem
3.12
that there is
(u_{n})_{n}
a sequence of functions of
W^{1,1}(\R^{d})
converging to
\overline{u}
in
L^{1}(\R^{d})
and such that
\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}|\overline{u}|_{BV(\R^{d})}
. This implies
\|u_{n}\|_{W^{1,1}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|\overline{u}\|_{BV(\R^{d})}
. This together with Lemma
3.47
and Fatou’s lemma, yield

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\leq\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|\overline{u}(x)-\overline{u}(y)|\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\leq 2\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{W^{1,1}(\R^{d})}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|)}

\displaystyle=2\|\overline{u}\|_{BV(\R^{d})}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|)}\leq C\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}\|\nu\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d},1\land|h|)}.

Willingly, one could proceed as in the Lemma
3.47
by means of the inequality (
3.15
).

∎

It is noteworthy to emphasize that Lemma
3.47
(respectively Lemma
3.48
) does not hold true if
\Omega
is not an extension domain, see the Counterexample
3.53
. As a result we have the following.

Theorem 3.49
.

If
\Omega
is an
W^{1,p}
-extension (resp.
BV
-extension) domain, then the following embedding is continuous

\displaystyle W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)\,\quad(\text{resp. for $p=1$}\quad BV(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega)\,).

The above embeddings fail if
\Omega
is not an extension domain (see the counterexample
3.53
).

Proof.
.

Let
\overline{u}\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be an extension of a function
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
with
\|\overline{u}\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}
for a constant
C
depending only on
\Omega
and
d
. Within the estimate (
3.14
) we easily get the continuous embedding
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)\,
.
Analogously using Lemma

3.48
one can also establish that
BV(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega)\,
. If
\Omega
is not an extension domain the counterexample
3.53
shows that the results do not always hold.
∎

Let us collect some trivial embeddings also involving the classical Sobolev spaces. Note the norm
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is not excluded in the following.

\bullet
It springs from (
3.14
) that the following continuous embeddings hold true:

\displaystyle W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

\bullet
We obviously have the following continuous embeddings

\displaystyle W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)\,.

\bullet
Let
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
be the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega))}
. Note that- the zero extension to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of any function in
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
belongs to
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Hence, using (
3.14
) we also have the following continuous embeddings:

\displaystyle W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The embeddings
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
and
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
follow from the fact that for all
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x+2\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

It is worth noticing that not every function
u\in H_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
has its zero extension in
H_{\nu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed for this to hold, one would need that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega^{c}}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

This is not always true because the measure
\nu
might be very singular at the origin. This purely nonlocal effect contrasts with the local function space
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
whose elements can be isometrically extended by zero on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as functions of
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This also shows that for some appropriate domain
\Omega
and for some appropriate measure
\nu
e.g.
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-\alpha}
the spaces
W_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
and
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
are strictly different although they both possess
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
as dense subspace.

Proposition 3.50
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open.

1.

Let
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
satisfying (

J
1

). Assume that there exist two constants
r>0
and
k>0
such that if
|h|\leq r
we have

\nu_{1}(h)\leq k\nu_{2}(h).

Then the following embeddings are continuous

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu_{2}}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu_{1}}(\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad W^{p}_{\nu_{2}}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu_{1}}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

2.

If
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{2}
then we have the continuous embeddings

\displaystyle W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega_{2})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega_{2}|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu}(\Omega_{1}|\R^{d}).

3.

For a ball
B\subset\Omega
we have the continuous embeddings

\displaystyle W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\nu_{B})\quad\text{and}\quad W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},\mathring{\nu}_{B}),\

where for
x\in\R^{d}
we let

\displaystyle\nu_{B}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in B}\nu(x-y)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\nu}_{B}(x)=\int_{B}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Proof.
.

Note that
(ii)
is obvious and for the proof of
(iii)
we use analogous methods as in Section
9
. Putting
C_{\delta}=\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{1}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h
, then for
u\in W^{p}_{\nu_{2}}(\Omega)
we get

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{1}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}C_{\delta}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\delta\}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{1}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}C_{\delta}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+k\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y||\leq\delta\}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{2}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}(k+1)C_{\delta}\|u\|^{p}_{W^{p}_{\nu_{2}}(\Omega)}

From this it follows that
W^{p}_{\nu_{2}}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu_{1}}(\Omega)
and likewise

W^{p}_{\nu_{2}}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{p}_{\nu_{1}}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

∎

In light of Proposition
3.50
it is legitimate to formulate the following definition.

Definition 3.51
.

Given two functions
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
satisfying (

J
1

) we shall say that
\nu_{2}
is regular than
\nu_{1}
there exist two constants
r_{0},\kappa>0
such that for all
h\in\R^{d}
with
|h|\leq r_{0}
one has
\nu_{1}(h)\leq\kappa\nu_{2}(h)
.

This definition is not fortuitous. In some sense it means that
\nu_{2}
is likely to be more singular than
\nu_{1}
near the vicinity of the original. Should the singularity of
\nu_{2}
be higher, more regularity of a function
u
is required for the integrability of the increment
(x,y)\mapsto(u(x)-u(y))\nu_{2}^{1/p}(x-y)
. A simple example is given as follows.

Example 3.52
.

For
0<s<s^{\prime}<1
we have
W^{s^{\prime},p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{s,p}(\Omega)
and
W^{s^{\prime},p}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. Indeed, this follows from
(i)
since for every
|h|\leq 1
we have
|h|^{-d-sp}\leq|h|^{-d-s^{\prime}p}
and hence it suffices to take
\nu_{1}(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
,
\nu_{2}(h)=|h|^{-d-s^{\prime}p}
.
Moreover we have the continuous embedding
W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\R^{d},(1+|h|)^{-d-sp}\,\mathrm{d}h).
Note that the norm of
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
can be rescaled so that in the limiting case
s=1
,
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
is precisely
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
whose functions are more regular.

Counterexample 3.53
.

For a simple instance, in one dimension, consider
\Omega=(-1,0)\cup(0,1)
and put
u(x)=-1
if
x\in(-1,0)
and
u(x)=1
if
x\in(0,1)
. Clearly, we have
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
for all
1\leq p<\infty
with
\nabla u=0
. However,
u
does not belong to any of the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
provided that
s\geq 1/p
. Recall that here we have
\nu(h)=|h|^{-1-sp}
. Indeed, since
s\geq 1/p

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{1+sp}}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y=2^{p+1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{0}\frac{\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{1+sp}}=2^{p+1}\int_{0}^{1}x^{-sp}+(1+x)^{-sp}\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\infty.

Moreover,
\Omega=(-1,0)\cup(0,1)
is not a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain. Indeed, if
\overline{u}\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
is an extension of
u
defined as above then in particular we would have
\overline{u}\in W^{1,p}(-1,1)
and
\overline{u}=u
on
\Omega=(-1,0)\cup(0,1)
. The distributional derivative of
\overline{u}
on
(-1,1)
is
\nabla\overline{u}=2\delta_{0}
(where
\delta_{0}
stands for the Dirac mass at the origin). Hence
\nabla\overline{u}
is not a function which contradicts the fact that
\overline{u}\in W^{1,p}(\R)
.

Next we prove that this example persists in the higher dimensional space
d\geq 2
.
Let
B^{+}_{1}(0)=B_{1}(0)\cap\{(x^{\prime},x_{d})\in\R^{d}:~x_{d}>0\}
and
B^{-}_{1}(0)=B_{1}(0)\cap\{(x^{\prime},x_{d})\in\R^{d}:~x_{d}<0\}
. Put
\Omega=B_{1}(0)\setminus\{(x^{\prime},x_{d})\in\R^{d}:~x_{d}=0\}=B^{+}_{1}(0)\cup B^{-}_{1}(0)
and
define the function

u(x)=\mathds{1}_{B^{+}_{1}(0)}(x)-\mathds{1}_{B^{-}_{1}(0)}(x)
, i.e.
u(x)=1
if
x\in B^{+}_{1}(0)
and
u(x)=-1
if
x\in B^{-}_{1}(0)
. Obviously we have
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
for all
1\leq p<\infty
with
\nabla u=0
. Further following the discussion above, one can check that
u
does not have any extension to the whole space. Hence
\Omega
cannot be an extension domain. On the other hand
u
does not belong to any of the fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
provided that
s\geq 1/p
. Recall that here we have
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
. Indeed, assume
s\geq 1/p
. Since integrals disregard sets of zero measure, we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y=2^{p+1}\int_{B^{+}_{1}(0)}\int_{B^{-}_{1}(0)}\frac{\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}=2^{p+1}\int_{B^{+}_{1}(0)}\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.91663pt\int\limits_{B_{1}(x)\cap\{h_{d}>x_{d}\}}\hskip-12.91663pt|h|^{-d-sp}\,\,\mathrm{d}h=\infty.

We justify this conclusion as follows. It is easy to show that
D^{\prime}\subset D
where

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\{(x,h)\in\R^{d}\times\R^{d}:x\in B^{+}_{1}(0),~~h\in B^{+}_{1}(x)\cap\{h_{d}>x_{d}\}\}

\displaystyle D^{\prime}

\displaystyle=B^{+}_{1/2}(0)\times\{(h^{\prime},h_{d})\in\R^{d}:~|h^{\prime}|<1/4,~~x_{d}<h_{d}<1/4\}.

Also note that
\{(x^{\prime},x_{d})\in\R^{d}:~|x^{\prime}|<1/4,~~0<x_{d}<1/4\}\subset B^{+}_{1/2}(0)
. Using the change of variables
h^{\prime}=h_{d}z^{\prime}
so that
\,\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}=h_{d}^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}
then since
s\geq 1/p
we get the following

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=2^{p+1}\iint_{D}|h|^{-d-sp}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}h\geq 2^{p+1}\iint_{D^{\prime}}|h|^{-d-sp}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=2^{p+1}\int\limits_{B^{+}_{1/2}(0)}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{x_{d}}^{1/4}\,\mathrm{d}h_{d}\int\limits_{|h^{\prime}|<1/4}\hskip-8.61108pt(|h^{\prime}|^{2}+h_{d}^{2})^{-(d+sp)/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2^{p+1}\int\limits_{B^{+}_{1/2}(0)}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{x_{d}}^{1/4}h_{d}^{-1-sp}\,\mathrm{d}h_{d}\int\limits_{|h^{\prime}|<1/4}\hskip-8.61108pt(1+|z^{\prime}|^{2})^{-(d+sp)/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=C_{s}\int\limits_{B^{+}_{1/2}(0)}(x^{-sp}_{d}-4^{-sp})\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{with}\quad C_{s}=2^{p+1}\int\limits_{|z^{\prime}|<1/4}\hskip-8.61108pt(1+|z^{\prime}|^{2})^{-(d+sp)/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq C_{s}\int_{0}^{1/4}(x^{-sp}_{d}-4^{-sp})\,\mathrm{d}x_{d}\int\limits_{|x^{\prime}|<1/4}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=\infty.

11
Fractional Sobolev spaces

Let us first observe that for
s\in\R
,
\nu:h\mapsto|h|^{-d-sp}
satisfies (

J
1

), i.e. belongs to
L^{p}(\R^{d},(1\land|h|^{p}))
if and only if
s\in(0,1)
. In this case the spaces
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega),W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega),W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
are respectively denoted by
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
,
W^{s,p}_{0}(\Omega)
,
W^{s,p}_{*}(\Omega)
and
W^{s,p}(\Omega|\R^{d})
.
The spaces
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
and
W^{s,p}_{0}(\Omega)
are recognized as the usual fractional Sobolev spaces. They are often called
Aronszajn, Gagliardo or Slobodeckij
spaces
[
Aro55
,
Gag61
,
Slo58
]
after the names of those who introduced them simultaneously. Those spaces are of particular interest in their own right and have been extensively studied throughout the literature in recent years. Rigorous treatises on fractional Sobolev spaces can be found in
[
Gri11
,
Maz13
]
.
See
[
NPV12
]
for a treatment of Sobolev spaces of fractional order. The terminologies
fractional
is on one hand owed to the fact that for on a smooth domain the space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
can be realized as the
s
-interpolation of
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
On the other hand the spaces
W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\Omega)
(with
\partial\Omega
viewed as an open manifold of
\R^{d-1}
) is the trace space of
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Precisely there exists a bounded operator
T:W^{1,p}(\Omega)\to W^{1-\frac{1}{p},p}(\partial\Omega)
such that if
u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\cap W^{1,p}(\Omega)
we have
Tu=u\mid_{\partial\Omega}
, i.e.
Tu=u
on
\partial\Omega
).
The existence of such an operator was proven by Emilio Gagliardo around 1957, a modern treatment of this result can be found in
[
Pon16
]
. Next we relate the fractional Sobolev spaces to the interpolation of

L^{p}
and
W^{1,p}
.

Theorem 3.54
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
,
1\leq p<\infty
. There is a constant
\theta(s,d,p)>0
such that for all
u\in W^{s,p}(\R^{d})
the following log-convex inequality (interpolation estimate) holds

(3.16)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{s,p}(\R^{d})}\leq\theta(s,d,p)\|u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}^{1-s}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}^{s}.

With
1\leq\theta(s,d,p)<C(s(1-s))^{-1/p}
where the constant
C
does not depend on
s
.

If
\Omega
is a
W^{1,p}
-extension domain there is
C>0
also depending on
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}\leq C(d,p,\Omega)(s(1-s))^{-1/p}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{1-s}\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}^{s}\quad\text{for all $u\in W^{s,p}(\Omega)$}.

Proof.
.

Assume
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
and let
r>0
. We have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\int\limits_{|h|\geq r}\frac{|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}}{|h|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{|h|\geq r}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{-d-sp}\,\mathrm{d}h=2^{p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\frac{r^{-sp}}{sp}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}.

Furthermore, using Fubini’s theorem we get

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\int\limits_{|h|<r}\frac{|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}}{|h|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|h|<r}\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}\big|\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\,\mathrm{d}t\big|^{p}|h|^{-d-sp}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\big|\nabla u(x)\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{|h|<r}|h|^{-d-(1-s)p}\,\mathrm{d}h=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\frac{r^{p(1-s)}p}{p(1-s)}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}.

Adding the two inequalities for
10
10

10

This value is obtained after solving for
r,

2^{p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\frac{r^{-sp}}{sp}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\frac{r^{p(1-s)}p}{p(1-s)}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}.

r=\Big(\tfrac{1-s}{s}\Big)^{1/p}\tfrac{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}}{\|u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}}
and letting
\theta^{p}(s,d,p)=2^{sp}\big(1+\tfrac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{ps^{1-s}(1-s)^{s}}\big)
yields

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+|u|^{p}_{W^{s,p}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{ps^{1-s}(1-s)^{s}}\|u\|^{p(1-s)}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\|\nabla u\|^{sp}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle 2^{-sp}\theta^{p}(s,d,p)\|u\|^{p(1-s)}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\big(\|u\|^{sp}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}+\|\nabla u\|^{sp}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\theta(s,d,p)\|u\|^{1-s}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\|u\|^{s}_{W{1,p}(\R^{d})}\Big]^{p}\,.

Note that
e^{-1/e}\leq t^{t}\leq 1
for
t\in(0,1)
hence
\theta(s,d,p)\leq C(s(1-s))^{-1/p}
. The claim is proved.
∎

We observe that (
3.16
) is sharp in the sense that tending
s\to 1
(resp.
s\to 0^{+}
) leads to the obvious inequality
\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}
( resp.
\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\leq C\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}
). We discuss this widely in Section
23
.
In addition, the log-convex inequality (
3.16
) somehow encodes an interpolation between
L^{p}
and
W^{1,p}
whose the resulting interpolation space is
W^{s,p}
. Truly speaking, the fractional Sobolev space results from the so called
real interpolation.
A concrete elaboration of this assertion can be found in
[
Tri95
,
BL12
]
.

Next we derive the fractional counterpart of the famous Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev embedding theorem. The spectacularly amazing elementary proof presented here is apparently due to Haim Brezis
[
Pon16
, Proposition 15.5]
from a personal communication. It is important to highlight that earlier proofs of this theorem exist in the literature as well. For instance another lengthy proof also using basic analysis tools is well incorporated in
[
NPV12
, Theorem 6.5]
. See also
[
CT04
]
where the inequality is established with the best constant for
p=2
.

Theorem 3.55

(
Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev
)
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
1\leq p<\frac{d}{s}
. For all
u\in L^{p^{*}_{s}}(\R^{d})
,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p^{*}_{s}}(\R^{d})}\leq 2^{p^{*}_{s}/p}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p-s/d}\Big(\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{-d-sp}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}.

Here
\dfrac{1}{p^{*}_{s}}=\dfrac{1}{p}-\dfrac{s}{d}>0
is the so-called fractional critical exponent or fractional Sobolev conjugate. In particular, the embedding
W^{s,p}(\R^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p^{*}_{s}}(\R^{d})
is continuous.

Proof.
.

First we fix
x\in\R^{d}
and
r>0
.
Integrating the inequality
|u(x)|\leq|u(y)|+|u(x)-u(y)|
over
y\in B_{r}(x)
and using Jensen’s inequality gives

\displaystyle|u(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\fint_{B_{r}(x)}|u(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y+\fint_{B_{r}(x)}|u(x)-u(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\fint_{B_{r}(x)}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p^{*}_{s}}+\Big(\fint_{B_{r}(x)}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\fint_{B_{r}(x)}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p^{*}_{s}}+\Big(r^{d+sp}\fint_{B_{r}(x)}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=r^{-d/p^{*}_{s}}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{B_{r}(x)}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p^{*}_{s}}+r^{s}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p}\Big(\int\limits_{B_{r}(x)}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq r^{-d/p^{*}_{s}}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p^{*}_{s}}+r^{s}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}.

Next solving the following equation for
r
,

r^{-d/p^{*}_{s}}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p^{*}_{s}}=r^{s}|B_{1}(0)|^{-1/p}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}

gives

\displaystyle r(x)=r=|B_{1}(0)|^{1/d-p/dp^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{p/dp^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{-1/d}.

Substituting this specific
r(x)
in the preceding estimate leads to

\displaystyle|u(x)|^{p^{*}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p^{*}_{s}}r^{-d}(x)|B_{1}(0)|^{-1}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)

\displaystyle=2^{p^{*}_{s}}|B_{1}(0)|^{-2+p/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1-p/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big).

Integrating both sides with respect to the variable
x
gives

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(x)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p^{*}_{s}}|B_{1}(0)|^{-2+p/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\int\limits_{\R^{d}}|u(y)|^{p^{*}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1-p/p^{*}_{s}}\Big(\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big).

Cancellation on both sides provides the desired estimate.

∎

It is interesting to know what could be the suitable definition of fractional Sobolev space
W^{s,p}
for
s\geq 1
. It turns out that the definition of
|\cdot|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}
cannot be extended to the situation where
s\geq 1
. This is justified by the following result.

Proposition 3.56
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open and let
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
u\neq 0
. Then for
1\leq p<\infty

\displaystyle|u|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}=\infty\quad\text{for all $s\geq 1$ and }\quad\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}|u|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}=\infty.

Proof.
.

Let
K\subset\Omega
be a compact and let
\delta>0
such that
K(\delta)=K+B_{\delta}(0)\subset\Omega
. Since
u\in C^{2}_{c}(\Omega)
, by mimicking
3.13
we find that

\displaystyle|u|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{K(\delta)}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\nabla u(x)\cdot w\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)\int_{0}^{\delta}r^{(1-s)p-1}\mathrm{d}r.

The claim follows since for all
s\geq 1
,
\int_{0}^{\delta}r^{(1-s)p-1}\mathrm{d}r=\infty
and we also have

\int_{0}^{\delta}r^{(1-s)p-1}=\frac{\delta^{(1-s)p}}{(1-s)p}\xrightarrow{s\to 1^{-}}\infty.

∎

On a smooth domain
\Omega
the spaces
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
with
0<s<1
can be realized as the interpolation space between
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Therefore it is interesting to know how close is the space
\|\cdot\|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}
to
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
as
s\to 0
and to
\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}
as
s\to 1^{-}
. Unpleasantly, for the asymptotic
\to 1^{-}
we know from Proposition
3.56
that
\lim\limits_{s\to 1^{-}}|u|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}=\infty
. This divergence was initially observed by Haim Brezis, Jean Bourgain and Pierre Mironescu in 2001
[
BBM01
]
. As one can foresee from the above computation, to correct this anomaly, putting the factor
(1-s)
in front of the term
|u|^{p}_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}
annihilates the singularity. In fact, they prove that for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)

(3.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}(1-s)|u|^{p}_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}=K_{d,p}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}:=K_{d,p}|u|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}.

for some appropriate universal constant
K_{d,p}
depending only on
d
and
p\geq 1
. We shall see this in a more general context later on. The case
s\to 0
was solved in
[
MS02
]
claiming that for all
u\in\bigcup\limits_{0<s<1}W^{s,p}(\R^{d})
,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}s|u|^{p}_{W^{s,p}(R^{d})}=\frac{2}{p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\|u\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}^{p}.

Seemingly, Proposition
3.56
suggests that the space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
for
s>1
must be defined in a different way. Assuming for now that the relation (
3.18
) is true the following definition plainly makes sense.

Definition 3.57
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be an open set,
1\leq p<\infty
and
s>0
. We put
s=m+\sigma
if
s
is not an integer, where
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in(0,1)
. The space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
assumes the equivalence classes of functions
u\in W^{m,p}(\Omega)
whose distributional derivatives
D^{\alpha}u
of order
|\alpha|=m
belong to
W^{\sigma,p}(\Omega)
, namely

\displaystyle W^{s,p}(\Omega):=\Big\{u\in W^{m,p}(\Omega)~:\;D^{\alpha}u\in W^{\sigma,p}(\Omega)\ \text{for any}\ \alpha\ \text{s.t.}\ |\alpha|=m\Big\}

The space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
is a Banach space with respect to the norm defined by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}:=\Big(\|u\|^{p}_{W^{m,p}(\Omega)}+\sum_{|\alpha|=m}\|D^{\alpha}u\|^{p}_{W^{\sigma,p}(\Omega)}\Big)^{\!\frac{1}{p}}.

Purposely we emphasize that

W^{\sigma,p}(\Omega)=\Big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\dfrac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\frac{d}{p}+\sigma}}\in L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega)\Big\}
and

\|u\|^{p}_{W^{\sigma,p}(\Omega)}=\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\;dx+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+\sigma p}}\;dxdy.

Furthermore,
W^{-s,p}(\Omega)
is the topological dual of
W_{0}^{s,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)=\overline{C_{c}(\Omega)}^{W^{s,p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}
where
1/p^{\prime}=1-1/p
. The space
W^{s,\infty}(\Omega)
boils down to the usual Hölder space
C^{m,\sigma}(\Omega)
.

Clearly, if
s=m
is an integer, the space
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
coincides with the Sobolev space
W^{m,p}(\Omega)
.

Remark 3.58
.

The fractional Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
fill the gaps between the classical Sobolev of integers orders. Actually there are two main different schools when it comes to define the notions of fractional Sobolev spaces.
The spaces
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
,
s\in\R
belong to the schools of Besov-Gagliardo-Nirenberg. There are also the spaces
H^{s,p}(\Omega)
(which we define below) belonging to the school of Triebel-Linzorkin. The latter spaces can be realized as the
complex interpolation
between
L^{p}(\R^{d})
and
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
whereas
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
is the
real interpolation
between
L^{p}(\R^{d})
and
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
.

Definition 3.59

(Via Fourier transform)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open,
1\leq p<\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. According to the definition due to Triebel-Linzorking the fractional Sobolev space denoted
H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the completion of the space

H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\Big\{u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\|(\langle{\xi}\rangle^{s}\widehat{u})^{\vee}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}<\infty\Big\},

equipped with the norm

\|u\|_{H^{s,p}(\R^{d})}=\|\big[(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s}\widehat{u}\big]^{\vee}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}.

Furthermore, we have the space
H^{s,p}(\Omega):=\Big\{u\mid_{\Omega}:\,u\in H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\Big\},

equipped with the norm

\|u\|_{H^{s,p}(\Omega)}=\inf\{\|v\|_{H^{s,p}(\R^{d})}:\,u=v\mid_{\Omega}\,,v\in H^{s,p}(\R^{d})\}.

Let us now illuminate some correlations between the spaces
W^{s,p}(\R^{d})
and
H^{s,p}(\R^{d})
.

•

By using the Fourier transform (see the presentation in Chapter
2
) one gets that for all
s\in\R
,
W^{s,2}(\R^{d})=H^{s,2}(\R^{d})
.

•

According to Mikhlin’s Theorem
[
Zie12
, Theorem 2.6.1]
we have
W^{m,p}(\R^{d})=H^{m,p}(\R^{d})
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1<p<\infty
. Moreover the equality holds with equivalent norms. This fails for
p=1
or
p=\infty
.

•

Furthermore, according to
[
Gri11
]
, for all
s\in\R^{d}
we have

\displaystyle W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{for $1<p\leq 2$}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{for $2\leq p<\infty$}.

•

In addition Lions and Peetre (1964)
[
Gri11
]
have proved that if
s_{1}<s_{2}<s_{3}
then

W^{s_{3},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset H^{s_{2},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset W^{s_{1},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

•

If
\Omega
is a Lipschitz domain then combining Proposition
3.50
and Theorem
3.49
it is easy to establish the following continuous embedding for all
1\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}

W^{s_{2},p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{s_{1},p}(\Omega).

Similarly, for all
1\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}
, one can show that

H^{s_{2},p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{s_{1},p}(\Omega).

Let us now quote some interesting results in connection to the spaces
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
.

Theorem 3.60

(
[
HT08
,
Gri11
]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open.

1.

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
s\in\R
.

2.

If
\Omega
has a continuous boundary, then the space
W_{*}^{s,p}(\Omega)=\{u\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,u=0~\text{a.e on}~\Omega^{c}\}
contains
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
as a dense subspace.

3.

Assume
\Omega
has a continuous boundary, then
C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is dense in
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
for all
s>0
, where we recall that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is the space of all functions which are restriction of
C^{\infty}
functions with compact support in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\Omega
.

4.

Assume
\Omega
has a Lipschitz boundary, then
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
for
0\leq s\leq 1/p
, i.e.,
W_{0}^{s,p}(\Omega)=W^{s,p}(\Omega)
for
s\leq 1/p
.

5.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a Lipschitz boundary, then
W_{*}^{s,p}(\Omega)=W_{0}^{s,p}(\Omega)
except if
s-1/p
is an integer.
Furthermore, if
0<s<1/p
,
W_{*}^{s,p}(\Omega)=W_{0}^{s,p}(\Omega)=W^{s,p}(\Omega)
.

The proofs of
(i)-(iv)
are included in Section
12
in a more general context.

Remark 3.61
.

To some authors e.g
[
FKV15
,
DK13
]
, the space
W_{0}^{s,p}(\Omega)
is the closure in
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)

in this case we will denote simply by
\overline{C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Whereas others e.g
[
NPV12
,
HT08
]

define
W_{0}^{s,p}(\Omega)
to be closure of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
in
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
. One should be careful since (see
(v)
) in general both definitions do not coincide especially on non-smooth domains.

12
Approximations by smooth functions

In practice it is rather laborious and demanding to work in a function space not containing smooth functions. In this section we show from different perspectives that functions from the nonlocal spaces of interest for us can be approximated by smooth functions. This part is stimulated by
[
EE87
]
. Throughout this section, the function
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
is assumed to satisfy the condition (

J
1

) see (page

J
1

). In addition we fix a function
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
supported in
B_{1}(0)
such that
\phi\geq 0
and
\int_{B_{1}}\phi=1
we denote the corresponding mollifier by
\phi_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\phi\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right)
. It is not difficult to establish that for each
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\phi_{\delta}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\phi_{\delta}\geq 0,\quad\operatorname{supp}\phi_{\delta}\subset B_{\delta}(0)\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{\delta}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=1.

A standard example is given by taking

\phi(x)=c\exp{-\tfrac{1}{1-|x|^{2}}}
if
|x|<1
and
\phi(x)=0
if
|x|\geq 1
where the constant
c>0
is chosen such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=1.

We start by recalling some basic remarks and some useful Lemmata.

Remark 3.62
.

Note that if we notationally set
U(x,y)=(u(x)-u(y))\nu^{1/p}(x-y)
then

•

u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
if and only if
(u,U)\in L^{p}(\Omega)\times L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega)

•

u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
if and only if
(u,U)\in L^{p}(\Omega)\times L^{p}(\Omega\times\R^{d})
.

Employing Jensen’s inequality we get

(3.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\phi_{\delta}*u-u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}&=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big|[\phi_{\delta}*u-u](x)-[\phi_{\delta}*u-u](y)\big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\Big|\int\limits_{\R^{d}}\phi_{\delta}(z)\big\{U(x-z,y-z)-U(x,y)\big\}\,\mathrm{d}z\Big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\\
