\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open and bounded. The embedding
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact. Further if
\Omega
is Lipschitz, then the embedding
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact.

Proof.
.

The embedding
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact because, with the choice
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp},
since in view of Theorem
3.81
the embedding
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact and the embedding
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is continuous.

Now if
\Omega
is Lipschitz then by Theorem
3.49
we know that the embedding
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
is continuous whereas from Theorem
3.89
it comes that the embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact. It thus turns out that
W^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact too.
∎

The efforts made to establish the compactness result of Theorem
3.89
will be rewarded for the elaboration of the Poincaré type inequality which will be useful in the forthcoming section.

Theorem 3.91

(Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\Omega
be an open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be a measurable function with full support. Assume the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3,4
. Then there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,\Omega,\nu)
depending only on
d,~\Omega
and
\nu
such that

(3.39)

\displaystyle\big\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{for every }~u\in L^{p}(\Omega)

and immediately,

(3.40)

\displaystyle\big\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\qquad\text{for every }~u\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,.

Proof.
.

Assume such constant does not exist then we can find a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
elements of
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
such that for every
n
,
\fint_{\Omega}u_{n}=0
,
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,.

The sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
is thus bounded in
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
which by Theorem
3.89
is compactly embedded in
L^{p}(\Omega)
whenever
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
. Therefore, if it is the case, passing through a subsequence,
(u_{n})_{n}
converges in
L^{p}(\Omega)
to some function
u
. Clearly it follows that
\fint_{\Omega}u=0
and
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
. Moreover, by Fatou’s lemma we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=0

which implies that
u
equals the constant function
x\mapsto\fint_{\Omega}u=0
almost everywhere on
\Omega
. This goes against the fact that
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
hereby showing that our initial assumption was wrong.

Next assume
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to the class
\mathscr{A}_{4}
then, as
\nu
has a full support, is radial and is almost decreasing and
\Omega
is bounded, there is a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that
\nu(x-y)\geq c^{\prime}
for all
x,y\in\Omega
. Using this and Jensen’s inequality we get the desired inequality as follows

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}|\Omega|\int_{\Omega}\fint_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}|\Omega|\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,.

Which ends the proof since (
3.40
) is clearly a consequence of (
3.39
).
∎

The above Poincaré inequality (
3.39
)-(
3.40
) can be seen has the nonlocal counterpart of the classical Poincaré inequality which states that for a connected bounded Lipschitz
\Omega
, there is
C>0
for which

\displaystyle\big\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,,\qquad\text{for all }~u\in L^{p}(\Omega)

where by convention we assume
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\infty
if
|\nabla u|
is not in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Alongside this, we also recall the classical Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality: there is
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,\qquad\text{for all }~u\in W_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\,.

With a close look at the proof of Theorem
3.46
one readily establishes the following generalization.

Theorem 3.92

(Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\Omega
be an open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be a measurable function with full support. Assume the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to the
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
. Assume that there
G\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
is a nonempty closed subset of
L^{p}(\Omega)
not containing non-zero constant functions. Then there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,\Omega,\nu,G)
such that

\displaystyle\big\|u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{for every }~u\in G.

By a subsequent analogy, one can also establish the following result as well.

Theorem 3.93

(Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\Omega
be an open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be a measurable function with full support. Assume the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to the
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
. Let
G\subset W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a nonempty closed subset of
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
not containing non-zero constant functions. Then there exists a positive constant
C=C(d,\Omega,\nu,G)
such that

\displaystyle\big\|u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{for every }~u\in G.

Remark 3.94
.

(i)
Let
E\subset\Omega
be a measurable set with a positive measure and
\delta>0
. Some special closed subsets of
L^{p}(\Omega)
to which Theorem
3.92
applies are given by,
G_{E}=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,\mbox{$\fint_{E}u$}=0\,\}
,
G^{0}_{E}=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,u=0\,\text{ a.e. on $E$}\}
and

G_{\delta}=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,|\{u=0\}|\geq\delta\}.

(ii)
Let
E\subset\R^{d}
be a measurable set with a positive measure and
\delta>0
. Some special closed subsets of
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
to which Theorem
3.93
applies are given by,

G_{E}=\{u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,\mbox{$\fint_{E}u$}=0\,\}
,
G^{0}_{E}=\{u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,u=0\,\text{ a.e. on $E$}\}
and

G_{\delta}=\{u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,|\{u=0\}|\geq\delta\}.

In the same spirit, as we will see below the corresponding nonlocal Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
(which we recall is the closure of the
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
) is much easier to obtain and no compactness argument is required. This provides an effortless alternative way to proof the Poincaé-Friedrichs inequality from
[
FKV15
, Lemma 2.7]
which uses iterated convolutions. Nevertheless, we point out that under the condition that the embedding is
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact, a similar inequality is derived in
[
JW19a
]
when
\Omega
is only bounded in one direction.

Theorem 3.95

(Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality for
W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be a function satisfying (

I
′

1

). There is a constant
C=C(d,\Omega,\nu)
such that

(3.41)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\quad\text{for every $u\in W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

Proof.
.

Let
R>0
be the diameter of
\Omega
then for all
x\in\Omega
we have
B^{c}_{R}(x)\subset\Omega^{c}
. For
u\in W_{\nu,\Omega}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
we recall that
u=0~a.e
on
\Omega^{c}
. It suffices to take C=
(2\|\nu_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})^{-1}
with
\nu_{R}=\nu\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(0)}
indeed

\displaystyle|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+2\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega^{c}}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq 2\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{B^{c}_{R}(x)}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y=2\|\nu_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

∎

In view of the compactness of
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
(the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
) into
L^{p}(\Omega)
it is very tempting to say that the nonlocal counterpart of the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality also holds on
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
under the only assumption that
(\nu,\Omega)
is in the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
. But this is not warranted especially if one considers the simple case
\nu(h)=|h|^{-d-sp}
with
0<s<1/p
. Indeed, is well known that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
when
0<s<1/p
(c.f.
[
Gri11
,
Tri83
]
) that is to say in this situation we have
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)=W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
. In fact, since
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)
contains constant functions, it is impossible to find a
C>0
for which the following Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality holds

(3.42)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\qquad\text{for all}~~u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)\,.

More generally, following the scheme of the proof of Theorem
3.91
one is able to establish the following.

Theorem 3.96

(Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality for
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be an open bounded set and a measurable function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
satisfying (

I
′

1

) such that the embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact. Then the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (
3.42
) holds true on the space
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
if and only if the constant
u=1
is not an element of
W^{p}_{\nu,0}(\Omega)
.

We are also in the mood to establish the so called nonlocal Friedrichs type inequality. To do this let us start with the following well known result.

Theorem 3.97
.

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be three Banach spaces such that
X
is reflexive. Let
K\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
be a compact operator and
S\in\mathcal{L}(X,Z)
be one-to-one. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for all
x\in X

\displaystyle\|Kx\|_{Y}\leq\varepsilon\|x\|_{X}+C_{\varepsilon}\|Sx\|_{Z},

Proof.
.

Assume the claim fails for some
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
of X such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\|Kx_{n}\|_{Y}>\varepsilon\|x_{n}\|_{X}+2^{n}\|Sx_{n}\|_{Z}.

Preferably we assume
\|x_{n}\|_{X}=1
. Hence, since
X
is reflexive, a subsequence
(x_{n})
weakly converges to some
x\in X
. So that
Sx_{n}
also converges weakly in
Z
to
Sx
which implies
\|Sx\|_{Z}\leq\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\|Sx_{n}\|_{Z}
and by compactness of
K
,
Kx_{n}
converges to
Kx
in
Y
. Simultaneously, the above relation forces
\|Sx_{n}\|_{Z}\to 0
that is
Sx=0
which means
x=0
since
S
is one-to-one and hence
\|Kx\|_{Y}>\varepsilon
which is a contradiction.
∎

As a consequence of this we have the following nonlocal version of Friedrichs’ inequality.

Theorem 3.98

(Nonlocal Friedrichs inequality)
.

Let
\Omega
be an open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
1<p<\infty
and
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
be a measurable function with full support. Assume the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to the
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
. Assume
K\subset\Omega
(eventually
K=\Omega
) and
O\subset\Omega^{c}
(eventually
O=\Omega^{c}
) be a measurable sets such that
|K|>0
and
|O|>0
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
u\in W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(O,\,\nu_{K})},

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(O,\,\mathring{\nu}_{K})}

where we recall that

\displaystyle\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}\nu(x-y)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathring{\nu}_{K}(x)=\int_{K}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

In particular the following norms are mutually equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,

\displaystyle u\mapsto\Big(|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(O,\nu_{K})}\Big)^{1/p}\quad\text{and}\quad u\mapsto\Big(|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(O,\mathring{\nu}_{K})}\Big)^{1/p}.

Proof.
.

The embedding
W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Omega)
is compact. A mere adaption of Proposition
3.39
shows that
\operatorname{Tr}:W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
and
\operatorname{Tr}:W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\mathring{\nu}_{K})
with
u\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(u)=u\mid_{\Omega^{c}}
are linear and continuous. On the other hand we trivially have that the embeddings
L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(O,\nu_{K})
and
L^{p}(\Omega^{c},\mathring{\nu}_{K})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(O,\mathring{\nu}_{K})
are continuous. Whence the mappings
S:W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{p}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\times L^{p}(O,\nu_{K})
and
\mathring{S}:W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{p}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\times L^{p}(O,\nu_{K})
with

\mathring{S}u=Su=\big(u(x)-u(y))\nu^{1/p}(x-y),\operatorname{Tr}u\big)

are linear bounded and one-to-one. In virtue of Theorem
3.97
, for
\varepsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\|u\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C_{\varepsilon}\|Su\|^{2}_{L^{2}(O,\nu_{K})}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+(\varepsilon+C_{\varepsilon})|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C_{\varepsilon}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(O,\nu_{K})}.

Likewise, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+(\varepsilon+C_{\varepsilon})|u|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C_{\varepsilon}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(O,\mathring{\nu}_{K})}.

Taking
\varepsilon=1/2
provides the required inequalities. That the norms are equivalent blatantly follows.
∎

Let us recall the classical Friedrichs inequality whose proof can be derived analogously.

Theorem 3.99

(c.f.
[
Maz13
]
)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
has a Lipschitz boundary. Let
\Gamma_{0}\subset\partial\Omega
be a surface with a positive area, i.e.
|\Gamma_{0}|>0
. Let
1<p<\infty
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}+C\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Gamma_{0})}

Moreover, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}
is equivalent to the norm

u\mapsto\Big(|u|^{p}_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}+\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Gamma_{0})}\Big)^{1/p}.

Chapter 4
Complement Value Problems For Nonlocal Operators

The overreaching goal of this chapter is to investigate weak solutions to some specific integrodifferential equations (IDEs) involving nonlocal operators of Lévy type. In many cases, this will be equivalent to proving the existence of minimizers via the direct method of calculus of variations
12
12

12

Calculus of variations is a branch of mathematical analysis dealing with optimization problems to find the extremum for a functional.
. At first, we shall be concerned with the well-posedness of Dirichlet (first), Neumann (second), Robin (third) and mixed complement value type problems for integrodifferential operators (IDEs) of Lévy type. Afterwards, we study the spectral decomposition of Lévy type operators that are subject of the aforementioned complement conditions. The latter will
allow us to study evolution IDEs problems on bounded domains, Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and essentially self-adjointness for integrodifferential operators. Our approaches substantially consist of developing the aforementioned notions using gadgets from
L^{2}
-theory. Analogous approaches treating standard elliptic PDEs of
the second order are referenced in
[
AA15
,
Eva10
,
Hun14
,
HT08
,
Jos13
,
LDL16
,
Leo13
,
Mik78
,
Tay11
]
. We begin by reviewing some important results from the theory of calculus of variations.

14
Review of variational principles

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(V,\|\cdot\|_{V})
be a normed space. Let
\ell:V\to\mathbb{R}
be a linear form and
a(\cdot,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be a bilinear form.

•

\ell
is bounded if there exists
M>1
such that

\displaystyle|\ell(v)|\leq M\|v\|_{V}\quad\text{for all}~~v\in V.

•

a(\cdot,\cdot)
is bounded if there exists
M>0
such that

\displaystyle|a(u,v)|\leq M\|u\|_{V}\|v\|_{V}\quad\text{for all}~~u,v\in V.

•

a(\cdot,\cdot)
is called to be coercive or
V-
elliptic if there exists
0<\theta<1
such that

\displaystyle a(v,v)\geq\theta\|v\|^{2}_{V}\quad\text{for all}~~v\in V.

In short, we will simply say that
a(\cdot,\cdot)
is
\theta
-coercive.

The dual space
V^{\prime}
is the collection of linear continuous forms on
V
and is a Banach space under the norm

\displaystyle\|\ell\|_{V^{\prime}}=\sup_{v\in V,~\|v\|_{V}=1}|\ell(v)|\,.

We omit the proof of the next theorem.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
(V,\|\cdot\|_{V})
be a normed space and
K\subset V
be a convex subset. Let

\ell:V\to\mathbb{R}
be a linear form and let
a(\cdot,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric
and positive definite bilinear form. Then the functional
J:V\to\mathbb{R}
with

J(v)=\frac{1}{2}a(v,v)-\ell(v)

is strictly convex. Moreover, there is at most one
u\in K
such that

J(u)=\min\limits_{v\in K}J(v).

Moreover, this minimization is equivalent to the variational inequality

\displaystyle a(u,v-u)\geq\ell(v-u)\quad\text{for all}~v\in K\,.

Furthermore, in the special case where
K
is an affine subspace of
V
, i.e.
K=v_{0}+U
with
v_{0}\in V
and
U
is a closed subspace of
V
, the above variational inequality becomes

\displaystyle a(u,v)=\ell(v)\quad\text{for all}~v\in K\,.

The next theorem shows that in the setting of Banach spaces, the above variational
inequality is well-posed in the sense of Hadamard. In other words, it possesses a unique
solution which continuously depends upon the data. This is useful to show the well-posedness of many variational equations.

Theorem 4.3

(Stampachia
13
13

13

This theorem was established by Guido Stampachia in the
setting of Hilbert spaces in 1964, he extended it later in 1967 to Banach spaces in a joint work with Jacques-Louis Lions. See for example
[
ET09
]
and other references therein
)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space and
K\subset V
be a nonempty, closed, convex set. Let
a(\cdot,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous and
\theta
-coercive bilinear form. Then for every continuous
linear form
\ell:V\to\mathbb{R}
there is a unique
u\in K
such that

(4.1)

\displaystyle a(u,v-u)\geq\ell(v-u)\quad\text{for all}~v\in K\,.

Moreover, if
\overline{u}\in K
corresponds to another continuous linear form
\overline{\ell}:V\to\mathbb{R}
then,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|u-\overline{u}\|_{V}\leq\frac{1}{\theta}\|\ell-\overline{\ell}\|_{V^{\prime}}

Proof.
.

Assume
u,\overline{u}\in K
be solutions corresponding to
\ell,\overline{\ell}
. Since
K
is convex, testing both
u
and
\overline{u}
with
v=\frac{u+\overline{u}}{2}\in K
and adding both inequalities one easily arrives at

\displaystyle-a(u-\overline{u},u-\overline{u})\geq(\ell-\overline{\ell})(u-\overline{u})\,.

The coercivity yields

\displaystyle\theta\|u-\overline{u}\|^{2}_{V}\leq a(u-\overline{u},u-\overline{u})\leq\|\ell-\overline{\ell}\|_{V^{\prime}}\|u-\overline{u}\|_{V}\,.

This entails the estimate (
4.2
) from which the uniqueness follows subsequently.
Now we prove the existence which in virtue of Theorem
4.2
corresponds to show that the functional

\displaystyle J(v)=\frac{1}{2}a(v,v)-\ell(v)\,.

has a minimizer on
K
. First of all, the quantity
d=\inf\limits_{v\in K}J(v)
exists, since
J
is bounded below on
V
. Indeed, for
v\in V,

\displaystyle J(v)\geq\frac{\theta}{2}\|v\|^{2}_{V}-\|\ell\|_{V^{\prime}}\|v\|_{V}=\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}\|u\|_{V}^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\theta}}\|\ell\|_{V^{\prime}}\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\theta}\|\ell\|^{2}_{V^{\prime}}\geq-\frac{1}{2\theta}\|\ell\|^{2}_{V^{\prime}}\,.

First, we assume that
a(\cdot,\cdot)
is symmetric. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we consider
u_{n}\in K
such that
d\leq J(u_{n})\leq d+\frac{1}{2^{n}}

Whence, since
J
is convex, from the relation
2ab=a^{2}+b^{2}-4\big(\frac{a+b}{2}\big)^{2}
we get

\displaystyle\theta\|u_{n}-u_{m}\|^{2}_{V}

\displaystyle\leq a(u_{n}-u_{m},u_{n}-u_{m})

\displaystyle=4J(u_{n})+4J(u_{m})-8J\big(\frac{u_{n}+u_{m}}{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 4\big(d+\frac{1}{2^{n}}\big)+4\big(d+\frac{1}{2^{m}}\big)-8d=4\big(\frac{1}{2^{n}}+\frac{1}{2^{m}}\big)\,.

Therefore,
(u_{n})_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in the Banach space
V
and hence converges to some
u\in K
since
K
is closed. By continuity of
J
, we have
d=J(u)
.

If
a(\cdot,\cdot)
is not symmetric, then for fixed
t\in[0,1]
we write

\displaystyle a_{t}(u,v)=a_{0}(u,v)+tb(u,v)

with

\displaystyle a_{0}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\big(a(u,v)+a(v,u)\big)\quad\text{and}\quad b(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\big(a(u,v)-a(v,u)\big)\,.

Clearly, the bilinear forms
b(\cdot,\cdot),a_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
are bounded and
a_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
is
\theta
-coercive since for every
v\in V
we have
a_{t}(v,v)=a_{0}(v,v)=a(v,v)
. For fixed
w\in V
, define
the bounded linear form
\ell_{w}(v)=\ell(v)-tb(w,v)
. Given that
a_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)
is symmetric,
from the previous case, there is a unique
u=u(w)\in K
satisfying the variational inequality

\displaystyle a_{0}(u,v-u)\geq\ell_{w}(v-u)\qquad\text{for all}~~v\in K.

Accordingly, the map
T:V\to K
with
u(w)=Tw
is well defined. Choosing
t
such that
0\leq t\leq\frac{\theta}{2M}
then utilizing the estimate (
4.2
) leads to

\displaystyle\|Tw-Tw^{\prime}\|_{V}\leq\frac{1}{\theta}\|\ell_{w}-\ell_{w^{\prime}}\|_{V^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{\theta}\|b(w-w^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{V^{\prime}}\leq\frac{M}{\theta}t\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{V}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{V}\,.

This shows that
T
is a contraction on
V
and thus has a unique fixed point
u_{t}\in K
. We have
u_{t}=Tu_{t}
which by definition implies that for every
v\in K
,

\displaystyle a_{t}(u_{t},v-u_{t})

\displaystyle=a_{0}(u_{t},v-u_{t})+tb(u_{t},v-u_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\ell_{u_{t}}(v-u_{t})+tb(u_{t},v-u_{t})=\ell(v-u_{t})\,.

We have shown that for
0\leq t\leq\frac{\theta}{2M}
, there is a unique
u_{t}\in K
such that

\displaystyle a_{t}(u_{t},v-u_{t})\geq\ell(v-u_{t})\quad\text{for all}~~\in v\in K\,.

A recursive argument shows that this remains true when
\frac{\theta n}{2M}\leq t\leq\frac{\theta(n+1)}{2M}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and thus for all
t\geq 0
. This terminates the proof since for
t=1
we have
a_{1}(u,v)=a(u,v)
for all
u,v\in V
.
∎

The celebrated Lax-Milgram lemma turns out to be a consequence of the Theorem
4.3
.

Corollary 4.4

(Lax-Milgram lemma)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space and
K\subset V
be a nonempty closed, convex set. Let
a(\cdot,\cdot):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous
\theta
-coercive bilinear form. Then for every continuous linear form
\ell:V\to\mathbb{R}
there is a unique
u\in V
such that

\displaystyle a(u,v)=\ell(v)\quad\text{for all}~v\in V\,.

Moreover, the mapping
\ell\mapsto u
is linear and continuous from
V^{\prime}
to
V
with

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V}\leq\frac{1}{\theta}\|\ell\|_{V^{\prime}}\,.

15
Lagrange multipliers

The Lagrange multiplier method is one of the most powerful tools used to solve certain types
of constrained minimization problems in the setting of Banach spaces. For a fair exposition we need some basics on differential calculus. We recommend
[
Che13
]
for supplementary notions on differential calculus. In this section,

V,W
are two Banach spaces and
U\subset V
is an open set of
V
. We recall the notion of Fréchet derivative.
A function
f:U\to W
is said to be Fréchet differentiable at a point
a\in U
if there is a linear bounded operator
L_{a}:V\to W
i.e
L_{a}\in\mathcal{L}(V,W)
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{\|h\|_{V}\to 0}\frac{\|f(a+h)-f(a)-L_{a}(h)\|_{W}}{\|h\|_{V}}=0.

It is often common that the operator
L_{a}
is synonymously denoted by d
f(a)
or
f^{\prime}(a)
. We will adopt the latter notation.
We say that
f
is
C^{1}
or continuously differentiable if
f^{\prime}(a)
exists for every
a\in U
and the function
f^{\prime}:U\to\mathcal{L}(V,W)
with
a\mapsto f^{\prime}(a)
is continuous.
Note that
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
is equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\mathcal{L}(V,W)}:=\sup\{\|Tv\|_{W}:~\|v\|_{V}=1\}.

If the Fréchet derivative of
f
at
a
exists then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{f(a+tv)-f(a)}{t}=f^{\prime}(a)v\qquad\text{for all}~v\in V.

In general, if the above expression exists then
f
is said to be gâteau differentiable at
a
in the direction
v
. However, the gâteau differentiability does not always imply the Fréchet differentiability. The Fréchet derivative is related to the open mapping theorem.

Theorem 4.5

(Open mapping Theorem)
.

[
Che13
, Section 3.4]

Let
f:U\to W
be a continuously differentiable function where
U\subset V
is open.
If the Fréchet derivative
f^{\prime}(a)
of
f
at point
a\in U
is surjective, then
f(U)
is a neighborhood of
f(a)
in
W
.

Theorem 4.6

(Lagrange Multiplier)
.

[
Che13
, Section 3.5]

Let
U\subset V
be open and let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:V\to W
be continuously differentiable functions. Let
K=\{x\in U:~g(x)=0\}
. Assume
a\in U\cap K
is the a local minimum of
f
on
U\cap K
such that
g^{\prime}(a)\in\mathcal{L}(V,W)
is surjective. Then there exists a bounded linear form
\ell\in W^{\prime}
so that
f^{\prime}(a)=\ell\circ g^{\prime}(a)
. To be more precise,
f^{\prime}(a)(v)=\ell\circ g^{\prime}(a)(v)
for all
v\in V.

This theorem can be proved by applying the implicit function theorem, see
[
Che13
]
. However, the Lagrange multiplier theorem above can be reformulated in the special case where
W=\mathbb{R}
as follows.

Theorem 4.7

(Lagrange Multiplier)
.

Let
U\subset V
be open and let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:U\to W
be continuously differentiable
functions. Let
K=\{x\in U:~g(x)=0\}
. Assume
a\in U\cap K
is the a local minimum of
f
on

U\cap K
such that
g^{\prime}(a)\neq 0
. Then there is
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
so that
f^{\prime}(a)=\lambda g^{\prime}(a)
. To be more precise,
f^{\prime}(a)(v)=\lambda g^{\prime}(a)(v)
for all
v\in V.

Proof.
.

Without loss of generality, assume that
f(a)=\min\limits_{x\in U\cap K}f(x)
. Define
F:U\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
F(x)=(f(x),g(x))
. Then
F(a)=(f(a),0)
and
F
is also
C^{1}
and for

x\in U
,
F^{\prime}(x)(v)=(f^{\prime}(x)(v),g^{\prime}(x)(v))
for all
v\in V
. As
f(a)
is the local minimum of
f
, it appears that for
\varepsilon>0
small enough,
(f(a)-\varepsilon,0))
is not an element of
F(U)
. This means that
F(U)
is not a neighborhood of
F(a)
.
Whence the function
F
cannot be open which in virtue of the open mapping Theorem
4.5
means that
F^{\prime}(a)
is not surjective.
Accordingly, the range
\operatorname{Im}(F^{\prime}(a))
of
F^{\prime}(a)
is at most of dimension one. Hence there is a bounded linear form
\ell\in V^{\prime}
and an element
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
F^{\prime}(a)(v)=\gamma\ell(v)
for all
v\in V
. We have
f^{\prime}(a)(v)=\gamma_{1}\ell(v)
and
g^{\prime}(a)(v)=\gamma_{2}\ell(v)
. The fact that
g^{\prime}(a)\neq 0
implies that
\gamma_{2}\neq 0
. Letting
\lambda=\gamma_{1}/\gamma_{2}
we obtain
f^{\prime}(a)=\lambda g^{\prime}(a)
.
∎

16
Integrodifferential equations (IDEs)

Here we study the well-posedness of various types of integrodifferential equations (IDEs) associated with symmetric integrodifferential operators of Lévy type. These are operators of the form

\displaystyle Lu(x)=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\text{($x\in\R^{d}$)}

where
\nu:\R^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
is even and Lévy integrable, i.e.

\displaystyle\nu(h)=\nu(-h)\,\,\text{for $h\neq 0$}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

We are interested in showing the well-posedness of IDEs on a domain
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
with Neumann, Dirichlet, Robin and mixed complement condition.
It is worth emphasizing that due to the nonlocal feature of
L
, the condition for each of the aforementioned problems is imposed on the complement of
\Omega
.
In each case, we briefly recall the local analog. From now on we use several notations from the previous chapters.
In particular, we associate with the Lévy density
\nu
, the bilinear form
\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined for
u,v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\big(u(x)-u(y)\big)\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)\,\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

In addition, the function spaces introduced in Chapter
3
play an important role in this section. It is worth recalling that the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is always endowed with the norm
\|v\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u,u)
. Also recall that
|\cdot|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)\leq 2|\cdot|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, where

\displaystyle|v|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}\big(v(x)-v(y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

16.1
Integrodifferential equations (IDEs) with Neumann condition

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set. Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable functions. The Neumann problem for the operator
L
is to find a measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(
N
)

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}~~~\Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\mathcal{N}u=g~~~\text{on}~~~\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega,

where
\mathcal{N}
, also called for an obvious reason the
nonlocal normal derivative operator
(see
[
DROV17
,
DGLZ12
]
) on
\Omega
with respect to
\nu
is the integrodifferential operator defined by

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}u(y)=\int_{\Omega}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\qquad(y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega).

Let us derive the so called
nonlocal Green-Gauss formula
cf. (
4.5
) which provides a nonlocal version to the classical Green-Gauss formula for
u\in H^{2}(\Omega)
and
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
(See
[
FSU19
, Theorem 2.20]
),

(4.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(-\Delta)u(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma_{1}u(x)\gamma_{0}v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x),

where we recall that
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{0}\circ\nabla
, and
\gamma_{0}
stands for the trace operator on
H^{1}(\Omega)
.

Proposition 4.8

(
Green-Gauss formula
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open and bounded. For every
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
and
v\in C_{b}^{1}(\R^{d})
the following Green-Gauss formula holds true

(4.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}[Lu(x)]v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y.

In particular, letting
v=1
one gets the integration by part formula,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)\mathrm{d}y.

Furthermore, if
u,v\in C_{b}^{2}(\R^{d})
then we have the following second Green-Gauss formula,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}[Lu(x)]v(x)-[Lv(x)]u(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega^{c}}[\mathcal{N}v(y)]u(y)-[\mathcal{N}u(y)]v(y)\mathrm{d}y.

Proof.
.

It is sufficient to prove (
4.5
) since it implies (
4.7
). Note that for
\varphi\in C_{b}^{1}(\R^{d})
, we have

(4.8)

\displaystyle|\varphi(x)-\varphi(x+h)|\leq 2\|\varphi\|_{C_{b}^{1}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|)\quad\text{for all $x,h\in\R^{d}$}.

Let
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. With the aid of Proposition
2.2
we can write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}[Lu(x)]u(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\Omega}v(x)\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x)}((u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{\Omega\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x)}(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x+\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

On one side, by a symmetry argument we have

\displaystyle\qquad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{\Omega\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x)}(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|>\varepsilon\}}\hskip-12.91663pt(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\hskip-0.86108pt\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\hskip-17.22217pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|>\varepsilon\}}\hskip-17.22217pt(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\hskip-2.15277pt\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

where one gets rid of the principal value using the estimate (
4.8
) applied to
u
and
v
.
On the other side, with the help of Fubini’s theorem we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x+\int\limits_{\Omega^{c}}v(y)\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{\Omega}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y\,.

Altogether inserted in the initial relation provide the desired relation.
∎

Regarding the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
in
V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see Theorem
3.70
), the nonlocal version of Green-Gauss formula (
4.4
) is given as follows.

Theorem 4.9
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open and bounded with Lipschitz boundary. Recall the definition of the space
V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
see Section
9
, for every
u\in V^{1}_{\nu}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and every
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}[Lu(x)]v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y

where, the operators
L
and
\mathcal{N}
are understood in the generalized sense.

Let us look at a connection between the trace space
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and the nonlocal normal derivative
\mathcal{N}
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Assume
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
is endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}
.
Then for any linear continuous form
\ell:T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to\mathbb{R}
there exists
w\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for every
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega}^{c})

(4.9)

\displaystyle\ell(v)=\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}w(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y

In particular, given a measurable function
g:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
, if the linear mapping
\ell_{g}:v\mapsto\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y
is continuous on
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, then there exists
w\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
g=\mathcal{N}w
almost everywhere on
\Omega^{c}
.

Proof.
.

Let
\ell\in(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
. Because of the continuity of the trace operator
\operatorname{Tr}:V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\to T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, the linear form
\ell\circ\operatorname{Tr}
is also continuous on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By Riesz’s representation theorem there exists
w\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\ell\circ\operatorname{Tr}(v)=\left(v,w\right)_{(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for each
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, for
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\Omega}^{c})
identified with its zero extension on
\Omega
so that
\operatorname{Tr}(v)=v
, we obtain the following

\displaystyle\ell(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}w(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(w(x)-w(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Omega^{c}}v(y)\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{\Omega}(w(y)-w(x))\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}w(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y.

Furthermore, if
g:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
is such that
\ell_{g}
is continuous on
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
then by the above computation, it follows that
g=\mathcal{N}w
almost everywhere on
\Omega^{c}
since

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}w(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\hbox{for all }~~v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}^{c}).

∎

Remark 4.11
.

The second statement of Theorem
4.10
particularly suggests that the space of all measurable functions
g:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
for which linear the form
v\mapsto\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y
is continuous on
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
is contained in
\mathcal{N}(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(the range of
\mathcal{N}
).

In light of the relation (
4.5
) it is reasonable to define weak solutions of the Neumann problem as follows.

Definition 4.12
.

A measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a weak solution or a variational solution of the inhomogeneous Neumann problem (

N

) if
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and satisfies the relation

(
V
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

In particular, if
\Omega
is bounded then taking
v=1
, (

V

) turns to the so called compatibility condition

(
C
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)\mathrm{d}y=0.

Remark 4.13
.

The compatibility condition (

C

) is an implicit necessary requirement that the data
f
and
g
must fulfill before any attempt at solving the problems (

V

) and (

N

). The local counterpart of this compatibility condition, where
g
is defined on
\partial\Omega
, is given by

(4.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(y)=0.

Let us recall that the variational formulation of the classical Neumann problem
-\Delta u=f
in
\Omega
and
\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}=g
on
\partial\Omega
is to find
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(y),\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in H^{1}(\Omega)\,.

Both integrodifferential operators
L
and
\mathcal{N}
annihilate additive constants.
Therefore, as long as
u
is a solution to the system (

N

) or to the variational problem (

V

) so does the function
\widetilde{u}=u+c
for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Accordingly, both problems are ill-posed in the sense of Hadamard. The situation is likewise in the local setting with the operators
L
and
\mathcal{N}
respectively replaced by the operators
-\Delta
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial n}
. In order to overcome this anomaly we introduce an appropriate functional space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
consisting of functions in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
with zero mean over
\Omega
. To be more precise,

\displaystyle V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}:=\Big\{u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\int_{\Omega}u(x)\mathrm{d}x=0\Big\}.

Assuming that
\Omega
is bounded, the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
endowed with the scalar product of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is also a Hilbert as well. With this at hand, we rewrite the variational problem (

V

) as follows

(
V^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

It is noteworthy emphasizing that in contrast to (

V

), the variational problem (

V
′

) possesses at most one solution since
\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
defines a scalar product on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
. Analogous observations can be carried out in the local setting by introducing the space
H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}=\big\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\int_{\Omega}u(x)\mathrm{d}x=0\big\}.

Under additional regularity assumptions, we show that both problems (

N

) and (

V

) are equivalent.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\Omega
be an open bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with Lipschitz boundary. Let
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu^{-1}_{K})
where
\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}\nu(x-y)
for some measurable set
K\subset\Omega
with
|K|>0
. Then
u
is a solution of (

N

) if and only if
f
and
g

are compatible in the sense of (

C

) and
u
is a solution of (

V

).

Proof.
.

If
u
solves (

N

), i.e.
Lu=f
in
\Omega
and
\mathcal{N}u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
, then by the Green-Gauss formula (
4.5
) we obtain the following

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

As shown in (
4.17
)-(
4.18
) below, all terms involved in (
4.12
) are linear and continuous on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to the variable
v
. Moreover, smooth functions of compact support are dense in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
hence the relation in (
4.12
) remains true for functions
v
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
so (

V

) is satisfied. In particular, taking
v=1
yields the condition (

C

). Conversely, assume
u
solves (

V

) then inserting the Green-Gauss formula (
4.5
) with
v\in C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
in (
4.12
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Specializing this relation for
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
and
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega})
respectively, we end up with

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}Lu(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=0\qquad\mbox{for all}~~v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y-\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y=0\qquad\mbox{for all}~~v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}).

According to Proposition
2.2
,
Lu
is well defined and bounded hence belongs to
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Similarly
\mathcal{N}u
is well defined and bounded, i.e. belongs to
L^{\infty}(\Omega^{c})
. Thus, up to null sets, the above equations lead to (

N

). Precisely, we have
Lu=f\,\,\text{in}~\Omega~\text{and}~\mathcal{N}u=g~\text{on}~\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega.

∎

By standard procedure, a solution of the variational problem (

V

) is characterized as a critical point (a minimizer) of the functional

(4.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{J}(v)&=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}fv\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega^{c}}gv\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\frac{1}{4}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))^{2}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y-\int_{\Omega}fv\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega^{c}}gv\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Proposition 4.15
.

A function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
is solution to (

V
′

) if and only if
u
is also a solution of the minimization problem

(
M^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)=\min_{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}(v)

Moreover,
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
solves (

V
′

) if and only if for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
u+c
solves the variational problem (

V

) and the latter problem is equivalent to the minimization problem

(
M
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)=\min_{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{J}(v).

Proof.
.

Let
u,v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
. Employing, Cauchy-Schwartz inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v)=\mathcal{E}(u,u)-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v).

Thus, if (

V
′

) holds true for all
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
then we get
\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}(v)
and thus
u
solves (

M
′

).
Conversely, assume that
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
satisfies (

M
′

). For all
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
and all
t\in\R
(

M
′

) implies
\mathcal{J}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}(u+tv)
since
u+tv\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
. Therefore, for fixed
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
the polynomial of second order
\mathcal{J}(u+\centerdot v):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle t\mapsto\mathcal{J}(u+tv)=\mathcal{J}(u)+t\Big[\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big]+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v)

has a critical point at
t=0
. From this we get that (

V
′

) is verified since

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}(u+tv)-\mathcal{J}(u)}{t}=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\mathrm{d}y.

Meanwhile, if the compatibility condition (

C

) holds, then it is easy to observe that the
relation in (

V
′

) remains unchanged under additive constant, i.e.
\mathcal{J}(v+c)=\mathcal{J}(v)
for all
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
c\in\mathbb{R}.
Accordingly, if
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
solves (

V
′

), then we have
\mathcal{J}(u+c)=\min\limits_{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{J}(v)
which by a similar arguments as above is equivalent to (

V

).
∎

We are now in a position to state the well-posedness of (

V
′

) and hence of (

V

) up to additive constant.

Theorem 4.16
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the classes
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3,4
. For a set
K\subset\Omega
with positive measure, assume
\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}~\nu(x-y)>0~a.e
for almost
x\in\Omega^{c}
.
Then, given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})
, there exists a unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
to the variational problem (

V
′

). Further, the solutions to (

V

) are of the form
u+c
with
c\in\mathbb{R}
provided that
f
and
g
are compatible in the sense that (

C

) is verified.

Moreover, there exists a constant
C:=C(d,\Omega,\nu)>0
independent of
f
and
g
such that any solution
w
of
(

V

)
satisfies the following weak regularity estimate

(4.14)

\displaystyle\|w-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})}\right)\,.

In particular, the operator
\Phi:L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})\to V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
mapping the Neumann data
(f,g)\in L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})

to the unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
of the variational problem
(

V
′

) is linear, one-to-one, continuous and we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi(f,g)\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|(f,g)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})}.

Remark 4.17
.

In some sense, a solution to the variational problem (

V

) exists only if the data
f
and
g
satisfy the compatibility condition (

C

).
This constraint corresponds to the situation arising in finite dimension when solving linear equations
Ax=b
with
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
where a unique solution exists if and only if
\,\mathrm{d}im(\ker A)=0
. Recall that
\,\mathrm{d}im(\ker A)+\,\mathrm{d}im(\operatorname{Im}A)=d\,.
This generalizes in infinite dimensional spaces via the so called Fredholm alternative (see Theorem
4.38
).

The next theorem offers an alternative formulation of Theorem
4.16
with a relaxed condition on
\nu_{K}
and a different (possibly larger) function space for
g
.

Theorem 4.18
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the classes
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3,4
(see page
3.30
). For some
K\subset\Omega
with positive measure, consider
\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}~\nu(x-y)
for almost all
x\in\Omega^{c}
.

Given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
, there exists a unique solution
u_{*}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
to the following variational problem (

V
∗

′

)

(
V_{*}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{*},v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\nu_{K}(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

Additionally, if
f
and
g
verify the condition (

C
∗

), then all solutions to the problem (

V
∗

) are of the form
u_{*}+c
with
c\in\mathbb{R}
, where we let

(
C_{*}
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)\nu_{K}(y)\mathrm{d}y=0

(
V_{*}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\nu_{K}(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

Moreover, there exists a constant
C:=C(d,\Omega,K,\nu)>0
independent of
f
and
g
such that any solution
w
of (

V
∗

) satisfies the following weak regularity estimate

(4.15)

\displaystyle\|w-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w$}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\right)\,.

In particular, the operator
\Phi_{*}:L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})\to V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
mapping the Neumann data
(f,g)\in L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})

to the unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
of the variational problem
(

V
∗

) is linear, one-to-one, continuous and we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{*}(f,g)\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|(f,g)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\,.

Remark 4.19
.

(i)
It is worthwhile noticing that Theorem
4.16
and Theorem
4.18

remain true with the weight
\nu_{K}
replaced by
\mathring{\nu}_{K}
, where we recall that

\displaystyle\mathring{\nu}_{K}(x)=\int_{K}1\land\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

The reason is that, according to Theorem
3.35
, the embedding
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\mathring{\nu}_{K})
is also continuous.

(ii)
Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, for
g=0
it is worthwhile to see that the variational problem (

V

) coincides with (

V
∗

) and both correspond to the variational (weak) formulation of the homogeneous Neumann problem
Lu=f
in
\Omega
and
\mathcal{N}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
.

Proof of Theorem
4.16
and
Theorem
4.18
.

The existence and the uniqueness of solutions of (

V
′

) and
(

V
∗

′

) on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
plainly spring from the Lax-Milgram lemma using the arguments below. The bilinear form
\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
is continuous on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
. From the Poincaré inequality (
3.40
) (Theorem
3.91
), for some constant
C>0
we have

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C\mathcal{E}(v,v)\quad\quad\text{for all }~~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

Equivalently, this inequality amounts to the coercivity of
\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
and we obtain

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(v,v)\geq\big(1+C\big)^{-1}\|v\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Let us show the continuity of the linear forms involved. For every
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
we have

(4.17)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|v\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

If
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})
along with the continuity of
\operatorname{Tr}:V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
(see Theorem
3.35
) then,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\leq\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})}\|v\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Similarly, if
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
we get

(4.19)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(x)v(x)\nu_{K}(x)\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\|v\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This conclude the existence and the uniqueness of a solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
(resp.
u_{*}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
) to the problem (

V
′

) (resp. (

V
∗

′

)).

For
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
set
v=\widetilde{v}+c^{\prime}
with
c^{\prime}=\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}v\mathrm{d}x$}
so that
\widetilde{v}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
.
In addition, a constant function
w=c
belongs to
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
because
\Omega
is bounded. This means that
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\oplus\mathbb{R}
. From this observation along with the identity

\mathcal{E}(u+c,v+c^{\prime})=\mathcal{E}(u,v)
for all
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and the uniqueness of
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}

solving (

V
′

), it becomes easy to check under the compatibility condition
(

C

) that all solutions of (

V

) are of the form
u+c
.
Analogously, if
u_{*}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
solves (

V
∗

′

) then under condition (

C
∗

) all solutions of (

V
∗

) are of the form
u_{*}+c
.

Conversely, if the problem (

V

) (resp. (

V
∗

))
has a solution, then testing with
v=1
provides the compatibility condition (

C

) (resp. (

C
∗

)).

Next observe that, if
w\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
solves (

V

) then
w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w\mathrm{d}x$}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
solves (

V
′

), and thus by uniqueness we have
u=w-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}w\,\mathrm{d}x$}
. Plunging
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
into (
4.16
)-(
4.18
) and (

V
′

) one easily arrives at (
4.14
). The estimate (
4.15
) is obtained analogously by taking into account the estimate (
4.19
) instead of (
4.19
).
The continuity of the linear mappings
\Phi
and
\Phi_{*}
follow immediately.

∎

16.2
Integrodifferential equations (IDEs) with Dirichlet condition

Assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set. Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable functions. The Dirichlet problem for the operator
L
associated with the data
f
and
g
is to find a measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(
D
)

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}~~\Omega\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad u=g~~\text{on}~~\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega.

Definition 4.20
.

A measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a weak solution or a variational solution of the inhomogeneous Dirichlet problem (

D

) if
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and satisfies

(
V_{0}
)

\displaystyle u-g\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

The weak formulation (

V
0

) makes sense regarding the Green-Gauss formula (
4.5
). Let us now see the corresponding minimization formulation.

Proposition 4.21
.

A function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is solution to (

V
0

) if and only if

(
M_{0}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)=\min_{v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})+g}\mathcal{J}_{0}(v)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)(v(x)-g(x))\,\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{ $v\in g+V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

Proof.
.

Let
u\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})+g
satisfying (

V
0

). Note that for
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})+g
we have
u-v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
thus from the relation (

V
0

) and Cauchy-Schwartz inequality we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(u,u-v)+\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)(u(x)-v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{E}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\int_{\Omega}f(x)(u(x)-g(x))\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)(v(x)-g(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

Equivalently, we have
\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}(v)
and hence
u
verifies (

M
0

). Conversely, assume that
u\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})+g
satisfies (

M
0

). For all
v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
t\in\R
(

M
0

) we have
u+tv\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})+g
which implies
\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)\leq\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+tv)
. Therefore, for fixed
v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
the polynomial of second order
\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+\centerdot v):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle t\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+tv)=\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)+t\left[\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\right]+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\mathcal{E}(v,v)

has a critical point at
t=0
. Hence (

V
0

) is verified since we have

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{J}_{0}(u+tv)-\mathcal{J}_{0}(u)}{t}=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x.

∎

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be bounded and open. Assume that the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
satisfies the mild condition (

I
′

1

).
Given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, there exists a unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the variational problem (

V
0

). Moreover, there exists a constant
C:=C(d,\Omega,\nu)>0
independent of
f
and
g
such that for any
\overline{g}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
extending
g
, we have

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq C\big(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})\big).

In addition, the following weak regularity estimate holds

(4.21)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\big(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\big)\,.

In particular, the operator
\Phi^{\prime}:L^{2}(\Omega)\times T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
mapping the Dirichlet data
(f,g)\in L^{2}(\Omega)\times T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})

to the unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the variational problem
(

V
0

) is linear, one-to-one, continuous and we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{\prime}(f,g)\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|(f,g)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}.

Proof.
.

From the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (
3.41
) (see Theorem
3.95
) one finds a constant
C=C(\Omega,\nu,d)>0
such that the following coercivity holds

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\mathcal{E}(v,v),\quad\text{for all }~~~v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

Let
\overline{g}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
be an extension of
g
. The linear form
v\mapsto-\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},v)+\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
is continuous on
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. It is clear that, the symmetric bilinear form
\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
is also continuous. By Lax-Milgram, there exists a unique
u_{0}\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{0},v)=-\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},v)+\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

Hence
u=u_{0}+\overline{g}
solves (

V
0

) since
u-g\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

With the aid of the coercivity one can easily show that
u
is unique and does not depend on the choice of
\overline{g}
.
Meanwhile, since
u-\overline{g}\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, using the definition of
u
and the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}(u,u-\overline{g})+\mathcal{E}(u,\overline{g})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}f(x)(u(x)-\overline{g}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{E}(u,\overline{g})

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|u-\overline{g}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u,u)^{1/2}\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathcal{E}(u-\overline{g},u-\overline{g})^{1/2}+\mathcal{E}(u,u)^{1/2}\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)^{1/2}(C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2})+C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}(C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2})^{2}+C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}(C^{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g}))+2C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}(C^{2}+1)(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g}))+C(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}(C+1)^{2}(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g})),

where all along we used the young inequality
|ab|\leq\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
. After cancellation we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq(C+1)^{2}(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\overline{g},\overline{g}).

Hence, (
4.20
) follows. Next, since
u-\overline{g}\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality (
3.41
) implies

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|u-\overline{g}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+2\|\overline{g}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\mathcal{E}(u-\overline{g},u-\overline{g})+2\|\overline{g}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 4C\big(\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\|\overline{g}\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big).

Recalling that
\|g\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}=\inf\big\{\|\overline{g}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}:\operatorname{Tr}(\overline{g})=g\big\}
, the estimate (
4.21
) follows as well by combining (
4.20
) with the above estimate.
∎

Next, we establish a nonlocal version of Weyl’s decomposition lemma which the local case asserts that
H^{1}(\Omega)=H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\oplus H_{\gamma}(\Omega)
where
H_{\gamma}(\Omega)=\big\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):-\Delta u=\gamma u,\text{ in $\Omega$}\text{ and }\,u=0\text{ on $\partial\Omega$}\big\}.

Proposition 4.23

(
Weyl’s lemma
)
.

For
\gamma>0
define the scalar product
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}(u,u)+\gamma(u,u)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
and the space of
\gamma-
Harmonic functions by

V_{\nu,\gamma}(\Omega|\R^{d})=\big\{u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,Lu=\gamma u\text{ in $\Omega$}\,\text{ and }\,u=0\text{ on $\Omega^{c}$}\big\}.

The following
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\cdot,\cdot)
-orthogonal decomposition is true

V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})=V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus V_{\nu,\gamma}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

Proof.
.

Let
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We know there is a unique
u^{\prime}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
u^{\prime\prime}=u-u^{\prime}\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(u^{\prime},v)=0
for all
v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
that is
u^{\prime}\in V_{\nu,\gamma}(\Omega|\R^{d})
. In particular we have
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})=0
, i.e.
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
are
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\cdot,\cdot)
-orthogonal. Thus,
u=u^{\prime}+u^{\prime\prime}\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus V_{\nu,\gamma}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

∎

16.3
Integrodifferential equations (IDEs) with mixed condition

Here we treat a special IDE whose complement condition is a mixture of the Dirichlet and the Neumann complement conditions. Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open set. Let
D\subset\Omega^{c}
and
N\subset\Omega^{c}
be measurable sets such that
|D|,|N|>0
,
\Omega^{c}=D\cup N
and
|D\cap N|=0
.
Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
g_{D}:D\to\mathbb{R}
and
g_{N}:N\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable functions. The mixed problem for the operator
L
associated with the data
f
,
g_{D}
and
g_{N}
is to find a measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(4.22)

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}~~~\Omega,\quad u=g_{D}\quad\text{on}~~D\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{N}u=g_{N}~~~\text{on}~~N.

The local counterpart of the problem (
4.22
) for the Laplace operator is to find a measurable function
u:\Omega\to\R
such that

(4.23)

\displaystyle-\Delta u=f\quad\text{in}~~~\Omega,\quad u=g_{D}\quad\text{on}~~D\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}=g_{N}~~~\text{on}~~N.

where the functions
g_{D}
and
g_{N}
are respectively defined on
D\subset\partial\Omega
and
N\subset\partial\Omega
so that
D\cup N=\partial\Omega
with
|D\cap N|=0
. As for the Neumann and the Dirichlet complement conditions, a weak solution of (
4.22
) is defined as follows.

Definition 4.24
.

A measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a weak solution or a variational solution of the inhomogeneous mixed problem (
4.22
) if
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and satisfies

(4.24)

\displaystyle u-g_{D}\in V_{D}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{N}g_{N}(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{D}\,

where
V_{D}=\big\{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):v=0~~\text{a.e. on D}\big\}
.

Theorem 4.25
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3,4
(see page
3.30
). For a set
K\subset\Omega
with positive measure, assume
\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}~\nu(x-y)>0~a.e
for almost
x\in\Omega^{c}
.
Then given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
g_{D}\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and
g_{N}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})
, there exists a unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the variational problem (
4.24
).

Moreover, there exists a constant
C:=C(d,\Omega,\nu)>0
independent of
f
and
g
such that
u
satisfies the following weak regularity estimate

(4.25)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g_{D}\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}+\|g_{N}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})}\right)\,.

Proof.
.

Since
|D|>0
, by Theorem
3.93
the Poincaré inequality holds on
V_{D}
, i.e. there exists a constant
C=C(d,\Omega,D,\nu,)
such that

\displaystyle\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\mathcal{E}(v,v)\qquad\text{for all $v\in V_{D}$}.

Therefore, the proof follows by adapting the proofs of Theorem
4.16
and Theorem
4.22
.
∎

16.4
Integrodifferential equations (IDEs) with Robin condition

We now treat the Robin type problem with respect to the nonlocal operator
L
on
\Omega
. In the classical setting for the Laplace operator, the Robin boundary problem also known as Fourier boundary problem or third boundary problem is a combination of the Dirichlet and Neumann boundary problem and is often given as follows.

\displaystyle-\Delta u=f\quad\text{in}\quad\Omega\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}+bu=g\quad\text{on}\quad\partial\Omega.

Here
f:\Omega\to\R
and
b,g:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
are given measurable functions. For a more extensive study on the local Robin boundary value problems see
[
Dan00
,
Nit14
]
. Analogously, in the nonlocal set up, we assume that
b,h:\Omega^{c}\to\mathbb{R}
are measurable functions. We now formulate the Robin problem with respect to
L
with data
f,h
and
b
. It consists of finding a measurable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(4.26)

\displaystyle Lu=f\quad\text{in}\quad\Omega\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{N}u+bu=h\quad\text{on}\quad\Omega^{c}.

For the sake of simplicity we assume
b(x)=\beta(x)\nu_{K}(x)
and
h(x)=g(x)\nu_{K}(x)
,
x\in\Omega^{c}
, for a measurable set
K\subset\Omega
with a positive measure. We recall that
\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}~\nu(x-y)
. If
\beta=0
one recovers a Neumann problem and if
\beta\to\infty
then it leads to a Dirichlet problem.
As for the weak formulation of the Neumann problem, we define a weak solution to
(
4.26
)
as follows.

Definition 4.26
.

A function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a weak solution of the Robin problem (
4.26
) if

(4.27)

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y~~\text{for all }~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where we introduce the bilinear form

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}(u,v)+\int_{\Omega^{c}}u(y)v(y)\beta(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Theorem 4.27
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
).
For a set
K\subset\Omega
with a positive measure, let
\nu_{K}(x)=\operatorname{essinf}_{y\in K}~\nu(x-y)
for almost every
x\in\Omega^{c}
. Let
\beta:\Omega^{c}\to[0,\infty)
be essentially bounded such that
\beta\nu_{K}>0~a.e
on a subset of positive of
\Omega^{c}
.

Then given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
, there exists a unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the variational problem (
4.27
). Moreover, there exists a constant
C:=C(d,\Omega,\nu,K,\beta)
independent of
f
and
g
such that

(4.28)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}\right).

In particular, the operator
\Psi:L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})\to V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
mapping the Robin data
(f,g)\in L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
to the unique solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the variational problem (
4.27
) is linear, one-to-one, continuous and we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi(f,g)\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|(f,g)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})}.

Proof.
.

First of all, we claim that the form
Q_{\beta}(\cdot,\cdot)
is coercive on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Assume it is not true then for each
n\geq 1
there exists
u_{n}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
preferably,
\|u_{n}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1
such that

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u_{n},u_{n})<\frac{1}{2^{n}}\,.

In virtue of compactness Theorem
3.89
,
(u_{n})_{n}
converges up to a subsequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to some
u
. It turns out that
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
, since
\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and for all
n\geq 1
,
\|u_{n}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1
. That
\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
imply that
u_{n}
converges to
u
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{E}(u,u)=0
and thus
u
is constant almost everywhere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. On the other hand, since
\beta
is bounded and the embedding
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
is continuous, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}u^{2}(y)\beta(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\Omega^{c}}u_{n}^{2}(y)\beta(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+2\|\beta\|_{\infty}\int_{\Omega^{c}}(u_{n}(y)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2Q_{\beta}(u_{n},u_{n})+C\|u_{n}-u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,.

From this, we have
u=0
since we know that
u
is a constant function and
\beta\nu_{K}>0
almost everywhere on a set of positive measure
U\subset\Omega^{c}
on which
u
vanishes. This negates the fact that
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
and hence our initial assumption was wrong. Therefore, there exists a constant
C=C(d,\Omega,\nu,K,\beta)>0
such that

(4.29)

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,u)\geq C\|u\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{for all }~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Beside this, it is easy to prove that the remaining assumptions of the Lax-Milgram lemma are met in order to guarantee the existence and uniqueness of a solution to (
4.27
). The estimate
(
4.28
) is a direct consequence of (
4.29
).
∎

Comments

A separate objective was to show that the function spaces studied in Chapter
3
are adapted for the study of IntegroDifferential Equations (IDEs) on domains with complement conditions similar to those present in the study of boundary values problems associated with elliptic Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) of second order. It is noteworthy to highlight that our context, however easily extends to the setting where the interacting kernel
\nu(x-y)
is substituted by
an elliptic symmetric kernel
k:\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
, i.e. satisfying the following weak elliptic condition:

(E_{k})
: There exists a constant
\Lambda>1
such that for every measurable function
u:\R^{d}\to\R
we have

(
E_{k}
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,u)\leq\Lambda\mathcal{E}(u,u),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,u)=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}k(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

It is worth noting that choosing a kernel
k:\R^{d}\times\R^{d}\to[0,\infty]
in the nonlocal setting is the same as choosing a matrix
A:\R^{d}\to\R^{d^{2}}
,
A(x)=(a_{ij}(x))_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
in the local setting. A concrete explanation is encoded in Chapter
2
and we elaborate more on this fact in Chapter
5
.

Let us mention that the Dirichlet problem for nonlocal operators has been extensively studied throughout the literature. The Dirichlet problem for nonsymmetric kernels is thoroughly carried out in
[
FKV15
]
. See also
[
RO15
]
and further references therein. The Neumann problem for nonsymmetric kernel requires some additional knowledge on a way to control the set of functions
u
on which the linear form
\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,\cdot)
is degenerated. This is not straightforward since if
k
is nonsymmetric, one can easily check that
\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}^{k_{s}}(u,u)
where
k_{s}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\big(k(x,y)+k(y,x\big)
is the symmetric part of
k
.

If
\nu
is fully supported on
\R^{d}
and
k
is symmetric and satisfying (

E
k

), then
\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,\cdot)
is degenerated if and only if
u
is a constant. From this the solvability of the nonlocal Neumann for a symmetric kernel
k
satisfying (

E
k

) easily extends since in this case the compatibility condition (

C

) persists and it thus suffices to work on the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
.

Furthermore, when working on the space
V_{k}(\Omega|\R^{d})
of measurable functions
u:\R^{d}\to\R
for which
\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,u)<\infty
in replacement of the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the lower elliptic bound condition (

L

-

E
k

) below would be sufficient as it guaranties the coercivity of the form
\mathcal{E}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
via Poincaré type inequalities.

(
L
-
E_{k}
): There exists a constant
\Lambda>1
such that for every measurable function
u:\R^{d}\to\R
we have

(
L
-
E_{k}
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq\mathcal{E}^{k}(u,u).

17
Spectral decomposition of nonlocal operators

The main subject of interest in this section is the spectral decomposition of a singular nonlocal operator of the
form
L
on a bounded domain
\Omega
subject to the Neumann, Dirichlet or Robin boundary condition.
Precisely, we are concerned about the pairs
(\lambda,u)
consisting of a real number
\lambda
called an eigenvalue and a function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
called eigenfunction so that (in the strong sense) we have
Lu=\lambda u
on
\Omega
plus some additional conditions which we formally consider in the next definitions. These spectral decomposition have some properties of important interest which we will
explore here and later. Let us first define the weak version eigenvalues and eigenfunctions.

Definition 4.28

(Neumann eigenvalue of
L
)
.

A non zero function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
will be called a Neumann eigenfunction of the operator
L
on
\Omega
if there exists a real number
\mu
which is the eigenvalue associated with
u
such that the following holds

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\mu\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

We will formally write
Lu=\mu u
in
\Omega
and
\mathcal{N}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
which in
fact corresponds to the weak formulation in (
4.30
) provided that
u
is regular enough.

It is worth noticing that if
u
is a Neumann eigenfunction of
L
with associated eigenvalue
\mu
then automatically, either
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
when
\mu\neq 0
or else
\mu=0
and the constant functions
u=c,~c\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
as the related eigenfunctions.

Definition 4.29

(Dirichlet eigenvalue of
L
)
.

A non zero function
u\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
will be called a Dirichlet eigenfunction of the operator
L
on
\Omega
if there exists a real number
\lambda
which is the eigenvalue associated with
u
such that the following holds

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\lambda\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

We will formally write
Lu=\lambda u
in
\Omega
and
u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
which in fact corresponds to the weak formulation in (
4.31
) provided that
u
is regular enough.

Definition 4.30

(Robin eigenvalue of
L
)
.

Let
\beta:\Omega^{c}\to[0,\infty]
be a measurable function and
K\subset\Omega
be a measurable set such that
|K|>0
. A non zero function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
will be called a Robin eigenfunction of the operator
L
on
\Omega
if there exists a real number
\gamma(\beta)
which is the eigenvalue associated with
u
such that the following holds

(4.32)

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\gamma(\beta)\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,,

where we recall that

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}(u,v)+\int_{\Omega^{c}}u(y)v(y)\beta(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

We will formally write
Lu=\gamma(\beta)u
in
\Omega
and
\mathcal{N}u+\beta\nu_{K}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
which in fact corresponds to the weak formulation in (
4.32
) provided that
u
is regular enough.

Theorem 4.31
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
. Then there exist a sequence
(\phi_{n})_{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
and an increasing sequence of real numbers
0=\mu_{0}<\mu_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{n}\leq\cdots.
such that
\mu_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and each
\phi_{n}
is a Neumann eigenfunction of
L
whose corresponding eigenvalue is
\mu_{n}
. Each eigenvalue is listed with its geometrical multiplicity.

Proof.
.

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
let us denote
u_{f_{k}}=\Phi_{0}(f_{k})=\Phi(f_{k},0)\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp},~k=1,2
the unique solution of (

V
′

) with Neumann data
f=f_{k}
and
g=0
. Precisely,

(4.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Phi_{0}(f_{k}),v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{k}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

Testing (
4.33
) against
v=\Phi_{0}(f_{2})
and
v=\Phi_{0}(f_{1})
successively when
k=1
and
k=2
yields

\displaystyle\big(f_{1},\Phi_{0}(f_{2})\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\mathcal{E}(\Phi_{0}(f_{1}),\Phi_{0}(f_{2}))=\mathcal{E}(\Phi_{0}(f_{2}),\Phi_{0}(f_{1}))=\big(f_{2},\Phi_{0}(f_{1})\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Therefore, the operator
R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{0}:L^{2}(\Omega)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{0}}V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\xrightarrow{R_{\Omega}}L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
is compact (by Theorem
3.89
) and symmetric hence self-adjoint. Recall that
R_{\Omega}
denotes the restriction operator to
\Omega
