. It is a fact from the spectral theory of compact self-adjoint operators that
L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
possesses an orthonormal basis
(e_{n})_{n}
whose elements are eigenfunctions of
R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{0}
and the sequence of the corresponding
eigenvalues are non-negative real numbers
(r_{n})_{n}
which we assume are ordered in the decreasing order,
r_{1}\geq r_{2}\geq\cdots\geq r_{n}\geq\cdots 0
such that
r_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Precisely, for each
n\geq 1
,
R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{0}(e_{n})=r_{n}e_{n}
or simply write
\Phi_{0}(e_{n})=r_{n}e_{n}
a.e in
\Omega
. Combining the latter relation with the definition of
\Phi_{0}(e_{n})
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Phi_{0}(e_{n}),v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}e_{n}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x=r^{-1}_{n}\int_{\Omega}\Phi_{0}(e_{n})(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

Equivalently, letting
\mu_{n}=r^{-1}_{n}
and
\phi_{n}=\Phi_{0}(e_{n})/\|\Phi_{0}(e_{n})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=r_{n}^{-1}\Phi_{0}(e_{n})
which is clearly an element of
\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\phi_{n},v)

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{n}(x)v(x)\mathrm{d}x\,\quad\mbox{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

Hereby, along with
\mu_{0}=0
and
\phi_{0}=|\Omega|^{-1}
provides the sequences sought for.
Now if we assume
\mu_{1}=0
then we have
\phi_{1}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{E}(\phi_{1},v)=0
for all
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
. In particular
\mathcal{E}(\phi_{1},\phi_{1})=0
, i.e
\phi_{1}
is a constant function in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
necessarily
\phi_{1}=0
since
\phi_{1}
has zero mean over
\Omega
. We have therefore reached a contradiction as
\phi_{1}
is supposed to be an eigenfunction, i.e.
\phi_{1}\neq 0
. Thus,
\mu_{1}>0
and this completes the proof.
∎

Next, by exploiting
[
Nic11
]
, we characterize the eigenpairs of the operator
L
using the Rayleigh quotient.

Theorem 4.32
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the
couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
) . The following assertions are true.

1.

There exists
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
which is a global minimum of
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}(v,v)
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
subject to the constraint
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
. In short,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)=\min\{\mathcal{E}(v,v):~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp},~\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}.

2.

Let
u
be a local extremum (minimum or maximum) of
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}(v,v)
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
subject to the constraint
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
, then
u
is an eigenfunction of
L
subject to Neumann condition. Moreover, the corresponding eigenvalue is a strictly positive real number given by
\mu=\mathcal{E}(u,u)
.

Proof.
.

To prove
(i)
, note that the quantity
m=\inf\{\mathcal{E}(v,v):v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp},\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}
exists according to the Poincaré inequality (
3.40
). Next, let
(u_{n})_{n}\subset V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
be a minimizing sequence such that for all
n\geq 1
,
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
and

m\leq\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq m+\frac{1}{2^{n}}.

The sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
is clearly bounded in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Therefore, according to Theorem
3.89
, there exists
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that a subsequence of
(u_{n})_{n}
weakly converges in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and strongly converges in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to
u
. Recalling that
\|\cdot\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))}=\|\cdot\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
, the weak convergence combined with
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
yields,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}(m+\frac{1}{2^{n}})=m.

That is,
\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq m
. In addition, the strong convergence implies that
\fint_{\Omega}u=0
so that
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
and
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
. It follows that

m=\mathcal{E}(u,u)=\min\{\mathcal{E}(v,v):~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp},~\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}.

Let us now tackle
(ii)
. On the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
, let us define
\Lambda:v\mapsto\mathcal{E}(v,v)
and
G:v\mapsto\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-1
. So that
u
is a local extremum of
\Lambda
subject to the constraint
G(u)=0
. It is routine to show that
\Lambda
and
G
are continuously Fréchet differentiable. Furthermore, the first variation of
\Lambda
and
G
reveals that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(u)(v)=2\mathcal{E}(u,v)\quad\text{and}\quad G^{\prime}(u)(v)=2(u,v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\qquad\text{for all }~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}.

Moreover,
G^{\prime}(u)\neq 0
since
G^{\prime}(u)(u)=2
, given that
G(u)=0
. Applying the Lagrange multiplier Theorem
4.7
with the space
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
serving the role of the Banach space,
one finds a real number
\mu
such that
\Lambda^{\prime}(u)=\mu G^{\prime}(u)
. Equivalently, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\mu\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all }~v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}.

We have reached the relation
4.30
claiming
u
as a Neumann eigenfunction of
L
and we have
\mu=\mathcal{E}(u,u)
since
G(u)=0
.
If
\mu=\mathcal{E}(u,u)=0
then
u
is constant with a vanishing mean over
\Omega
since
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
.
Necessarily,
u=0
which goes against the fact that
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
. Thus
\mu>0
which achieves the proof.

∎

Remark 4.33
.

The assertions
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
4.32
reveal that a global minimum
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
of
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}(v,v)
subject to the constraint
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
exists. Moreover, this minimum is a Neumann eigenfunction of
L
whose corresponding eigenvalue is
\mu=\mathcal{E}(u,u)>0
. In fact
\mu
is the smallest positive Neumann eigenvalue of
L
. For this reason, we shall call this eigenvalue
\mu_{1}
and one of the associated eigenfunction
\varphi_{1}
. Note that the smallest eigenvalue is
\mu_{0}=0
and the associated eigenfunctions are constant functions
\varphi_{0}=c
with
c\neq 0
.

Another important remark is that, minimizing
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}(v,v)
on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
subject to

\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
is equivalent as minimizing the quotient
v\mapsto\tfrac{\mathcal{E}(v,v)}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}
with
v
running in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
. This
is the
Rayleigh quotient
. Wherefore, we have the following explicit representation

\displaystyle\mu_{1}=\inf_{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{E}(v,v)}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}~\Big\}=\min_{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}}\{\mathcal{E}(v,v):~\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}.

Next, we explain how to find the other eigenvalues by employing a similar technique.
The following theorem is a refinement Theorem
4.31
providing a more constructive approach to the eigenvalues
(\mu_{n})_{n}
.

Theorem 4.34
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support. Assume that the
couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
). Then there exist a sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of positive real numbers and a sequence of functions
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for each
n\geq 0
the pair
(\mu_{n},\varphi_{n})
satisfies

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mu_{n}=\mathcal{E}(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n})=\min_{v\in V_{n}}\{\mathcal{E}(v,v):~\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}=\inf_{v\in V_{n}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{E}(v,v)}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}~\Big\}.

Here we denote
V_{0}=V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\varphi_{0}=|\Omega|^{-1}
and for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle V_{n}=\{\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{n-1}\}^{\perp}=\{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):(v,\varphi_{i})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0,~~i=0,1,\cdots,n-1\}.

Moreover, the following assertions hold true.

1.

For each
n\geq 0,

\mu_{n}
is a Neumann eigenvalue of
L
the operator
L
constrained with the Neumann boundary condition
\mathcal{N}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
and the associated eigenfunction is
\varphi_{n}.

2.

0=\mu_{0}<\mu_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{n}\leq\cdots
and
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}=\infty
.

3.

The family
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.
.

Obviously, we have
V_{0}=V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mu_{0}=0
and
\varphi_{0}=|\Omega|^{-1}
. We also have
V_{1}=V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}=\{\varphi_{0}\}^{\perp}=\{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):\int_{\Omega}v=0\}
.
The existence of
(\mu_{1},\varphi_{1})
clearly springs from Theorem

4.31
.
Repeating this procedure inductively by modifying appropriately with
V_{1}
replaced by
V_{2},V_{3},\cdots
one obtains the existence of
\mu_{n}
and
\varphi_{n}
for each
n\geq 1
.
The argument is based on the grounds that for each
n\geq 1
the linear forms
v\mapsto(v,\varphi_{i})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}~i=0,1,\cdots,n-1
are continuous on
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
which implies that
V_{n}
is a closed subspace of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and hence can be thought as a Hilbert space in its own right.
Thus (
4.34
) is verified and
(i)
follows by identical argument as in Theorem
4.31
.
It is worth noticing that by construction all elements of the sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n}
are mutually orthogonal in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Besides,
V_{0}\supset V_{1}\supset V_{2}\supset\cdots\supset V_{n}\supset\cdots
wherefrom it follows that
0=\mu_{0}\leq\mu_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{n}\leq\cdots
thus we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}=B\quad\text{with }\quad B=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{n}.

Assume
B
is a real number, i.e.
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{n}=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{E}(\varphi_{n},\varphi_{n})=B<\infty
. For all
n\geq 1
we also have
\|\varphi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
.
In other words, the sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n}
is bounded in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In virtue of the compactness Theorem
3.89
there exists a subsequence
(\varphi_{n_{k}})_{k}
converging in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Which is impossible since by orthogonality

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{n_{k+1}}-\varphi_{n_{k}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\|\varphi_{n_{k+1}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\varphi_{n_{k}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=2\quad\text{for all}\quad k\geq 0.

Consequently the assumption that
B<\infty
is impossible hence

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}=B=\infty
. We have proved
(ii)
. Now to show
(iii)
we solely need to show that the span of
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. For
\varepsilon>0
small enough consider
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|f-g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\varepsilon
. Since the elements
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are mutually orthogonal, the Bessel inequality tells us that

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{k=0}^{n}(f-g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\Big|\leq\|f-g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\varepsilon.

Therefore, we have the following estimate for all
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\Big\|f-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(f,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|f-g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\Big\|g-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle+\Big\|\sum_{k=0}^{n}(f-g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(4.35)

\displaystyle<2\varepsilon+\Big\|g-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

For
n\geq 1
, consider
g_{n}=g-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}.

Since
g\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by definition of
(\mu_{j},\varphi_{j})
we have

\mathcal{E}(g,\varphi_{j})=\mu_{j}(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\qquad\text{for all}~~~0\leq j\leq n.

Also by orthogonality, we have

\mathcal{E}(\varphi_{k},\varphi_{j})=\mu_{k}(\varphi_{k},\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\mu_{k}\delta_{k,j}\qquad\text{for all}~~~0\leq j,k\leq n.

Whence, for
0\leq j\leq n
, combining the above relations we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(g_{n},\varphi_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(g,\varphi_{j})-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathcal{E}(\varphi_{k},\varphi_{j})

\displaystyle=\mu_{j}(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\mu_{k}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\delta_{k,j}

\displaystyle=\mu_{j}(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\mu_{j}(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0

which yields
\mathcal{E}(g_{n},g-g_{n})=\mathcal{E}\big(g_{n},\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{j}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{j})=0.

Therefore we get

(4.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(g,g)=\mathcal{E}(g_{n},g_{n})+\mathcal{E}(g-g_{n},g-g_{n})\geq\mathcal{E}(g_{n},g_{n}).

Again by orthogonality we have

\displaystyle(g_{n},\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}(\varphi_{k},\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\delta_{k,j}

\displaystyle=(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-(g,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0.

So that
g_{n}\in V_{n+1}=\{\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{n}\}^{\perp}
. By the expression of
\mu_{n+1}
together with (
4.36
) we get

\displaystyle\mu_{n+1}\|g_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\mathcal{E}(g_{n},g_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}(g,g).

Since this holds for arbitrarily chosen
n\geq 1
the fact
\mu_{n+1}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
forces
\|g_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
that is

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|g-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(g,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0.

Inserting this in our initial estimate (
4.35
) yields

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|f-\sum_{k=0}^{n}(f,\varphi_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{k}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq 2\varepsilon.

Wherefore letting
\varepsilon\to 0
leads to the relation

\displaystyle f=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(f,\varphi_{n})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\varphi_{n}\qquad\textrm{in the $L^{2}(\Omega)$ sense}.

This gives precisely the density result we are striving for and the proof is completed.
∎

By analogous arguments as in Theorem
4.34
one is able to prove the following similar result when the operator
L
is subject to Dirichlet or Robin boundary condition.

Theorem 4.35
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set and let the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
be the density of a symmetric Lévy measure. Assume the conditions (

I
2

) and (

I
′

1

) (see page

I
2

) hold. Then there exist a sequence of positive real numbers
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a sequence of functions
(\psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for each
n\geq 1
the pair
(\lambda_{n},\psi_{n})
satisfies the relations

(4.37)

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}=\mathcal{E}(\psi_{n},\psi_{n})=\min_{v\in V_{n}}\{\mathcal{E}(v,v):~\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}=\inf_{v\in V_{n}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{E}(v,v)}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}~\Big\}.

Here we denote
V_{1}=V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle V_{n}=\{\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{n-1}\}^{\perp}=\{v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):(v,\psi_{i})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0,~~i=1,\cdots,n-1\}.

Moreover, the following assertions hold true.

1.

Each
\lambda_{n}
is a Dirichlet eigenvalue of the operator
L
constrained with the Dirichlet boundary condition
u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
and the associated eigenfunction
\psi_{n}.

2.

0<\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{n}\leq\cdots
and
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}=\infty
.

3.

The family
(\psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Theorem 4.36
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set and let the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]

be the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support. Assume that the
couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
). Let
K\subset\Omega

be a measurable set such that
|K|>0
and let
\beta:\Omega^{c}\to[0,\infty]

be a measurable function such that
\beta\nu_{K}>0~~a.e
on a set of positive measure.
Then there exist a sequence of positive real numbers
(\gamma(\beta)_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a sequence of functions
(\chi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for each
n\geq 1
the pair
(\chi_{n},\gamma(\beta)_{n})
satisfies the relations

(4.38)

\displaystyle\gamma(\beta)_{n}

\displaystyle=Q_{\beta}(\chi_{n},\chi_{n})=\min_{v\in V_{n}}\{Q_{\beta}(v,v):~\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1\}=\inf_{v\in V_{n}}\Big\{\frac{Q_{\beta}(v,v)}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}~\Big\}

with

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}(u,v)+\int_{\Omega^{c}}u(y)v(y)\beta(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Here we denote
V_{1}=V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle V_{n}=\{\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\cdots,\chi_{n-1}\}^{\perp}=\{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):(v,\chi_{i})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0,~~i=1,\cdots,n-1\}.

Moreover the following assertions hold true.

1.

Each
\gamma(\beta)_{n}
is a Robin eigenvalue of the operator
L
constrained with the Robin boundary condition
\mathcal{N}u+\beta\nu_{K}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
and the associated eigenfunction is
\chi_{n}
.

2.

0<\gamma(\beta)_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\gamma(\beta)_{n}\leq\cdots
and
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\gamma(\beta)_{n}=\infty
.

3.

The family
(\chi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Remark 4.37
.

Recall that it is possible to replace
\nu_{K}
by
\mathring{\nu}_{K}
in the foregoing. It is worthwhile mentioning that the constants
\mu_{1}>0
and
\lambda_{1}>0
respectively satisfy the following Poincaré type inequalities.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,u)\geq\mu_{1}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\,\,\text{for all}~u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}(u,u)\geq\lambda_{1}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\,\,\text{for all}~u\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In addition, the following comparison holds

\displaystyle\mu_{n-1}\leq\gamma_{n}(\beta)\leq\lambda_{n},\quad\text{for all $n\geq 1$}.

In short, one merely writes Neumann
\leq
Robin
\leq
Dirichlet.

18
Helmholtz equation for nonlocal operators

There are several applications of spectral decomposition. We shall illuminate this through the Helmholtz type equations subject with the Neumann complement condition. The other types of complement conditions can be derived likewise. The Helmholtz equation with the Neumann condition is of the form

(4.39)

\displaystyle Lu-\lambda u=f\quad\text{in}~~~\Omega\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\mathcal{N}u=0~~\text{on}~~\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega.

Here
\lambda
is a potential and for brevity we assume that
\lambda\in\R
. Define
\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\lambda(u,v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.
Obviously, the weak formulation of (
4.39
) consists of finding
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(4.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

To study (
4.40
), we will need the so called Fredholm alternative.

Theorem 4.38

(Appendix D, Theorem 5,
[
Eva10
]
)
.

Let
K:H\to H
be a compact operator on a Hilbert space
(H,(\cdot,\cdot)_{H})
. The following assertions are true.

1.

\ker(I-K)
has finite dimension and
\operatorname{Im}(I-K)
is closed.

2.

If
K^{*}
denotes the adjoint of
K
then
\ker(I-K)=\operatorname{Im}(I-K^{*})^{\perp}
and
\dim\operatorname{Im}(I-K)=\dim\operatorname{Im}(I-K^{*})
.

3.

\ker(I-K)=\{0\}
if and only if
\operatorname{Im}(I-K)=H
.

Theorem 4.39
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open set and the function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is the density of a symmetric Lévy measure with full support such that the couple
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
). Let
0=\mu_{0}<\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{n}\leq\cdots
be the eigenvalues of
L
subject to the Neumann condition (c.f Theorem
4.31
). Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. The following assertions are true.

1.

If
\lambda\in\R^{d}\setminus\{\mu_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
there exists a unique function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
such that

(4.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}$}.

Moreover, there is a constant
C:=C(d,\Omega,\nu,\lambda)>0
such that

(4.42)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,.

2.

Let
j\geq 1
. Assume
\lambda=\mu_{j}
and let
\varphi_{j},\varphi_{j+1},\cdots,\varphi_{j+r_{j}}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
be the linear independent eigenfunctions corresponding to the eigenvalue
\mu_{j}
. Consider the direct decomposition
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}=W_{j}\oplus W_{j}^{\perp}
where
W_{j}=\operatorname{span}\{\varphi_{j},\varphi_{j+1},\cdots,\varphi_{j+r_{j}}\}
and
W_{j}^{\perp}=\{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}:(v,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\cdots=(v,\varphi_{j+r_{j}})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0\}.

Then there exists a unique
u_{j}\in W_{j}^{\perp}
such that

(4.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-\mu_{j}}(u_{j},v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in W_{j}^{\perp}$}.

Moreover, there exists
C:=C(d,\Omega,\nu,\mu_{j})>0
independent of
f
such that the following estimate holds

(4.44)

\displaystyle\|u_{j}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,.

Furthermore, the problem (
4.41
) has a solution if and only if
(f,\varphi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\cdots=(f,\varphi_{j+r_{j}})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0
. In this case, all solutions to the problem (
4.41
) are of the form
u_{j}+\varphi
for any
\varphi\in W_{j}
.

Proof.
.

(i)
Consider the linear operator
\Phi_{0}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
mapping
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
to
u=\Phi_{0}f\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
the unique solution of the problem (

V
′

) with
g=0
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}$}.

As in the proof of Theorem
4.31
, we know that the operator
S=R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{0}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
is compact. Let
w\in\ker(I-\lambda S)
that is
\lambda Sw=w
. Equivalently, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Phi_{0}w,v)=\lambda\int_{\Omega}\Phi_{0}w(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}$}.

Necessarily,
w=0
since by assumption,
\lambda
is not a Neumann eigenvalue. Hence
\ker(I-\lambda S)=\{0\}
. According to Theorem
4.38
(iii), we have

\operatorname{Im}(I-\lambda S)=L^{2}(\Omega)
. Hence for
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, we can write
f=w-\lambda Sw=w-\lambda R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{0}w
for some
w\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. We have
w=f+\lambda R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{0}w
. Let
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
, by definition of
\Phi_{0}w
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Phi_{0}w,v)=\int_{\Omega}w(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\lambda\int_{\Omega}\Phi_{0}w(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Therefore the function
u=\Phi_{0}w
, satisfies (
4.41
). Furthermore, the uniqueness of
u
is due to the fact that
\lambda
is not a Neumann eigenvalue.
Next, consider the operator
T_{\lambda}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
such that for
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
u=T_{\lambda}f
solves (
4.41
). Let
(f_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
tending to zero such that
u_{n}=\Phi f_{n}
converges to
u
in
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
then for
n\geq 1,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{n},v)-\lambda\int_{\Omega}u_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Letting
n\to\infty
, this amounts to
\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u,v)=0
.
Necessarily
u=0
since by assumption
\lambda
is
not a Neumann eigenvalue. Therefore, in virtue of the closed graph Theorem
T_{\lambda}
is a bounded operator.
There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|T_{\lambda}h\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|h\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\quad\text{for all $h\in L^{2}(\Omega)$.}

Hence the estimate (
4.42
) follows.

(ii)
Assume
\lambda=\mu_{j}
. The procedure is analogous to the proof of
(i)
. Consider the linear operator
\Phi_{j}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to W_{j}^{\perp}
mapping
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
to
u_{j}=\Phi_{j}f
the unique function in
W_{j}^{\perp}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{j},v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in W_{j}^{\perp}$}.

This makes sense since
W_{j}^{\perp}
is a closed subspace of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
and hence Lax-Milgram’s lemma can be applied on
W^{\perp}_{j}
. Similarly, we can show that
S=R_{\Omega}\circ\Phi_{j}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
is compact. Moreover,
\ker(I-\mu_{j}S)=\{0\}
. Indeed, if
w\in\ker(I-\mu_{j}S)
then since
\mu_{j}
is a Neumann eigenvalue we can easily show that

w=\lambda Sw\in W_{j}\cap W_{j}^{\perp}=\{0\}
.
Again by Fredholm alternative (Theorem
4.38

(iii)
) for
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
there exists
u_{j}\in W_{j}
such that
f=u_{j}-\mu_{j}Su_{j}
and
u_{j}
is unique since
W_{j}\cap W_{j}^{\perp}=\{0\}
. We get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-\mu_{j}}(u_{j},v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in W_{j}$}.

One can also show the estimate (
4.44
) by applying the closed graph theorem as above. Let us recall that
W_{j}
is the eigenspace associated with the eigenvalue
\mu_{j}
. If
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
solves (
4.41
) then since
\mathcal{E}_{-\mu_{j}}(u,\varphi)=0
for all
\varphi\in W_{j}
. In particular this implies that
\big(f,\varphi_{j}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=,\cdots,=\big(f,\varphi_{j+r_{j}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0
. The converse remains true. Indeed, every
\varphi\in W_{j}
is an eigenfunction associated with the eigenvalue
\mu_{j}
, i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{-\mu_{j}}(\varphi,v)=0\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}$}.
Accounting the fact that
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}=W_{j}\oplus W_{j}^{\perp}
one easily show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-\mu_{j}}(u_{j}+\varphi,v)=0\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}$}.

Conversely, if
u
solves (
4.41
) then one can easily show that
u-u_{j}\in W_{j}
and
f\in W_{j}^{\perp}
. Thus all solutions of (
4.41
) are of the form
u_{j}+\varphi
with
\varphi\in W_{j}
if and only if
f\in W_{j}^{\perp}
.
∎

Remark 4.40
.

It is easy to deduce solutions of the problem (
4.40
) form those of the problem (
4.41
). Note that the situation where
\lambda=0
is already encoded in the variational problem (

V

). Furthermore, in a similar manner one is able to treat the Helmholtz type equation associated with other types of complement value conditions.

19
Profiling solutions of evolution of IDEs

In this section the spectral decomposition also plays an essential role. We do some accustomed heuristic on certain IDEs with time evolution.
Our exposition here is mostly for illustrative purposes, so we will not give precise arguments.
We shall only focus on Neumann complement type condition. Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an open set and let
0<T\leq\infty
. Let us consider the heat equation with complement Neumann condition,

(4.45)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+Lu=f~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$},\quad\mathcal{N}u=g~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$},\quad u=u_{0}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

Here
f,g:\Omega\times[0,T)\to\R
and
u_{0}:\R^{d}\to\R
are given data. In practice
f
is called the forcing term and
u_{0}
is the initial condition. For the sake of simplicity, from now on we assume that
g=0
, i.e.

(4.46)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+Lu=f~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$},\quad\mathcal{N}u=0~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$},\quad u=u_{0}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

According to
[
DROV17
]
the problem (
4.46
) encodes the evolution of a Lévy process
whose jumps from
x
to
y
are driven by
\nu(x-y)
with the following features:

1.

u(x,t)
represents the probability distribution of the position of a Lévy process moving randomly inside
\Omega
. In other words the generator of this process is
-L
(See Chapter
2
). Thus
u(x,t)
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+Lu=0~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$}.

2.

When the process exits
\Omega
, it instantaneously comes back to
\Omega
almost surely. If the process sites at the position
x\in\Omega^{c}
at the time
t>0
, it will jump back at the same time to some position
y\in\Omega
with probability
1
according to the jump intensity
\nu(x-y)
. Thus at the position
x\in\Omega^{c}
and at the time
t
the probability
u(x,t)
is the sum of all possible positions that process may occupy in
\Omega
at time
t
. This results with the following relation

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\kappa(x)\int_{\Omega}u(y,t)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

The constant
\kappa
ensures that the function
y\mapsto\kappa(x)\nu(x-y)
is a probability density on
\Omega
. That is
\kappa^{-1}(x)=\int_{\Omega}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Therefore the above relation becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x,t)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\Omega}u(y,t)\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y~~\text{equivalently,}~~\mathcal{N}u(x,t)=0.

3.

The process starts in
\Omega
at time
t=0
with some probability distribution
u_{0}
.

In sum the probability distribution solves the equation (
4.46
) with
f=0
, i.e.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+Lu=0~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$}\quad\mathcal{N}u=0~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$}\quad u=u_{0}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

In practice, it would makes sense to call such a process
gravity process
since
\Omega
it acts on the process like an attractor. Another possible name could be
reflected jumps process
or
pull-back process
.

Remark 4.41
.

Assume the above process is modified in such a way that it terminates in
\Omega^{c}
upon exiting

\Omega
instead of coming back then the corresponding complement value condition is given by
u(x,t)=0

on
[0,T)\times\Omega^{c}
(Dirichlet condition). This process is well-known as
a
killed process
and its probability described by the following equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+Lu=0~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$},\quad u=0~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$},\quad u=u_{0}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

Now we want to analyze the equation (
4.46
) heuristically. We adopt the notation
v(t)=v(\cdot,t)
.

Definition 4.42
.

A function
u:\R^{d}\times[0,T)\to\R
is called to be a weak solution of the evolution problem
(
4.46
) if
u\in L^{2}(0,T;V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\partial_{t}u\in L^{2}(0,T;V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\prime})
and

(4.47)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}u(t),v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u(t),v)=\big(f(t),v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$ and $t\in[0,T)$}.

Henceforward, the spaces
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
,
L^{2}(0,T;V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
L^{2}(0,T;(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime})
are understood in the Bochner sense.

Next, we are interested in establishing some basic properties of a weak solution of (
4.46
) whenever it exists. The spectral decomposition of
L
is one of the key points to do this. Before, let us see what could be the profile of a weak solution. Under the assumption of Theorem
4.31
, assume
(\phi_{n},\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
constitutes the Neumann eigenpairs of the operator
L
. We emphasize that
(\phi_{n})_{n}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\phi_{n}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
n\geq 0
. Recall that if
h\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
h_{k}=\big(h,\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
stands for the
k^{\text{th}}
Fourier coefficient of
h
then the Fourier expansion and the Parseval identity of
h
respectively give

\displaystyle h=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}h_{k}\phi_{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}|h_{k}|^{2}.

Assume
u_{0}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
. Assume
u\in L^{2}(0,T;V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
solves (
4.47
) such that
\partial_{t}u(t)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
for all
t\in[0,T)
. By definition of
\phi_{k}
we have
\mathcal{E}(u(t),\phi_{k})=\mu_{k}\big(u(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\mu_{k}u_{k}(t)
, thus testing (
4.47
) against
v=\phi_{k}
amounts to the ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{k}(t)+\mu_{k}u_{k}(t)=f_{k}(t)\quad\text{equivalently}\quad\big(e^{\mu_{k}t}u_{k}(t)\big)^{\prime}=e^{\mu_{k}t}f_{k}(t),\qquad t\in[0,T).

Integrating gives

\displaystyle u_{k}(t)=e^{-\mu_{k}t}u_{k}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\mu_{k}(s-t)}f_{k}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, since
u(0)=u_{0}
,
u_{k}(0)=\big(u_{0}(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
and
f_{k}(t)=\big(f(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
we have

(4.48)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\big(u_{0}(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}e^{-\mu_{k}t}\phi_{k}(x)+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}(x)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\mu_{k}(t-s)}\big(f(s),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}s.

The expression (
4.48
) shows that
u(x,t)
is uniquely defined whenever it exists. Further, it is worth emphasizing that the equality in (
4.48
) is understood in
L^{2}(\Omega)
sense. On the other hand, assume
T<\infty
then for
k\geq 1
given that
0<\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{k}
we find that

\displaystyle|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\leq 2e^{-2\mu_{1}t}|u_{k}(0)|^{2}+2T\int_{0}^{T}|f_{k}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Recalling that
\phi_{0}=|\Omega|^{-1}
and
\mu_{0}=0
, the Parseval identity together with the preceding estimate yield

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|u(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\fint_{\Omega}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\leq 2e^{-2\mu_{1}t}\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+2T\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}f(t)$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Meanwhile, using
v=1
in (
4.47
) implies that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}u_{0}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}f(x,s)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s\qquad\text{for all $0\leq t\leq T$}.

Altogether we get

(4.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|u(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u(t)$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\leq 2e^{-2\mu_{1}t}\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+2T\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}f(t)$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

It is worth emphasizing that the estimate (
4.49
) can also be established by using Poincaré inequality and Gronwall’s lemma. In particular, we have

(4.50)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\big\|u(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u(t)$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\leq 2\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+2T\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}f(t)$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Exploiting the relation (
4.46
) together with the Poincaré inequality whose sharp constant is given by
\mu_{1}^{-1}
, leads to the following estimate

(4.51)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|u(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u(t)$}\big\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}&\leq Ce^{-2\mu_{1}t}\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}f(t)$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Here
C
is a generic constant only depending on
T
and
\mu_{1}
. Finally, (
4.51
) ,(
4.50
) and (
4.47
) yield

(4.52)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|u-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}&+\big\|u-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\big\|u-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime})}\\
&\leq C\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\|f-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}f$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}.\end{split}

A rigorous analysis using some basics on Bochner integrals allows us to establish the existence and uniqueness of a weak solution solving (
4.47
) under appropriate choices of the initial and complement condition. In practice this analysis is based on the so called Galerkin approximation method which consists of projecting the problem on the finite dimensional Hilbert space generated by the
n^{\text{th}}
first vectors
\phi_{0}\cdots,\phi_{n-1}
. The Galerkin approximation method also allows us to establish the estimate (
4.52
) more consistently. A curious readers may consult
[
Eva10
,
Hun14
,
LDL16
]
for more about this theoretical approach. Roughly, we can summarize the well-posedness of (
4.47
) as follows.

Theorem 4.43
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
4.31
, i.e
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs
to one of the class
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
). Then for
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
and
u_{0}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a unique solution
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
of (
4.47
) such that
\partial_{t}u\in L^{2}(0,T;(V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime})
. Here we assume
T<\infty
. Moreover, the estimate (
4.52
) holds true.

One is able to establish an analogous result replacing the Neumann complement condition with the Dirichlet complement condition. Next, we would like to investigate the long time behavior of
u(x,t)
upon assuming
f=0
and
T=\infty
. That is
\partial_{t}u+Lu=0
in
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
,
\mathcal{N}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}\times[0,\infty)
and
u=u_{0}
on
\R^{d}\times\{0\}
. Thus (
4.47
) reduces to

(4.53)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}u(t),v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}(u(t),v)=0\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$ and $t\in[0,T)$}.

To avoid technicality, we assume
(x,t)\mapsto u(x,t)
is sufficiently regular so that the interchanges between integral sign and derivative are possible.

Mass conservation:
As a consequence of (
4.53
) with
v=1
it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}u_{0}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all $t\geq 0$}.

Now taking
v=u(t)
we get that
\frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=-\mathcal{E}(u(t),u(t))\leq 0
for all
t\geq 0
which implies

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\qquad\text{for all $t\geq 0$}.

Energy conservation:
Assume
\partial_{t}u(t)\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let the energy
E(t)=\mathcal{E}(u(t),u(t))
then testing (
4.53
) with
v=\partial_{t}u(t)
yields

\displaystyle E^{\prime}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}(u(t),u(t))=2\mathcal{E}(u(t),\partial_{t}u(t))=-2\|\partial_{t}u(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq 0.

Therefore, we have
E(t)\leq E(0)
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u(t),u(t))\leq\mathcal{E}(u_{0},u_{0})\qquad\text{for all $t\geq 0$}.

Dissipation effect:
A close look at the proof the estimate (
4.49
) reveals that

(4.54)

\displaystyle\big\|u(t)-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\mu_{1}t}\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0.

In other words
u(\cdot,t)
exponentially tends to
\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
t\to\infty
.

Stability at the equilibrium:
Now assume that in (
4.45
),
f
and
g
are independent of the time variable. An equilibrium solution of (
4.46
) is any solution which we denote
u_{e}
such that
\partial_{t}u_{e}=0
that is
u_{e}
satisfies the Neumann problem

\displaystyle Lu_{e}=f~~\text{in $\Omega$}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{N}u_{e}=g~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}$}.

Next assume
u
is a solution to(
4.45
) then
w(x,t)=u(x,t)-u_{e}(x)
with
w_{0}(x)=u_{0}(x)-u_{e}(x)
satisfies

(4.55)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w+Lw=0~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$},\quad\mathcal{N}w=0~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$},\quad w=w_{0}=u_{0}-u_{e}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

From the estimate in (
4.54
) we have

(4.56)

\displaystyle\big\|u(t)-u_{e}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}[u_{0}-u_{e}]$}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\mu_{1}t}\|u_{0}-u_{e}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}[u_{0}-u_{e}]$}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0.

Note that if
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})
are compatible then, Theorem
4.16
implies that for all
t>0
,

(4.57)

\displaystyle\big\|u(t)-u_{e}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}[u_{0}-u_{e}]$}\big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\mu_{1}t}\big(\|u_{0}-\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u_{0}$}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K}^{-1})}\big).

Let us highlight other types of evolution IDEs. The spectral decomposition can be further applied with a parallel analysis on nonlocal Schrödinger equation of the form

(4.58)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u+Lu=f~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$},\quad\mathcal{N}u=0~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$},\quad u=u_{0}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

If
u_{0}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
then a profile of solution of (
4.58
) is of the form

\displaystyle u(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\big(u_{0}(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}e^{i\mu_{k}t}\phi_{k}(x)-i\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}(x)\int_{0}^{t}e^{i\mu_{k}(t-s)}\big(f(s),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Note that if
f=0
then in this case we have mass conservation
\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
for all
t\geq 0
. We also point out the nonlocal hyperbolic equation of the form

(4.59)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{tt}u+Lu=f~~\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T)$},\quad\mathcal{N}u=0~~\text{on $\Omega^{c}\times[0,T)$},\quad u=u_{0}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\partial_{t}u=u_{1}~~\text{on $\Omega\times\{0\}$}.

If
u_{0},u_{1}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
then a profile of solution of (
4.59
) is of the form

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big[\cos(\sqrt{\mu_{k}}t)\big(u_{0}(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\mu_{k}}t)}{\sqrt{\mu_{k}}}\big(u_{1}(t),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Big]\phi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\phi_{k}(x)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\mu_{k}}(t-s))}{\sqrt{\mu_{k}}}\big(f(s),\phi_{k}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}s.

20
Dirichlet-to-Neumann map for nonlocal operators

In this section we wish to define the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map related to the nonlocal operator
L
under consideration. After we show its eigenvalues are strongly connected to the Robin eigenvalues of the operator
L
. Our exposition here is largely influenced by
[
AM07
,
AM12
]
where the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map is treated in the local setting for the Laplacian. However, we point out that an attempt to define the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map is proved in
[
Von19
]
. For the case for the fractional Laplacian
L=(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2})
, a sightly different Dirichlet-to-Neumann map to ours is derived in
[
GSU20
]
. Let us start this section by recalling that if
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, there exists a unique weak solution
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
to the nonlocal Dirichlet problem
Lu=f
in
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
, i.e. we have
u=g
a.e. on
\Omega^{c}
and

(4.60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(4.61)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}).

It is noteworthy recalling that under the conditions (

I
′

1

) and (

I
2

) (see page

I
2

), by Theorem
4.35
there exist of a family of element
(\psi_{n})_{n}
elements of
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
and an increasing sequence of real number
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{n}\leq\cdots
such that
\lambda_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and each
\psi_{n}
is a Dirichlet eigenfunction of
L
whose corresponding eigenvalue is
\lambda_{n}
namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\psi_{n},v)=\lambda_{n}\int_{\Omega}\psi_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Before we formally define the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map, some prerequisites are required. Let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
g\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. Assume
\lambda<\lambda_{1}
, then the bilinear form

\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}(u,u)-\lambda\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

is coercive on
V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thus there exists a function
u\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is a unique weak solution to the Dirichlet problem
Lu-\lambda u=f
in
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
. Explicitly,
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
and

(4.62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)-\lambda\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Moreover, the estimate (
4.61
) (with the estimating constant depending on
\lambda
) remains true. More generally, by the mean of the Fredholm alternative and the closed graph Theorem, the preceding facts (
4.62
) and (
4.61
) respectively remain true for the operator
L-\lambda
, whenever
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\lambda_{n}:n\geq 1\}
.

From now on we suppose
f=0
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\lambda_{n}:n\geq 1\}
and label the solution of (
4.62
) by
u=u_{g}
. Then the mapping
g\mapsto u_{g}
is linear and continuous from
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
to
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
since by (
4.61
) we have

\displaystyle\|u_{g}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|g\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}.

Given
v\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, put
\widetilde{v}=\operatorname{ext}(v)\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
be an extension of
v
.

Definition 4.44
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\lambda_{n}:n\geq 1\}
. We call the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map with respect to the operator
L-\lambda
, the mapping
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}:T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
defined by
g\mapsto\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{\cdot})
such that
\langle\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g,v\rangle=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{v})
. Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
stands for the dual pairing between
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
and
(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
.

Theorem 4.45
.

The Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}:T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
with
g\mapsto\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{\cdot})
is well defined, linearly bounded and self-adjoint. Moreover there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
g\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g,g\rangle\geq c\|u_{g}\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Proof.
.

Let
v^{\prime}\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
be another extension of
v
thus
v^{\prime}-\widetilde{v}\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By definition of
u_{g}
we get’

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},v^{\prime}-\widetilde{v})=0\quad\text{or equally}\quad\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{v})=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},v^{\prime}).

Therefore the mapping
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{v})
is well defined, linear and bounded on
T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
. Indeed,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{v})|\leq(|\lambda|+1)\|u_{g}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\widetilde{v}\|_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Since the extension
\widetilde{v}
of
v
is arbitrarily chosen, upon the estimate (
4.62
) we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{v})|\leq C\|g\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}\|v\|_{T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})}.

This shows that
\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{\cdot})
belongs
(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
. Subsequently it also
follows from this estimate that the mapping
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}:T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
with
g\mapsto\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\widetilde{\cdot})
is linearly bounded. Now let
g,h\in T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})
, specializing the definition of
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
with
\widetilde{g}=u_{g}
and
\widetilde{h}=u_{h}
. The self-adjointness is obtained as follows

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g,h\rangle=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},u_{h})=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{h},u_{g})=\langle\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}h,g\rangle.

We end this proof by taking
c=\min(1,-\lambda)
, since
\langle\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g,g\rangle=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},u_{g})\geq\min(1,-\lambda)\|u_{g}\|^{2}_{V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

∎

Remark 4.46
.

The above definition is plainly motivated by the following observation. Assume
u_{g}
be as before and let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Green-Gauss formula (
4.5
) gives

(4.63)

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u_{g},\varphi)=\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathcal{N}u_{g}(y)\varphi(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\Omega^{c}}\nu_{K}^{-1}(y)\mathcal{N}u_{g}(y)\varphi(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

From the second equality we can identify
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g=\mathcal{N}u_{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu^{-1}_{K})\subset(T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c}))^{\prime}
. Hence
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g\mapsto\mathcal{N}u_{g}
which, regarding the definition of the operator
\mathcal{N}
, agrees with conceptual idea behind the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map. For the sake of consistency, in the next result, we will rather consider the following equivalent alternative identification (up to a multiplicative weight) of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}:T_{\nu}(\Omega^{c})\to L^{2}(\Omega^{c},\nu_{K})
with
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g=\nu_{K}^{-1}\mathcal{N}u_{g}
.

Theorem 4.47
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
4.27
be in force. For
\beta>0
, denote
L_{\beta}
the operator
L
subject to the Robin boundary condition
\mathcal{N}u+\beta\nu_{K}u=0
. Then the point spectrum
\sigma_{p}(L_{\beta})=(\gamma_{n}(\beta))_{n}
of
L_{\beta}
is infinitely countable say
0<\gamma_{1}(\beta)\leq\gamma_{2}(\beta)\leq\cdots\leq\gamma_{n}(\beta)\leq\cdots
and the corresponding eigenfunctions are elements of
V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.
.

It suffices to proceed as the in proof of Theorem
4.31
.
∎

Next we observe the relation between the spectrum of the operator
L
subject to the Robin boundary condition and that of Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator.

Theorem 4.48
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\lambda_{n}:n\geq 1\}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Under the previous notations, consider the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}g=\nu_{K}^{-1}\mathcal{N}u_{g}
. Let
\sigma_{p}(\mathscr{D}_{\lambda})
and
\sigma_{p}(L_{\beta})
respectively denote pure point spectrum of
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
and
L_{\beta}
.
Then,
-\beta\in\sigma_{p}(\mathscr{D}_{\lambda})
if and only if
\lambda\in\sigma_{p}(L_{\beta})
. In addition,
\dim\ker(L_{\beta}-\lambda)=\dim\ker(\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}+\beta)
.

Proof.
.

Let
u\in\ker(L_{\beta}-\lambda)
then for all
v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle Q_{\beta}(u,v)=\lambda\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{equivalently}\quad\mathcal{E}_{-\lambda}(u,v)=-\beta\int_{\Omega^{c}}u(y)v(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Set
g=\operatorname{Tr}(u)=u|_{\Omega^{c}}
, with the aid of (
4.63
) the above relation reduces to

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{c}}\nu_{K}^{-1}(y)\mathcal{N}u_{g}(y)v(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=-\beta\int_{\Omega^{c}}g(y)v(y)\nu_{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Thus
g\in\ker(\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}+\beta)
. We have shown that the mapping
T:\ker(L_{\beta}-\lambda)\to\ker(\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}+\beta)
with
u\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(u)
is well defined and is onto. Both assertions will follow once we show that
T
defines a bijection.
In other words we only have to show that
T
is one-to-one. For
u\in\ker(L_{\beta}-\lambda)
if
\operatorname{Tr}(u)=0
then from the first relation above, we have
\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\lambda\int_{\Omega}u(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
for all
v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Necessarily,
u=0
otherwise
\lambda
is a Dirichlet eigenvalue which is not the case by assumption.
∎

21
Essentially self-adjointness for nonlocal operators

We now investigate the unboundedness aspect of the operator
L
subject with the Dirichlet and the Neumann complement condition. Beforehand, let us recall some basics on unbounded operators. We refer to
[
Dav96
,
Kow09
,
RS80
]
for a more extensive expositions on unbounded operators. Let
(H,(\cdot,\cdot)_{H})
be a Hilbert space. Throughout this section, we write
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
to denote a densely defined linear operator, i.e.
T:\mathcal{D}(T)\to H
is a linear operator whose domain
\mathcal{D}(T)
is a dense subspace of
H
. The adjoint of the operator
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
will be denoted by
(T^{*},\mathcal{D}(T^{*}))
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(T^{*})

\displaystyle=\big\{w\in H:~\text{ such that }v\mapsto(Tv,w)_{H}~\text{is continunous on $\mathcal{D}(T)$}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{w\in H:\exists!\,w^{*}\in H\text{ such that }(Tv,w)_{H}=(v,w^{*})_{H}\text{ for all $v\in\mathcal{D}(T)$}\big\}

and
T^{*}w=w^{*}
for all
w\in\mathcal{D}(T)
. Note that
T^{*}w
is well defined since the existence and uniqueness of
w^{*}
is due to the Riesz representation theorem.

Let
(T_{1},\mathcal{D}(T_{1}))
and
(T_{2},\mathcal{D}(T_{2}))
be two densely defined operators on
H
such that
\mathcal{D}(T_{1})\subset\mathcal{D}(T_{2})
and
T_{2}\mid_{\mathcal{D}(T_{1})}
then we say that
(T_{1},\mathcal{D}(T_{1}))
is the restriction on
\mathcal{D}(T_{1})
of
(T_{2},\mathcal{D}(T_{2}))
and
(T_{2},\mathcal{D}(T_{2}))
is an extension of
(T_{1},\mathcal{D}(T_{1}))
and write
T_{1}\subset T_{2}
. In particular,
T_{1}=T_{2}
if and only if
T_{1}\subset T_{2}
and
T_{2}\subset T_{1}
.

A densely defined operator
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is said to be self-adjoint if
T^{*}=T
. Obviously, we have
\mathcal{D}(T)\subset\mathcal{D}(T^{*})
if the operator
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is symmetric i.e
(Tu,v)_{H}=(u,Tv)_{H}
for all
u,v\in\mathcal{D}(T)
.

The operator
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is called to be closed if

(\mathcal{D}(T),\|\cdot\|_{T})
is Hilbert space under the graph norm defined be
\|v\|^{2}_{T}=\|v\|^{2}_{H}+\|Tv\|^{2}_{H}
for
v\in\mathcal{D}(T)
. In other words,
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is closed if and only its graph

\Gamma(T)
is a closed subspace of
H\times H
. Recall that
\Gamma(T):=\{(v,Tv)\in H\times H:~v\in\mathcal{D}(T)\}
.

Note that the adjoint
(T^{*},\mathcal{D}(T^{*}))
of
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is always closed.

Instead,
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is called closable if it possesses a closed extension. Equivalently,
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is closable if for every sequence
(v_{n})_{n}\subset\mathcal{D}(T))
such that
v_{n}\to 0
and
Tv_{n}\to y
for
y\in H
we have that
y=0
.

We write
(\overline{T},\mathcal{D}(\overline{T}))
to denote the closure of a closable operator
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
, i.e. the smallest closed extension of
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
. Note that
(\overline{T},\mathcal{D}(\overline{T}))
is the closure of
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is equivalent to say that
\overline{\Gamma(T)}=\Gamma(\overline{T})
.

Definition 4.49
.

A symmetric operator
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is said to be essentially self-adjoint if it admits a unique self-adjoint extension. This is merely equivalent to saying that the closure
(\overline{T},\mathcal{D}(\overline{T}))
of
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
is self-adjoint.

The following result is adapted from
[
Kow09
, Lemma 5.10]
. One should note that the author intentionally ignores the identification of the space
H
with
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
although it might be natural to do so.

Lemma 4.50

(Lemma 5.10,
[
Kow09
]
)
.

Let H be a separable Hilbert space and
(T,\mathcal{D}(T))
a positive symmetric unbounded operator on H. Assume there exists an orthonormal basis
(e_{j})_{j}
of H such that
e_{j}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
for all
j\geq 1
and which are eigenfunctions of
T
, i.e.
Te_{j}=\lambda_{j}e_{j}
for some
\lambda_{j}>0
for all
j\geq 1
. Then
T
is essentially self-adjoint and its closure is unitarily equivalent with the multiplication operator
(M,\mathcal{D}(M))
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, i.e.
M:\mathcal{D}(M)\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(M)=\big\{(u_{j})_{j}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})~:\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda^{2}_{j}|u_{j}|^{2}<\infty\big\},\quad\text{and}\quad M((u_{j})_{j})=\big(\lambda_{j}u_{j}\big)_{j}.

To be more precise we have
\overline{T}=UMU^{-1}
where
U:\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})\to H
is the unitary operator defined for
(u_{j})_{j}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
by
U((u_{j})_{j})=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}u_{j}e_{j}
and
U^{-1}

is defined for
u\in H
by
U^{-1}(u)=((u,e_{j})_{H})_{j}
.

One can formally write
Mu(x)=\lambda(x)u(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{N}
where
u,\lambda:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\lambda(j)=\lambda_{j}
and
u\in H
with
u(j)=u_{j}
. So that if
m
denotes the counting measure on
\mathbb{N},
one has

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda^{2}_{j}|u_{j}|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{N}}\lambda^{2}(x)u^{2}(x)m(\,\mathrm{d}x).

Note that if the sequence
(\lambda_{j})
has an accumulation point, the spectrum is not the same as the set of eigenvalues (this already occurred for compact operators, where
0
may belong to the spectrum even if the kernel is trivial).

Proof.
.

First of all for
u\in H
consider the identification
u_{j}=(u,e_{j})_{H}
. From the Bessel and the Parseval identities we know that

\displaystyle u=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}u_{j}e_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\|u\|_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|u_{j}|^{2}.

This shows that the operators
U
and
U^{-1}
are well defined. Moreover,
U
is a unitary operator. Indeed for
(u_{j})_{j}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
and
v\in H
, set
u=U((u_{j})_{j})\in H
with
u_{j}=(u,e_{j})_{H}
since
U^{-1}(v)=((v,e_{j})_{H})_{j}
we get

\displaystyle(U((u_{j})_{j}),v)_{H}=(u,v)_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(u,e_{j})_{H}(v,e_{j})_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}u_{j}(v,e_{j})_{H}=\big((u_{j})_{j},U^{-1}(v)\big)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})}.

We will show
\overline{T}=UMU^{-1}
is the closure of
T
and is self adjoint. We proceed in several steps.

Step 1:
We show that
M
is self-adjoint, this would imply that
\overline{T}=UMU^{-1}
is self-adjoint too since
U^{*}=U^{-1}
. It is sufficient to show that
\mathcal{D}(M^{*})\subset\mathcal{D}(M)
. In fact, since
M
is symmetric, we get

\mathcal{D}(M)\subset\mathcal{D}(M^{*})
. Let
w\in\mathcal{D}(M^{*})
then by definition, the linear form
v\mapsto(Mv,w)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}}
is bounded on
\mathcal{D}(M)\subset\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. In other words, there is a constant
C>0
such that

|(Mv,w)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})}|\leq C\|v\|_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})},\quad\text{ for all $v\in\mathcal{D}(M)$}.

Specializing with
v=v_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(M)
where
v_{n,j}=\lambda_{j}(w,e_{j})e_{j}
for
j\leq n
and
v_{n,j}=0
for
j\geq n+1
for fixed
n\geq 1,
we have
v_{n}=((v_{n,j}))_{j}\in\mathcal{D}(M)
and
\|v_{n}\|^{2}_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}^{2}|(w,e_{j})|^{2}=(Mv_{n},w)_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})}.
This amounts the above inequality to

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}^{2}|(w,e_{j})|^{2}\leq C\quad\text{ for all $n\geq 1$}.

Hence we conclude that
Mw=(\lambda_{j}w_{j})_{j}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, i.e.
w\in\mathcal{D}(M)
. Finally
\mathcal{D}(M)=\mathcal{D}(M^{*})
altogether with symmetry we get that
M
is self-adjoint.

Step 2:

\overline{T}=UMU^{-1}
is closed since it is self-adjoint.

Step 3:
Let us check that
\overline{T}=UMU^{-1}
is an extension of
T
. Consider
u\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and set
u_{j}=\big(u,e_{j}\big)_{H}
. Since each
e_{j}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
is an eigenfunction, the symmetry of
T
gives
(Tu,e_{j})_{H}=(u,Te_{j})_{H}=\lambda_{j}(u,e_{j})_{H}
. Thus accounting that
(e_{j})_{j}
is an orthonormal basis of
H
we find that

\displaystyle Tu=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\big(Tu,e_{j}\big)_{H}e_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\big(u,e_{j}\big)_{H}e_{j}=U\big((\lambda_{j}u_{j})_{j}\big)=UM((u_{j})_{j})=UMU^{-1}u.

In other words,
T\subset UMU^{-1}
, i.e.
UMU^{-1}
is an extension of
T
since
Tu=UMU^{-1}u
for all
u\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda^{2}_{j}|\big(u,e_{j}\big)_{H}|^{2}=\|Tu\|^{2}_{H}<\infty.

Step 4:
It remains to show that
\overline{\Gamma(T)}=\Gamma(\overline{T})
which will imply that
\overline{T}
is the closure of
T
. From Step 3 we know that
\Gamma(T)\subset\Gamma(\overline{T})
. Let
(u,\overline{T}u)\in\Gamma(\overline{T})
with
u\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{T})
. For all
n\geq 1

u_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(u,e_{j})_{H}e_{j}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
since
e_{j}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and
Tu_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}(u,e_{j})_{H}e_{j}
since
T
is symmetric and each
e_{j}

is an eigenvalue of
T
. Thus,
(u_{n},Tu_{n})\in\Gamma(T)
. Meanwhile, we know that

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|(u,e_{j})_{H}|^{2}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\|\overline{T}u\|^{2}_{H}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{2}|(u,e_{j})_{H}|^{2}<\infty

since

\displaystyle u=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(u,e_{j})_{H}e_{j}\in H\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{T}u=UMU^{-1}u=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}(u,e_{j})_{H}e_{j}\in H.

Therefore, it follows that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
\|Tu_{n}-Tu\|_{H}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. Hence,
\overline{\Gamma(T)}=\Gamma(\overline{T})
.
From the foregoing, it follows that
\overline{T}=UMU^{-1}
is self-adjoint and is the closure of
T
which means that
T
is essentially self-adjoint.

∎

Armed with these prerequisites, let us turn our attention to some concrete examples to which the above notions apply.
Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an open bounded set.
On
L^{2}(\Omega)
, consider the operator
(L_{D},V_{D})
where
V_{D}=V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
L_{D}
is the integrodifferential operator
L
subject to the Dirichlet complement condition. In other words,
L_{D}u=f
if and only
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

Likewise, on
L^{2}(\Omega)
, consider the operator
(L_{N},V_{N})
where
V_{N}=\big\{v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}):~\mathcal{N}v=0~\text{on $\Omega^{c}$}\big\}
and
L_{N}
is the integrodifferential operator
L
subject to the Neumann complement condition. In other words,
L_{N}u=f
if and only
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and

(4.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

We adopt the convention that
\mathcal{N}u=0
on
\Omega^{c}
for function
u\in V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
if there is
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that (
4.64
) holds. The next theorem is the core result of this section showing that under appropriated conditions on
\Omega
and the jump kernel
\nu
the operators
L_{D}
and
L_{N}
are essentially self-adjoint.

Theorem 4.51
.

The operators
(L_{D},V_{D})
and
(L_{N},V_{N})
are symmetric and positive on
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Furthermore, the following assertions are true.

1.

Assume
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the classes
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
(see page
3.30
), then
(L_{N},V_{N})
is a densely defined, unbounded and essentially self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

2.

Assume
\nu
satisfies the conditions (

I
2

) and (

I
′

1

) (see page

I
2

), then
(L_{D},V_{D})
is a densely defined, unbounded and essentially self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.
.

Proceeding as in the proof of Theorem
4.31
one finds that
(L_{D},V_{D})
and
(L_{N},V_{N})
are symmetric and positive on
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Assume
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the classes
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
then by Theorem
4.34
there exists a sequence of eigenpairs
(\mu_{n},\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
such that for each
n\geq 0
,
\phi_{n}\in V_{N}
,
L_{N}\phi_{n}=\mu_{n}\phi_{n}
and the family
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
. It turns out that
V_{N}
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and thus
(L_{N},V_{N})
is essentially self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\Omega)
according to Lemma
4.50
. Furthermore, due to the orthonormal basis
(\phi_{n})_{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, it becomes clear that
(L_{N},V_{N})
is unbounded on
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Likewise, from Theorem
4.35
and Lemma
4.50
it follows that

(L_{D},V_{D})
is densely defined and essentially self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
∎

Remark 4.52
.

Under the assumption that
(\nu,\Omega)
belongs to one of the classes
\mathscr{A}_{i},~i=1,2,3
, the corresponding formulation of the above theorem is true if the operator
L
is subject to the mixed or Robin complement condition.

Corollary 4.53
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open and bounded. Let
\nu(h)=C_{d,\alpha}|h|^{-d-\alpha}

h\neq 0
for some
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Let
V_{D}^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d})=\big\{v\in V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d}):\,v=0\,\text{on $\Omega^{c}$}\big\}
.
Then
((-\Delta)^{\alpha/2},V_{D}^{\alpha/2})
is essentially self-adjoint and also
((-\Delta)^{\alpha/2},V_{N}^{\alpha/2})
if
\Omega
is Lipschitz where
V_{N}^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d})=\big\{v\in V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d}):\,\mathcal{N}v=0\,\text{on $\Omega^{c}$}\big\}
.

It is relevant to mention that the characterization of essentially self-adjointness of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
subject to the Dirichlet complement condition on an unbounded open set
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is the central discussion in
[
HKM17
]
and several other references therein. Next, in spirit of Theorem
4.51
we have the following result in the local setting for the Laplace operator.

Theorem 4.54
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open and bounded.
Let
(\Delta_{D},H_{D})
be the Dirichlet Laplacian
\Delta_{D}
with
H_{D}=H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
. Let
(\Delta_{N},H_{N})
be the Neumann Laplacian with
H_{N}=\big\{v\in H^{1}(\Omega):~\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}=0~\text{on $\partial\Omega$}\big\}
where by convention we say that
\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}=0
on
\partial\Omega
if there exists
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in H^{1}(\Omega)$}.

Then
(\Delta_{D},H_{D})
is densely defined, unbounded and essentially self-adjoint. If
\Omega
is Lipschitz, the same holds for
(\Delta_{N},H_{N})
.

Chapter 5
From Nonlocal To Local

This Chapter is essentially devoted to investigating local objects as limits of nonlocal ones. We especially focus on the following concepts: Sobolev spaces, elliptic operators, energy forms, Poincaré type inequalities, elliptic PDEs of second order with Dirichlet or Neumann boundary condition, Dirichlet and Neumann eigenvalues. Roughly speaking, this chapter serves as a needed bridge between basic elliptic partial differential equations of second order and elliptic integrodifferential equations that require a significant background in functional analysis. To begin this journey let us first introduce our central tool.

22
Approximation of the Dirac mass by Lévy measures

In this section we introduce the tool that will help us to move from nonlocal objects to their corresponding local notions. We begin by coining the concept of
p
-Lévy measure for
1\leq p<\infty
.

Definition 5.1
.

A nonnegative Borel measure
\nu(\,\,\mathrm{d}h)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
will be called a
p
-Lévy measure for
1\leq p<\infty
if
\nu(\{0\})=0
and it satisfies the
p
-Lévy integrability condition. That is to say

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(\,\,\mathrm{d}h)<\infty.

Patently, one recovers the usual definition a Lévy measure when
p=2
. Notationally, we shall intentionally ignore the dependence of
\nu
on
p
. In addition, for a family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}}
for a fixed real number
\varepsilon_{0}>0
we shall merely write
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
or
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
. It is important to keep in mind that
\varepsilon
is a quantity near
0
(
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
).

We start with the following motivating rescaling result.

Proposition 5.2
.

Assume
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is a positive measurable function satisfying the
p
-Lévy integrability condition, i.e.
\nu\in L^{1}((1\land|h|^{p})dh
. Define the rescaled family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
as follows

(5.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{-d-p}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}~~|h|\leq\varepsilon\\
\varepsilon^{-d}|h|^{-p}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}~~\varepsilon<|h|\leq 1\\
\varepsilon^{-d}\nu\big(h/\varepsilon\big)&\text{if}~~|h|>1.\end{cases}\end{split}

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)dh=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)dh\,\quad\text{for each $\delta>0$ and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)dh=0.

Proof.
.

Observe that since
\nu\in L^{1}((1\land|h|^{p})dh)
by dominated convergence theorem we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)dh=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta/\varepsilon}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)dh=0.

We omit the remaining details as it solely involves straightforward computations.
∎

There are two keys observations that govern the rescaled family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
for
p=2
. The first is that it gives raise to a family of measures with a concentration property. Secondly, from the probabilistic point of view one obtains a family of pure jumps Lévy processes
(X_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
each associated with the measure
\nu_{\varepsilon}
from a Lévy process
X
associated with
\nu
. Viewed from this scope we aim to show that
(X_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges in some sense (to be clarified later see Theorem
5.64
and Theorem
5.73
) to a Brownian motion provided that one in addition assumes that
\nu
is radial. This, in certain sense, could be one more argument to justify the ubiquity of the Brownian motion.

From now on, for
1\leq p<\infty
,
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
denotes a family of nonnegative functions such that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}~~\text{is radial}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=1,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all }\quad\delta>0.

Note that the relation (
5.3
) is often known as the concentration property and because of (
5.2
) it is merely equivalent to

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all }\quad\delta>0.

Consequently, we also have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=1\quad\text{for all }\quad\delta>0.

Definition 5.3
.

Under the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
will be called
a radial Dirac approximation of
p
-Lévy measures.

Remark 5.4
.

Under the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) we have that for all
\beta,R>0

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\leq R}(1\land|h|^{\beta})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=\begin{cases}0\quad\text{if}\quad\beta>p\\
1\quad\text{if}\quad\beta=p.\end{cases}.

Indeed, for fixed
\delta>0

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\delta<|h|\leq R}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|>\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h=1.

Thus, for
\beta>p
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\leq R}(1\land|h|^{\beta})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big(R^{\beta-p}\int\limits_{\delta<|h|\leq R}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+\delta^{\beta-p}\int\limits_{|h|<\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)

\displaystyle=\delta^{\beta-p}.

Letting
\delta\to 0
provides the claim.

The following result infers the weak convergence of the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
to the Dirac mass at origin and thus withstands Definition
5.3
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Assume
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) for
p=1
. Then
\nu_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\delta_{0}
(weakly) in the sense of distributions, i.e. for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
,
\langle\nu_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle\delta_{0},\varphi\rangle=\varphi(0).
Here
\delta_{0}
stands for the Dirac mass at the origin.

Proof.
.

Take
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and in virtue of (
5.2
) write

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{d}}\varphi(h)\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\varphi(0)+\int_{|h|<1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h+\int_{|h|\geq 1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0))\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\varphi(0)+\int_{|h|<1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0)-\nabla\varphi(0)\cdot h)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h+\int_{|h|\geq 1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0))\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\varphi(0)+\int_{|h|<1}\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla\varphi(th)-\nabla\varphi(0)\cdot h)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}h+\int_{|h|\geq 1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0))\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\varphi(0)+\int_{|h|<1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}s((D^{2}\varphi(tsh)\cdot h)\cdot h)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}h+\int_{|h|\geq 1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0))\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

The conclusion clearly follows since

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{|h|\geq 1}(\varphi(h)-\varphi(0))\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big|\leq 2\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\int_{|h|\geq 1}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0

and by Remark
5.4
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{|h|<1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}s((D^{2}\varphi(tsh)\cdot h)\cdot h)\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}h\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big|\leq\|D^{2}\varphi\|_{\infty}\int_{|h|<1}|h|^{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

∎

Another motivating reason to consider the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is given by the following.

Proposition 5.6
.

For
p=2
and
\alpha=2-\varepsilon\in(0,2)
under the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) suppose the function
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is bounded and
C^{2}
on a neighborhood of
x
then

\lim_{\alpha\to 2}L_{\alpha}u(x)=-\frac{1}{2d}\Delta u(x)

where
L_{\alpha}
is the nonlocal operator

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}u(x):=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))\nu_{2-\alpha}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

Proof.
.

It suffices to adapt the proof of Proposition
2.38

(iv)
.
∎

Let us mention some examples of particular interest. The first class relates to the fractional Laplacian.

Example 5.7
.

For
p=2
consider
(\nu_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
the family of
\alpha
-stable kernel defined for
\alpha=2-\varepsilon\in(0,2)
and
h\neq 0
by
\nu_{\alpha}(h)=a_{d,\alpha}|h|^{-d-\alpha}
with
a_{d,\alpha}=\tfrac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}.

Let us show that (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) are fulfilled for
p=2
. Passing through polar coordinates yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})|h|^{-d-\alpha}\,\,\mathrm{d}h=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\Big(\int_{0}^{1}r^{1-\alpha}\,\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{1}^{\infty}r^{-1-\alpha}\,\,\mathrm{d}r\Big)

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\Big(\frac{1}{2-\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big)=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\frac{2}{\alpha(2-\alpha)}=a^{-1}_{\alpha,d}.

For
\delta>0
a similar computation gives

\displaystyle a_{d,\alpha}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{2})|h|^{-d-\alpha}\,\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{2}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}r^{-1-\alpha}\,\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{(2-\alpha)\delta^{-\alpha}}{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0.

Note that
\lim\limits_{\alpha\to 2}\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{a_{d,\alpha}}=1
(see Proposition
2.21
) where
C_{d,\alpha}
is the norming constant of the fractional Laplacian.

More generally, in connection with the fractional Sobolev spaces we have the following example.

Example 5.8
.

The family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
of kernels defined for
h\neq 0
by

\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{\varepsilon,d,p}|h|^{-d-p+\varepsilon}\quad\text{with }\quad a_{\varepsilon,d,p}=\frac{\varepsilon(p-\varepsilon)}{p|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}

fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
).

The next class is that of Proposition
5.2
.

Example 5.9
.

Assume
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is radial and consider the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
such that each
\nu_{\varepsilon}
is the rescaling of
\nu
defined as in (
5.1
) provided that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=1.

A subclass is obtained if one considers an integrable radial function
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
and defines
\nu(h)=c|h|^{-p}\rho(h)
for a suitable normalizing constant
c>0
.

The following class is of important interest and is related to the so called approximation of the identity.

Example 5.10
.

Assume
(\rho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is an approximation of the identity, i.e.
\varepsilon>0
,
\rho_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is radial,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=1\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\rho_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all }\quad\delta>0.

For instance, define
\rho_{\varepsilon}(h)=\varepsilon^{-d}\rho(h/\varepsilon)
where
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty]
is radial and such that
\int_{\R^{d}}\rho(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=1
.
Define the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
by

\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=c_{\varepsilon}|h|^{-p}\rho_{\varepsilon}(h)

where
c_{\varepsilon}>0
is a normalizing constant for which (
5.2
) holds true.

The resulting class obtained here is somewhat restrictive and does not capture good classes
such that as the kernels from Example
5.8
. To be more precise, there is no sequence
(\rho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the conditions above, for which
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=c_{\varepsilon}|h|^{-p}\rho_{\varepsilon}(h)=|h|^{-d-p+\varepsilon}
for all
h
.

To remedy to this, the truncation
\widetilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}(h)=c_{\varepsilon}\rho_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathds{1}_{B_{1}(0)}
is
actually sufficient where
c_{\varepsilon}>0
is such that
\int\widetilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=1
. Afterwards define
\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(h)=\widetilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}(h)|h|^{-p}
.

Note that the class
(\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
(where each
\widetilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}(h)
is supported in a ball centered at the origin) is precisely the one considered in
[
BBM01
,
Pon03
]
and used as main tool on related topics.

Now we collect some concrete examples of sequence
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
5.2
) and (
5.3
).

Example 5.11
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and
\beta>-d
. Set

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{d+\beta}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\varepsilon^{d+\beta}}|h|^{\beta-p}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}(h).

For the limiting case
\beta=-d
consider
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}<1
and put

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\log(\varepsilon_{0}/\varepsilon)}|h|^{-d-p}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}}(h).

It is important to notice that
\frac{\log(\varepsilon_{0}/\varepsilon)}{|\log(\varepsilon)|}\to 1.
Some special cases are obtained with
\beta=0
,
\beta=p
and
\beta=(1-s)p-d
for
s\in(0,1)
.

Example 5.12
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}<1
and
\beta>-d
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
consider

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{(|h|+\varepsilon)^{\beta}|h|^{-p}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|b_{\varepsilon}}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon_{0}}}(h)\qquad\hbox{with }\quad b_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{d+\beta}\int^{1}_{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0}}}t^{-d-\beta-1}(1-t)^{d-1}dt.

For the limiting case
\beta=-d
consider
