\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{(|h|+\varepsilon)^{-d}|h|^{-p}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|b_{\varepsilon}}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon_{0}}(h)}\qquad\hbox{with }\quad b_{\varepsilon}=\int^{1}_{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0}}}t^{-1}(1-t)^{d-1}dt.

In either case the constant
b_{\varepsilon}
is such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)dh=1
. Note that one can check that

a_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{|\log(\varepsilon)|}{b_{\varepsilon}}\to 1\quad\hbox{as }~~~\varepsilon\to 0^{+}.

Another example familiar to the case
\beta=-d
is

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{(|h|+\varepsilon)^{-d-p}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|b_{\varepsilon}}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}(h)}\qquad\hbox{with }\quad b_{\varepsilon}=\int^{1}_{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0}}}t^{-1}(1-t)^{d+p-1}dt.

23
Characterization of classical Sobolev spaces

In this section we show the convergence of the nonlocal Sobolev like spaces
(W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega))_{\varepsilon}
to the usual Sobolev space
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
Further we will see that the
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
with
1<p<\infty
can be characterized by the mean of
(W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega))_{\varepsilon}
. The case
p=1
will be obtained separately as it involves the space of bounded variation functions
BV(\Omega)
. Our exposition here mainly relies upon
[
BBM01
,
Bre02
]
. Different approaches can be found in
[
Pon04
,
PS17
,
LS11
]
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
u\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open (not necessarily bounded). Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) then for
1\leq p<\infty
the following convergence occurs in both pointwise and
L^{1}(\Omega)
sense

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}

here
K_{d,p}
is a universal constant
14
14

14

The geometrical constant
K_{d,p}
is the same as initially established for in
[
BBM01
]
. A similar constant also appears in
[
IN10
, Section 7]
.
independent of the geometry of
\Omega
and is defined for any unit vector
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
by

\displaystyle K_{d,p}=\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)\Gamma((p+1)/2)}{\Gamma((p+d)/2)\Gamma(1/2)}.

Proof.
.

Let
\sigma>0
be sufficiently small. By assumption
\nabla u
is uniformly continuous and hence one can find
0<\eta<1
such that if
|x-y|<\eta
then

(5.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(x)|\leq|\nabla u(y)|\leq\frac{3}{2}|\nabla u(x)|\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad|\nabla u(y)-\nabla u(x)|\leq\sigma.

Let
\eta_{x}=\min(\eta,\delta_{x})
with
\delta_{x}=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
so that
B(x,\eta_{x})\subset\Omega
. Consider the mapping
F:\Omega\times(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle F(x,\varepsilon):=\int\limits_{\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\eta_{x}\}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

In virtue of the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

\displaystyle F(x,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot h|^{p}\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h+R(x,\varepsilon)

with

\displaystyle R(x,\varepsilon)=\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}\left(\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}-\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\right)\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

Consider the function
s\mapsto G_{p}(s)=|s|^{p}
which belongs to
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
and
G^{\prime}_{p}(s)=pG_{p}(s)s^{-1}
. Further, we are able to write

\displaystyle G_{p}(b)-G_{p}(a)=(b-a)\int_{0}^{1}G^{\prime}_{p}(a+s(b-a))\,\,\mathrm{d}s.

Applying this with
a=\nabla u(x)\cdot h
and
b=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\,\,\mathrm{d}t
by taking into account (
5.4
) leads to the following estimates

\displaystyle|G_{p}(b)-G_{p}(a)|

\displaystyle\leq|b-a|\int_{0}^{1}|a+s(b-a)|^{p-1}\,\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p-1}|h|^{p-1}\int_{0}^{1}|\nabla u(x+th)-\nabla u(x)||h|\,\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq c_{p}\sigma|\nabla u(x)|^{p-1}|h|^{p}.

From this we get

\displaystyle|R(x,\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle:=\Big|\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}\left(\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}-\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x)\cdot h\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\right)\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{p}\sigma|\nabla u(x)|^{p-1}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=c_{p}\sigma|\nabla u(x)|^{p-1}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon,\sigma\to 0}0.

On the other side, if we consider
O\in\mathcal{O}(d)
to be a rotation
15
15

15

An affine mapping
O
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called a rotation if
O^{\top}O=I_{d}.
assigning
\tfrac{\nabla u(x)}{|\nabla u(x)|}
to the unit vector
e
then passing through polar coordinates, the rotation invariance of the
d-1
-dimensional Lebesgue surface measure together with Remark
5.4
we find that

\displaystyle\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot h|^{p}\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\eta_{x}}r^{d+p-1}\mathrm{d}r\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla u(x)\cdot w|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\fint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big|w\cdot\tfrac{\nabla u(x)}{|\nabla u(x)|}\big|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\fint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta_{x}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

Therefore, we have shown that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}F(x,\varepsilon)=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

Further, a close look to our reasoning reveals that we have subsequently shown that for all
\delta>0

(5.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\delta\}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

In fact since for all
\delta>0

(5.6)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\geq\delta\}}\hskip-12.91663pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq 2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

We thus have the pointwise convergence as claimed

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

In order to proceed with the convergence in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, for
0<\eta<1
as above we write

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\eta\}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|>\eta\}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

First and foremost, observe that for this specific choice of
0<\eta<1
, using (
5.4
) one gets

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\eta\}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta}|u(x)-u(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x+th)\cdot h\,\,\mathrm{d}t\Big|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\eta}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\leq 2^{p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

Since
x\mapsto|\nabla u(x)|^{p}
belongs to
L^{1}(\Omega)
, the above estimate in combination with the pointwise limit in (
5.5
) and the dominated convergence theorem yield that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq\eta\}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Thus it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega}\hskip-4.30554pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x

since one has

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|>\eta\}}\hskip-12.91663pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\int\limits_{|h|>\eta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

The claimed convergence in
L^{1}(\Omega)
is a straightforward consequence of Corollary
6.23
(Schéffé lemma). Moreover, the explicit value of
K_{d,p}
is already established in Proposition
2.21
.

∎

Alternative proof of Lemma
5.13
under a weaker condition
.

We assume in addition that
u\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Note that
G_{p}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
with
G_{p}(s)=|s|^{p}
. The Taylor formula implies

\displaystyle u(y)-u(x)=\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x)+O(|x-y|^{2})\quad x,y\in\R^{d},

\displaystyle G_{p}(b)-G_{p}(a)=G^{\prime}_{p}(a)(b-a)+O(b-a)^{2}\quad a,b\in\R\setminus\{0\}.

Hence for almost all
x,y\in\R^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}=G_{p}(\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x)+O(|y-x|^{2}))=|\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x)|^{p}+O(|y-x|^{p+1}).

Set
\delta_{x}=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
. Passing through polar coordinates and the rotation invariance of the Lebesgue measure yields

\displaystyle\int\limits_{B(x,\delta_{x})}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta_{x}}\Big|\nabla u(x)\cdot h\Big|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+O\Big(\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta_{x}}|h|^{p+1}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Big|\nabla u(x)\cdot w\Big|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\int_{0}^{\delta_{x}}r^{d-1}\nu_{\varepsilon}(r)\mathrm{d}r+O\Big(\hskip-4.30554pt\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta_{x}}|h|^{p+1}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big)

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\left|\nabla u(x)\right|^{p}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta_{x}}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h+O\Big(\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta_{x}}|h|^{p+1}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\mathrm{d}h\Big).

Therefore, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
in the latter expression, by taking into account Remark
5.4
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{B(x,\delta_{x})}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

On the other side, we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega\setminus B(x,\delta_{x})}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\leq 2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\int\limits_{|x-y|\geq\delta_{x}}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

We have proved that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}.

For the remaining details one can proceed as in the previous proof.
∎

Following
[
BBM01
, Theorem 2]
, we derive the next theorem which is a direct consequence of Lemma
5.13
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
W^{1,p}
-extension set (not necessarily bounded),
1\leq p<\infty
. Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) then for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

By Lemma
3.47
there is
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that for
u,v\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\big|\|U_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega)}-\|V_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega)}\big|\leq\|U_{\varepsilon}-V_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\times\Omega)}\leq C\|u-v\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}.

where we set

\displaystyle U_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}^{1/p}(x-y)\quad\text{and}\quad V_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=|v(x)-v(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}^{1/p}(x-y)\,.

Therefore, it suffices to establish the result for
u
in a dense subset of
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Note that,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
since
\Omega
is an
W^{1,p}
-extension domain. We conclude by using Lemma
5.13
. Alternatively, the result follows by combining the forthcoming Theorem
5.16
and Theorem
5.17
.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

The counterexample
3.53
shows that the conclusion of Theorem
5.14
might be erroneous whenever
\Omega
is not an extension domain. However, even if
\Omega
is not an extension domain, the next result, very close to Theorem
5.14
, shows that one can still get the following inequality which also appears in
[
Pon03
]
and was first established in
[
Bre02
]
for the case
\Omega=\R^{d}
.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open (not necessarily bounded). Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Let
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
with
1<p<\infty
or
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
. Adopting the convention that
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\infty
if
|\nabla u|
does not belong to
L^{p}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

For
\delta>0
sufficiently small,
\Omega_{\delta}=\{x\in\Omega:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)>\delta\}.
Define the mollifier
\phi_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\phi\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right)
with support in
B_{\delta}(0)
where
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is supported in
B_{1}(0)
,
\phi\geq 0
and
\int\phi=1
.
For the sake of the simplicity we will assume
u
is extended by zero off
\Omega
and let
u^{\delta}=u*\phi_{\delta}
denote the convolution product of
u
and
\phi_{\delta}
.

Assume
z\in\Omega_{\delta}
and
|h|\leq\delta
then
z-h\in\Omega_{\delta}-h\subset\Omega
so that by a simple change of variables, we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}-h\Omega_{\delta}-h}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u(x-h)-u(y-h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Thus given that
\int\phi_{\delta}=1
integrating both sides over the ball
B_{\delta}(0)
with respect to

\phi_{\delta}(h)dh
and employing Jensen’s inequality afterwards, yields

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\R^{d}}\phi_{\delta}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u(x-h)-u(y-h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x-h)-u(y-h)|^{p}\phi_{\delta}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}\Big|\int_{\R^{d}}u(x-h)-u(y-h)\phi_{\delta}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u*\phi_{\delta}(x)-u*\phi_{\delta}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Thus we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u^{\delta}(x)-u^{\delta}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Note that
u^{\delta}\in C^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and
\Omega_{\delta,j}=\Omega_{\delta}\cap B_{j}(0)
has a compact closure for each
j\geq 1
. Then for each
j\geq 1
the Lemma
5.13
implies

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega_{\delta,j}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta,j}\Omega_{\delta,j}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Tending
j\to\infty
in the latter we get

(5.8)

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

The only interesting scenario occurs if the right hand side is finite. If it is the case, then the upcoming result Theorem
5.23
ensures that
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Thus clearly
\nabla u^{\delta}=\nabla(u*\phi_{\delta})=\nabla u*\phi_{\delta}
and we know that
\|\phi_{\delta}*\nabla u-\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
.
The desired inequality follows by letting
\delta\to 0
since

\displaystyle\Big|\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}-\|\nabla u*\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{\delta})}\Big|\leq\|\nabla u*\phi_{\delta}-\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\Big(\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}0.

∎

The next theorem is a the counterpart of Theorem
5.16
and is a refinement version of Theorem
5.21
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
W^{1,p}
-extension set (not necessarily bounded). Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) with
1\leq p<\infty
. Adopting the convention that
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\infty
if
|\nabla u|
does not belong to
L^{p}(\Omega)
then for all
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.
.

The case where
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\infty
is trivial. Now for
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, we let
\overline{u}\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
be its extension to
\R^{d}
. Consider

\Omega(\delta)=\Omega+B_{\delta}(0)=\{x\in\R^{d}~:\operatorname{dist}(x,\Omega)<\delta\}
be a neighborhood of
\Omega
with
0<\delta<1
sufficiently small. Let us start by proving the following estimate which holds for each
\varepsilon
,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla\overline{u}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

We know that there is
(u_{n})_{n}
a sequence of functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
converging to
\overline{u}
in
W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
. For each
n\geq 1
, exploiting the computations from the proof of Lemma
5.13
(see the step before (
5.5
)) we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq\delta}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{n}(x+th)\cdot h|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x~\,\,\mathrm{d}t~\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla u_{n}(z)\cdot h|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}z~\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\Big(\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla u_{n}(z)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}z\Big)\Big(\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)

\displaystyle\leq K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla u_{n}(z)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}z.

Therefrom, applying Fatou’s lemma we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq\delta}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq\delta}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla u_{n}(z)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}\,|\nabla\overline{u}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x.

On the other hand, we obviously have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\geq\delta\}}\hskip-12.91663pt|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

Altogether, (
5.9
) clearly follows. Therefore, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and
\delta\to 0
successively the relation (
5.9
) becomes

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

since recalling that
u=\overline{u}\mid_{\Omega}
, we have

\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla\overline{u}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The desired estimate is proved.

∎

Remark 5.18
.

The counterexample
3.53
clearly shows that the conclusion of Theorem
5.17
might be false whenever
\Omega
is not an extension domain. It is also highly remarkable that Theorem
5.16
and Theorem
3.53
together imply Theorem
5.14
.

The following lemma is relevant for the sequel and is somewhat a revisited version of
[
BBM01
, Lemma 1]
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
u\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\nu\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},1\land|h|^{p})
with
1\leq p<\infty
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then for any unit vector
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663ptu(x)\frac{\varphi(y)-\varphi(x)}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle+\Big|\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-12.91663ptu(x)\frac{\varphi(y)-\varphi(x)}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|}|\varphi(x)|(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

We begin by introducing
\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(h)=|h|^{-1}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)~\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{\delta}}(h)
for
\delta>0
which enables us to rule out an eventual singularity of
\nu
at the origin. Moreover, note that
\nu_{\delta}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. It tuns that the mappings
(x,y)\mapsto u(x)\varphi(y)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)
and
(x,y)\mapsto u(x)\varphi(x)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)
are integrable. Indeed, using Hölder inequality combined with Fubini’s theorem lead to the following

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u(x)\varphi(x)|\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\iint\limits_{{|x-y|\geq\delta}}|u(x)\varphi(x)||x-y|^{-1}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-1}\Big(\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{{|x-y|\geq\delta}}\hskip-8.61108pt|u(x)|^{p}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\Big(\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{{|x-y|\geq\delta}}\hskip-8.61108pt|\varphi(x)|^{p^{\prime}}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-1}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

Likewise we also have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u(x)\varphi(y)|\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-1}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty.

Consequently, by interchanging
x
and
y
, using Fubini’s theorem and the symmetry of
\nu
we obtain

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}u(x)\varphi(x)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{(x-y)\cdot e\geq 0}u(y)\varphi(y)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y)\varphi(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y)\varphi(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{h\cdot e\leq 0}\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y)\varphi(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(y)\varphi(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(y-x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.91663pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663ptu(x)\varphi(y)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x-\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663ptu(x)\varphi(x)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663ptu(x)\varphi(y)\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x-\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}u(y)\varphi(y)|\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt(u(x)-u(y))\widetilde{\nu_{\delta}}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi(y)|\,\,\mathrm{d}y\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

Thus, letting
\delta\to 0
implies

(5.10)

\displaystyle\hskip-7.86081pt\Big|\hskip-11.79121pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-11.79121ptu(x)(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\hskip-11.79121pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-11.79121pt|\varphi(x)|\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Likewise one has

(5.11)

\displaystyle\Big|\hskip-11.79121pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-11.79121ptu(x)(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\hskip-7.86081pt\iint\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-11.79121pt|\varphi(x)|\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Adding (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) gives the desired inequality

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.0082pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.0082pt\frac{\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|+\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.0082pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-12.0082pt\frac{\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.0082pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.0082pt\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-4.00273pt|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.0082pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-12.0082pt\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

∎

Proposition 5.20
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an open set. Let
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
with
1\leq p<\infty
. Then for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with support in
\Omega
and for every unit vector
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
the following estimate holds true

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e~\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq\frac{A_{p}^{1/p}}{K_{d,1}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}.

With

\displaystyle A_{p}:=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

and the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
).

Proof.
.

Throughout, to alleviate the notation we denote
\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)=(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)
. Let
\overline{u}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the zero extension of
u
outside
\Omega
. Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with support in
\Omega
. First of all we have the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\overline{u}(y)-\overline{u}(x)|}{|x-y|}\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}|\varphi(x)|\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\supp(\varphi)}|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega}\frac{|u(x)|}{|x-y|}\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

There are two keys observations. First, since
\delta=\operatorname{dist}(\supp(\varphi),\partial\Omega)>0
, Hölder inequality implies

\displaystyle\hskip-8.61108pt\int\limits_{\supp(\varphi)}\hskip-4.30554pt|\varphi(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-4.30554pt\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega}\hskip-4.30554pt\frac{|u(x)|}{|x-y|}\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\delta^{-1}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\hskip-4.30554pt\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\hskip-4.30554pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0.

Second, from Hölder inequality and
|h|^{-p}(1\land|h|^{p})\leq 1
we find that

\displaystyle\hskip-4.30554pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|}{|x-y|}|\varphi(x)|

\displaystyle\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{p}}\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|\varphi(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\pi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{1/p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(y)-u(x)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/p}.

Therefore inserting these two observations in the previous identity and combining the resulting estimate with that of Lemma
5.19
and that of the assumptions imply

(5.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.91663pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|~+\\
&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-12.91663pt\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq A_{p}^{1/p}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}.\end{split}

The next step of the proof will consist into computing the limits appearing on the left hand side (
5.13
). We have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt

\displaystyle\frac{(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla\varphi(x+th)\cdot\frac{h}{|h|}\,\,\mathrm{d}t(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\int_{0}^{1}\big[\nabla\varphi(x+th)-\nabla\varphi(x)\big]\cdot\frac{h}{|h|}\,\,\mathrm{d}t(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\frac{h}{|h|}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

Recall that
|\nabla\varphi(x+z)-\nabla\varphi(x)|\leq C(1\land|z|)
for all
x,z\in\R^{d}
so that using Remark
5.4
we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\int_{0}^{1}\Big|\big[\nabla\varphi(x+th)-\nabla\varphi(x)\big]\cdot\frac{h}{|h|}\Big|\,\,\mathrm{d}t(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\leq C\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p+1})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0.

Thus, passing through polar coordinates in the other term of the above expression and taking into account (
5.2
) implies

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{h\cdot e\geq 0}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\frac{h}{|h|}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-17.22217pt\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot w\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\int_{0}^{\infty}(1\land|r|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(r)\,\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-17.22217pt\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot w\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\Big)\times|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663pt\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot w\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Let
(e,v_{2},\cdots v_{d})
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in which we write the coordinates
w=(w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{d})=(w_{1},w^{\prime})
that is
w_{1}=w\cdot e
and
w_{i}=w\cdot v_{i}
. Similarly, in this basis one has
\nabla\varphi(x)=(\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e,(\nabla\varphi(x))^{\prime})
. Observe that
\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot w=[\nabla\phi(x)]_{1}w_{1}+\cdots+[\nabla\phi(x)]_{d}w_{d}=\big(\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e\big)(w\cdot e)+[\nabla\phi(x)]^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}
. From this we find that

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663pt\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot wd\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663pt(\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e)(w\cdot e)d\sigma_{d-1}(w)+\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663pt(\nabla\varphi(x))^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}d\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Consider the rotation
O(w)=(w_{1},-w^{\prime})=(w\cdot e,-w^{\prime})
then the rotation invariance of the Lebesgue measure entails that
\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(O(w))
and we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663pt(\nabla\varphi(x))^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}d\sigma_{d-1}(w)=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663pt(\nabla\varphi(x))^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}d\sigma_{d-1}(w)=0.

Whereas, by symmetry we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663ptw\cdot e\,\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=-\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\leq 0\}}\hskip-12.91663ptw\cdot ed\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|d\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Altogether yields that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\{w\cdot e\geq 0\}}\hskip-17.22217pt\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot w\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e}{2}\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\frac{1}{2}K_{d,1}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e.

In conclusion,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\geq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{2}K_{d,1}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e.

Analogously one is able to show that

(5.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{(y-x)\cdot e\leq 0}\hskip-12.91663pt\frac{(\varphi(y)-\varphi(x))}{|x-y|}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{2}K_{d,1}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e.

By substituting the two relations (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) in (
5.13
), using the dominate convergence theorem one readily ends up with the desired estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e~\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq\frac{A_{p}^{1/p}}{K_{d,1}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}.

∎

Next we proceed with the following little observation which can be easily derived from the foregoing steps.

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
\Omega
be a
W^{1,p}
-extension set with
1\leq p<\infty
. Assume the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). There is a constant
C>0
only depending on
\Omega,~p
and
d
such that for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}.

If
p=1
and
\Omega
is a
BV
-extension set then for all
u\in BV(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\leq C\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}.

Proof.
.

The claims blatantly follow from Lemma
3.47
and Lemma
3.48
..
∎

Conversely to Theorem
5.21
, the following result helps to characterize functions in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
.

Theorem 5.22
.

Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Assume
\Omega
is an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
with
1<p<\infty
such that

\displaystyle A_{p}:=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Then
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and the following estimate holds

(5.16)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq d^{2}\frac{A_{p}^{1/p}}{K_{d,1}}.

Proof.
.

An obvious observation is that (
5.12
) holds true for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, all
1\leq p<\infty
and all
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Now we assume
e=e_{i},~~i=1,\cdots,d
so that
\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi(x)
. In virtue of the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
, it readily follows from (
5.12
) that for each
i=1,\cdots,d
the linear mapping

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto\int_{\Omega}u(x)\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi(x)~\mathrm{d}x

uniquely extends as a linear and continuous functional on
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
. Note that
1<p^{\prime}<\infty
and hence referring to
[
Bre10
, Theorem 4.11 & 4.14]
, the Riesz representation
16
16

16

The result infers that if
1\leq p<\infty
then
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
is isomorphic to the dual of
L^{p}(\Omega)
and any linear continuous functional on
L^{p}(\Omega)
is of the form
u\mapsto\int_{\Omega}gu
with
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}\Omega)
.
for Lebesgue spaces reveals that there exists a unique
g_{i}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x)\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi(x)~\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}g_{i}(x)\varphi(x)~\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{x_{i}}u(x)\varphi(x)~\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all}\quad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega),

where we let
\partial_{x_{i}}u=-g_{i}
. In order words, we have
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
. Furthermore, with the aid of (
5.12
) and the
L^{p}
-duality, we obtain the estimate (
5.16
) as follows

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|\partial_{x_{i}}u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sup_{\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}=1}\Big|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\,\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot e_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq d^{2}\frac{A_{p}^{1/p}}{K_{d,1}}.

∎

Note that the estimate (
5.16
) is not better than the estimate
\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\frac{A_{p}}{K_{d,p}}
of Theorem
5.16
. Indeed, Jensen’s inequality implies
K_{d,1}^{p}d^{-2p}\leq K_{d,1}^{p}\leq K_{d,p}
because

\Big(\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\Big)^{p}\leq\fint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|w\cdot e|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

The counterpart of Theorem
5.22
for the case
p=1
rather falls into the class of functions with bounded variations
BV(\Omega)
(see Theorem
5.30
). As we shall see later in the proof, because of the lack of reflexivity of
L^{1}(\Omega)
, assuming that
A_{1}<\infty
is not enough to conclude that
u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)
.

Next we resume the characterization of
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
with
1<p<\infty
as follows.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
W^{1,p}
-extension set with
1<p<\infty
.
Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Let
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
.
Then
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
if and only if

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Moreover, with the convention that
\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x=\infty
when
|\nabla u|
is not in
L^{p}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

If
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
then Theorem
5.14
asserts that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

The converse is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.22
. Now assume
|\nabla u|
does not belong to
L^{p}(\Omega)
then once more Theorem
5.22
. implies that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\infty,

in other words we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x=\infty.

∎

Let us now mention some concrete examples.

Corollary 5.24
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is an extension domain. Then the following

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}(1-s)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+p(1-\varepsilon)}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{p}K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-d}\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{d}K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega\cap\{|x-y|>\varepsilon\}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+p}}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

For the first relation, take
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=a_{\varepsilon,d,p}|h|^{-d-p(1-\varepsilon)}
with
a_{\varepsilon,d,p}=\tfrac{p\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}
. For the second take
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\tfrac{d}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}|h|^{-p}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}(h)
. Last, take
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\tfrac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}||\log\varepsilon|}|h|^{-d-p}\mathds{1}_{B_{1}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}}(h)
or more generally, for fixed
\varepsilon_{0}\geq 1
, take

\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\tfrac{b_{\varepsilon}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}||\log\varepsilon|}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon_{0}}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}}(h)
with
b_{\varepsilon}=\tfrac{|\log\varepsilon|}{(1-\varepsilon_{0}^{-p})/p+|\log\varepsilon|}\to 1
.
∎

A noteworthy application of this section is given by the following result. It infers that the spaces
(W_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}^{p}(\Omega|\R^{d}))_{\varepsilon}
collapse to the space
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Theorem 5.25

(Collapsing convergence across the boundary )
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open with a compact Lipschitz boundary. The following are true for every
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=0.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\ dx.

Proof.
.

Note that both
\Omega
and
\Omega^{c}
have the same compact Lipschitz boundary and are thus
W^{1,p}
-extension domains. Whence the claims follow since for
u\in W^{1,p}(\R^{d})
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega^{c}\Omega^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega^{c}}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Thus it suffices to observe that
\Omega\times\Omega^{c}\cup\Omega^{c}\times\Omega=(\R^{d}\times\R^{d})\setminus[\Omega\times\Omega\cup\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c}]
,
\Omega\times\R^{d}=\Omega\times\Omega\cup\Omega\times\Omega^{c}
and
(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}=(\R^{d}\times\R^{d})\setminus(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})
.

∎

24
Characterization of the spaces of bounded variation

A natural question raised by the authors in
[
BBM01
]
was to know if Theorem
5.14
persists for functions in
BV(\Omega)
. They were able to obtain a positive answer in one dimension with
\Omega=(0,1)
. To be more precise, recalling Example
5.10
they showed that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|}\rho_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=\ |u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

The case
d\geq 2
was thoroughly completed later for a bounded Lipschitz domain by
[
Dáv02
]
. However, our proof of this result as stated in the below (see Theorem
5.28
) is much simpler. Let us start with a variant of Theorem
5.16
for the case
p=1
.

Theorem 5.26
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any open set (not necessarily bounded). Assume that for
p=1
the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Adopting the convention that
|\nabla u|_{BV(\Omega)}=\infty
if the Radon measure
|\nabla u|
does not have finite total variation, then for all
u\in L^{1}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle K_{d,1}|\nabla u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

We extend the proof of Theorem
5.16
wherein we recall
u^{\delta}=u*\phi_{\delta}\in C^{\infty}(\R^{d})
,
\phi_{\delta})(x)=\delta^{-d}\phi(\frac{x}{\delta})
is a mollification sequence with
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
and
u
is extended off
\Omega
by
0
. The relation (
5.8
) asserts that for all
\delta>0,

\displaystyle K_{d,1}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Hence the estimate we are seeking occurs once we show that

\displaystyle K_{d,1}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x.

Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\R^{d})
such that
\|\chi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we find that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}u^{\delta}(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}(u(x)-u*\phi_{\delta}(x))\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}}\hskip-12.91663ptu*\phi_{\delta}(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\|u*\phi_{\delta}-u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\operatorname{div}\chi\|_{\infty}\|\phi\|_{\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}}|u(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}0.

This implies the following by using the fact that
u
is a distribution on
\Omega
.

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}u^{\delta}(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}-\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{\delta}(x)\cdot\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}}\hskip-12.91663ptu*\phi_{\delta}(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}-\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}\nabla u^{\delta}(x)\cdot\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}}\hskip-12.91663ptu*\nabla\phi_{\delta}(x)\cdot\chi(x)+u*\phi_{\delta}(x)\operatorname{div}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x+C\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}}|u(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x

with
C=\big(\|\chi\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\phi\|_{\infty}+\|\operatorname{div}\chi\|_{\infty}\|\phi\|_{\infty}\big)
.
Therefore, since the above holds for arbitrarily chosen
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\R^{d})
such that
\|\chi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, by definition of
BV(\Omega)
(see the relation (
3.5
)) we get

\displaystyle K_{d,1}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x

which completes the proof.
∎

Now we establish the limsup inequality which is a counterpart of Theorem
5.26
and extends Theorem
5.17
.

Theorem 5.27
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open
BV
-extension set (not necessarily bounded). Assume that for
p=1
the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) then for all
u\in BV(\Omega)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,1}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

Proof.
.

The case where
|\nabla u|_{BV(\Omega)}=\infty
is trivial. Now for
u\in BV(\Omega)
, we let
\overline{u}\in BV(\R^{d})
be its extension to
\R^{d}
. Consider

\Omega(\delta)=\Omega+B_{\delta}(0)=\{x\in\R^{d}~:\operatorname{dist}(x,\Omega)<\delta\}
with
0<\delta<1
sufficiently small. We know from Theorem
3.12
that there is
(u_{n})_{n}
a sequence of functions of
C^{\infty}(\R^{d})\cap W^{1,1}(\R^{d})
converging to
\overline{u}
in
L^{1}(\R^{d})
and such that
\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\R^{d})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}|\overline{u}|_{BV(\R^{d})}
. For each
n\geq 1
, by the estimate (
5.9
) we have

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,1}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla u_{n}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x+2\|u_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

The Fatou lemma implies

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{d,1}\int_{\Omega(\delta)}|\nabla u_{n}(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}x+2\|u_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

\displaystyle=K_{d,1}|\overline{u}|_{BV(\Omega(\delta))}+2\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

Correspondingly, we have

(5.17)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,1}|\overline{u}|_{BV(\Omega(\delta))}+2\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h.

The claimed estimate follows by letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and
\delta\to 0
successively. Indeed, since
\overline{u}\in BV(\R^{d})
and
u=\overline{u}\mid_{\Omega}
we have

\int_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\overline{u}|_{BV(\Omega(\delta))}\xrightarrow{\delta\to 0}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

∎

Theorem 5.28

(c.f
[
Dáv02
]
)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an open
BV
-extension set(not necessarily bounded). Assume that for
p=1
the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) then for all
u\in BV(\Omega)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,1}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

Proof.
.

The result blatantly follows by combining Theorem
5.26
and Theorem
5.27
since

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq K_{d,1}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

∎

The following theorem is a revisited version of
[
Dáv02
, Lemma 2]
which provides an alternative to Theorem
5.14
and
5.28
if
\Omega
is not an extension domain..

Theorem 5.29
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set. Assume that for
p=1
the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). For every
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
the family of measures
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
with

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

converges weakly on
\Omega
(in the sense of Radon measures) to the Radon measure
\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=K_{d,p}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x
,
i.e.
\mu_{\varepsilon}(E)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\mu(E)
for every compact set
E\subset\Omega
. Moreover, if
p=1
and
u\in BV(\Omega)
then
\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=K_{d,1}\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|(x)
.

Proof.
.

Let
E\subset\Omega
be compact with a nonempty interior. Consider the open set
E(\delta)=E+B_{\delta}(0)
where
0<\delta<\operatorname{dist}(\partial\Omega,E)
. The family of functions
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{1}(E)
. Indeed, if we denote
\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|^{p}(x)=|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x
for
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, then the estimates (
5.9
) and (
5.17
) with
\Omega
replaced by
E
imply

(5.18)

\displaystyle\int_{E}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq K_{d,p}\int_{E(\delta)}\hskip-8.61108pt\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|^{p}(x)+2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h,

with

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\leq(1\land\delta^{p})^{-1},

In virtue of the weak compactness of
L^{1}(E)
, (see
[
Bre10
, p.116]
) we may assume that
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges in the weak-* sense to a Radon measure
\mu_{E}
otherwise, one may pick a converging subsequence, i.e
\langle\mu_{\varepsilon}-\mu_{E},\varphi\rangle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0
for all
\varphi\in C(E)
. For a suitable
(\Omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
exhaustion of
\Omega
, i.e.
\Omega_{j}^{\prime}s
are open, each
K_{j}=\overline{\Omega}_{j}
is compact,
K_{j}=\overline{\Omega}_{j}\subset\Omega_{j+1}
and
\Omega=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\Omega_{j}
, it would suffice to let
\mu=\mu_{K_{j}}=K_{d,p}|\nabla u|^{p}
on
K_{j}
. We aim to show that
\mu=K_{d,p}|\nabla u|^{p}
. Noticing
\mu
and
K_{d,p}|\nabla u|^{p}
are Radon measures it sufficient to show that both measures coincide open compact sets, i.e. we have to show that

\displaystyle\mu_{E}(E)=K_{d,p}\int_{E}\,\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|^{p}(x).

On the one hand, given that
\mu_{\varepsilon}(E)\to\mu(E)
and
\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0,
the fact that
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
or
u\in BV(\Omega)
enables us to successively let
\varepsilon\to 0
and
\delta\to 0
in (
5.18
) which becomes

(5.19)

\displaystyle\int_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\leq K_{d,p}\int_{E}\,\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|^{p}(x).

On other hand, since
E
has an nonempty interior, from Theorem
5.16
we get

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{E}\,\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|^{p}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{EE}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{E}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{E}(x).

Therefore, we get
\,\mathrm{d}\mu=K_{d,p}\,\mathrm{d}|\nabla u|^{p}
as claimed.

Let us provide an alternative proof to the case
p=1
and
u\in BV(\Omega)
. Exploiting Lemma
5.19
together with the relations (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) it clearly appears that for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
\varphi\geq 0
and
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
the following occurs

\displaystyle K_{d,1}\Big|\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}(\nabla u(x)\cdot e)\Big|\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|\varphi(x)\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y+\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\varphi(x)\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\mu_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x+2\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\R^{d})}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Provided that
\mu_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\mu
and the vector measure
\nabla u
is the weak derivative of
u
and where we put
\delta=\operatorname{dist}(\supp\varphi,\partial\Omega)>0
. This proves that for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega),

\varphi\geq 0
and
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

\displaystyle K_{d,1}\Big|\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}(\nabla u(x)\cdot e)\Big|\leq\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

In a sense, this implies
17
17

17

Technically since we are dealing with Radon measures, it is sufficient to show the due inequality for all compacts subset of
\Omega
. The latter statement can be accomplished by choosing appropriate cut-off functions
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
that for all Borel set
B\subset\Omega
we have

\displaystyle K_{d,1}(\nabla u\cdot e)(B))\leq\mu(B),\quad\text{for every }\quad e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

Choosing especially the unit vector
e=\nabla u(B)/|\nabla u(B)|
(vector measure of
B
) so that

(\nabla u\cdot e)(B)=|\nabla u|(B)
we get that

(5.20)

\displaystyle K_{d,1}|\nabla u|(B)\leq\mu(B).

Recalling (
5.19
) we end up showing that
K_{d,1}|\nabla u|=\mu
as claimed.

∎

The following theorem is the appropriate variant of Theorem
5.22
in the case
p=1
.

Theorem 5.30
.

Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Assume
\Omega
is an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
u\in L^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle A_{1}:=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Then
u\in BV(\Omega)
and the following estimate holds

(5.21)

\displaystyle|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq\frac{A_{1}}{K_{d,1}}.

Proof.
.

Let
\chi=(\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\cdots,\chi_{d})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\R^{d})
such that
\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1
and
e=e_{i},\,\,i=1,2\cdots,d
. Since
\nabla\chi_{i}\cdot e_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}\chi_{i}
with
\chi_{i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, the estimate (
5.12
) implies

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}u(x)\,\operatorname{div}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}u(x)\,\nabla\chi_{i}(x)\cdot e_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq d\frac{A_{1}}{K_{d,1}}.

Hence
u\in BV(\Omega)
and we have
|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq d\frac{A_{1}}{K_{d,1}}

∎

In the same spirit, a variant of Theorem
5.23
with
p=1
, characterizing the space
BV(\Omega)
is given as follows.

Theorem 5.31
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
BV
-extension domain. Assume that for
p=1
the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
fulfills the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Let
u\in L^{1}(\Omega)
. Then
u\in BV(\Omega)
if and only if

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Moreover, with the convention that
|u|_{B(\Omega)}=\infty
when
|\nabla u|
is not of finite total variation we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,1}|u|_{BV(\Omega)}.

Proof.
.

If
u\in BV(\Omega)
then Theorem
5.28
asserts that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=K_{d,1}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

The converse is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.30
. Now assume
|\nabla u|
does not have finite total variation then once more Theorem
5.30
implies that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\infty

in other words we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=|u|_{BV(\Omega)}=\infty.

∎

25
Asymptotically compactness

In the foregoing sections we dealt with convergence of seminorms when the associated functions are independent of the parameter
\varepsilon>0
. Throughout this section we examine the case where the functions also depend on
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore it is expected that we will encounter a situation where sequential compactness arguments are needed. In order to alleviate the notations we shall replace
\nu_{\varepsilon}
with
\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}
where
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n}
is any sequence of positive real numbers tending to 0 as
n\to\infty
. We recall that our standing assumptions on
\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{\prime}s
is as follows:
1\leq p<\infty
,

(5.22)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}~~\text{is radial}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=1

(5.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all }\quad\delta>0.

The authors in
[
Pon03
,
BBM01
]
solely deal with the restrictive case where
\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)=\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(h)|h|^{-p}\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)
where
\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}
’s satisfy the following condition

\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}~~\text{is radial}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=1\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta}\rho_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\,\mathrm{d}h=0\quad\text{for all }\quad\delta>0.

In the present section we upgrade this within a fairly large class of family
(\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
under consideration.

Next, we start by borrowing some results from
[
Pon03
]
.

Lemma 5.32
.

For
\,0<s<t
, write
t=ks+\theta s
with
k=\lfloor\frac{t}{s}\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in[0,1)
. For
g\in L^{p}(\R)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{G(t)}{t^{p}}\leq 2^{p-1}\Big(\frac{G(s)}{s^{p}}+\frac{G(\theta s)}{t^{p}}\Big),\quad\quad\text{with}\quad G(t)=\int_{\R}|g(\tau+t)-g(\tau)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Proof.
.

Applying Jensen’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle|g(\tau+t)-g(\tau)|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}|g(\tau+ks)-g(\tau)|^{p}+2^{p-1}|g(\tau+ks)-g(\tau+ks+\theta s)|^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{p-1}\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}g(\tau+js+s)-g(\tau+js)\Big|^{p}+2^{p-1}|g(\tau+ks)-g(\tau+ks+\theta s)|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}k^{p-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}|g(\tau+js+s)-g(\tau+js)|^{p}+2^{p-1}|g(\tau+ks)-g(\tau+ks+\theta s)|^{p}.

Integrating both sides and noticing that
k\leq t/s
the result follows since

\displaystyle G(t)=2^{p-1}k^{p}G(s)+2^{p-1}G(\theta s)\leq 2^{p-1}(t/s)^{p}G(s)+2^{p-1}G(\theta s).

∎

Let us derive the following integration formula on the unit sphere.

Theorem 5.33
.

Assume
d\geq 2
. Let
h:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,\infty]
be a Borel measurable function. For
v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we consider that
v
-section of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
denoted by
\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap(\R^{d}_{v})^{\perp}
with
\R^{d}_{v}=\{sv:\,s\in\R\}
. Then

(5.24)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}h(w)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v}}h(w)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-2}(w)\Big)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(v)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}.

Proof.
.

By standards approximation of positive measurable functions, it is sufficient to prove the claim solely for indicator functions. Let
A\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
be a Borel measurable set. Define the measure

\displaystyle\mu(A)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sigma_{d-2}(A\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(v)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}.

Given that the Borel(Hausdorff) measures
\sigma_{d-1}
and
\sigma_{d-2}
are respectively rotation invariant on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
so does the measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Indeed, for each
v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
O\in\mathcal{O}(d)
, a rotation on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one has that
O(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})=O(A\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{O^{\top}(v)})
since
O^{\top}O=I_{d}
. The rotation invariance of
\sigma_{d-2}
implies

\displaystyle\sigma_{d-2}(O(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})=\sigma_{d-2}(O(A\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{O^{\top}(v)}))=\sigma_{d-2}(A\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{O^{\top}(v)}).

Hence, by the rotation invariance of
\sigma_{d-1}
we find that

\displaystyle\mu(O(A))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sigma_{d-2}(O(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(v)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sigma_{d-2}(A\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{O^{\top}(v)})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(v)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sigma_{d-2}(A\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(O(v))}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}=\mu(A).

That is,
\mu(O(A))=\mu(A)
and the claim is proved. Recalling that rotation invariant Borel measures on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
are unique up to a multiplicative constant, we get
\mu=c\sigma_{d-1}
. On the other hand, once again the rotation invariance of
\sigma_{d-2}
implies that
\sigma_{d-2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})=\sigma_{d-2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v})
which implies that
\mu(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=\sigma_{d-1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|
. In conclusion,
\mu=\sigma_{d-1}
. This ends the proof.

∎

Lemma 5.34
.

Given
u\in L^{p}(\R^{d})
define

\displaystyle U(h)=\int_{\R^{d}}|u(x+h)-u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $h\in\R^{d}$}.

Then for every
0<s<t
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq 2^{2p-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(sw)}{s^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Proof.
.

Applying Lemma
5.32
to the function
g(t)=u(x+tw)
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=2^{p-1}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(sw)}{s^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(\theta sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\Big).

Thus it would suffice to show that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(\theta sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq 2^{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

We distinguish two cases.

If
d
is even then there exists
O\in\mathcal{O}(d)
, a rotation on
\R^{d}
, such that
Ow\cdot w=0
for all
w\in\R^{d}
. Such a rotation can be constructed by using the block matrix

\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)
.
Thereupon, consider

\displaystyle O_{1}w=\frac{\theta}{2}w+\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}Ow\quad\text{and}\quad O_{2}w=\frac{\theta}{2}w-\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}Ow\;\;\text{ with $\theta^{\prime}=\sqrt{4-\theta^{2}}$}.

A routine check shows that
O_{1},O_{2}\in\mathcal{O}(d)
are rotations too and
\theta w=O_{1}w+O_{2}w
. Given that
\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}
is invariant under rotation, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(\theta sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(sO_{1}w)+U(sO_{2}w)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)=2^{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Now if
d
is odd then
d-1
is even thus the previous case reveals that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v}}\frac{U(\theta sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-2}(w)\leq 2^{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{v}}\frac{U(sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-2}(w)\quad\text{for all $v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$}.

Combining this with the integration formula of Theorem
5.33
finishes the proof since

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(\theta sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq 2^{p}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U(sw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

∎

The next result is a variant of the Riesz-Fréchet-Kolmogorov theorem.

Theorem 5.35
.

Assume
d\geq 2
. Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a bounded sequence in
L^{p}(\R^{d})
such that

\displaystyle A_{p}:=\sup_{n\geq 1}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Then under the assumptions (
5.23
) the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
is relatively compact in
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\R^{d})
.

Proof.
.

For fixed
\delta>0
, from the assumption (
5.23
) we can choose
n_{\delta}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\geq\frac{1}{2}\qquad\text{for all}\quad n\geq n_{\delta}.

In virtue of Lemma
5.34
, for
0<s<\delta<t
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq 2^{2p-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(sw)}{s^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w).

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\,s^{d-1}(1\land s^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-1}\int_{0}^{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(sw)}{s^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\,s^{d-1}(1\land s^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}U_{n}(sw)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\,s^{d-1}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=2^{2p-1}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x+h)-u_{n}(x)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\ dx\leq 2^{2p-1}A_{p}.

We have shown that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{U_{n}(tw)}{t^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq 2^{2p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|A_{p},\quad\text{ for all $t\geq\delta$ and all $n\geq n_{\delta}$}.

In particular letting
C_{p}=2^{2p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|A_{p}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}U_{n}(\delta w)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\leq C_{p}\delta^{p}\quad\text{ for all $n\geq n_{\delta}$}.

Now for
0<s<\delta
, writing
s=-\delta+(s+\delta)
, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}U_{n}(sw)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}U_{n}(\delta w)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)+2^{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}U_{n}((s+\delta)w)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}C_{p}(\delta^{p}+(\delta+s)^{p})\leq 2^{2p}C_{p}\delta^{p}.

Therefore we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}U_{n}(sw)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{4p}A_{p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\delta^{p},\quad\text{ for all $0<s<\delta$ and for all $n\geq n_{\delta}$}.

Now assume
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is radial, supported in the unit ball
B_{1}(0)
,
\phi\geq 0
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
and
\phi_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\phi(\frac{x}{\delta})
. Using this, the
Jensen inequality and the last estimate it follows that, for
n\geq n_{\delta}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\R^{d}}|\phi_{\delta}*u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{\delta}}\phi_{\delta}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\int_{\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x+h)-u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\delta}\phi_{\delta}(s)s^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x+sw)-u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{4p}A_{p}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\delta^{p}\int_{0}^{\delta}\phi_{\delta}(s)s^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}s=2^{4p}A_{p}\delta^{p}.

That is we have

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq n_{\delta}}\int_{\R^{d}}|\phi_{\delta}*u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 2^{4p}A_{p}\delta^{p}.

For
\varepsilon>0
, we fix
\delta_{\varepsilon}:=\delta>0
such that

(5.25)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq n_{\delta}}\int_{\R^{d}}|\phi_{\delta}*u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x<\varepsilon/2.

Let
K\subset\R^{d}
be a compact with positive measure, to conclude we need to show that
\mathcal{F}=\{u_{n}:\,n\geq 1\}
is totally bounded in
L^{p}(K)
. Note that
(\phi_{\delta}*u_{n})
is a bounded and equicontinuous sequence in
C(K)
.
Indeed, if we let
C=\sup\limits_{n\geq 1}\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
then by Young’s inequality we find that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\|u_{n}*\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\hskip-4.30554pt\sup\limits_{n\geq 1}\|\tau_{h}u_{n}*\phi_{\delta}-u_{n}*\phi_{\delta}\|_{C(K)}\leq C|h|\|\nabla\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\xrightarrow{|h|\to 0}0.

According to the Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem
3.5
, the set
\mathcal{F}*\phi_{\delta}\mid_{K}
is precompact in
C(K)
and hence is totally bounded. Whence there exist
g_{1},\cdots,g_{N}\in\mathcal{F}*\phi_{\delta}\mid_{K}
such that

\mathcal{F}*\phi_{\delta}\mid_{K}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{\infty}_{\varepsilon_{K}}(g_{i}),\quad\text{with $\varepsilon_{K}=|K|^{-1/p}\varepsilon/2$ }.

From this we have

\mathcal{F}\mid_{K}\subset\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{n_{\delta}}\}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B_{\varepsilon}(g_{i}).

Indeed if
\phi_{\delta}*u_{n}\in B^{\infty}_{\varepsilon_{K}}(g_{i})
, with
n\geq n_{\delta}
then
u_{n}\in B_{\varepsilon}(g_{i})
since

\displaystyle\|u_{n}-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(K)}\leq\|u_{n}-\phi_{\delta}*u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(K)}+\|\phi_{\delta}*u_{n}-g_{i}\|_{L^{p}(K)}<\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon_{K}|K|^{1/p}=\varepsilon.

We conclude that
\mathcal{F}=(u_{n})_{n}
is precompact in
L^{p}(K)
. This achieves the proof.
∎

Let us now look to the one dimensional case. The proof in one dimension can be obtained by modifying the higher dimension case analogously.

Theorem 5.36
.

[
Pon03
, Lemma 7.1]

Assume
d=1
and without loss of generality that
\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}
is defined for every
x\in\R
. Assume that there exist
\theta_{0}\in(0,1)
and
c_{0}>0
such that

(5.26)

\displaystyle\nu_{n,\theta_{0}}(x):=\inf_{\theta_{0}\leq\theta\leq 1}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(\theta x)>\frac{c_{0}}{4}.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a bounded sequence in
L^{p}(\R)
such that

\displaystyle A_{p}:=\sup_{n\geq 1}\iint\limits_{\R\R}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Then the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
is relatively compact in
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\R)
.

We are now in a position to establish asymptotic compactness on a bounded domains
\Omega
. Let us proceed with the following result.

Theorem 5.37
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open. Assume
(u_{n})_{n}
is a sequence converging to
u
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle A_{p}:=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

1.

If
1<p<\infty
, then
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq A_{p}K_{d,p}^{-1}
.

2.

If
p=1
, then
u\in BV(\Omega)
and
|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq A_{1}K_{d,1}^{-1}
.

Proof.
.

Let us fix
\delta>0
sufficiently small and recall,
\Omega_{\delta}=\{x\in\Omega:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)>\delta\}.
Define the mollifier
\phi_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\phi\left(\frac{x}{\delta}\right)
with support in
B_{\delta}(0)
where
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is supported in
B_{1}(0)
,
\phi\geq 0
and
\int\phi=1
.
Assume that
u_{n}
and
u
are extended by zero off
\Omega
. Let
u^{\delta}_{n}=u_{n}*\phi_{\delta}
and
u^{\delta}=u*\phi_{\delta}
. Regarding (
5.7
) we get

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u^{\delta}_{n}(x)-u^{\delta}_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

By Minkowski’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u^{\delta}(x)-u^{\delta}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u^{\delta}_{n}(x)-u^{\delta}_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|[u^{\delta}-u^{\delta}_{n}](x)-[u^{\delta}-u^{\delta}_{n}](y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle+\Big(\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|[u^{\delta}-u^{\delta}_{n}](x)-[u^{\delta}-u^{\delta}_{n}](y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

Passing to the liminf, according to the estimate (
3.14
), the assumption (
5.22
) and Young’s inequality we obtain

(5.27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Big(\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u^{\delta}(x)-u^{\delta}(y)|^{p}&\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}\\
&\leq A_{p}^{1/p}+2\|u^{\delta}-u^{\delta}_{n}\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\\
&=A_{p}^{1/p}+2\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\phi_{\delta}*(u-u_{n})\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\\
&\leq A_{p}^{1/p}+2\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{W^{1,p}(\R^{d})}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u-u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\R^{d})}\\
&=A_{p}^{1/p}.\end{split}

In view of Theorem
5.16
we get

(5.28)

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\nabla u^{\delta}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega_{\delta}\Omega_{\delta}}|u^{\delta}(x)-u^{\delta}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq A_{p}\quad\text{ for all $\delta>0$ .}\end{split}

Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
i=1,\cdots,d
then it is not difficult to check that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi(x)u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Big|\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}\varphi(x)\partial_{x_{i}}u^{\delta}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}{\Omega}}\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\partial_{x_{i}}u^{\delta}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}{\Omega}}A_{p}^{1/p}K_{d,p}^{-1}.

Hence if
1<p<\infty
then the linear form
\varphi\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi(x)u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

uniquely extends to a continuous linear form on
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)
. Wherefore there exists
g_{i}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi(x)u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}g_{i}u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\text{ for all $\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)$.}

It turns out that
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
\partial_{x_{i}}u=-g_{i}
. Therefore
\nabla u^{\delta}=\phi_{\delta}*\nabla u
so that letting
\delta\to 0
in

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{\delta\to 0}K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega_{\delta}}|\phi_{\delta}*\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq A_{p}.

If
p=1
then
