u\in BV(\Omega)
because an analogous procedure yields that (see (
3.5
))

\displaystyle|u|_{BV(\Omega)}=\sup\Big\{\Big|\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}\varphi(x)u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|:\,\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1\Big\}\leq A_{1}K_{d,1}^{-1}.

∎

Next we proceed with some estimates near the boundary of a Lipschitz domain.

Lemma 5.38
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open bounded.
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
satisfies (

J
1

) (see page

J
1

). For
\delta>0
small enough, let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
be such that
\varphi=0
on
\Omega_{\delta}
,
\varphi=1
on
\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta/2}
,
0\leq\varphi\leq 1
and
|\nabla\varphi|\leq c/\delta
(cf. Lemma
3.83
by taking
\varphi=1-\varphi_{\delta}
). Then for every
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
, the following estimate holds true

(5.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big|[u\varphi](x)-[u\varphi](y)\big|^{p}&\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq 2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&+\frac{2^{p-1}c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\Big(\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{B_{R}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h+\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-8.61108pt\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\delta/4}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big).\end{split}

Proof.
.

Since
\big|[u\varphi](x)-[u\varphi](y)\big|^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}+\frac{2^{p-1}c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}|u(x)|^{p}|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)|^{p}
we get

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big|[u\varphi](x)-[u\varphi](y)\big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+2^{p-1}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega}\big|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Note that
\Omega\subset B_{R}(x)
for all
x\in\Omega
with
R=\operatorname{diam}(\Omega)
and for all

x\in\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta/4}
we have
|x-y|\geq\big|\dist(x,\partial\Omega)-\dist(y,\partial\Omega)\big|\geq\delta/4
and
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)=1
. Moreover,
|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)|\leq\frac{c}{\delta}(1\land|x-y|)
for every
x,y\in\Omega
, since
1\leq\varphi\leq 1
and
|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)|\leq\frac{c}{\delta}|x-y|
. Hence, we get the following estimates

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega}\big|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega}\big|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int\limits_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/2}}\big|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\big|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Omega}|x-y|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\frac{c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/2}}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{B_{R}(x)}|x-y|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\frac{c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\delta/4}(x)}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{B_{R}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h+\frac{c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\delta/4}(0)}(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h,

Altogether, gives (
5.29
).
∎

Lemma 5.39
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is open bounded with a Lipschitz boundary. Assume
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to[0,\infty]
is a radial function and satisfies (

J
1

) (see page

J
1

). Then there exists a positive constant
C=C(\Omega,d,p)
independent of
\nu
such that for every
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and every
\delta>0
small enough,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x&\leq CB(\delta,\nu)\int_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+CD(\delta,\nu)\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&+C\delta^{p}A(\delta,\nu)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\end{split}

where

(5.31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(\delta,\nu)&=\Big(\int\limits_{B_{2\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{-1},~~\\
B(\delta,\nu)&=A(\delta,\nu)\int\limits_{B_{R}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h,\,\\
D(\delta,\nu)&=A(\delta,\nu)\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\delta/4}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.\end{split}

Proof.
.

Recall that by the relation (
3.38
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int\limits_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta/2}}|[u\varphi](x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x&\leq C\delta^{p}A(\delta,\nu)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|[u\varphi](y)-[u\varphi](x)|^{p}\nu(y-x)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

Combining this with Lemma
5.38
leads to the following

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta/2}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+C\delta^{p}A(\delta,\nu)\Big\{2^{p-1}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\frac{2^{p-1}c^{p}}{\delta^{p}}\Big(\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{B_{R}(0)}\hskip-4.30554pt|h|^{p}\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h+\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip-8.61108pt\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\delta/4}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq CB(\delta,\nu)\int_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+CD(\delta,\nu)\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad+C\delta^{p}A(\delta,\nu)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Note that here
C
is a generic constant which neither depends on
\nu
nor on
\delta
.
∎

Theorem 5.40
.

Assume
d\geq 2,1\leq p<\infty
. Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open with a Lipschitz boundary.
Assume that the sequence
(\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})_{n}
satisfies the conditions (
5.22
) and (
5.23
). Assume
(u_{n})_{n}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{p}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle A_{p}:=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Then
(u_{n})_{n}
has a converging subsequence in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Moreover, assume
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)

is the limit of a subsequence of
(u_{n})_{n}
then the following hold true.

1.

If
1<p<\infty
, then
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq A_{p}K_{d,p}^{-1}
.

2.

If
p=1
, then
u\in BV(\Omega)
and
|u|_{BV(\Omega)}\leq A_{1}K_{d,1}^{-1}
.

The same holds true in dimension
d=1
, provided that the condition (
5.26
) holds.

Proof.
.

For
\delta>0
small enough, we let
\varphi_{\delta}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
be such that
\varphi_{\delta}=1
on
\Omega_{\delta}
and
0\leq\varphi_{\delta}\leq 1
as in Lemma
3.83
. From estimate (
3.21
) we get

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|\varphi_{\delta}(x)u_{n}(x)-\varphi_{\delta}(y)u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+2^{p}\|\varphi_{\delta}\|^{p}_{W^{1,\infty}}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{\R^{d}}(1\land|x-y|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle+2\int\limits_{\Omega_{\delta/2}}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{|h|\geq\delta/2}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq 2^{p}A_{p}+C_{\delta}\int\limits_{\Omega}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Therefore, for each
\delta>0
,
(\varphi_{\delta}u_{n})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\R^{d})
and we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|\varphi_{\delta}(x)u_{n}(x)-\varphi_{\delta}(y)u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

According to Theorem
5.35
, the sequence
(\varphi_{\delta}u_{n})_{n}
is relatively compact in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Employing Cantor’s diagonalization procedure as in the proof of Corollary
3.84
one is able to find that
(u_{n})_{n}
has a subsequence that we still denote by
(u_{n})_{n}
converging to some measurable function
u
in
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
and a.e in
\Omega
. Necessarily,
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. Indeed by Fatou’s lemma,
\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}<\infty
since
(u_{n})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\Omega)
.

Next, we show that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.
In reference to Lemma

5.39
for
0<\delta<1
sufficiently small, we have the following

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x&\leq CB(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\int_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+CD(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\int\limits_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x,\\
&+C\delta^{p}A(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)-[u_{n}(y)-u(y)]|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y,\\
&\leq CB(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\int_{\Omega_{\delta/4}}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x+CM\big\{D(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})+\delta^{p}A(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\big\}.\end{split}

Here, the constant
C=C(d,p,\Omega)
is independent of
\delta
and
n
, and

M=2^{p}(\|u\|^{p}_{W_{\nu}^{p}(\Omega)}+\sup_{n}\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+A_{p})<\infty.

Further, in view of the assumptions (
5.22
) and (
5.23
), by taking into account Remark
5.4
we have

\displaystyle A(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})=\Big(\int\limits_{B_{2\delta}(0)}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{-1}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1

\displaystyle B(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})=A(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\int\limits_{B_{R}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}1

\displaystyle D(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})=A(\delta,\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}})\int\limits_{B^{c}_{\delta/4}(0)}\hskip-8.61108pt(1\land|h|^{p})\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

By the convergence in
L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\Omega)
, it follows that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{\delta/4})}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.
Altogether, passing the previous estimate to the
\limsup
, we remain with the following

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq CM\delta^{p}.

Letting
\delta\to 0
, it follows that
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
since we have
\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=0.

The proof is complete since the statements
(i)
and
(ii)
follow immediately from Theorem
5.37
.
∎

26
Robust Poincaré inequalities

Poincaré type inequalities are crucial tools to show the well-posedness for certain classes of variational problems.
Moreover, the sharp constants for such inequalities are strongly connected to the analysis of eigenvalues of
certain operators. In this section, we aim to establish
robust Poincaré type inequalities
for the class of

nonlocal Sobolev-like spaces
introduced in Chapter
3
. The Robustness should be understood in the sense that within such inequalities, one is able to recover the corresponding classical Poincaré inequalities for classical Sobolev spaces. See the upcoming Corollary
5.44
.

Recall that for an open bounded connected and Lipschitz set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 1
) and
1\leq p<\infty
, in the local setting we say that a non-empty subset
G
of
L^{p}(\Omega)
is admissible for the Poincaré inequality if there exists a constant
A=A(\Omega,d,p,G)>0
such that

(5.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}dx\leq A\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,dx\quad\quad\hbox{for all}~~u\in G.

Here
\nabla u
is the distributional gradient of
u
. By convention, we let
\int\limits_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,dx=\infty
if
\nabla u\not\in L^{p}(\Omega)
.

Remark 5.41
.

It is worth noticing that an admissible set
G
for inequalities of types (
5.32
) does not contain non-zero constant functions. This is obvious since these inequalities cannot not hold true for such functions.
Let us mention some standard examples of admissible set
G
on which the inequality
(
5.32
)
holds true.

•

G_{0}=W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. In this case the inequality
(
5.32
)
is known as Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality.

•

G_{1}=L^{p}(\Omega)^{\perp}=\big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u:=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}u(x)dx=0$}\big\}.
In this case the inequality
(
5.32
)
corresponds to the Poincaré inequality. For all
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
then,
u-\fint_{\Omega}udx\in L^{p}(\Omega)^{\perp}
. Thus the inequality (
5.32
) can be rewritten as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}u$}|^{p}dx\leq A\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,dx,\quad\quad\hbox{for all}~~u\in L^{p}(\Omega).

•

G_{2}=\big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,\big|\{u=0\}\big|\geq\delta\big\}
for some
0<\delta<|\Omega|
.

•

G_{3}=\big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):\,\mbox{$\fint_{E}u=0$ }\big\}
for a measurable set
E\subset\Omega
with a positive measure.

•

G_{4}=\big\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega):u=0\quad\hbox{a.e on }~E\big\}
for a measurable set
E\subset\Omega
with a positive measure.

It is important to remark that the dominant link between
G_{i}^{{}^{\prime}s}
is that the null function
u=0
is the only constant belonging to
G_{i}
.

Theorem 5.42

(Robust Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a bounded connected Lipschitz open set. Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) with
1\leq p<\infty
. Let
G
be a nonempty closed subset of
L^{p}(\Omega)
such that
c\not\in G
for all
c\in\R\setminus\{0\}
. Assume
d\geq 2
then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a constant
B=B(\varepsilon_{0},d,p,\Omega,G)
such that for every
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and every
u\in G

(5.33)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

The same is true in dimension
d=1
provided that the condition (
5.26
) holds.

Proof.
.

Assume the statement is false. Then for all
n\geq 1
we can find
0<\varepsilon_{n}<\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and
u_{n}\in G
such that
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x<\frac{1}{2^{n}}.

According to Theorem
5.40
the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
has a subsequence (which we again denote by
u_{n}
) converging in
L^{p}(\Omega)
to some
u
with
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
for
1<p<\infty
and
u\in BV(\Omega)
if
p=1
. Moreover, in either case we have that

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

This implies that
\nabla u=0
almost everywhere on
\Omega
which is a connected set. Necessarily,
u=c
is a constant function. In addition, since
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
, it follows that
\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
which implies that
u\neq 0
. Hence,
u=c\neq 0
, and by assumption we have
u=c\not\in G
. On the other hand,
u\in G
because
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and by assumption
G
is a closed subspace of
L^{p}(\Omega)
. This contradicts the fact that
u\not\in G
. We have reached a contradiction which means that our initial assumption was wrong.
∎

Corollary 5.43
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
5.42
, there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a constant
B=B(\varepsilon_{0},d,p,\Omega)
such that the following inequalities hold

\displaystyle\|u-\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq B\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{ for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ and $u\in L^{p}(\Omega)$}

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq B\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{ for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ and $u\in G_{i},\,\,i=2,3,4$}.

Note that the constant
B=B(\varepsilon_{0},d,p,\Omega,G_{i})
is independent of
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore, a noteworthy consequence of Theorem
5.42
is obtained letting
\varepsilon\to 0
by means of Theorem
5.23
and Theorem
5.31
thereby recovering the classical Poincaré type inequality.

Corollary 5.44
.

Assume
d\geq 2,1\leq p<\infty
. Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a connected Lipschitz open set. Assume
G
is a closed subset of
L^{p}(\Omega)
which does not contains non-zero constant functions. Then we have

(5.34)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq BK_{d,p}\int\limits_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for every $u\in G$.}

The analog robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality, is delicate and deserves a different treatment.

Theorem 5.45

(Robust Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a bounded connected open set. Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) with
1\leq p<\infty
. Assume
d\geq 2
then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a constant
B=B(\varepsilon_{0},d,p,\Omega)
such that for every
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and every
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}u\subset\Omega
it holds

(5.35)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

The same is true in dimension
d=1
provided that the condition (
5.26
) holds.

Proof.
.

Assume the statement is false. Then for each
n\geq 1
we can find
0<\varepsilon_{n}<\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
and

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x<\frac{1}{2^{n}}.

According to Theorem
5.40
the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
has a subsequence (which we again denote by
u_{n}
) converging in
L^{p}(\Omega)
to some
u
with
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
for
1<p<\infty
and
u\in BV(\Omega)
if
p=1
. Moreover, in either case we have that

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

This implies that
\nabla u=0
almost everywhere on
\Omega
which is a connected set. Necessarily,
u=c
is a constant function. In addition, since
\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
, it follows that
\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1
which implies that
u\neq 0
. Hence,
u=c\neq 0
. Next we need to show that
u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
. For
\delta>0
small enough since each
\nu_{\varepsilon}
is radial, by mimicking the relation (
3.13
) we get the following

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\iint_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{|h|\leq\delta}|u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(x+h)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{-1}\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\nabla u_{n}(x)\cdot w\right|^{p}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{d-1}(w)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)

\displaystyle=K_{d,p}\Big(\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big).

Therefore, from Remark
5.4
we have

\displaystyle K_{d,p}\int_{\R^{d}}|\nabla u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Big(\int_{B_{\delta}}|h|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon_{n}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\Big)^{-1}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Finally, we have
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. Thus
\|u_{n}-u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0

and we get that
u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
since
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
This contradicts the fact that
u=c\neq 0
since the null function is the only constant function belonging to
W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
∎

Remark 5.46
.

Note that statement for the analog inequality

(5.36)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x

holding for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, is not fully satisfactory since the kernels
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
are also allowed to be integrable. If this is the
case, then the spaces
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{p}_{\nu_{\varepsilon}}(\Omega)
coincide. This would imply that the estimate (
5.36
) holds true for all functions in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, which is impossible since it fails for the constant function
u=1
.
Example
5.11
(with
\beta=p
), i.e.
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{d+p}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\varepsilon^{d+p}}\mathds{1}_{B_{\varepsilon}}(h)
is typical illustration withstanding the present argument applies. It might be challenging to classify families
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
for which the inequality (
5.36
) for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
.
For instance, we believe it (
5.36
) is true for
\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)=\frac{\varepsilon(p-\varepsilon)}{p|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}|h|^{-d-p+\varepsilon}
with
0<\varepsilon<1/p
and all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

As a consequence of Theorem
5.45
, by letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, one recovers the following classical Poincaré-Friedrichs inequality.

Corollary 5.47
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is open, bounded and connected. Then we have

(5.37)

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq BK_{d,p}\int\limits_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for every $u\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)$ }.

Next, we would like to study the asymptotic behavior of the sharp constant of estimate (
5.33
). It is not so surprising that this asymptotic behavior is related to the sharp constant of estimate (
5.32
).

Theorem 5.48

(
convergence of sharp constants I
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open, bounded and connected Lipschitz. Let
1\leq p<\infty
. Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Let
G
be a nonempty closed subset of
L^{p}(\Omega)
such that
c\not\in G
for all
c\in\R\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\Lambda_{1,p}
be the sharp constant of the inequality (
5.34
) and
\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}
be the sharp constant of the inequality (
5.33
) that is,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,p}:=\sup\left\{\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}:u\in G,~~\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1\right\}

and for each
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}:=\sup\left\{\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}:u\in G~~\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,p}(u,u)=1\right\}.

Here we adopt the notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon,p}(u,u)=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,dy.

Then the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}
with
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, is bounded and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}=\Lambda_{1,p}K^{-1}_{d,p}.

Proof.
.

The boundedness comes from Theorem
5.42
. For every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and every
u\in G
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}dx\leq\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,dy.

According to Theorem
5.23
and Theorem
5.31
, letting
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
implies

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{p}dx\leq K_{d,p}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}\int\limits_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x.

It follows that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,p}\leq K_{d,p}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}.

On the other hand, if we fix
\eta\in(0,~\Lambda_{1,p}K^{-1}_{d,p})
then, for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
u_{\varepsilon}\in G
such that

(5.38)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}-\eta<\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon,p}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})=1.

In virtue of Theorem
5.42
the family
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\Omega)
since
\|u_{\varepsilon}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,p}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})=B
with the constant
B
is independent of
\varepsilon
. Passing through a subsequence we may assume as for the proof of Theorem
5.42
, that it converges in
L^{p}(\Omega)
to some
u
with
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
when
p>1
and
u\in BV(\Omega)
when
p=1
. Further, applying Theorem
5.16
and Theorem
5.26
yields

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq K^{-1}_{d,p}\liminf\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\varepsilon,p}(u_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})=K^{-1}_{d,p}.

Subsequently, we have
u\in G
since
G
is closed in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. Thus, by applying the Poincaré inequality (
5.32
) to
u
and taking into account (
5.38
) we obtain the following

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}-\eta\leq\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\Lambda_{1,p}\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\Lambda_{1,p}K^{-1}_{d,p}.

Finally, letting
\eta\to 0
, completes the proof since we reach the following inequality

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Lambda_{1-\varepsilon,p}\leq\Lambda_{1,p}K^{-1}_{d,p}.

∎

Analogously we also have the following convergence of sharps constants.

Theorem 5.49

(
convergence of sharp constants II
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open, bounded and connected Lipschitz. Let
1\leq p<\infty
. Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Let
\Lambda^{\prime}_{1,p}
be the sharp constant of the inequality (
5.37
) and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{1-\varepsilon,p}
be the sharp constant of the inequality (
5.35
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}_{1,p}:=\sup\left\{\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}:u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega),~~\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=1\right\}

and for each
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}_{1-\varepsilon,p}:=\sup\left\{\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}:u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega),~~\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,p}(u,u)=1\right\}.

Here we adopt the notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,p}(u,u)=\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,dy.

Then the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\Lambda^{\prime}_{1-\varepsilon,p}
with
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
is bounded and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Lambda^{\prime}_{1-\varepsilon,p}=\Lambda^{\prime}_{1,p}K^{-1}_{d,p}.

Next, we deal with a situation where the robustness holds globally in terms of the parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

Theorem 5.50
.

Assume
d\geq 2,1\leq p<\infty
. Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a bounded connected Lipschitz open set. Assume that the family
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{*})}
satisfies the conditions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). In addition assume that for
R>0
and
\tau\in(0,\varepsilon_{*})
there exists
\theta>0
such that

(5.39)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(h)\geq\theta,\quad\quad\text{for all $|h|\leq R$ and all $\varepsilon\in(\tau,\varepsilon_{*})$}.

Then there exists
C=C(d,p,\Omega)
independent of
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{*})
and
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\big\|u-\hbox{ $\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

The same is true in dimension
d=1
provided that the condition (
5.26
) holds.

Proof.
.

According to Theorem
5.42
, there exist
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{*})
and a constant
B=B(d,p,\Omega)
such that for all
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})

\displaystyle\big\|u-\hbox{ $\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x.

The condition (
5.39
) with
\tau=\varepsilon_{0}
implies that
\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\geq\theta
for all
\varepsilon\in(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{*})
and all
|x-y|\leq R
, where
R=\operatorname{diam}(\Omega)
is the diameter of
\Omega
. This, together with Jensen’s inequality yield

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\nu_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\geq\theta\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\geq\theta|\Omega|\int_{\Omega}\big|u(x)-\hbox{ $\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big|^{p}\,dx.

The proof is now complete.
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
5.50
we have following.

Corollary 5.51
.

Assume
d\geq 1,~1<p<\infty
. Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open, bounded and connected with Lipschitz boundary. Then there exists a constant,
C=C(p,d,\Omega)>0
depending only on
d,p
and
\Omega
such that for every
s\in(0,1)
and every
u\in L^{p}(\Omega)
we have

(5.40)

\displaystyle\big\|u-\hbox{ $\fint_{\Omega}$}u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C(1-s)\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,dx\,dy.

Remark 5.52
.

It is worth pointing out that Corollary
5.51
provides a global estimate in terms of the fractional order
s\in(0,1)
.
In other words, the estimate holds true for all
s\in(0,1)
and the estimating constant
C
is independent of
s
. In
[
Pon03
]
, the author established the inequality (
5.40
), provided that
s>s_{0}
for some
s_{0}
depending on
d,p
and
\Omega
. Our
present result fills the gap
0<s\leq s_{0}
. See also
[
BBM02
,
LBP14
]
for further applications.

As a consequence of Theorem
5.45
we have following.

Corollary 5.53
.

Assume
d\geq 1,~1<p<\infty
. Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a bounded connected Lipschitz open set. Then there exist
s_{0}\in(0,1)
and a constant
C=C(p,d,\Omega)>0
depending only on
d,p
and
\Omega
such that for every
s\in(s_{0},1)
and every
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
with
\operatorname{supp}u\subset\Omega
, we have

(5.41)

\displaystyle\big\|u\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C(1-s)\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,dx\,dy.

27
Convergence of Hilbert spaces

In order to establish the main goal of this chapter, we are forced to deal with the family of Hilbert spaces that vary according to limiting variables. Therefore, we need to understand certain aspects and notions of convergence in varying spaces. Here we have in mind concepts like the Gamma convergence, the Mosco convergence and the convergence in varying Hilbert spaces. Our exposition in this section closely follows
[
KS03
]
. Throughout this section
(H,\big(\cdot,\cdot\big)_{H})
and
\big(H_{n},\big(\cdot,\cdot\big)_{H_{n}}\big)_{n}
is a family of separable Hilbert spaces over
\R
.

Definition 5.54
.

We say that a sequence of Hilbert spaces
(H_{n})_{n}
converges to a Hilbert space
H
if
H
has dense a subspace
\mathscr{C}
and for each
n\geq 1
there is a linear map
\Phi_{n}:\mathscr{C}\to H_{n}
such that for all
u\in\mathscr{C}

(5.42)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}u\|_{H_{n}}=\|u\|_{H}.

This is equivalent to say that for all
u,v\in\mathscr{C}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(\Phi_{n}u,\Phi_{n}v)_{H_{n}}=(u,v)_{H}.

Indeed, it suffices to write

\displaystyle(\Phi_{n}u,\Phi_{n}v)_{H_{n}}=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\|\Phi_{n}(u+v\|^{2}_{H_{n}}-\|\Phi_{n}(u-v)\|^{2}_{H_{n}}\Big)\quad\text{and}\quad(u,v)_{H}=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\|u+v\|^{2}_{H}-\|u-v\|^{2}_{H}\Big).

In practice it is common to take
\Phi_{n}
be the inclusion operator when
H\subset H_{n+1}\subset H_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.

Example 5.55
.

We let
H=H^{1}(\Omega)
be the standard Sobolev space and
H_{n}=H^{\alpha_{n}/2}(\Omega)
be the fractional Sobolev space where
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is a Lipschitz open set and
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
is any sequence,
0<\alpha_{n}<2
tending to
2
. It results as a consequence of Corollary
5.24
that
(H_{n})_{n}
converges to
H
.

In what follows, it is assumed in this section that
H
has dense a subspace
\mathscr{C}
such that (
5.42
) holds true.

Definition 5.56
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a sequence such that
u_{n}\in H_{n}
and
u\in H
.

1.

We say that
(u_{n})_{n}
strongly converges (or simply converges ) to
u
if there exists another sequence
(\widetilde{u}_{m})_{m}\subset\mathscr{C}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0.

Note that the first limit says that
(\widetilde{u}_{m})_{m}
tends to
u
in the topology of
H
.

2.

We say that
(u_{n})_{n}
converges in the weak sense (
weakly
) to
u
if for every sequence
(v_{n})_{n}
with
v_{n}\in H_{n}
, strongly converging to
v\in H
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(u_{n},v_{n}\big)_{H_{n}}=\big(u,v\big)_{H}.

Let us now visit some related properties.

Proposition 5.57
.

Assume that the sequence of Hilbert spaces
(H_{n})_{n}
converges to a Hilbert space H.
Let
(u_{n})_{n}
and
(v_{n})_{n}
be sequence such that
u_{n},v_{n}\in H_{n}
and
u,v\in H
.

1.

(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
0\in H
if and only if
\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
.

2.

If
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
then
\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u\|_{H}
.

3.

If
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
and
(v_{n})_{n}
converges to
v
then
(u_{n}+\lambda v_{n})_{n}
converges to
u+\lambda v
for
\lambda\in\R
.

4.

If
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
and
(v_{n})_{n}
converges to
v
then
(u_{n},v_{n})_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}(u,v)_{H}
.

5.

\|u_{n}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
then
(v_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
.

6.

For any
w\in H
there is a sequence
(w_{n})_{n}
with
w_{n}\in H_{n}
such that
(w_{n})_{n}
converges to
w
.

7.

If
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
and
(v_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
then
\|u_{n}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
.

Proof.
.

Throughout, we assume that the sequence
(\widetilde{u}_{m})_{m}\subset\mathscr{C}
is such that
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
and

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0.

(i)
If
u_{n}\to 0
then
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
and since
\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H}
we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}+\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H}=0.

Conversely, assume
\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\to 0
, by density we can approximate
0\in H
i.e there exists a sequence
(u^{\prime}_{m})_{m}\subset\mathscr{C}
such that
\|u^{\prime}_{m}\|_{H}\to 0
. We know that
\|\Phi_{n}u^{\prime}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u^{\prime}_{m}\|_{H}
for each
m
. Hence the conclusion follows since

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}u^{\prime}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}u^{\prime}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}+\lim_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\|u^{\prime}_{m}\|_{H}+\lim_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0.

(ii)
For all
n,m
by triangle inequality it is effortless to see that

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}-\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\leq\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\leq\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}+\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}

from which we get

\displaystyle\Big|\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}-\|u\|_{H}\Big|\leq\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}+\Big|\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}-\|u\|_{H}\Big|.

Hence the conclusion holds since
\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H}
,
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
and

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big|\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}-\|u\|_{H}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}+\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big|\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}-\|u\|_{H}\Big|

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\Big|\|\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H}-\|u\|_{H}\Big|=0.

(iii)
This is a straightforward consequence of the definition.

(iv)
From
(ii)
and
(iii)
the result is obtained as follows

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(u_{n},v_{n})_{H_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{4}\Big(\|u_{n}+v_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}-\|u_{n}-v_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Big(\|u+v\|^{2}_{H}-\|u-v\|^{2}_{H}\Big)=(u,v)_{H}.

(v)
Since
\|u_{n}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
then
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
and

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}+\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0

which shows that
(v_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
.

(vi)
By density there exists
\widetilde{u}_{m}\in\mathscr{C}
with
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}\to 0
it suffices to take

u_{n}=\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{n}\in H_{n}
such that
\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{n}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0
for every
n\geq 1
.

(vii)
Note that

\displaystyle\|u_{n}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\leq\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}+\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{v}_{m}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}+\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-\Phi_{n}\widetilde{v}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}.

The claim readily follows since
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
,
\|\widetilde{v}_{m}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-\Phi_{n}\widetilde{v}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}(\widetilde{u}_{m}-\widetilde{v}_{m})\|_{H_{n}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-\widetilde{v}_{m}\|_{H}=0.

∎

Lemma 5.58
.

Assume that the sequence of Hilbert spaces
(H_{n})_{n}
converges to the Hilbert space H. A sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
with
u_{n}\in H_{n}
converges to
u
if and only if
(u_{n},w_{n})_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}(u,w)_{H}
for every sequence

(w_{n})_{n}
,
w_{n}\in H_{n}
converging weakly to
w\in H
.

Proof.
.

The forward implication is patently a consequence of the definition of the weak convergence. Reciprocally assume
(u_{n},w_{n})_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}(u,w)_{H}
for every sequence

(w_{n})_{n}
,
w_{n}\in H_{n}
converging weakly to
w\in H
. From Proposition
5.57

(iv)
we know that strong convergence implies the weak convergence, then choosing
(w_{n})_{n}
strongly converging to
w
implies that
(u_{n})
is in particular converging weakly to
u
. Thus taking
w_{n}=u_{n}
implies
\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u\|_{H}.
Next by density we have
\|\widetilde{u}_{m}-u\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
with
\widetilde{u}_{m}\in\mathscr{C}
. To conclude, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0.

To do this, let us fix
m\geq 1
. We claim that
(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m})_{n}
is converging weakly to

\widetilde{u}_{m}
. Indeed, for a sequence
(w_{n})_{n}
,
w_{n}\in H_{n}
strongly converging to
w\in H
there exists
(\widetilde{w}_{j})_{j}
such that
\|\widetilde{w}_{j}-w\|_{H}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0
and

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{w}_{j}-w_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0.

We have

\displaystyle\big|(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m},w_{n})_{H_{n}}-(\widetilde{u}_{m},w)_{H}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m},\Phi_{n}\widetilde{w}_{j})_{H_{n}}-(\widetilde{u}_{m},w)_{H}\big|+\big|(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m},w_{n}-\Phi_{n}\widetilde{w}_{j})_{H_{n}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m},\Phi_{n}\widetilde{w}_{j})_{H_{n}}-(\widetilde{u}_{m},w)_{H}\big|+\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|_{H_{n}}\|w_{n}-\Phi_{n}\widetilde{w}_{j}\|_{H_{n}}.

The claim follows once we show that
(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m},w_{n})_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}(\widetilde{u}_{m},w)_{H}
. Indeed, let us see that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m},\Phi_{n}\widetilde{w}_{j})_{H_{n}}=\lim_{j\to\infty}(\widetilde{u}_{m},\widetilde{w}_{j})_{H}=(\widetilde{u}_{m},w)_{H}.

As
(\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m})_{n}
is converging weakly to
\widetilde{u}_{m}
then in particular we have
(u_{n},\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m})_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}(u,\widetilde{u}_{m})_{H}
.
Finally, combining the foregoing steps yields

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}-u_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big(\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}+\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}-2(u_{n},\Phi_{n}\widetilde{u}_{m})_{H_{n}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\Big(\|\widetilde{u}_{m}\|^{2}_{H_{n}}+\|u\|^{2}_{H}-2(u,\widetilde{u}_{m})_{H}\Big)=0.

∎

Now we derive what can be viewed as a weakly sequential compactness result in connection with the spaces
(H_{n})_{n}
and
H
. We believe that our proof here, although inspired by
[
KS03
]
, is more comprehensible and uses rudimentary arguments.

Theorem 5.59
.

[
KS03
, Lemma 2.2
\&
2.3]

Assume the sequence of separable Hilbert spaces
(H_{n})_{n}
converges to a separable Hilbert space H. Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a sequence with
u_{n}\in H_{n}
.

1.

If
(u_{n})_{n}
weakly converges to some
u\in H
then the sequence
(\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}})_{n}
is bounded and we have

\|u\|_{H}\leq\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}.

2.

Conversely, if the sequence
(\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}})_{n}
is bounded then there is a subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
converging weakly to some
u\in H
.

Proof.
.

(i)
Assume
\sup\limits_{n\geq 1}\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=\infty
then one is able to construct a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
such that
\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{H_{n_{k}}}\geq 2^{k}
for all
k\geq 1
. We have the right to set
v_{k}=\frac{u_{n_{k}}}{2^{k}\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{H_{n_{k}}}}
. It clearly appears that
\|v_{k}\|_{H_{n_{k}}}=2^{-k}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0
. In virtue of Proposition
5.57
,
(v_{k})_{k}
converges to
0\in H
. On the other hand, it is also clear that the subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
weakly converges to
u
. By the definition of weak convergence it follows that
(u_{n_{k}},v_{k})_{H_{n_{k}}}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0
which contradictory to the fact that

\displaystyle(u_{n_{k}},v_{k})_{H_{n_{k}}}=\Big(u_{n_{k}},\frac{u_{n_{k}}}{2^{k}\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{H_{n_{k}}}}\Big)_{H_{n_{k}}}=\frac{\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{H_{n_{k}}}}{2^{k}}\geq 1.

In conclusion, our initial assumption was wrong and hence
(\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}})_{n}
must be bounded.

Let us prove the liminf inequality. By invoking once more Proposition
5.57
, there exists
v_{n}\in H_{n}
such that
(v_{n})_{n}
converging to
u
and we also have
\|v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u\|_{H}
. By definition of weak convergence it follows that
(u_{n},v_{n})_{H_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}(u,u)_{H}
. For
\varepsilon>0
and certain
j_{0}\geq 1
we have
\|v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\leq\|u\|_{H}+\varepsilon
for all
n\geq N
. Thus by Cauchy Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle|(u_{n},v_{n})_{H_{n}}|\leq\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\|v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}\leq\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}(\|u\|_{H}+\varepsilon)\quad\text{for all}\quad n\geq N.

Passing to the liminf as
n\to\infty
and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
after, yields

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}}.

(ii)
Assume that
(\|u_{n}\|_{H_{n}})_{n}
is bounded. Let
(\varphi_{k})_{k}
be an orthonormal basis of
H
. By density,
for each
k\geq 1
there exists a sequence
(\varphi_{k,m})_{m}\subset\mathscr{C}
such that

(5.43)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{k,m}-\varphi_{k}\|_{H}\leq 2^{-m}\quad\text{for every }\quad m\geq 1.

Observing that
\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\varphi_{k,m}\|_{H_{n}}=\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\|\varphi_{k,m}\|_{H}=1
it is not difficult to obtain that for each
k\geq 1
, the doubling sequence of real numbers
\big((u_{n},\Phi_{n}\varphi_{k,m})_{H_{n}}\big)_{n,m}
is bounded in
\R
. We may find a cluster point
a_{k}
, two suitable subsequences
(n_{p})_{p}
and
(m_{j})_{j}
and another sequence
(a_{k}(m_{j}))_{j}
obeying the following rule: if we set
a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j}):=(u_{n_{p}}\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{k,m_{j}})_{H_{n_{p}}},
then

(5.44)

\displaystyle a_{k}(m_{j})=\lim_{p\to\infty}a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j})\quad\text{and }\quad\Big|a_{k}(m_{j})-a_{k}\Big|\leq 2^{-j}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle a_{k}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{p\to\infty}a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{p\to\infty}(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{k,m_{j}})_{H_{n_{p}}}\quad\text{for every }\quad k\geq 1.

Let us show the convergence of the series
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}|a_{k}|^{2}
. Since
\|\varphi_{k,m}-\varphi_{k}\|_{H}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0
it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}(\Phi_{n}\varphi_{k,m},\Phi_{n}\varphi_{i,m})_{H_{n}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}(\varphi_{k,m},\varphi_{i,m})_{H}=(\varphi_{k},\varphi_{i})_{H}=\delta_{k,i}.

Further, recalling that
a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j}):=(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{k,m_{j}})_{H_{n_{p}}}
the following holds for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big\|u_{n_{p}}-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j})\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{k,m_{j}}\Big\|^{2}_{H_{n_{p}}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\liminf_{p\to\infty}\left[\|u_{n_{p}}\|^{2}_{H_{n_{p}}}\hskip-8.61108pt-2\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}|a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j})|^{2}+\hskip-2.58334pt\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{k}(n_{p},m_{j})a_{i}(n_{p},m_{j})(\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{k,m_{j}},\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{i,m_{j}})_{H_{n_{p}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\liminf_{p\to\infty}\|u_{n_{p}}\|^{2}_{H_{n_{p}}}-2\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}|a_{k}|^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{k}a_{i}\delta_{k,i}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{p\to\infty}\|u_{n_{p}}\|^{2}_{H_{n_{p}}}-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}|a_{k}|^{2}.

As a result, if we put
u:=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\varphi_{k}
then it is clear that
u\in H
since letting
\ell\to\infty
, the above implies

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{H}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|a_{k}|^{2}\leq\liminf_{p\to\infty}\|u_{n_{p}}\|^{2}_{H_{n_{p}}}<\infty.

Next, we show that
(u_{n_{p}})_{p}
weakly converges to
u
. Let
(v_{n})_{n}
, with
v_{n}\in H_{n}
be a sequence strongly converging to some
v\in H
. That is there exists
(\widetilde{v}_{m})_{m}\subset\mathscr{C}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\|\widetilde{v}_{m}-v\|_{H}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n}\widetilde{v}_{m}-v_{n}\|_{H_{n}}=0.

On the other hand, since
(\varphi_{k})_{k}
is an orthonormal basis of
H
we know that

\displaystyle v=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}\varphi_{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\|v\|^{2}_{H}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|b_{k}|^{2}\quad\text{with}\quad b_{k}=(u,v)_{H}.

To conclude, we show that
(u_{n_{p}},v_{n_{p}})_{H_{n_{p}}}\xrightarrow{p\to\infty}(u,v)_{H}.
We put
v^{*}_{j}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{j}b_{k}\varphi_{k,m_{j}}\in\mathscr{C}.
From (
4.35
) we obtain

\displaystyle\|v^{*}_{j}-v\|^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum_{k=1}^{j}b_{k}(\varphi_{k,m_{j}}-\varphi_{k})\Big\|^{2}_{H}+\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}|b_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{j}|b_{k}|^{2}\Big)\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{j}\|\varphi_{k,m_{j}}-\varphi_{k}\|^{2}_{H}\Big)+\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}|b_{k}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq j4^{-m_{j}}\|v\|^{2}_{H}+\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}|b_{k}|^{2}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0.

It clearly follows that
\|v^{*}_{j}-\widetilde{v}_{j}\|^{2}_{H}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0
so that we have

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big|(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}v^{*}_{j}-\Phi_{n_{p}}\widetilde{v}_{j})_{H_{n_{p}}}\Big|\leq C\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{p\to\infty}\|\Phi_{n_{p}}(\widetilde{v}_{j}-v^{*}_{j})\|_{H_{n_{p}}}=C\lim_{j\to\infty}\|v^{*}_{j}-\widetilde{v}_{j}\|_{H}=0.

Meanwhile recalling that
(u,v)_{H}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}
using the above procedure we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big|(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}v^{*}_{j})_{H_{n_{p}}}-(u,v)_{H}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{j}|b_{k}|\lim_{p\to\infty}|(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}\varphi_{k,m_{j}})_{H_{n_{p}}}-a_{k}|+\Big|\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}\Big|

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{j}|b_{k}||a_{k}(m_{j})-a_{k}|+\Big|\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}j2^{-j}\|v\|_{H}+\Big|\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}\Big|=0.

Finally putting together the previous we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{p\to\infty}\Big|(u_{n_{p}},v_{n_{p}})_{H_{n_{p}}}-(u,v)_{H}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{p\to\infty}\Big|(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}v^{*}_{j})_{H_{n_{p}}}-(u,v)_{H}\Big|

\displaystyle+\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{p\to\infty}\Big|(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}(v^{*}_{j}-\widetilde{v}_{j}))_{H_{n_{p}}}\Big|

\displaystyle+\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{p\to\infty}\Big|(u_{n_{p}},\Phi_{n_{p}}\widetilde{v}_{j}-v_{n_{p}})_{H_{n_{p}}}\Big|=0.

∎

28
Mosco convergence of nonlocal to local quadratic forms

Here we establish the convergence in the sense of Mosco of a certain class of nonlocal quadratic
forms to some elliptic forms of gradient type. We essentially deal with Dirichlet forms.

Definition 5.60
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{B},m)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space, where is
X
is a locally compact separable metric space,
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel set of
X
and
m
is a Radon measure on
X
with full support. A Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(X,m)
is a couple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
satisfying
D_{1}-D_{4}
. Moreover
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
is called to be a regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(X,m)
if in addition
D_{5}
holds true.

1.

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
is dense in
L^{2}(X,m)
.

2.

\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})\times\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})\to\R
is a positive, (i.e.
\mathcal{E}(u,u)\geq 0
for all
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
) symmetric bilinear form.

3.

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
is closed if the space
(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})})
is a Hilbert space. Here
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})}
represents the graph norm defined by
\|u\|^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})}:=\mathcal{E}_{1}(u,u)=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X,m)}+\mathcal{E}(u,u).

4.

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
is Markovian if
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
and
v=(u\lor 0)\land 1
(called the normal contraction of
u
) then
v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathcal{E}(v,v)\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)
.

5.

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
is regular if it possesses a
core
i.e. there is a subset
\mathcal{C}
of
(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})\cap C_{c}(X)
such that
\mathcal{C}
is dense in
(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})})
and
\mathcal{C}
is dense in
(C_{c}(X),\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}).

Referring to
[
MR12
,
FOT11
]
, any Dirichlet form is uniquely associated to a Generator
(A,\mathcal{D}(A))
, a strong semigroup of contractions
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and a resolvent
(G_{\lambda})_{\lambda>0}
respectively characterized as follows.

\bullet
Generator

1.

(A,\mathcal{D}(A))
is a negative self-adjoint operator whose domain
\mathcal{D}(A)
is dense in
L^{2}(X,m)
,

2.

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{D}(\sqrt{-A})
and
\mathcal{E}(u,v)=(\sqrt{-A}u,\sqrt{-A}v)_{L^{2}(X,m)}
for all
u,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
.

3.

A
is closed, i.e.
(\mathcal{D}(A),\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}(A)})
is a Hilbert space where
\|u\|^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X,m)}+\|Au\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X,m)}.

4.

\mathcal{D}(A)\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathcal{E}(u,v)=(-Au,v)_{L^{2}(X,m)}
for all
u\in\mathcal{D}(A),\,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
.

\bullet
Semigroup

1.

(T_{t})_{t>0}
is a semigroup on
L^{2}(X,m)
:
T_{t}
is linear on
L^{2}(X,m)
and
T_{s}T_{t}=T_{t+s}
for all
s,t>0
.

2.

The contraction property holds, i.e.
\|T_{t}u\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}
,
t>0
,
u\in L^{2}(X,m)
.

3.

(T_{t})_{t>0}
is strongly continuous, i.e.
\|T_{t}u-u\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}\xrightarrow{t\to 0}0
.

4.

T_{t}u=e^{-At}u,

t>0
,
u\in L^{2}(X,m)
.
Au=\lim\limits_{t\to 0}\frac{T_{t}u-u}{t}
,
u\in\mathcal{D}(A)
.

\bullet
Resolvent

1.

(G_{\lambda})_{\lambda>0}
is a resolvent:
G_{\lambda}
is linear on
L^{2}(X,m)
and
G_{\lambda}-G_{\mu}+(\lambda-\mu)G_{\lambda}G_{\mu}=0
,
\lambda,\mu>0
.

2.

The contraction property holds, i.e.
\|\lambda G_{\lambda}u\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}
,
\lambda>0
,
u\in L^{2}(X,m)
.

3.

(G_{\lambda})_{\lambda>0}
is strongly continuous, i.e.
\|\lambda G_{\lambda}u-u\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}\xrightarrow{\lambda\to\infty}0
.

4.

G_{\lambda}u=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T_{t}u

\lambda>0
,
u\in L^{2}(X,m)
.
Au=\lambda u-G_{\lambda}^{-1}u
,
u\in\mathcal{D}(A)
.

Let us recall the notion of Mosco convergence and Gamma convergence of quadratic forms on
L^{2}
- spaces.

Definition 5.61

(Mosco convergence and Gamma convergence )
.

Assume
(\mathcal{E}^{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
are quadratic forms with dense domains in
L^{2}(X,m)
. One says that
(\mathcal{E}^{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
L^{2}(X,m)
in the Mosco sense (resp. in the Gamma sense) to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
if the following two conditions
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
(resp.
M_{1}
and
M^{\prime}_{2}
) are satisfied.

M_{1}.
(limsup):
For every
u\in L^{2}(X,m)
there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
in
L^{2}(X,m)
such that
u_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n})
,
u_{n}\to u
(read
u_{n}
strongly converges to
u
) in
L^{2}(X,m)
and

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{n}(u_{n},u_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u).

M_{2}.
(liminf):
For every sequence,
(u_{n})_{n}
with
u_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n})
and every
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
such that
u_{n}\rightharpoonup u
(read
u_{n}
weakly converges to
u
) in
L^{2}(X,m)
we have,

\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{n}(u_{n},u_{n}).

M^{\prime}_{2}.
(liminf):
For every sequence,
(u_{n})_{n}
with
u_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n})
and every
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
such that
u_{n}\to u
(read
u_{n}
strongly converges to
u
) in
L^{2}(X,m)
we have,

\mathcal{E}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{n}(u_{n},u_{n}).

Remark 5.62
.

(i)
It is worth emphasizing that combining the
\limsup
and
\liminf
conditions, the
\limsup
condition is equivalent to the existence of a sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
in
L^{2}(X,m)
such that
u_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n})
,
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}(X,m)
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{n}(u_{n},u_{n})=\mathcal{E}(u,u).

(ii)
It is clear that the convergence in the sense of Mosco implies Gamma convergence. The converse is true provided that the asymptomatic compactness holds, see Proposition
5.63
below.

(iii)
We adopt the convention that for a given quadratic form
\big(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})\big)
, we have

\mathcal{E}(u,u)=\infty
whenever
u\not\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
.

(iv)
Note that the Mosco convergence on Banach spaces in general is defined in
[
Töl10
]
.

Proposition 5.63

(Mosco vs Gamma )
.

Assume
(\mathcal{E}^{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n}
converges in Gamma sense to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
. Then
(\mathcal{E}^{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n}
converges in Mosco sense to
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))
if
(\mathcal{E}^{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is asymptotically compact, i.e. for any sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
such that
u_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}^{n})
and
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\big(\mathcal{E}^{n}(u_{n},u_{n})+\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(X,m)}\big)<\infty
has a strongly convergent subsequence.

Proof.
.

The proof is immediate.
∎

To a closed form
\mathcal{E}
corresponds a semigroup
(T_{t})_{t}
, Generator
(G_{\lambda})_{\lambda}
and a stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t}
. According to
[
FOT11
]
, if
(X_{t})_{t}
is a Markov process (resp. a Hunt process) then
\mathcal{E}
is a Dirichlet form (resp. a regular Dirichlet form). The Mosco convergence relates the convergence of Markov processes and the convergence of their corresponding Dirichlet forms.

Theorem 5.64

(
[
KS03
,
Mos94
,
Kol06
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{E}^{n},D(\mathcal{E}^{n}))
and
(\mathcal{E},D(\mathcal{E}))
be closed Dirichlet forms. The following are equivalent:

1.

(\mathcal{E}^{n},D(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n}
Mosco converges to
(\mathcal{E},D(\mathcal{E})).

2.

(G^{n}_{\lambda})_{n}
strongly converges to
G_{\lambda}
for every
\lambda
.

3.

(T^{n}_{t})_{n}
strongly converges to
T_{t}
for every
t
.

Moreover, if
(\mathcal{E}^{n},D(\mathcal{E}^{n}))_{n}
Mosco converges to
(\mathcal{E},D(\mathcal{E}))
then
(X^{n})_{n}
converges to
X
in finite dimensional distribution.

Next, we explain for which sequences of nonlocal quadratic forms we can prove convergence to a classical local gradient form. Let us recall our standing set-up in this section. Let
(\nu_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in(0,2)}
be a family of Lévy radial functions approximating the Dirac measure at the origin, i.e. for every
\alpha,\delta>0

(5.45)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nu_{\alpha}\geq 0,\,\,\text{ is radial},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=1,\quad\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int_{|h|>\delta}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0\,.\end{split}

Moreover, we assume that
h\mapsto\nu_{\alpha}(h)
is almost decreasing, i.e., for some
c\geq 1
and all
x,y
with
|x|\leq|y|
we have
\nu_{\alpha}(y)\leq c\,\nu_{\alpha}(x)
. Note that all examples of
(\nu_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
from Section
22
apply here as well. Moreover it is important to stress that
(\nu_{\alpha})_{0<\alpha<2}
defined as above generalizes the set-up of
[
FKV19
]
.

Given
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
J^{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\operatorname{diag}\to[0,\infty]
and sufficiently smooth functions
u,v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, define

(5.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}\big(u(y)-u(x)\big)\big(v(y)-v(x)\big)J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\,,

(5.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}\big(u(y)-u(x)\big)\big(v(y)-v(x)J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y.

Here, for sequence
(J^{\alpha})_{0<\alpha<2}
of positive symmetric kernels
J^{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\operatorname{diag}\to[0,\infty]
we set-up the following conditions:

(E)

There exists a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
such that for every
\alpha\in(0,2)
and all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
0<|x-y|\leq 1

(
E
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\leq J^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\nu_{\alpha}(x-y).

(L)

For every
\delta>0

(
L
)

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{|h|>\delta}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh=0.

(I)

For each
\alpha\in(0,2)
the kernel
J^{\alpha}
is translation invariant, i.e., for every
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(I)

\displaystyle J^{\alpha}(x+h,y+h)=J^{\alpha}(x,y).

(G-E)

There exists a constant
\Lambda\geq 1
such that for every
\alpha\in(0,2)
and all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
x\neq y

(
G
-
E
)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\leq J^{\alpha}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\nu_{\alpha}(x-y).

Clearly (

G

-

E

) implies (

E

) and (

L

). Finally, let us define the limit object, which is a local quadratic form of gradient type. Given
x\in\R^{d}
and
\delta>0
, we define the symmetric matrix
A(x)=(a_{ij}(x))_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
by

(5.48)

\displaystyle a_{ij}(x)=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh

and for
u,v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
the corresponding bilinear form by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v):=\int\limits_{\Omega}\big(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Remark 5.65
.

Let us discuss the assumption on the family
(J^{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.

(i)
Under conditions (

E

) and (

L

) the expression
\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha_{n}}(x,x+h)dx
converges for a suitable subsequence of
(\alpha_{n})
. The existence of the limit in (
5.48
) poses an implicit condition on the family
(J^{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.

(ii)
(

E

) and (

L

) ensure that the quantity
a_{ij}(x)
does not depend on the choice of
\delta
and is bounded as a function in
x
, i.e. for all
\delta,r>0
,

\displaystyle a_{ij}(x)=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int_{B_{r}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh.

(iii)
Under condition (
I
) the functions
a_{ij}(x)
,
1\leq i,j\leq d,
are constant in
x
.

(iv)
Regarding Proposition
2.38
, which asserts that

\displaystyle d^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\langle A(x)\xi,\xi\rangle\leq d^{-1}\Lambda|\xi|^{2},\quad\text{ for every }x,\xi\in\R^{d}\,,

condition (

E

) is a sufficient condition for what can be seen as nonlocal version of the classical ellipticity condition for second order operators in divergence form.

(v)
Condition (

L

) ensures that long-range interactions encoded by
J^{\alpha}(x,y)
vanish as
\alpha\to 2^{-}
. As a result, for some
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}=\sup_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{|h|>1}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh<\infty.

Hence conditions (

E

) and (

L

) imply the following uniform Lévy integrability type property:

(5.49)

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\,.

Let us begin with a simple but important observation.

Proposition 5.66
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
is a sufficiently smooth open bounded set or
\Omega=\R^{d}
. Let
\nu
be a unimodal Lévy kernel. Let
\nu_{\alpha}
,
J^{\alpha}
and
A
be as above.

1.

The following are regular Dirichlet forms on
L^{2}(\Omega)
:
(\mathcal{E}^{A},H^{1}(\Omega))
,
(\mathcal{E}^{A},H^{1}_{0}(\Omega))
,
(\mathcal{E}_{\Omega},H_{\nu}(\Omega))
,
(\mathcal{E}_{\Omega},H_{\nu,0}(\Omega))
,
(\mathcal{E},V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega},H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega))
,
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega},H_{\nu_{\alpha},0}(\Omega))
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha},V_{\nu}^{\Omega}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

2.

(\mathcal{E},V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\R^{d}))
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha},V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\R^{d}))
are regular Dirichlet forms on
L^{2}(\R^{d})
.

3.

The form
(\mathcal{E},V_{\nu}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu})
. The form
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha},V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\R^{d},\widetilde{\nu}_{\alpha})
. In particular for
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=C_{d,\alpha}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
then the form
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha},V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d}))
is a regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\R^{d},(1+|x|)^{-d-\alpha})
.

Proof.
.

For
(iii)
the regularity of the forms is a consequence of Theorem
3.70
and Lemma
3.24
. The remaining details are easy to establish using Theorem
3.70
, Theorem
3.75
and Theorem
3.76
.
∎

Proposition 5.67
.

Let
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
be as in (
5.49
). The quadratic forms
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(\cdot,\cdot),H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega))
and
\big(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot),V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
are well defined for every
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)
.

Proof.
.

Let
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)
and
u\in H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)
. By the assumption (

E

) and relation (
5.49
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y|>1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega\cap\{|x-y|\leq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+4\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{|x-y|>1}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+4\kappa_{0}\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)dx\leq(\Lambda+4\kappa_{0})\|u\|^{2}_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)}<\infty\,.

Now if
u\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
then, from the above we deduce
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(u,u)<\infty
. By the same argument we obtain

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}\cap\{|x-y|\leq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+2\hskip-8.61108pt\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}\cap\{|x-y|>1\}}(u^{2}(x)+u^{2}(y))J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}\cap\{|x-y|\leq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+2\kappa_{0}\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{2}(x)dx+2\kappa_{0}\int\limits_{\Omega^{c}}u^{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+2\kappa_{0}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{2}(x)dx<\infty\,.

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(u,u)+2\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty\,.

∎

Lemma 5.68
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an
H^{1}
-extension domain. Assume
J^{\alpha}
satisfies (

E

) and (

L

) and let
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
be as in (
5.49
). Then, there exists a constant
C:=C(D,\Lambda,d,\alpha_{0})
such that for every
u\in H^{1}(D)
and every
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{D}(u,u)\leq C\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}(D)}.

Proof.
.

From the symmetry of
J^{\alpha}(x,y)
and (
5.49
) we have the following estimates

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{D\times D\cap\{|x-y|\geq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2\int\limits_{D}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{|x-y|\geq 1}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\leq 2\kappa_{0}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D)}.

By Lemma
3.47
we get

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{D\times D\cap\{|x-y|\leq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}(D)}.

Combining the above estimates along with the condition (

E

) we get
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{D}(u,u)\leq C\|u\|^{2}_{H^{1}(D)}.

∎

The following result is reminiscent of Theorem
5.23
.

Theorem 5.69
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
H^{1}
-extension domain (or
D=\R^{d}
). Then, under assumptions (

E

) and (

L

), for all
u\in H^{1}(D)
we have

(5.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\iint\limits_{DD}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{D}(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla u(x))\,\mathrm{d}x.

In particular, if
C_{d,\alpha}
is the normalization constant of the fractional Laplacian then we have

(5.51)

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\iint\limits_{DD}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{d}\int_{D}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}\iint\limits_{DD}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{D}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.
.

Lemma
5.68
suggests that it suffices to prove (
5.50
) for
u
in a dense subset of
H^{1}(D)
. For instance, let us choose
u\in C_{c}^{2}(\overline{D})

(5.52)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{D\times D\cap\{|x-y|\geq 1\}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y\leq 4\int\limits_{D}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\int\limits_{|x-y|\geq 1}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\,\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2^{-}}0.

Now, we consider the mapping
F:D\times(0,2)\to\mathbb{R}
with

F(x,\alpha):=\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq 1}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

By Taylor expansion we have
u(y)-u(x)=\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x)+r_{1}(x,y)|x-y|^{2}
and therefore, we can write

(u(y)-u(x))^{2}=(\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x))^{2}+r(x,y)|x-y|^{3}

with bounded remainders
r(x,y)
and
r_{1}(x,y)
. Hence,
F(x,\alpha)
becomes

\displaystyle F(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq 1}[\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x)]^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+R(x,\alpha)\,

with

\displaystyle|R(x,\alpha)|

\displaystyle:=\Big|\,\int_{|x-y|\leq 1}r(x,y)|x-y|^{3}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq C\int_{|h|\leq 1}|h|^{3}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2^{-}}0.

Here, we have applied (

E

) and Remark
5.4
. Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}F(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq 1}[\nabla u(x)\cdot(y-x)]^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i,j\leq d}\partial_{i}u(x)\partial_{j}u(x)\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{|x-y|\leq 1}(y_{i}-x_{i})(y_{j}-x_{j})J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i,j\leq d}a_{ij}(x)\partial_{i}u(x)\partial_{j}u(x)=\langle A(x)\nabla u(x),\nabla u(x)\rangle.

Applying the dominated convergence theorem yields

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\hskip-12.91663pt\iint\limits_{D\times D\cap\{|x-y|\leq 1\}}\hskip-8.61108pt(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\,\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\int\limits_{D}F(x,\alpha)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int\limits_{D}\langle A(x)\nabla u(x),\nabla u(x)\rangle\ \,\mathrm{d}x.

The case
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)
follows from Theorem
5.14
since
K_{d,2}=\frac{1}{d}
. In fact, the cases
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)
and
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{C_{d,\alpha}}{2}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
follow from Example
2.37
.

∎

Corollary 5.70
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a
H^{1}
-extension domain. Then, under assumptions (

E

) and (

L

), for all
u\in H^{1}(D)
the family of Hilbert spaces
\big(H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}\big)_{\alpha}
converges to the Hilbert space
\big(H^{1}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{A}\big)
as
\alpha\to 2
in the sense of Definition
5.54
. Here we consider the norms

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(u,u)\quad\text{and}\quad\|u\|^{2}_{A}=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u).

Proof.
.

Note that Lemma
5.34
and the regularity of
\Omega
imply that
H^{1}(\Omega)
is a dense subspace of
H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)
. Thus result follows since by Theorem
5.69

\|u\|_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\|u\|_{A}
for every
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
.
∎

Theorem 5.71
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be a
H^{1}
-extension domain.
Let
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
with
\alpha_{n}\in(0,2)
be a sequence such that
\alpha_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}2
. Assume
(u_{n})_{n}
with
u_{n}\in H_{\nu_{\alpha_{n}}}(\Omega)
is a sequence converging in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. Under the assumptions (

E

) and (

L

) we have

(5.53)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla u(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}(u_{n}(x)-u_{n}(y))^{2}J^{\alpha_{n}}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Proof.
.

The claim clearly holds if
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})=\infty
. Assume
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})<\infty
. Then the sequence of real numbers
(\|u_{n}\|_{\alpha_{n}})_{n}
has a bounded subsequence. By Corollary
5.70
we know that the sequence of Hilbert spaces
\big(H_{\nu_{\alpha_{n}}},\|\cdot\|_{\alpha_{n}}\big)_{n}
converges to the Hilbert space
\big(H^{1}(\Omega),\|\cdot\|_{A}\big)
. Therefore, in view of Theorem
5.59
, there exists a subsequence of
(u_{n})_{n}
that we still denote by
u_{n}
converging weakly (in the sense of Definition
5.56
) to some
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
that is for every sequence
(v_{n})_{n}
with
v_{n}\in H_{\alpha_{n}}(\Omega)
strongly converging to
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\big(u_{n},v_{n}\big)_{H_{\alpha_{n}}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\big(u^{\prime},v\big)_{H^{1}(\Omega)}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|u^{\prime}\|_{A}^{2}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{\alpha_{n}}(\Omega)}.

In particular, for
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
the constant sequence
v_{n}=v
strongly converges to
v
. Thus since
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to u in
L^{2}(\Omega)
we have,

\displaystyle\big(u,v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}_{\Omega}(u_{n},v)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(u_{n},v_{n}\big)_{H_{\alpha_{n}}(\Omega)}=\big(u^{\prime},v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{A}(u^{\prime},v).

Wherefrom, we get
\big(u,v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big(u^{\prime},v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
for all
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. This implies that
u=u^{\prime}
a.e. on
\Omega
. Finally, since
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, the above inequality yields
\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u)\leq\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n}).
Indeed,

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u)=\|u\|^{2}_{A}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{H_{\alpha_{n}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n})

\displaystyle=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}_{\Omega}(u_{n},u_{n}).

∎

As a consequence of Theorem
5.69
and Theorem
5.71
the following is true.

Corollary 5.72
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
H^{1}
-extension domain (or
\Omega=\R^{d}
). Assume (

E

) and (

L

).
Then the quadratic forms
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(\cdot,\cdot),H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega))_{\alpha}
and converges to
(\mathcal{E}^{A}(\cdot,\cdot),H^{1}(\Omega))
in the Gamma sense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2^{-}
.

Finally, we now are in a position to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 5.73
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open bounded set with a Lipschitz continuous boundary. Assume (

E

) and (

L

).
Then the two families of quadratic forms
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(\cdot,\cdot),H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega))_{\alpha}
and

(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot),V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\alpha}
both converge to
(\mathcal{E}^{A}(\cdot,\cdot),H^{1}(\Omega))
in the Mosco sense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2^{-}
.

Proof.
.

Note that by Theorem
3.70

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and by Theorem
3.76

C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is dense in
H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)
. Hence it follows that
V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)
are dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. We proof the
\limsup
and the
\liminf
conditions separately.

Limsup:

Let
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, if
u\not\in H^{1}(\Omega)
then the
\limsup
statement holds true since
\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u)=\infty
. Now let
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. By identifying
u
to one of its extensions
\overline{u}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for the sake of simplicity we can always assume that
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. On the one hand, Theorem
5.69
shows that
\lim\limits_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u)
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(u,u)+2\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Since
\Omega
is Lipschitz, adapting the proof of Theorem
5.25
by applying Theorem
5.69
we find that

(5.54)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\to 0\quad\text{as $\alpha\to 2^{-}$.}

Thus, we conclude that for
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
,

\limsup_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(u,u)=\limsup_{\alpha\to 2^{-}}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,u)=\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u).

Thus, choosing the constant sequence
u_{\alpha}=u
for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
we are provided with the
\limsup
condition for both forms
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega},H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega))_{\alpha}
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha},V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\alpha}
.

Liminf
: Let
u,u_{n}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
be such that
u_{n}\rightharpoonup u
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Necessarily,
(u_{n})_{n}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Let
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
(0,2)
such that
\alpha_{n}\to 2^{-}
as
n\to\infty
. If
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=\infty
then,

\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,u)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})=\infty.

Assume
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}_{\Omega}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{n},u_{n})<\infty
then according to Theorem
5.40
the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
has a subsequence (which we again denote by
u_{n}
) converging in
L^{2}(\Omega)
to some
\widetilde{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. Consequently, as
u_{n}\rightharpoonup u
it readily follows that
(u_{n})_{n}
converges strongly to
u
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Therefore, taking into account that
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, the desired liminf inequality is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.71
. ∎

The next result is a variant of Theorem
5.73
with
H^{1}(\Omega)
replaced by
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
.

Theorem 5.74
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open bounded set with a continuous boundary. Assume (

E

) and (

L

).
Then the two families of quadratic forms
\big(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}_{\Omega}(\cdot,\cdot),\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)}\big)_{\alpha}
and

\big(\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\cdot,\cdot),V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)_{\alpha}
both converge to
(\mathcal{E}^{A}(\cdot,\cdot),H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))
in the Mosco sense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2^{-}
.

The result relies on the density of
\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)}
resp.
V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The density of the first space is trivial. The density of the second space is formulated in Theorem
3.75
. Apart from the density issue, the details of the proof are the same as in the proof of Theorem
5.73
.

29
Convergence of Dirichlet and Neumann problems

The main purpose of this section is to prove the convergence of weak solutions, eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of the nonlocal Dirichlet and Neumann problems the local ones. Here again we assume that
(\nu_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in(0,2)}
satisfies the condition (
5.45
) and the family
(J^{\alpha})_{\alpha}
of symmetric kernels either satisfies the conditions (

E

) and (

L

) or the global elliptic condition (

G

-

E

). Define the operators
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}
and
\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u(x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y~~\text{and}~~\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}u(x)=\int_{\Omega}(u(x)-u(y))J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Recall that we note

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(v,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\quad\text{for all}~~v\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(v,v)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Omega}\big(A(x)\nabla v(x)\cdot\nabla v(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\quad\text{for all}~~v\in H^{1}(\Omega)\,.

We remind that
A
is the elliptic symmetric matrix satisfying

d^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\langle A(x)\xi,\xi\rangle\leq d^{-1}\Lambda|\xi|^{2},
for every
x,\xi\in\R^{d}\,
given by (see (
5.48
))

\displaystyle a_{ij}(x)=\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int_{B_{\delta}}h_{i}h_{j}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)dh.

We also introduce the outwards normal derivative of a function
v
on
\partial\Omega
with respect to the matrix
A
(also called co-normal derivative of
v
) which is defined for
x\in\partial\Omega
by

\frac{\partial v}{\partial n_{A}}(x)=A(x)\nabla v(x)\cdot n(x).

We start with the following preparation result.

Lemma 5.75
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Lipschitz, open and bounded. Let
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
. With the conditions (
5.45
), (

E

), (

L

) and (
I
) in force, the following assertions hold.

1.

There exist
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
and a constant
C>0
independent of
\alpha
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)}\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}\varphi(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|v\|_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

2.

Assume
