v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}\varphi(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial n_{A}}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,.

In particular if
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=C_{d,\alpha}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
then
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial n_{A}}(x)=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial n}(x)
. Here,
C_{d,\alpha}
is the normalization constant given by the formula (
2.36
).

Proof.
.

The condition (
I
) gives
J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)=J^{\alpha}(x,x-h)
which implies that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u(x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{p.v.}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x)-u(y))J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\R^{d}}(u(x+h)+u(x-h)-2u(x))J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

Wherefrom, since
A
is constant, it is easy to show that (see Proposition
2.38
)

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u(x)=-\operatorname{tr}(A(\cdot)D^{2}u)(x)=-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla u)(x).

Combing the estimates (
5.49
), (
2.4
) and
|\varphi(x+h)-\varphi(x)|\leq 2\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\R^{d})}(1\land|h|)
we have

\displaystyle|\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi|\leq 4\kappa\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi,\varphi)\leq 4\kappa|\Omega|\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{for all }~~\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)\,

where

\displaystyle\kappa=\sup_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\,.

Therefore, since the linear mapping
v\mapsto\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi,v)-\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
is continuous on
V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Green-Gauss formula (
4.5
) is applicable for
\varphi\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
and hence the above estimates imply
(i)
as follows

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}\varphi(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi,v)-\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi,\varphi)^{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(v,v)^{1/2}+\|\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|v\|_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

We known that
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla\varphi)(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and since
|\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi|\leq 4\kappa\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, the Lebesgue dominated convergence Theorem yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\-\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla\varphi)(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

On the other hand, Theorem
5.25
combined with Theorem
5.69
implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi,v)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x.

Finally from the foregoing and the classical Green-Gauss formula we obtain
(ii)
as follows

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}\varphi(y)v(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi,v)-\lim_{\alpha\to 2}\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\varphi(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla\varphi)(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial n_{A}}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,.

The case
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=C_{d,\alpha}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
follows since
A=(\delta_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
see from Example
2.37
.

∎

The following result is crucial for our purpose.

Theorem 5.76
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, bounded and connected with Lipschitz boundary. Assume the conditions (
5.45
) and (

G

-

E

) hold. Let
u_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{*},2)}\|u_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},u_{\alpha})<\infty

Then there exist
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
and a subsequence
\alpha_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}2
such that
\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{\alpha_{n}},v)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\quad\text{for all $v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})$}.

The same is true for
\Omega=\R^{d}
with the exception that the strong convergence on
L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\R^{d})
, i.e. for every compact
K\subset\R^{d}
we have
\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u\|_{L^{2}(K)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
.

Proof.
.

On
H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)
and
H^{1}(\Omega)
we define the scalar products,

\displaystyle\big(w,\,v\big)_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}w(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}(w(x)-w(y))(v(x)-w(y))J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\big(w,v\big)_{H^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}w(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla w(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

In view of Theorem
5.69
, for both cases
\Omega\neq\R^{d}
and
\Omega=\R^{d}
, we have
\|v\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\|v\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}
for all
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. In other words,
the family of Hilbert spaces
(H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega))_{\alpha}
converges to the Hilbert space
H^{1}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2
in the sense of Definition
5.54
. Whence we deduce from Theorem
5.59
, there exists
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
and a subsequence
\alpha_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}2
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(u_{\alpha_{n}},v\big)_{H_{\nu_{\alpha_{n}}}(\Omega)}=\big(u,v\big)_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\quad\text{for all $v\in H^{1}(\Omega)$}.

On the other hand, for the case
\Omega\neq\R^{d}
Theorem
5.40
asserts there exist a further subsequence that we still denote by
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
and a function
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
such that
\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
. Instead, if
\Omega=\R^{d}
, then fact that
\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(K)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
for every compact
K\subset\R^{d}
is due to Theorem
5.35
. In either case, it is not difficult by taking the test function
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\R^{d})
to show that
u^{\prime}=u
a.e in
\Omega
. In both cases, from this we get the weak convergence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
that is,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(u_{\alpha_{n}},v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big(u,v\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\quad\text{for all $v\in H^{1}(\Omega)$}.

Therefore, this and the above weak convergence imply that

(5.55)

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega}(u_{\alpha_{n}}(x)-u_{\alpha_{n}}(y))(v(x)-v(y))J^{\alpha_{n}}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla u^{\prime}(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

The proof is thus complete for case
\Omega=\R^{d}
. Next, if
\Omega\neq\R^{d}
then by the uniform boundedness in (
5.57
) together with (

G

-

E

) we find that

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}\big|(u_{\alpha_{n}}(x)-u_{\alpha_{n}}(y))(v(x)-v(y))\big|

\displaystyle J^{\alpha_{n}}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\iint\limits_{\Omega\Omega^{c}}(v(x)-v(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,

where the convergence follows from Theorem
5.25
. This combined with (
5.55
) implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{\alpha_{n}},v)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla u(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

∎

Here is our first convergence theorem concerning weak solutions.

Theorem 5.77

(
Convergence of weak solution I
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, bounded and connected with Lipschitz boundary.Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a family converging weakly to some
f
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2
. For
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
define
g_{\alpha}=\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}\varphi
and
g=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial n_{A}}
. Assume the conditions (
5.45
) and (

G

-

E

) hold. Suppose
u_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
is a weak solution of the nonlocal Neumann problem
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u=f_{\alpha}
on
\Omega
and
\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}u=g_{\alpha}
on
\Omega^{c}
that is we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\alpha}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}\,.

Let
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
be the unique weak solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
of the Neumann problem
-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla u)=f
on
\Omega
and
\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial n_{A}}=g
on
\partial\Omega
that is we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\quad\text{for all }~~v\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}\,.

Under the condition (
I
) or else, if
g_{\alpha}=g=0
, then
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
, i.e.
(u_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
converges to
u
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Moreover, we have the weak convergence
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},v)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)
for all
v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
.

Proof.
.

In virtue of Theorem
5.42
combined with condition (

G

-

E

) there exist
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
and a constant
B>0
depending only on
\alpha_{0},\Omega
and
d
such that for all
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
and all
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(v,v)\quad\text{with $C=\Lambda B$}.

Therefore, up to relabelling the constant
C
, for all
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
and all
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)
we have

(5.56)

\displaystyle\|v\|^{2}_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(v,v).

On one hand, by the weak convergence of
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
we may assume
\sup\limits_{\alpha\in(0,2)}\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\infty
. This together with the definition of
u_{\alpha}
along with Lemma
5.75

(i)
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},u_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha}(x)u_{\alpha}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\alpha}(y)u_{\alpha}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{\alpha}\|_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\varphi\|_{C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{\alpha}\|_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})},

for some constant
C>0
independent of
\alpha
and
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)
. Combining this with (
5.56
) then for a generic constant
C>0
independent of
\alpha
we have the following uniform boundedness

(5.57)

\displaystyle\|u_{\alpha}\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega)}\leq\|u_{\alpha}\|_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)\,.

In accordance to Theorem
5.76
, there exist a subsequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
such that
\alpha_{n}\to 2
and a function
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
such that
\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{\alpha_{n}},v)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u^{\prime},v)=\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla u^{\prime}(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\,\quad\text{for all $v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})$}.

In addition we have
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
since
u_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
. The proof will be completed if we show that
u=u^{\prime}
. To this end, we fix
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
, given that
\Omega
has a Lipschitz boundary we let
\overline{v}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be an extension of
v
. We know that
\overline{v}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Thus by definition of
u_{\alpha_{n}}
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{\alpha_{n}},\overline{v})=\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha_{n}}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\alpha_{n}}(y)\overline{v}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,.

Now, given that
(f_{\alpha_{n}})_{n}
weakly converges to
f
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, letting
n\to\infty
in the above yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(u^{\prime},v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\,

if
g_{\alpha}=g=0
or by applying Lemma
5.75

(ii)
, if condition (
I
) holds. This holds true for any
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
. By uniqueness we have
u=u^{\prime}
on
\Omega
since
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
is the weak solution of the corresponding local Neumann problem.

The same reasoning is true for any sequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
with
\alpha_{n}\to 2
. Thereupon, the uniqueness of
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
implies that
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
and
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},v)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)
for all
v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
.
∎

The general case can be captured as follows.

Theorem 5.78

(
Convergence of weak solution II
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.77
assume that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\alpha}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\Omega}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=0\qquad\text{for all $\alpha\in(0,2)$}.

Assume
w_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a weak solution of the nonlocal Neumann problem
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u=f_{\alpha}
on
\Omega
and
\mathscr{N}_{\alpha}u=g_{\alpha}
on
\Omega^{c}
, i.e.

(5.58)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(w_{\alpha},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha}(x)v(x)+\int_{\Omega^{c}}g_{\alpha}(x)v(x)\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

Assume that the family
(c_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
with
c_{\alpha}=\hbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}w_{\alpha}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
is bounded. Then there exist
w\in H^{1}(\Omega)
and a sequence
\alpha_{n}\to 2
such that
\|w_{\alpha_{n}}-w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, where
w\in H^{1}(\Omega)

is a weak solution of the Neumann problem
-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla u)=f
on
\Omega
and
\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial n_{A}}=g
on
\partial\Omega
, i.e.

(5.59)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}g(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)\quad\text{for all }~~v\in H^{1}(\Omega)\,.

Conversely, if
w\in H^{1}(\Omega)
solves (
5.59
) then there exists
w_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
solving (
5.58
) such that we have
\|w_{\alpha}-w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
and
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(w_{\alpha},v)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\mathcal{E}^{A}(w,v)
for all
H^{1}(\R^{d})
.

Proof.
.

It suffices to observe that
w_{\alpha}=u_{\alpha}+c_{\alpha}
and
w=u+c
where
c_{\alpha}=\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}w_{\alpha}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
,
c=\mbox{$\fint_{\Omega}$}w(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
and the functions
u_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\perp}
,
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
are uniquely determined as in Theorem
5.77
such that
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
.
Thus, the forwards statement follows since by assumption
(c_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)}
is a bounded family of real numbers and thus has a converging subsequence. For the converse it suffices to take
w_{\alpha}=u_{\alpha}+c
with
c\in\R
.

∎

We will need the following lemma to establish the convergence of Dirichlet problem.

Lemma 5.79
.

Let
\Omega\subset\R^{d}
be open and bounded with continuous boundary. Assume the conditions (
5.45
) and (

E

) hold. Then there exist
\alpha_{*}\in(0,2)
and a constant
B>0
independent of
\alpha
such that for all
\alpha\in(\alpha_{*},2)
we have

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2B\Lambda\,\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,u)\quad\text{for all}~u\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d}).

Proof.
.

Since
\Omega
has continuous boundary, by Theorem
3.76
it is sufficient to prove the inequality for
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. In virtue of Theorem
5.45
there exist
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
and a constant
B>0
independent of
\alpha
such that for every
\alpha\in(\alpha_{0},2)
we have

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq B\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

From the condition (
5.45
) there exists
\alpha_{1}\in(0,2)
such that for all
\alpha\in(\alpha_{1},2)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{|h|\geq 1}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\frac{1}{16B}.

Let
\alpha_{*}=\max(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
, thus for
\alpha\in(\alpha_{*},2)
, employing the above and the condition (

E

) yields

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq B\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu_{\alpha}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq B\Lambda\iint\limits_{(\Omega^{c}\times\Omega^{c})^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}}(x-y)J^{\alpha}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+8B\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\int_{|h|\geq 1}\nu_{\alpha}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq B\Lambda\,\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Thereupon we get
\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq 2B\Lambda\,\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u,u).

∎

Theorem 5.80

(
Convergence of weak solution III
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, bounded and connected with continuous boundary. Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a family converging weakly to some
f
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2
. Let
g\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
.
Under the conditions (
5.45
), (

E

) and (

L

) assume
u_{\alpha}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a weak solution of the nonlocal Dirichlet problem
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}u=f_{\alpha}
on
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\Omega^{c}
, i.e.

u_{\alpha}-g\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha}(x)v(x)\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

Then
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
where
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
is the unique weak solution of the Dirichlet problem
\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla)u=f
on
\Omega
and
u=g
on
\partial\Omega
, i.e. we have
u-g\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\,.

Moreover, we have the weak convergence
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},v)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)
for all
v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
.

Proof.
.

Since,
u_{\alpha}-g\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
, in view of Lemma
5.79
there exist
\alpha_{*}\in(0,2)
and a constant
B>0
independent of
\alpha
such that for every
\alpha\in(\alpha_{*},2)
we have

\displaystyle\|u_{\alpha}-g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq 2B\Lambda\,\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)

which implies that

\displaystyle\|u_{\alpha}-g\|^{2}_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq(2B\Lambda+1)\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g).

In accordance to the definition of
u_{\alpha}
, taking
C=\sqrt{2B\Lambda+1}
, the following holds

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}-g)|+|\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,u_{\alpha}-g)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha}(x)(u_{\alpha}(x)-g(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|+|\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,u_{\alpha}-g)|

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|u_{\alpha}-g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,g)^{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)^{1/2}\big(\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,g)^{1/2}\big).

That is
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)^{1/2}\leq C\big(\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,g)^{1/2}\big).
Further, given that
u_{\alpha}-g=0
on
\Omega^{c}
, we get
\|u_{\alpha}-g\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\R^{d})}=\|u_{\alpha}-g\|_{V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})}.
Hence we have

\displaystyle\|u_{\alpha}-g\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\|u_{\alpha}-g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}C\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha}-g,u_{\alpha}-g)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{2}\big(\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,g)^{1/2}\big).

Wherefrom, we find that

\displaystyle\|u_{\alpha}\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\R^{d})}+\|u_{\alpha}-g\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\R^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{2}\big(\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\R^{d})}+2\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,g)^{1/2}\big).

On other the hand, under the conditions (

E

) and (

L

) the estimate (
5.49
) implies the following uniform Lévy integrability type property:

\displaystyle\kappa_{*}=\sup_{\alpha\in(\alpha_{*},2)}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\land|h|^{2})J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h<\infty\,\quad\text{with $\alpha_{*}>\alpha_{0}$.}

Next, exploiting the estimate (
3.14
) we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(g,g)\leq\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}(g(x)-g(y))^{2}J^{\alpha}(x,x+h)\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}x\leq 4\kappa_{*}\|g\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\R^{d})}<\infty\,.

By the weakly convergence of
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
we may assume
\sup\limits_{\alpha\in(0,2)}\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\infty
. Finally, we have shown that

(5.60)

\displaystyle\|u_{\alpha}\|_{H_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\R^{d})}\leq 2C^{2}\big(\sup\limits_{\alpha\in(0,2)}\|f_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(\R^{d})}+8\kappa_{*}\|g\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\R^{d})}\big):=M\quad\text{for all $\alpha\in(\alpha_{*},2)$}

where
M
does not depend on
\alpha
.
In view of Theorem
5.76
, there exist
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
and a subsequence
\alpha_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}2
such that for all compact
K\subset\R^{d},

\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(K)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
and for all
v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})

(5.61)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\iint\limits_{\R^{d}\R^{d}}(u_{\alpha_{n}}(x)-u_{\alpha_{n}}(y))(v(x)-v(y))J^{\alpha_{n}}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{\R^{d}}(A(x)\nabla u^{\prime}(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

We claim that
u^{\prime}-g=0
on
\Omega^{c}
. In fact for any compact
K\subset\Omega^{c}
since
u_{\alpha_{n}}-g=0
on
\Omega^{c}
we have

\|u^{\prime}-g\|_{L^{2}(K)}=\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(K)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Thus we have
u^{\prime}-g\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
and
u^{\prime}-g=0
on
\Omega^{c}
which means that
u^{\prime}-g\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. On the other hand, we also have

\|u_{\alpha_{n}}-u^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
since
\Omega
is bounded.
The proof will be completed if we show that
u=u^{\prime}
. To this end, we fix
v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. We naturally assume that
v=0
on
\Omega^{c}
so that
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The relation (
5.61
) implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{\alpha_{n}},v)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u^{\prime},v)=\int_{\Omega}(A(x)\nabla u^{\prime}(x)\cdot\nabla v(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

We know that
v\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
for all
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Thus by definition of
u_{\alpha_{n}}
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{n}}(u_{\alpha_{n}},v)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\alpha_{n}}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Now, given that
(f_{\alpha_{n}})_{n}
weakly converges to
f
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, letting
n\to\infty
in the above gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(u^{\prime},v)=\int_{\Omega}f(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

This holds true for any
v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and
u^{\prime}-g\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. Thus,
u^{\prime}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
is the weak solution of the corresponding local Dirichlet problem. By uniqueness we have
u=u^{\prime}
on
\Omega
.
The same reasoning is true for any sequence
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
with
\alpha_{n}\to 2
. Thereupon, the uniqueness of
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
implies that
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
and
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(u_{\alpha},v)\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\mathcal{E}^{A}(u,v)
for all
v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
.

∎

Another result on the convergence of Dirichlet problems can be found in
[
Voi17
]
. Next, we prove the convergence of nonlocal eigenvalues and eigenfunctions to the local ones.

Theorem 5.81

(
Convergence of eigenvalues I
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, bounded and connected with Lipschitz boundary. Assume that the conditions (

G

-

E

) and (
5.45
) are satisfied.
For each
\alpha
, assume that the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}
has a family of normalized Neumann eigenpairs
(\mu_{\alpha,n},\phi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
. That is for all
n\geq 0
,
\phi_{\alpha,n}\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\|\phi_{\alpha,n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
the elements
(\phi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
are mutually
L^{2}(\Omega)
-orthogonal, i.e.
(\phi_{\alpha,i},\phi_{\alpha,k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0
if
i\neq k
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\phi_{\alpha,n},v)=\mu_{\alpha,n}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{\alpha,n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

Then for each
n\geq 0
, the eigenpair
(\mu_{\alpha,n},\phi_{\alpha,n})_{\alpha}
converges to
(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\phi^{\prime}_{n})
in
\R\times L^{2}(\Omega)
up to a subsequence as
\alpha\to 2
. To be more precise,
\mu_{\alpha_{j},n}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}\mu^{\prime}_{n}
in
\R
and
\|\phi_{\alpha_{j},n}-\phi^{\prime}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
for some
\alpha_{j}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}2
. Moreover the family
(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\phi^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
is the sequence of the normalized Neumann eigenpairs of the
\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla)
, i.e.
(\phi^{\prime}_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\delta_{i,k}
and for each
n\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(\phi^{\prime}_{n},v)=\mu^{\prime}_{n}\int_{\Omega}\phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in H^{1}(\Omega)\,.

Proof.
.

First of all, we know that
\phi_{\alpha,0}=\phi^{\prime}_{0}=|\Omega|^{-1}
and
\mu_{\alpha,0}=\mu^{\prime}_{0}=0
. By proceeding as in the proof of Theorem
5.48
, it can be shown that

\mu_{\alpha,1}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}\mu^{\prime}_{1}
. It can be shown that
\phi_{\alpha,1}
converges to some
\phi^{\prime}_{1}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
up to a subsequence. More generally, for fixed
n\geq 1
assume there exists a sequence
\alpha_{j}:=\alpha_{j}(n)\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}2
such that for every
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
(\mu_{\alpha_{j},k},\phi_{\alpha_{j}(n),k})_{n}
converges to
(\mu^{\prime}_{k},\phi^{\prime}_{k})
in
\R\times L^{2}(\Omega)
as
j\to\infty
. Then by Rayleigh’s quotient (see Chapter
4
) we have

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha_{j},n}=\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{j}}(\phi_{\alpha_{j},n},\phi_{\alpha_{j},n})=\min_{v\in V_{\alpha_{j},n}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{j}}(v,v)}{\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}\Big\}

where
V_{\alpha_{j},n}=\big\{v\in V_{\alpha_{j}}(\Omega|\R^{d}):(\phi_{\alpha_{j},k},v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0,~i=0,1,\cdots,n-1\big\}
. Observing that
V_{\alpha_{j},n}\subset L^{2}(\Omega)^{\perp}
, it follows from the robust Poincaré inequality (see Corollary
5.43
) that there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\alpha_{j}
for which we have

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}_{\alpha_{j},n}\leq C\quad\text{for all $j\geq 1$}.

That is, the sequence
(\mu^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}n})_{j}
is bounded. Thus, without loss of generality we may assume that
\mu^{-1}_{\alpha_{j},n}
converges to some
\mu^{\prime-1}_{n}\geq 0
as
j\to\infty
. On the other hand, the sequence
(\mu_{\alpha_{j},n})_{j}
being bounded, we have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{\alpha_{j},n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\mathcal{E}^{\alpha_{j}}(\phi_{\alpha_{j},n},\phi_{\alpha_{j},n})\leq 1+\sup_{j\geq 1}\mu_{\alpha_{j},n}:=M<\infty\quad\text{for all $j\geq 1$}.

Therefore, from Theorem
5.76
one is able to find a further subsequence
(\alpha_{j}(n+1))_{j}
which we denote
\alpha^{\prime}_{j}=\alpha_{j}(n+1)
of the sequence
(\alpha_{j})_{j}
( recall
\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j}(n)
) and
\phi^{\prime}_{n}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\|\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},n}-\phi^{\prime}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0
and for all
v\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(\phi^{\prime}_{n},v)

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{j}}(\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},n},v)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mu_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},n}\int_{\Omega}\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\mu^{\prime}_{n}\int_{\Omega}\phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

In particular, since
\partial\Omega
is Lipschitz we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(\phi^{\prime}_{n},v)=\mu^{\prime}_{n}\int_{\Omega}\phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all $v\in H^{1}(\Omega)$}.

Moreover, for
0\leq i\leq k\leq n
we have

\displaystyle(\phi^{\prime}_{i},\phi^{\prime}_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\lim_{j\to\infty}(\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},i},\phi^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}_{j},k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\delta_{i,k}.

By induction the result remains true for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
∎

A similar reasoning leads to the convergence of eigenpairs associated with Dirichlet condition.

Theorem 5.82

(
Convergence of eigenvalues II
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, bounded and connected. Assume that the conditions (

G

-

E

) and (
5.45
) are satisfied.
For each fixed
\alpha
, assume that the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}
has a family of normalized Dirichlet eigenpairs
(\lambda_{\alpha,n},\varphi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
. That is, for all
n\geq 1
,
\phi_{\alpha,n}\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d})
,
\|\varphi_{\alpha,n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=1
the elements of
(\varphi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
are mutually
L^{2}(\Omega)
-orthogonal, i.e.
(\varphi_{\alpha,i},\varphi_{\alpha,k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0
if
i\neq k
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}(\varphi_{\alpha,n},v)=\lambda_{\alpha,n}\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{\alpha,n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in V^{\Omega}_{\nu_{\alpha}}(\Omega|\R^{d})\,.

Then for each
n\geq 1
, the eigenpair
(\lambda_{\alpha,n},\varphi_{\alpha,n})_{\alpha}
converges to
(\lambda^{\prime}_{n},\varphi^{\prime}_{n})
in
\R\times L^{2}(\Omega)
up to a subsequence as
\alpha\to 2
. To be more precise,
\lambda_{\alpha_{j},n}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}\lambda^{\prime}_{n}
in
\R
and
\|\varphi_{\alpha_{j},n}-\phi^{\prime}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0
for some
\alpha_{j}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}2
. Moreover the family
(\lambda^{\prime}_{n},\varphi^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
is the sequence of the normalized Dirichlet eigenpairs of the
\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla)
, i.e.
(\varphi^{\prime}_{i},\varphi^{\prime}_{k})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\delta_{i,k}
and for each
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{A}(\varphi^{\prime}_{n},v)=\lambda^{\prime}_{n}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{\prime}_{n}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }~~v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\,.

Specializing the aforementioned results with
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=C_{d,\alpha}|x-y|^{-d-\alpha}
that is we get
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}=(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
and
-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla)=-\Delta
we get following corollaries.

Corollary 5.83
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open bounded and connected with Lipschitz boundary. Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a family converging weakly to some
f
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2
. For
\varphi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
define
g_{\alpha}=\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}\varphi
and
g=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial n}
. Let
u_{\alpha}\in V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
be the weak solution in
V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d})^{\perp}
of the nonlocal Neumann problem for the fractional Laplacian, i.e.
u_{\alpha}
is a weak solution of the following

\displaystyle\text{$(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=f_{\alpha}$ on $~\Omega$ \hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt$\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}u=g_{\alpha}$ on $~\Omega^{c}$}.

Then we have
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
where
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
is the unique weak solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)^{\perp}
of the Neumann problem

\displaystyle-\Delta u=f\,\,\text{ on ~~$\Omega$}\quad\text{ and}\quad\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}=g\text{ on ~$\partial\Omega$}.

Furthermore, let
(\mu_{\alpha,n},\phi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
be the family of normalized Neumann eigenpairs of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
. Let
(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\phi^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
be the sequence of normalized Neumann eigenpairs of the Laplacian
-\Delta
. Then for each
n\geq 0
, the eigenpair
(\mu_{\alpha,n},\phi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
converges to
(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\phi^{\prime}_{n})
in
\R\times L^{2}(\Omega)
up to a subsequence as
\alpha\to 2
.

Corollary 5.84
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open, bounded and connected. Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
be a family converging weakly to some
f
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as
\alpha\to 2
. Let
g\in H^{1}(\R^{d})
. Let
u_{\alpha}\in V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d})
be the weak solution in
V^{\alpha/2}(\Omega|\R^{d})
of the nonlocal Dirichlet problem for the fractional Laplacian, i.e.
u_{\alpha}
is a weak solution of the following

\displaystyle\text{$(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=f_{\alpha}$ on $~\Omega$ \hskip 10.22217ptand\hskip 10.22217pt$u=g$ on $~\Omega^{c}$}.

Then
\|u_{\alpha}-u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\xrightarrow{\alpha\to 2}0
where
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
be the unique weak solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)
of the Dirichlet problem

\displaystyle-\Delta u=f\,\,\text{ on ~~$\Omega$}\quad\text{ and}\quad u=g\text{ on ~$\partial\Omega$}.

Furthermore, let
(\lambda_{\alpha,n},\varphi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
be the family of normalized Dirichlet eigenpairs of the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
. Let
(\lambda^{\prime}_{n},\varphi^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
be the sequence of normalized Dirichlet eigenpairs of the Laplacian
-\Delta
. Then for each
n\geq 1
, the eigenpair
(\lambda_{\alpha,n},\varphi_{\alpha,n})_{n}
converges to
(\lambda^{\prime}_{n},\varphi^{\prime}_{n})
in
\R\times L^{2}(\Omega)
up to a subsequence as
\alpha\to 2
.

Another special case is to consider
J^{\alpha}(x,y)=\frac{1}{d}\nu_{\alpha}(x,y)
with
(\nu_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
satisfying the condition (
5.45
). In this case we also have
-\operatorname{div}(A(\cdot)\nabla)=-\Delta
.

Chapter 6
Lebesgue Spaces

Appendix 6.A
Lebesgue spaces

Let the triplet
(X,\mathcal{A},\mu)
be a measure space that is
X
is a set,
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra on
X
and
\mu:\mathcal{A}\to[0,\infty]
is positive measure on
X
. Basic notions related to such a triplet are recorded in
[
Alt16
,
Bog07
,
Gra08
]
especially in
[
EG15
]
. Our exposition here and in the next section abundantly relies upon these materials .

Definition 6.1
.

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, the space
L^{p}(X)
is the space of class of measurable functions
u:X\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}<\infty
with

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/p}\quad\textrm{for}\quad 1\leq p<\infty

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}

\displaystyle=\inf\{c>0:|u|\leq c~~\mu-\textrm{a.e on $X$}\}\quad\textrm{for}\quad p=\infty.

Let us quote without proofs some significant results on
L^{p}(X)
. We start with Hölder inequality which appears to be the most important inequality on
L^{p}
-spaces.

Theorem 6.2

(Hölder inequality)
.

For
1\leq p,p^{\prime}\leq\infty
, such that
p+p^{\prime}=pp^{\prime}
then for all measurable functions
u,v:X\to\mathbb{R}
the following inequalities
\|uv\|_{L^{1}(X)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}\|v\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(X)}
holds true that is

\displaystyle\int_{X}|u(x)v(x)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\Big(\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/p}\Big(\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p^{\prime}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)^{1/p^{\prime}}.

More generally assume
r,p_{1}\cdots,p_{n}\in[1,\infty]
satisfy the relation

\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\cdots+\frac{1}{p_{n}}=\frac{1}{r}.

Then for all measurable functions
u_{1},\cdots,u_{n}:X\to\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\|u_{1}\cdots u_{n}\|_{L^{r}(X)}\leq\|u_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(X)}\cdots\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p_{n}}(X)}.

In particular,
u_{1}\cdots u_{n}\in L^{r}(X)
once
u_{i}\in L^{p_{i}}(X),~i=1,\cdots,n
.

One of the most influential inequalities on the integration theory is the Jensen’s inequality.

Theorem 6.3

(Jensen inequality)
.

Let
u:X\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be a convex function such that
\varphi\circ u\in L^{1}(X)
. Assume
\mu(X)<\infty
then,

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\frac{1}{\mu(X)}\int_{X}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)\leq\frac{1}{\mu(X)}\int_{X}\varphi\circ u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Actually, it is possible to derive Hölder inequality from Jensen’s inequality.
Exploiting the Hölder inequality we are able to prove the Minkowski inequality.

Theorem 6.4

(Minkowski inequality)
.

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, then for all measurable functions
u,v:X\to\mathbb{R}
one has the following triangle inequality

\displaystyle\|u+v\|_{L^{p}(X)}\leq\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}+\|v\|_{L^{p}(X)}.

The Minkowski inequality shows that
L^{p}(X)
is a normed space. We have the following.

Theorem 6.5

(Riesz-Fischer)
.

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, the space
L^{p}(X)
is a Banach space under the norm
u\mapsto\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}
.

The next very important result is the monotone convergence theorem due
to Lebesgue and Beppo Levi.

Theorem 6.6

(Monotone convergence or Beppo Levi)
.

Assume
(u_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of nonnegative measurable functions on
X
such that
0\leq u_{n}\leq u_{n+1}
a.e for each
n\geq 1
then

\displaystyle\int_{X}\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}u_{n}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

An alternative to the monotone convergence theorem when the monotonicity is violated is the so called Fatou’s lemma.

Theorem 6.7

(Fatou’s lemma)
.

Assume
(u_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of nonnegative measurable functions on
X
then

\displaystyle\int_{X}\liminf_{n\to\infty}u_{n}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}u_{n}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Of course to complete the discussion on convergence one needs to recalls the Lebesgue Dominated convergence theorem but we defer this to the next section which will be derived as an immediate consequence of the Vitali convergence Theorem (cf. Theorem
6.25
).

Theorem 6.8

(Fubini Theorem)
.

Let
(X_{1},\mathcal{A}_{i},\mu_{i})~i=1,2
be two
\sigma
-finite measurable spaces and let
\big(X_{1}\times X_{2},\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}\big)
be the corresponding product space. Assume
v:X_{1}\times X_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{2}
-measurable then
18
18

18

Fubini theorem for nonnegative functions is often known as the Fubini-Tonelli theorem

\displaystyle\hskip-5.48096pt\int\limits_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}|v(x_{1},x_{2})|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}\hskip-1.82698pt\otimes\hskip-1.82698pt\mu_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\hskip-0.73077pt=\int\limits_{X_{1}}\Big(\hskip-3.65398pt\int\limits_{X_{2}}\hskip-1.82698pt|v(x_{1},x_{2})|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{2})\hskip-0.73077pt=\int\limits_{X_{2}}\Big(\hskip-3.65398pt\int\limits_{X_{1}}|v(x_{1},x_{2})|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{2})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{1})

additionally if
v\in L^{1}(X_{1}\times X_{2})
then,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}v(x_{1},x_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\hskip-1.82698pt=\int\limits_{X_{1}}\Big(\hskip-1.82698pt\int\limits_{X_{2}}v(x_{1},x_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{1})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{2})\hskip-1.82698pt=\int\limits_{X_{2}}\Big(\hskip-1.82698pt\int\limits_{X_{1}}v(x_{1},x_{2})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x_{2})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(x_{1}).

Appendix 6.B
The Vitali convergence theorem

It is the aim of this section to establish the Vitali convergence theorem which provides necessary and sufficient conditions for convergence in
L^{p}
-spaces. Throughout, we assume that

(X,\mathcal{A},\mu)
is a measure space and by "measurable" or "integrable" we refer to this triplet. We start with some basics.

Definition 6.9
.

A sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of measurable functions is said to converge in measure to
u
if for all
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu\{x\in X:~~|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|\geq\varepsilon\}=0.

We said that
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy in measure if for all
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\lim_{m,n\to\infty}\mu\{x\in X:~~|u_{m}(x)-u_{n}(x)|\geq\varepsilon\}=0.

Let us see the connections between the convergence in measure and other types of convergence.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
u
and
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be measurable functions. The following statements hold true.

1.

Suppose
\mu(X)<\infty
. If the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
converges pointwise
\mu
-almost everywhere to
u
then it converges in measure to
u
.

2.

If the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
converges in
L^{p}(X)~~(1\leq p<\infty)
to
u
then it converges in measure to
u
.

3.

If the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
converges in measure to
u
then there is a subsequence
(u_{n_{j}})_{j}
of
(u_{n})_{n}
which converges pointwise
\mu
-almost everywhere to
u
.

Proof.
.

Without any loss of generality we assume that
u=0
.

(i)
Given that
u_{n}\to 0

\mu
-a.e., for any
\varepsilon>0
the measurable set
E=\{x\in X:~\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}|u_{n}(x)|>\varepsilon\}
has measure zero and
contains the measurable sets
B_{n}=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq n}\{|u_{k}|\geq\varepsilon\}
with
B_{n+1}\subset B_{n}
. Since
\mu(X)<\infty
, by monotonicity of the measure
\mu
we get

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mu(\{x\in X:~|u_{n}(x)|>\varepsilon\})\leq\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mu(\bigcup\limits_{k\geq n}\{|u_{k}|\geq\varepsilon\})=\mu(\bigcap\limits_{n\geq 1}B_{n})\leq\mu(E)=0.

(ii)
Assume
|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(X)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
then the convergence in measure is a straightforward consequence of Chebyshev’s inequality, since for all
\varepsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mu(\{x\in X:~|u_{n}(x)|>\varepsilon\})\leq\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(X)}}{\varepsilon}=0.

(iii)
Assume
u_{n}
converges in measure to
0
then for every
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2^{k}}
we capable to gradually construct
(n_{k})_{k}
with
n_{k}<n_{k+1}
such that

\displaystyle\mu(\{x\in X:~|u_{n}(x)|>\frac{1}{2^{k}}\})\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\qquad\textrm{
for all }\quad n\geq n_{k}.

Now let

\displaystyle E=\bigcap\limits_{n\geq 1}\bigcup\limits_{k\geq n}\{x\in X:~|u_{n_{k}}(x)|>\frac{1}{2^{k}}\}=\bigcap\limits_{n\geq 1}E_{n}.

Observing that
E_{n+1}\subset E_{n}
and
\mu(X)<\infty
then the monotonicity of the measure
\mu
yields

\displaystyle\mu(E)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu(E_{n})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\mu(\{|u_{n_{k}}|>\frac{1}{2^{k}}\})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n-1}}=0.

If
x\in X\setminus E
then there exists
N_{x}\geq 1
such that
x\not\in\{x\in X:~|u_{n_{k}}(x)|>\frac{1}{2^{k}}\}
for all
k\geq N_{x}
i.e
|u_{n_{k}}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}
for all
k\geq N_{x}
which means that
u_{n_{k}}(x)\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0
. Hereby providing the subsequence sought for.
∎

Setwise the convergence in measure on a finite measure space may be defined by means of a topology.

Proposition 6.11
.

Assume the measure
\mu
is finite, i.e.
\mu(X)<\infty
. Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of measurable functions. A necessary and sufficient condition for
(u_{n})_{n}
to converge in measure is that
\rho(u_{n})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
where

\displaystyle\rho(u)=\int_{X}\frac{|u(x)|}{1+|u(x)|}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Proof.
.

First and foremost, it clearly appears that for each
\varepsilon>0
and every
n\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle A_{n}=\{x\in X:~|u_{n}(x)|\geq\varepsilon\}=\Big\{x\in X:~\frac{|u_{n}(x)|}{1+|u_{n}(x)|}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\Big\}.

Utilizing this remark, entails on the one hand that
\mu(A_{n})\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\rho(u_{n})
and on the other hand that

\displaystyle\rho(u_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{A_{n}}\frac{|u_{n}(x)|}{1+|u_{n}(x)|}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int_{X\setminus A_{n}}\frac{|u_{n}(x)|}{1+|u_{n}(x)|}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A_{n}}1\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int_{X\setminus A_{n}}\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\mu_{(}A_{n})+\varepsilon\mu(X).

The claim readily follows by letting
n\to\infty
and after
\varepsilon\to 0
.

∎

Remark 6.12
.

It clearly appears that the convergence in measure induces a topology on the set of class of measurable
functions on
X
which is metrisable relatively to the distance function

\displaystyle\rho(u,v):=\rho(u-v)=\int_{X}\frac{|u(x)-v(x)|}{1+|u(x)-v(x)|}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

The convergence in measure induces a topology on the space of class of measurable functions. The following theorem states its completeness under this topology. For a proof we refer to
[
Bog07
,
Gra08
]
.

Theorem 6.13
.

A sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
of measurable functions on
X
that is Cauchy in measure converges in measure up to a subsequence.

Theorem 6.14

(Ergorov’s Theorem)
.

Assume that
E\subset X
is a measurable subset with finite measure, i.e.
\mu(E)<\infty
. If
(u_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of measurable functions converging almost everywhere on
A
to a function
u
then for every
\varepsilon>0
there is
A\subset E
such that
\mu(A)<\varepsilon
and the convergence of
(u_{n})_{n}
to
u
holds uniformly on
E\setminus A
.

Proof.
.

Let us fix
k\geq 1
and consider the measurable set

\displaystyle A_{k}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}A_{n,k}\qquad\text{with}\quad A_{n,k}=\bigcup_{m\geq n}\{x\in E:~|u_{m}(x)-u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{k}\}.

Let
x\in A_{k}
, it turns out that for all
n\geq 1
there is
m\geq n
such that
|u_{m}(x)-u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{k}
.
In other words,
x
belongs to a set of measure zero given that
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
almost everywhere on
E
. Hence it is easy to recognise that
\mu(A_{k})=0
.
Moreover
\mu(E)<\infty
and
A_{n+1,k}\subset A_{n,k}
so that by the monotonicity of the measure
\mu
,
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mu(A_{n,k})=\mu(A_{k})=0
. Now for fixed
\varepsilon>0
there is
n_{k}
such that
\mu(A_{n_{k},k})<\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k}}.

Therefore setting

A=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 1}A_{n_{k},k}=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 1}\bigcup\limits_{m\geq n_{k}}\{x\in E:~|u_{m}(x)-u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{k}\}

one obtains
\mu(A)<\varepsilon
and the uniform convergence
(u_{n})_{n}
on
E\setminus A
follows since for each
k\geq 1

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in E\setminus A}|u_{m}(x)-u(x)|<\frac{1}{k}\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad m\geq n_{k}.

∎

It is significant to mention that the conclusion of Ergorov’s theorem may fail if the finiteness of
\mu(E)
is violated. Indeed on
\mathbb{R}
, the sequence
u_{n}=\mathds{1}_{[n,n+1]}
converges pointwise everywhere to
u=0
on
\mathbb{R}
and does not uniformly since for each
n\geq 1

\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|u_{n}(x)|=1
.

Let us now introduce some important notions such as the equiintegrability and the tightness.

Definition 6.15
.

A set
\mathcal{F}
of measurable functions on
X
is called uniformly integrable (or equiintegrable) if: for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for a measurable set
E\in\mathcal{A}
, if
\mu(E)<\delta
, then

\displaystyle\int_{E}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad u\in\mathcal{F}.

This can be fairly written in short form as

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu(E)\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{E}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

More generally,
\mathcal{F}
is called
p
-uniformly integrable (or
p
-equiintegrable) for some
1\leq p<\infty
if

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu(E)\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{E}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

The next result gives an equivalent definition of the equiintegrability.

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of measurable functions. The following assertions are true.

1.

A necessary and sufficient condition for
\mathcal{F}
to be uniformly integrable is that

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu(E)\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\int_{E}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|=0.

2.

If in addition,
\mathcal{F}
is a bounded subset of
L^{p}(X)
then
\mathcal{F}
is uniformly integrable if and only if

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\{|u|\geq R\}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\inf_{R\geq 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\{|u|\geq R\}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

Proof.
.

(i)
It is clear by definition that the uniform integrability implies

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu(E)\to 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\int_{E}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|=0.

For the converse, let
\varepsilon
and
\delta
be such that if
\mu(E)<\delta
,

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{E}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|<\varepsilon/2\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad u\in\mathcal{F}.

Observe that for every
u\in\mathcal{F}
and fix a measurable set
E
with
\mu(E)<\delta
, the sets
E_{1}=E\cap\{u\geq 0\}
and
E_{2}=E\cap\{u<0\}
are measurable too. Hence, the desired claim follows from the above since
u
is arbitrarily chosen and
\mu(E_{1}),\mu(E_{2})<\delta
and

\displaystyle\int_{E}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{E\cap\{u\geq 0\}}\hskip-21.52771ptu(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int_{E\cap\{u<0\}}\hskip-21.52771pt-u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{E_{1}}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|+\Big|\int_{E_{2}}u(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|<\varepsilon.

(ii)
Assume
\mathcal{F}
is bounded in
L^{p}(X)
and let
M=\sup\limits_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}
. For
u\in\mathcal{F}
and every
R>0
we have

\displaystyle\mu\{|u|>R\}=\frac{1}{R^{p}}\int\limits_{\{|u|>R\}}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\frac{1}{R^{p}}\int_{X}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\frac{M}{R^{p}}.

This forces
\mu\{|u|>R\}\to 0
as
R\to\infty
and hence if
\mathcal{F}
is uniformly integrable then

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\{|u|\geq R\}}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\inf_{R\geq 0}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\{|u|\geq R\}}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

The converse holds straightforwardly since for each measurable set
E
with a small measure, the following is true.

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{E}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{E\cap\{|u|<R\}}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{E\cap\{|u|\geq R\}}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq M\mu(E)+\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\{|u|\geq R\}}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

∎

We now introduce the tightness which an is accurate expedient concept preventing a family of measurable function from escaping to infinity.

Definition 6.17
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. A set
\mathcal{F}
of measurable functions on
X
is called to be
p
-tight (or simply tight for
p=1
) if for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists measurable set
E
with a finite measure i.e
\mu(E)<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\int_{X\setminus E}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad u\in\mathcal{F}.

This can be written in compact form as

\displaystyle\inf_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu(E)<\infty}}{{E~\textrm{measurable}}}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{X\setminus E}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

When
X=\mathbb{R}^{d}
is equipped with the Lebesgue measure the
p
-tightness
\mathcal{F}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{F}}\int\limits_{\{|x|\geq R\}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

Warning: Some authors define equiintegrability as uniform integrability plus
tightness. Of course if
\mu(X)<\infty
then the tight is gratuitous.

Remark 6.18
.

Let
u\in L^{p}(X)
then the set
\mathcal{F}=\{u\}
is
p
-tight and
p
-uniformly integrable. Indeed, since
u
is integrable, it emanates form the monotone convergence theorem that for
\varepsilon>0
there is
n\geq 1
large enough such that

\displaystyle\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{\{|u|\leq\frac{1}{n}\}}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon

and as well

\displaystyle\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{\{|u|>n\}}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon/2.

On one hand, the tightness follows from the first estimate since letting
A_{n}=\{x\in X:~|u(x)|>\frac{1}{n}\}
the Chebyshev’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mu(A_{n})=\mu(\{x\in X:~|u(x)|>\frac{1}{n}\})\leq n\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}<\infty

and we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{X\setminus A_{n}}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{\{|u|\leq\frac{1}{n}\}}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon.

On the other hand, for any measurable set
E
with
\mu(E)<\delta=\varepsilon/2n^{p}
the second inequality leads to the uniform integrability through the following.

\displaystyle\int_{E}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}|u(x)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{\{|u|\leq n\}}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int_{E}|u(x)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{\{|u|>n\}}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq n^{p}\mu(E)+\int_{X}|u(x)|^{p}\mathds{1}_{\{|u|>n\}}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon.

Correspondingly, upon the above observation any finite subset
\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{N}\}
of
L^{p}(X)
is also
p
-tight and
p
-uniformly integrable.

We now are in a position to state the Vitali convergence theorem.

Theorem 6.19

(Vitali convergence theorem)
.

Let
u
and
(u_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of measurable functions. Then
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(X)
with
1\leq p<\infty
if and only if
(u_{n})_{n}
is
p
-uniformly integrable,
p
-tight and converges in measure to
u
.

Proof.
.

Assume
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(X)
then the convergence in measure follows from to Proposition
6.10
. Note that for every measurable set
A\subset X
we have

\displaystyle\int_{A}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{A}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+2^{p-1}\int_{X}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Given that for each
N\geq 1
the set
\{u,u_{1},\cdots,u_{N}\}
is
p-
uniformly integrable and
p
-tight as a finite subset of
L^{p}(X)
so is the sequence
(u_{n})_{n}
since
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(x)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
.

Let us now show that the converse holds true. Again from Proposition
6.10
we can assume that
(u_{n})_{n}
converges almost everywhere to
u
on
X
. Now for fixed
\varepsilon>0
the
p
-uniform integrability and the
p
-tightness imply that there exist
\delta>0
and a measurable set
E\subset X
with
\mu(E)<\infty
both depending solely on
\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{X\setminus E}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{A}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon\quad\textrm{for all}\quad\mu(A)<\delta.

Given the pointwise convergence, with the aid of the Fatou’s lemma the above estimates respectively imply

\displaystyle\int_{X\setminus E}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{X\setminus E}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon

and

\displaystyle\int_{A}|u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\liminf_{n\geq 1}\int_{A}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\varepsilon\quad\textrm{for all}\quad\mu(A)<\delta.

Combining these estimates with the previous ones gives

(6.1)

\displaystyle\hskip-4.30554pt\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{X\setminus E}\hskip-12.91663pt|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<2^{p}\varepsilon~~\textrm{and}~~\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{A}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<2^{p}\varepsilon,~\forall~\mu(A)<\delta.

Since
\mu(E)<\infty
, for the choice of
\delta>0
as above the Ergorov’s Theorem
6.14
reveals that there is a measurable set
A\subset E
with
\mu(A)<\delta
such that
(u_{n})_{n}
converges uniformly to
u
on
E\setminus A
, i.e.

\sup\limits_{x\in E\setminus A}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

For the particular choices of
E
and
A
with
\mu(A)<\delta
in combination with the estimates in (
6.1
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{X}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X\setminus E}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int_{A}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{E\setminus A}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\varepsilon+2^{p}\varepsilon+\mu(E\setminus A)\sup_{x\in E\setminus A}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}.

The expected convergence in
L^{p}(X)
thus follows by letting
n\to\infty
and
\varepsilon\to 0
successively.
∎

Remark 6.20
.

Note that the tightness cannot be completely dropped. Indeed on
\mathbb{R}
, if we consider
u_{n}=\mathds{1}_{[n,n+1]}
then
(u_{n})_{n}
converges pointwise everywhere to
u=0
and is uniformly integrable since for each
n\geq 1
and every measurable set
E
with
|E|<\varepsilon
,

\int_{E}u_{n}dx=|E\cap[n,n+1]|\leq|E|<\varepsilon.

Nevertheless,

\displaystyle 1=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}u_{n}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\neq\int_{X}\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

We now visit some consequences of Vitali’s convergence theorem.

Corollary 6.21
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of
L^{p}(X)
with
(1\leq p<\infty)
converging almost everywhere to
u\in L^{p}(X)
. Then
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(X)
if and only if
(|u_{n}|^{p})_{n}
converges to
|u|^{p}
in
L^{1}(X)
.

Proof.
.

The result plainly springs from Theorem
6.19
based on the observation that
(u_{n})_{n}
is
p
-uniformly integrable and
p
-tight if and only if
(|u_{n}|^{p})_{n}
is uniformly integrable and tight.
∎

A deeper version of the above result is the following.

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of
L^{p}(X)
with
(1\leq p<\infty)
converging almost everywhere to
u\in L^{p}(X)
. Then
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(X)
if and only if
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(X)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}
.

Proof.
.

The forward implication is obvious hence let us prove the converse statement.
Assume
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(X)}\to\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}
as
{n\to\infty}
. Put
g_{n}=\max(0,|u|^{p}-|u_{n}|^{p})
. We have
g_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
pointwise almost everywhere,
(g_{n})_{n}
uniformly integrable and tight since
0\leq g_{n}\leq|u|^{p}
for each
n\geq 1
and
|u|^{p}\in L^{1}(X)
. Wherefore, Theorem
6.19
implies

\displaystyle\int_{X}\max(0,|u|^{p}-|u_{n}|^{p})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{X}g_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Taking into account the identity
|u_{n}|^{p}-|u|^{p}=h_{n}-g_{n}
with
h_{n}=\max(0,|u_{n}|^{p}-|u|^{p})
and
g_{n}=\max(0,|u|^{p}-|u_{n}|^{p})
, the assumption also entails

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}h_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}-\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}\big)-\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}g_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

Finally, since
||u_{n}|^{p}-|u|^{p}|=h_{n}+g_{n}=\max(0,|u_{n}|^{p}-|u|^{p})+\max(0,|u|^{p}-|u_{n}|^{p})
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}||u_{n}(x)|^{p}-|u|^{p}(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

That is
(|u_{n}|^{p})_{n}
converges to
|u|^{p}
in
L^{1}(X)
thus by Corollary
6.21

(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{p}(X)
.

Alternatively one may consider the sequence of positive functions
(2^{p-1}(|u_{n}|^{p}+|u|^{p})-|u_{n}-u|^{p})_{n}
which converges almost everywhere to
2^{p}|u|
. Therefore, in view of Fatou’s lemma and the assumption we get

\displaystyle 2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}\big[2^{p-1}(|u_{n}|^{p}+|u|^{p})-|u_{n}-u|^{p}\big]\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=2^{p}\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}-\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}|u_{n}-u|^{p}\big)\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

The result follows since we have shown that
\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(X)}=0
.
∎

The special case
p=1
provides the following well-known and established Scheffé lemma.

Corollary 6.23

(Scheffé Lemma,
[
Wil91
]
, p.55)
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of
L^{1}(X)
converging almost everywhere to
u\in L^{1}(X)
. Then
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{1}(X)
if and only if

\int_{X}|u_{n}(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\int_{X}|u(x)|\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

A typical application of Vitali’s theorem is provided by the next result.

Corollary 6.24
.

Assume
\mu(X)<\infty
and
1<p<\infty
. Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a bounded sequence of
L^{p}(X)

converging almost everywhere to
u
. Then
(u_{n})_{n}
converges to
u
in
L^{r}(X)
for all
1\leq r<p
.

Proof.
.

The
r
-tightness
(u_{n})_{n}
obviously holds true and the
r
-uniform integrability follows from Hölder inequality

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{E}|u_{n}(x)|^{r}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\mu(E)^{1-\frac{r}{p}}\sup_{n\geq 1}\Big(\int_{X}|u_{n}(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)^{r/p}\leq C\mu(E)^{1-\frac{r}{p}}\xrightarrow{\mu(E)\to 0}0.

∎

The celebrated Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem appears as an immediate consequence of Vitali’s convergence theorem.

Theorem 6.25

(Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem)
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a bounded sequence of
L^{p}(X)
with
(1\leq p<\infty)
converging almost everywhere to
u
. Assume that there exists
g\in L^{p}(X)
such that

\sup_{n\geq 1}|u_{n}(x)|\leq g(x)\qquad\textrm{for almost every }\quad x\in X

then
u\in L^{p}(X)
and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{X}|u_{n}(x)-u(x)|^{p}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0.

Proof.
.

The function
g\in L^{p}(X)
is
p
-uniformly integrable and
p
-tight so is
(u_{n})_{n}
because
|u_{n}|\leq g
for all
n\geq 1
and thus the result follows from Theorem
6.19
.
∎

Another consequence of the Vitali theorem is Brezis-Lieb lemma
[
Bre10
, Section 4.5]
is the following.

Theorem 6.26

(Brezis-Lieb lemma)
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a bounded sequence of
L^{p}(X)
with
(1<p<\infty)
converging almost everywhere to
u
.
Then

\displaystyle\|u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\{\|u_{n}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}-\|u_{n}-u\|^{p}_{L^{p}(X)}\big\}.

In particular, if
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{p}(X)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\|u\|_{L^{p}(X)}
then
\|u_{n}-u\|_{L^{p}(X)}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

Proof.
.

Note that boundedness plus almost everywhere convergence imply the weak convergence. Then
|u_{n}-u|\rightharpoonup 0
(weakly) in
L^{p}(X)
and
|u_{n}-u|^{p-1}\rightharpoonup 0
(weakly) in
L^{p^{\prime}}(X)
. One concludes by noticing that There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
a,b\in\R
(taking
a=u_{n}-u
,
b=u
),

\displaystyle||a+b|-|a|-|b||\leq C(|b|^{p-1}a+|a|^{p-1}b).

∎
